On the equivalence of quasirandomness and exchangeable representations independent from lower-order variables
Leonardo N. Coregliano, Henry P. Towsner

Abstract
It is often convenient to represent a process for randomly generating a graph as a graphon. (More precisely, these give vertex exchangeable processes—those processes in which each vertex is treated the same way.) Other structures can be treated by generalizations like hypergraphons, permutatons, and, for a very general class, theons. These representations are not unique: different representations can lead to the same probability distribution on graphs. This naturally leads to questions (going back at least to Hoover’s proof of the Aldous–Hoover Theorem on the existence of such representations) that ask when quasirandomness properties on the distribution guarantee the existence of particularly simple representations. We extend the usual theon representation by adding an additional datum of a random permutation to each tuple, which we call a ∗ \ast -representation. We show that if a process satisfies the unique coupling property UCouple ​ [ ℓ ] \texttt{UCouple}[\ell] , which says roughly that all ℓ \ell -tuples of vertices “look the same”, then the process is ∗ \ast - ℓ \ell -independent: there is a ∗ \ast -representation that does not make use of any random information about ℓ \ell -tuples (including tuples of length < ℓ <\ell ). Simple examples show that the use of ∗ \ast -representations is necessary. This resolves a question of Coregliano and Razborov, since it easily follows that UCouple ​ [ ℓ ] \texttt{UCouple}[\ell] implies Independence ​ [ ℓ ′ ] \texttt{Independence}[\ell^{\prime}] (the existence of an ℓ ′ \ell^{\prime} -independent ordinary representation) for ℓ ′ < ℓ \ell^{\prime}<\ell .



1
Introduction

Consider a rule for randomly generating a graph on a set of vertices
V
:

•

for each vertex
v\in V
we pick a random value
\bm{\omega}_{v}\in[0,1]
uniformly and independently,

•

for each pair of vertices
\{v,w\}\in\binom{V}{2}
we pick a random value

\bm{\omega}_{v,w}\in[0,1]
, also uniformly and independently,

•

we have a measurable set
{\mathcal{N}}_{E}\subseteq[0,1]^{3}
, and we place an edge
1
1

1

Our
notation here foreshadows the general situation, where we might be considering structures with
multiple edge relations simultaneously. In that setting, we will need a different set

{\mathcal{N}}_{E}
for each edge relation
E
.
between the pair
\{v,w\}
if

(\bm{\omega}_{v},\bm{\omega}_{w},\bm{\omega}_{v,w})\in{\mathcal{N}}_{E}
.

Such a construction is sometimes called a
2
-hypergraphon
2
2

2

A
graphon
is a
closely related object: specifically, given
{\mathcal{N}}_{E}
, we may define a graphon

f_{E}\colon[0,1]^{2}\to[0,1]
by
f_{E}(\omega_{v},\omega_{w})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\lambda(\{\omega_{v,w}\mid(\omega_{v},\omega_{w},\omega_{v,w})\in{\mathcal{N}}_{E}\})
, where
\lambda
is the Lebesgue
measure.
. These arise, for instance, as a way to represent the limit of a suitably convergent
sequence of graphs, and they have been studied under using several essentially equivalent
formalisms—exchangeable relations
[
Aus08
,
DJ08
]
, graph limits and graphons and their
generalizations
[
LS06
,
Lov12
,
ES12
,
CR20
]
, flag algebras
[
Raz07
]
and
ultraproducts
[
ES12
,
NOdM12
,
AC14
,
NOdM20
]
. The Aldous–Hoover Theorem
[
Hoo79
,
Ald81
,
Ald85
]
(see
also
[
Kal05
, Chapter 7]
) says that
any
method for randomly generating a graph that
treats the vertices symmetrically (that is, which is
exchangeable
) can be represented as a
distribution over
2
-hypergraphons.

Example 1.1
(The Quasirandom Graph)
.

A fundamental example is the case where
{\mathcal{N}}^{\qr}_{E}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{(\omega_{v},\omega_{w},\omega_{v,w})\mid\omega_{v,w}<1/2\}
. This is essentially the process where we flip a coin for each pair
\{v,w\}

and place an edge if the coin comes up heads.

The representation given by a
2
-hypergraphon is not unique: there are other
2
-hypergraphons that
are essentially the same. For instance, any measure-preserving permutation of
[0,1]
gives us
essentially the same rule—
{\mathcal{N}}^{\qr^{\prime}}_{E}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{(\omega_{v},\omega_{w},\omega_{v,w})\mid\omega_{v,w}<1/4\text{ or }\omega_{v,w}\geq 3/4\}
is not meaningfully different from

{\mathcal{N}}^{\qr}_{E}
.

The more fundamental object is the probability distribution that
{\mathcal{N}}
induces on a set of
vertices. That is, if we fix a finite vertex set
V
and let
{\mathcal{K}}_{V}
be the set of graphs on

V
(with vertices labeled by the elements of
V
),
{\mathcal{N}}
induces a probability
distribution
\mu^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}
on
{\mathcal{K}}_{V}
where the probability of

K\in{\mathcal{K}}_{V}
is precisely the probability that generating a graph using
{\mathcal{N}}
by
the method described above produces the graph
K
. (This idea also makes sense, with slightly more
care about measurability, when
V
is infinite.) For example, when
V
is finite,

\mu^{{\mathcal{N}}^{\qr}}_{V}
is the uniform distribution on (labeled) graphs on
V
.

We can then say that
{\mathcal{N}}
and
{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}
are equivalent if
\mu^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}

and
\mu^{{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}}_{V}
are the same for all finite sets
V
.

The example
{\mathcal{N}}^{\qr}
has a distinctive property: the variables
\omega_{v}
and
\omega_{w}

are not actually used, that is,
{\mathcal{N}}^{\qr}_{E}=[0,1]^{2}\times{\mathcal{H}}^{\qr}_{E}
for a set

{\mathcal{H}}^{\qr}_{E}
. We call this property
1
-independence
, because the
2
-hypergraphon is
independent of the unary variables
\omega_{v},\omega_{w}
.

Complicating matters, a
1
-independent
2
-hypergraphon may be equivalent to one that is not

1
-independent.

Example 1.2.

Consider the rule where
{\mathcal{N}}^{\twist}_{E}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{(\omega_{v},\omega_{w},\omega_{v,w})\mid(\omega_{v}+\omega_{w}+\omega_{v,w})\bmod 1<1/2\}
.

It is not difficult to check that
\mu^{{\mathcal{N}}^{\twist}}_{V}
is the same as
\mu^{{\mathcal{N}}^{\qr}}_{V}
for
every finite set
V
.

To get a property of the underlying object, instead of just the representation, we can say that

{\mathcal{N}}
has
\texttt{Independence}[1]
if it is equivalent to some
1
-independent set. For graphs, there is
not much to say: any
2
-hypergraphon with
\texttt{Independence}[1]
is equivalent to

\{(\omega_{v},\omega_{w},\omega_{v,w})\mid\omega_{v,w}<p\}
for some
p\in[0,1]
. However we will see
that the situation is more complicated when we consider directed graphs and hypergraphs.

The main topic of this paper is understanding how the quasirandomness properties of the measures

(\mu^{\mathcal{N}}_{V})_{V}
relate to whether
\mu^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}
has a
1
-independent—or, more generally,

\ell
-independent—representation. This question was already raised in Hoover’s original
paper
[
Hoo79
]
, where he asks when
{\mathcal{N}}
has a representation omitting some
variables. Nevertheless, our result here is the first general result showing how to take a suitably
quasirandom
(\mu^{\mathcal{N}}_{V})_{V}
and produce a representation omitting some of the variables.

To explain our motivation for doing this, we need to consider what happens when we try generalize
the notion of quasirandomness beyond graphs. For the purposes of the introduction, it will suffice
to consider
3
-hypergraphs. We will let
H
, rather than
E
, be the name of our hypergraph
relation. A
3
-hypergraphon is given by a measurable set
{\mathcal{N}}_{H}\subseteq[0,1]^{7}
. The seven here is
the number of non-empty subsets of a set of three elements
3
3

3

In our general notation below,
where we will consider relations on
d
-tuples, we will write
{\mathcal{N}}_{H}\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}_{k(H)}
. Here

k(H)
is the arity of
H
, in this case
3
, and
{\mathcal{E}}_{k(H)}
will be defined to be

[0,1]^{r(k(H))}
, where
r(k(H))
is the set of non-empty subsets of

[k(H)]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{1,\ldots,k(H)\}
.
. To generate a
3
-hypergraph on
V
, we select random variables

(\bm{\omega}_{v})_{v\in V}
,
(\bm{\omega}_{v,w})_{\{v,w\}\in\binom{V}{2}}
, and

(\bm{\omega}_{v,w,x})_{\{v,w,x\}\in\binom{V}{3}}
uniformly and independently in
[0,1]
—that is,
we choose random variables for each vertex, each unordered pair of vertices, and each unordered
triple of vertices—and we place a
3
-edge on the triple
\{v,w,x\}
if

\displaystyle(\bm{\omega}_{v},\bm{\omega}_{w},\bm{\omega}_{x},\bm{\omega}_{v,w},\bm{\omega}_{v,x},\bm{\omega}_{w,x},\bm{\omega}_{v,w,x})

\displaystyle\in{\mathcal{N}}_{H}.

We abbreviate the long tuple by
(\bm{\omega}_{s})_{s\in r(\{v,w,x\})}
to emphasize that we are indexing
the variables by non-empty subsets of the index set
\{v,w,x\}
, where we write
r(V)
for the set
of non-empty subsets of
V
.

There are a number of ways we might generalize the properties of
{\mathcal{N}}^{\qr}
to a

3
-hypergraph
[
LM15b
,
Tow17
,
AHCH+18
]
. Let us focus just on those ways that
{\mathcal{N}}_{H}
might be
quasirandom relative to the unary data
\{\omega_{v},\omega_{w},\omega_{x}\}
.

Before stating these, it is helpful to generalize the
\mu^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}
notation to allow the possibility
that some of our random variables have already been chosen. Given a finite set
V
and a set

S\subseteq r(V)
, let us write
\mu^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}[(\omega_{s})_{s\in S}]
for the distribution on
{\mathcal{K}}_{V}

given by taking the given values
(\omega_{s})_{s\in S}
, randomly choosing all those
\bm{\omega}_{s}

with
s\in r(V)\setminus S
and placing
(v,w,x)\in H
exactly when
((\omega_{s})_{s\in S},(\bm{\omega}_{s})_{s\in r(V)\setminus S})\in{\mathcal{N}}_{H}
.

Then saying that
{\mathcal{N}}_{H}
is quasirandom could mean:

(1)

\mu^{\mathcal{N}}_{\{v,w,x\}}[(\omega_{v},\omega_{w},\omega_{x})]
is independent of the choice of

\omega_{v},\omega_{w},\omega_{x}
(except on a set of measure
0
). This property is called
\texttt{Disc}[1]
,
standing for “discrepancy”, and has been extensively
studied
[
CGW89
,
KRS02
,
KNRS10
,
LM15a
,
LM15b
,
LM17
,
Tow17
,
AHCH+18
]
.

(2)

For every finite set
V
,
\mu^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}[(\omega_{v})_{v\in V}]
is independent of the choice of

(\omega_{v})_{v\in V}
(except on a set of measure
0
). This property was introduced
in
[
CR23
]
under the name
\texttt{UCouple}[1]
. (This stands for “unique coupling”, a formulation
of this property introduced in that paper and proven equivalent.)

(3)

There is a representation equivalent to
{\mathcal{N}}
of the form
[0,1]^{3}\times{\mathcal{M}}_{H}
. This
property is called
\texttt{Independence}[1]
, and is also introduced in
[
CR23
]
.

The first two properties can be shown to be representation independent. It is not hard to see that
these are ordered from weakest to strongest. For
2
-hypergraphons, these are all
equivalent
4
4

4

This follows as an easy exercise from the equivalence
P_{1}(t)\iff P_{1}(4)\iff P_{4}
of the seminal paper on graph quasirandomness
[
CGW89
]
.
. For
3
-hypergraphs, however, these
notions are all distinct.

To see that the
\texttt{UCouple}[1]
is stronger than
\texttt{Disc}[1]
, consider the following example, slightly
modified from one in
[
CR23
, Theorem 3.15]
.

Example 1.3.

Let
{\mathcal{N}}^{\disc}_{H}
be the set of

(\omega_{v},\omega_{w},\omega_{x},\omega_{v,w},\omega_{v,x},\omega_{w,x},\omega_{v,w,x})
such that
either:

•

\min\{\omega_{v},\omega_{w},\omega_{x}\}<1/2
and
\omega_{v,w,x}<1/2
, or

•

\min\{\omega_{v},\omega_{w},\omega_{x}\}\geq 1/2
and an odd number of the values

\omega_{v,w},\omega_{v,x},\omega_{w,x}
are
<1/2
.

Observe that
\mu^{{\mathcal{N}}^{\disc}}_{\{v,w,x\}}[(\omega_{v},\omega_{w},\omega_{x})]
always places measure

1/2
on the case where the
3
-edge appears. However on a set of four vertices

V=\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}\}
, the distribution is no longer independent of the
\omega_{v}
. For instance,
if
\omega_{v}<1/2
for all
v\in V
then
\mu^{{\mathcal{N}}^{\disc}}_{V}[(\omega_{v})_{v\in V}]
is the uniform
distribution on
{\mathcal{K}}_{V}
, while if
\omega_{v}\geq 1/2
for all
v\in V
then

\mu^{{\mathcal{N}}^{\disc}}_{V}[(\omega_{v})_{v\in V}]
assigns probability
0
to any
3
-hypergraph on
V
with
an odd number of edges.

To see that
\texttt{Independence}[1]
is stronger than
\texttt{UCouple}[1]
, it helps to first consider the random
tournament.

Example 1.4.

{\mathcal{N}}^{\qrT}_{\rightarrow}\subseteq[0,1]^{3}
is the set of triples

(\omega_{v},\omega_{w},\omega_{v,w})
such that exactly one of
\omega_{v}<\omega_{w}
and

\omega_{v,w}<1/2
is true.

We write
\rightarrow
rather than
E
for the name of our relation because it is asymmetric:

\mu^{{\mathcal{N}}^{\qrT}}_{V}
concentrates on structures where, for any pair
\{v,w\}
of distinct vertices,
exactly one of the edges
(v,w)
or
(w,v)
is present—that is, a tournament. In fact, it is the
uniform distribution on tournaments, and further,
\mu^{{\mathcal{N}}^{\qrT}}_{V}((\omega_{v})_{v\in V})
is still
the uniform distribution, no matter what the values
(\omega_{v})_{v\in V}
are, so this has

\texttt{UCouple}[1]
.

On the other hand, any representation of the form
[0,1]^{2}\times{\mathcal{H}}
must concentrate on symmetric
structures, so
{\mathcal{N}}^{\qrT}
cannot have
\texttt{Independence}[1]
. This can be extended to an example on

3
-hypergraphs that similarly has
\texttt{UCouple}[1]
but not
\texttt{Independence}[1]
, as is done
in
[
CR23
, Theorem 3.8]
: for instance, take
{\mathcal{N}}^{\cycle}_{H}
to be the
3
-hypergraphon
consisting of triples on which
{\mathcal{N}}^{\qrT}_{\rightarrow}
would induce a cycle.

The first author and Razborov
[
CR23
]
introduced
\texttt{UCouple}[1]
and
\texttt{Independence}[1]
, as well
as higher-arity generalizations, and settled most questions about which combinations are possible,
leaving only the question of whether
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
implies
\texttt{Independence}[\ell^{\prime}]
when

\ell^{\prime}<\ell
. Our original motivation was settling this implication: given
{\mathcal{N}}_{H}

satisfying
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
, we show how to construct an equivalent representation satisfying

\texttt{Independence}[\ell^{\prime}]
—that is, a representation that does not use any of the variables
\omega_{s}

with
\lvert s\rvert\leq\ell^{\prime}
.

Our approach begins by looking at the proof that the quasirandom tournament does not have

\texttt{Independence}[1]
. That argument turned on a quirk of the representation: the binary data in a

2
-hypergraphon is intrinsically symmetric, forcing us to use the unary data to encode the
asymmetry of the quasirandom tournament. However, like the quasirandom graph, the quasirandom
tournament seems like an essentially binary object, the representation just has no way to express
that.

Facing a similar issue, Crane and the second author proposed
[
CT18
]
a modified representation
that adds in an antisymmetric datum at each level. For binary relations, our modified representation
takes
{\mathcal{N}}_{E}
to be a subset of
[0,1]^{3}\times{\mathcal{O}}_{\{v,w\}}
, where
{\mathcal{O}}_{\{v,w\}}
is just the set
of orders of
\{v,w\}
with the uniform distribution. For example, in this modified representation,
we can take
{\mathcal{N}}^{\qrT}_{\rightarrow}
to be
\{(\omega_{v},\omega_{w},\omega_{v,w},<_{v,w})\mid v<_{v,w}w\}
. We should view
(\omega_{v},\omega_{w})
as the unary data and
(\omega_{v,w},<_{v,w})
as
the binary data, so this gives a representation of the quasirandom tournament that is independent of
the unary data, but only using our modified representation. We will call this a

\ast
-
1
-independent representation. (We could, in fact, add the requirement that
{\mathcal{N}}_{E}
satisfy
a suitable symmetry property in the
\omega_{s}
variables, essentially requiring that
all
the
asymmetry be expressed by the
<_{v,w}
variables, but since we do not need this restriction, we do
not impose it here.)

The bulk of this paper is devoted to showing, as part of Theorem
2.2
, that

\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
is
equivalent
to the existence of a
\ast
-
\ell
-independent
representation. This shows that the quasirandom tournament is the essential difference between

\texttt{UCouple}[1]
and
\texttt{Independence}[1]
, and similarly a “quasirandom orientation” describes the gap between

\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
and
\texttt{Independence}[\ell]
. We also show, in Corollary
3.8
,
that we can easily convert a
\ast
-
\ell
-independent representation into an
\ell^{\prime}
-independent
representation for any
\ell^{\prime}<\ell
(because we can use any lower arity data, not just the unary
data, to simulate the asymmetry).

Our construction begins with the following observation. When we have a representation
{\mathcal{N}}_{H}
and
choose a value
\omega_{v}
for some vertex
v
, we determine, in some probabilistic sense, how
v

will behave in the broader structure—for instance
\omega_{v}
determines the degree of
v
(with
high probability, up to a small error). However different values of
\omega_{v}
might determine the
same behavior for
v
; for instance, in
{\mathcal{N}}^{\qr}
,
\omega_{v}
is entirely ignored—there is
only one “type” of point. In the twisted representation, the same thing is true: even though the
value
\omega_{v}
appears in the definition of
{\mathcal{N}}^{\twist}_{E}
, it does not ultimately affect the
behavior of
v
: every vertex “looks the same”, no matter what
\omega_{v}
was. On the other hand,
there are exactly two “types” of pairs—the types with an edge and the types without.

We construct a new representation in which we randomly choose, for each tuple, the type that tuple
should have. To say this slightly more formally, the shape of our construction will be as follows:

•

for each
\ell
, we will define a space
\Lambda_{\ell}
of types of
\ell
-tuples,

•

for each finite set
A
, we will define a function

\mathbbm{p}^{\mathcal{N}}_{A}\colon[0,1]^{r(\ell)}\to\Lambda_{\lvert A\rvert}

mapping the random data
(\omega_{s})_{s\in r(A)}
to the type in
\Lambda_{\lvert A\rvert}
that

{\mathcal{N}}
determines on the data
(\omega_{s})_{s\in r(A)}
.

Under these definitions,
{\mathcal{N}}
determines a probability measure on
\Lambda_{1}
,
{\mathcal{N}}
together with
a pair
(p,p^{\prime})\in\Lambda_{1}^{2}
determines a probability measure on
\Lambda_{2}
, and so on. We can
identify these with
[0,1]
by choosing measure-preserving functions
u\colon[0,1]\to\Lambda_{1}
,

u_{p,p^{\prime}}\colon[0,1]\to\Lambda_{2}
, and so on. Then our new representation is given by taking

p_{v}=u(\omega_{v})
,
p_{v,w}=u_{u(\omega_{v}),u(\omega_{w})}(\omega_{v,w})
, and similarly at higher
arity. We need the data from
<_{u,v}
to break symmetry: if
p_{v},p_{w}
are the same then

\omega_{v,w}
can determine something about the unordered set
\{v,w\}
, but if it specifies
something asymmetric—say, that exactly one of the two edges
(v,w)
and
(w,v)
is present—then
our representation needs the asymmetric datum
<_{v,w}
to choose among the ways to resolve the
asymmetry.

It would be trivial to just take
\Lambda_{\ell}=[0,1]^{r(\ell)}
with
\mathbbm{p}^{\mathcal{N}}_{\ell}
as the
identity. However, since we want to extract a
\ast
-
\ell
-independent representation from this, we
want to show that
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
implies that there is only one type of
\ell
-tuple. This will
force us to choose a definition in which the type does not contain unused information—points which
behave the same way really should get the same type.

On the other hand, our notion of type has to contain enough information to reconstruct

\mu^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}
. Certainly this means that
\mathbbm{p}^{\mathcal{N}}_{k(H)}({\mathcal{N}}_{H})
has to be disjoint from

\mathbbm{p}^{\mathcal{N}}_{k(H)}([0,1]^{r(k(H))}\setminus{\mathcal{N}}_{H})
—that is, a
k(H)
-tuple that is an edge needs to
have a different type from one that does not have an edge. Slightly more generally, points that lead
to different distributions need to have different types: that is, if
\mu^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}[(\omega_{s})_{s\in r(A)}]\neq\mu^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}[(\omega^{\prime}_{s})_{s\in r(A)}]
for some finite set
V
containing
A
, then we
need to have
\mathbbm{p}^{\mathcal{N}}_{A}((\omega_{s})_{s\in r(A)})\neq\mathbbm{p}^{\mathcal{N}}_{A}((\omega^{\prime}_{s})_{s\in r(A)})
.

This gives our first real candidate for the definition of a type, which we will call a
weak
type
: we could take
\mathbbm{p}^{\weak,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A}((\omega_{s})_{s\in r(A)})
to be
(\mu^{\mathcal{N}}_{A\cup B}[(\omega_{s})_{s\in r(A)}])_{B\text{ a finite set}}
. That is, two tuples have the same weak type
if, whenever we have a finite set containing that tuple, the distributions of structures on that set
are the same
5
5

5

The reader familiarized with flag algebras should note that this is almost a
flag algebra homomorphism extension. More specifically, if
\phi
is the flag algebra homomorphism
corresponding to
{\mathcal{N}}
, then the flag algebra extension
\bm{\phi^{\sigma}}
corresponds to the

distribution of the weak type

\mathbbm{p}^{\weak,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A}((\bm{\omega}_{s})_{s\in r(A)})
when

(\bm{\omega}_{s})_{s\in r(A)}
is picked uniformly at random conditioned to

\mu^{{\mathcal{N}}_{H}}_{A}[(\bm{\omega}_{s})_{s\in r(A)}]
being concentrated on
\sigma
.
.

A standard example shows that this is insufficient.

Example 1.5
(Complete Bipartite Graph)
.

Let
{\mathcal{N}}^{\bp}_{E}
be the set of
(\omega_{v},\omega_{w},\omega_{v,w})
such that exactly one of

\omega_{v}
and
\omega_{w}
is
<1/2
.

The random structure generated by
\mu^{{\mathcal{N}}^{\bp}}_{V}
is a complete bipartite graph, with each vertex
having equal chance to belong to each of the parts. Since the parts are unlabeled, there is only one
possible value of
\mu^{{\mathcal{N}}^{\bp}}_{\{v\}\cup B}[\omega_{v}]
. However there is no way to
replicate
{\mathcal{N}}^{\bp}_{E}
with only one type of point: our construction should have two types
of points, corresponding to the two parts of the bipartition.

Formally, what goes wrong is a failure of
amalgamation
. If we know the types of
v
and
w
,
they should determine a distribution on the possible types of the pair
\{v,w\}
. That means that if
we have two different pairs,
\omega_{v},\omega_{w}
and
\omega^{\prime}_{v},\omega^{\prime}_{w}
that lead to the same
pair of types—that is,
\mathbbm{p}^{\weak,{\mathcal{N}}}_{\{v\}}(\omega_{v})=\mathbbm{p}^{\weak,{\mathcal{N}}}_{\{v\}}(\omega^{\prime}_{v})
and

\mathbbm{p}^{\weak,{\mathcal{N}}}_{\{w\}}(\omega_{w})=\mathbbm{p}^{\weak,{\mathcal{N}}}_{\{w\}}(\omega^{\prime}_{w})
—then we need the distribution on types of pairs
extending
\omega_{v},\omega_{w}
to be the same as the distribution on types of pairs extending

\omega^{\prime}_{v},\omega^{\prime}_{w}
.

With weak types for
{\mathcal{N}}^{\bp}_{E}
, this fails: we have

\mathbbm{p}^{\weak,{\mathcal{N}}^{\bp}}_{\{v\}}(1/4)=\mathbbm{p}^{\weak,{\mathcal{N}}^{\bp}}_{\{v\}}(3/4)
, but the pair
(1/4,1/4)
needs to
place measure
1
on the
2
-type that is not an edge, while the pair
(1/4,3/4)
needs to place
measure
1
on the
2
-type that is an edge.

Our types need more information than weak types. To do this, we want to replace the distribution

\mu^{\mathcal{N}}_{A\cup B}[(\omega_{s})_{s\in r(A)}]
with some pointwise information. We define the

dissociated type

\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A}((\omega_{s})_{s\in r(A)})
to be the family of
functions

\displaystyle(\omega_{s})_{s\in r(B)}\mapsto\mu^{\mathcal{N}}_{A\cup B}[(\omega_{s})_{s\in r(A)\cup r(B)}],

where
B
ranges over finite sets. That is, in order to have the same type, two sets of values

\{\omega_{s}\}_{s\in r(A)}
,
\{\omega^{\prime}_{s}\}_{s\in r(A)}
not only need to lead to the same
distribution on
{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup B}
, they need to lead to the same distribution for each fixed
set of data on
B
. (We call these dissociated types because we look at data on
A
and
B

separately, but not at any data on sets overlapping
A
and
B
.)
6
6

6

There is a very close
analogy to the situation in model theory: types over a set are not well-behaved. Our solution is
analogous to considering only types over
models
: allowing data from additional sets
B

amounts to letting us name additional fixed elements which we use, for example, to distinguish the
equivalence classes. In the model theoretic setting, a great deal of work has gone into replacing
types over models with notions of strong types, which add only enough information to resolve these
obstacles. Hrushovski has asked whether there is a way to transfer the model theoretic
approach
[
Hru22
]
.

This will turn out to be the correct definition for
1
-types, and we can prove that amalgamation
holds going from
1
-types to
2
-types: if

\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{\{v\}}(\omega_{v})=\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{\{v\}}(\omega^{\prime}_{v})

and

\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{\{w\}}(\omega_{w})=\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{\{w\}}(\omega^{\prime}_{w})

then
\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}_{H}}_{\{v,w\}}(\omega_{v},\omega_{w},\bm{\omega}_{v,w})
and

\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}_{H}}_{\{v,w\}}(\omega^{\prime}_{v},\omega^{\prime}_{w},\bm{\omega}_{v,w})
have the same
distribution when
\bm{\omega}_{v,w}
is picked uniformly at random in
[0,1]
.

Unfortunately, the next step of amalgamation fails, as the following example illustrates.

Example 1.6.

Let
{\mathcal{N}}^{\odd}_{H}
consist of those
(\omega_{s})_{s\in r(3)}
such that an odd number
of the values
\omega_{1,2},\omega_{1,3},\omega_{2,3}
are
<1/2
.

This has a unique dissociated
2
-type
7
7

7

This representation is
1
-independent, so we will
ignore the unary data. We need to show that

\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}^{\odd}}_{\{v,w\}}(\omega_{v,w})=\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}^{\odd}}_{\{v,w\}}(\omega^{\prime}_{v,w})

for any
\omega_{v,w},\omega^{\prime}_{v,w}
. Obviously the interesting case is when, without loss of
generality,
\omega_{v,w}<1/2
while
\omega^{\prime}_{v,w}\geq 1/2
. In this case, observe that, for any
finite set
B
and data
\{\omega_{s}\}_{s\in r(\{v,w\}\cup B)}
, replacing
\omega_{v,w}
with

\omega^{\prime}_{v,w}
and also replacing each
\omega_{s}
with
s\cap\{v,w\}=\{v\}
with
1/2-\omega_{s}

will generate the same structure. This is a measure-preserving transformation on

\{\omega_{s}\}_{s\in r(\{v,w\}\cup B)\setminus(r(\{v,w\})\cup r(B))}
, so in particular shows that

\mu^{\mathcal{N}}_{\{v,w\}\cup B}[(\omega_{v,w},(\omega_{s})_{s\in r(B)})]=\mu^{\mathcal{N}}_{\{v,w\}\cup B}[(\omega^{\prime}_{v,w},(\omega_{s})_{s\in r(B)})]
.
. However, the
3
-type is completely determined by
the information at the
2
-ary level: for instance, the values
(1/4,1/4,3/4)
and
(1/4,3/4,3/4)

for
(\omega_{1,2},\omega_{1,3},\omega_{2,3})
lead to different distributions on
3
-types.

We conclude that dissociated types still do not contain enough information when the arity is greater
than
1
. Examining the proof that
1
-types can amalgamate to
2
-types suggested the following
strengthening, which we call
overlapping
types.
\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A}((\omega_{s})_{s\in r(A)})

to be the family of functions

\displaystyle(\omega_{s})_{s\in r(A\cup B),A\not\subseteq s\not\subseteq A}\mapsto\mu^{\mathcal{N}}_{A\cup B}[(\omega_{s})_{s\in r(A\cup B),A\not\subseteq A},\omega_{A}],

where
B
ranges over finite sets. That is, we now consider more pointwise information, namely all
the data which overlaps some
but not all
of
A
. (Including any
\omega_{s}
with
A\subseteq s
would certainly be too much information, because it would amount to keeping all the data of the
original representation regarding
A
, leading to failure of uniqueness of
\ell
-types for

\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
.) This gives the same definition of a
1
-type, but adds in more information at
higher arity.

This definition works for
{\mathcal{N}}^{\odd}
—there are now two overlapping
2
-types, as there should
be, depending on whether
\omega_{v,w}
is
<1/2
or
\geq 1/2
. We also get some progress towards
amalgamation: overlapping types amalgamate to dissociated types, in any arity. That is, if we have
two
k
-tuples
(\omega_{s})_{s\in r(k)\setminus\{[k]\}}
missing only the top variable and such that,
for each
(k-1)
-tuple, the overlapping types are the same, then the distribution on dissociated

k
-types obtained by choosing
\bm{\omega}_{[k]}
uniformly at random in
[0,1]
is the same.

However the overlapping type has too much information. For example,
{\mathcal{N}}^{\twist}
has many
overlapping
2
-types; indeed, if
(\omega_{v},\omega_{w},\omega_{v,w})
and

(\omega^{\prime}_{v},\omega^{\prime}_{w},\omega^{\prime}_{v,w})
have the same overlapping
2
-type in
{\mathcal{N}}^{\twist}
then not
only does
\omega_{v}+\omega_{w}+\omega_{v,w}<1/2
if and only if

\omega^{\prime}_{v}+\omega^{\prime}_{w}+\omega^{\prime}_{v,w}<1/2
, but
\omega_{v}=\omega^{\prime}_{v}
and
\omega_{w}=\omega^{\prime}_{w}
as
well
8
8

8

This is because the overlapping
2
-type of
(\omega_{v},\omega_{w})
knows for each

(\omega_{u},\omega_{v,u},\omega_{w,u})
whether
(\omega_{v},\omega_{u},\omega_{v,u})
and

(\omega_{w},\omega_{u},\omega_{w,u})
are edges, so it can subtract out
\omega_{u}+\omega_{v,u}
and

\omega_{u}+\omega_{w,u}
modulo
1
to determine
\omega_{v}
and
\omega_{w}
, respectively.
: the
overlapping
2
-type has completely learned the pointwise
1
-ary data, which is information about
the individual points
v
and
w
that wasn’t present in the overlapping
1
-types. To see why this
is a failure of amalgamation, note that since
{\mathcal{N}}^{\twist}
has a unique overlapping
1
-type
(recall that overlapping and dissociated
1
-types are the same) and since the overlapping
2
-type
knows full pointwise
1
-ary information, changing
1
-ary point data changes the overlapping

2
-type distribution without changing the overlapping
1
-types.

One of our guiding principles is that the right definition ought to properly stratify information:
all unary information should show up in the
1
-types, all binary information in the
2
-types, and
so on. But this example shows that overlapping
2
-types can contain new unary information that
wasn’t already present in the
1
-types.

Our final notion, which we call an
amalgamating type
, is in between a dissociated type and an
overlapping type. Essentially, we want it to be a dissociated type together with all the information
from the overlapping type which isn’t really lower arity data. Informally, it is essentially a pair
consisting of the dissociated type together with a function taking any
(\omega^{\prime}_{s})_{s\in r(A)\setminus\{A\}}
consistent with the dissociated type to an overlapping type consistent with

(\omega^{\prime}_{s})_{s\in r(A)\setminus\{A\}}
.

That is, the amalgamating type does not know what the overlapping type is, because that would be too
much information. But it knows everything about the overlapping type that isn’t too much
information, because once you tell it the extra information, it can recover the overlapping type.

In the case of
2
-types, this works roughly as follows. Given
\omega_{v},\omega_{w},\omega_{v,w}
, we
will choose a dissociated
2
-type, which includes a choice of dissociated
1
-types. We then also
want to remember as much as we can about the overlapping
2
-type while still forgetting the
specific values
\omega_{v},\omega_{w}
. Therefore, we will remember a recipe that says “for any

\omega^{\prime}_{v},\omega^{\prime}_{w}
consistent with my
1
-types, I know which overlapping
2
-type I would have
had if my unary data had been
\omega^{\prime}_{v},\omega^{\prime}_{w}
”.

It is not immediately obvious that such a function makes sense, but its existence almost falls out
of the fact that we have amalgamation. Consider any
\omega^{\prime}_{v},\omega^{\prime}_{w}
with the same dissociated

1
-types as
\omega_{v},\omega_{w}
. Amalgamation tells us that the distribution of dissociated

2
-types is the same. That is, there is a measure-preserving function
9
9

9

This is not quite
correct, but for simplicity, we will assume this in the introduction only.

f_{\omega_{v},\omega_{w},\omega^{\prime}_{v},\omega^{\prime}_{w}}\colon[0,1]\to[0,1]
so that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{\{v,w\}}(\omega_{v},\omega_{w},\omega_{v,w})=\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{\{v,w\}}(\omega^{\prime}_{v},\omega^{\prime}_{w},f_{\omega_{v},\omega_{w},\omega^{\prime}_{v},\omega^{\prime}_{w}}(\omega_{v,w})).

Therefore we would like to take the amalgamating
2
-type of
(\omega_{v},\omega_{w},\omega_{v,w})
to
be the function

\displaystyle(\omega^{\prime}_{v},\omega^{\prime}_{w})\mapsto\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{\{v,w\}}(\omega^{\prime}_{v},\omega^{\prime}_{w},f_{\omega_{v},\omega_{w},\omega^{\prime}_{v},\omega^{\prime}_{w}}(\omega_{v,w}))

defined on those
\omega^{\prime}_{v},\omega^{\prime}_{w}
with the correct dissociated
1
-types.

There are some additional measure-theoretic formalities to make this work correctly. For example, we
need the functions
f_{\omega_{v},\omega_{w},\omega^{\prime}_{v},\omega^{\prime}_{w}}
to satisfy a cocycle condition

f_{\omega^{\prime}_{v},\omega^{\prime}_{w},\omega^{\prime\prime}_{v},\omega^{\prime\prime}_{w}}\circ f_{\omega_{v},\omega_{w},\omega^{\prime}_{v},\omega^{\prime}_{w}}=f_{\omega_{v},\omega_{w},\omega^{\prime\prime}_{v},\omega^{\prime\prime}_{w}}
. Therefore it
is more natural to identify some target space
\Lambda
, obtain almost everywhere bijective
functions
f_{\omega_{v},\omega_{w}}\colon[0,1]\to\Lambda
, and then set

f_{\omega_{v},\omega_{w},\omega^{\prime}_{v},\omega^{\prime}_{w}}=f^{-1}_{\omega^{\prime}_{v},\omega^{\prime}_{w}}\circ f_{\omega_{v},\omega_{w}}
. Some work is needed to identify the target space in a way that depends
measurably on
\omega_{v},\omega_{w}
.

Nevertheless, after solving the measure-theoretic issues, this definition suffices: amalgamating

(k-1)
-types do determine the distribution of amalgamating
k
-types
(Lemma
9.5
(iii)
) and
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
implies that there is only one
amalgamating
\ell
-type (Remark
9.3
). (Actually, we are not quite able to
prove the second: we need the additional assumption that the representation is

(\ell-1)
-independent; however this weaker result is sufficient because we can iterate the
construction.)

1.1
Organization

In Section
2
, we setup basic notation and definitions and formally state our main
theorems formally.

Section
3
is devoted to the concept of
d
-realizations, which allows us to transform
a representation that uses order variables into one that does not use order variables at the expense
of at most one level of independence (Corollary
3.8
).

Section
4
is devoted to extending the characterization of when two
representations are equivalent to cover order variables (Theorem
4.2
). As a consequence,
we will derive that if
{\mathcal{N}}
is
\ast
-
\ell
-independent, then it must satisfy
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]

(Proposition
4.4
).

In Section
5
, we begin our journey to prove the converse direction, by formalizing the
notions of dissociated and overlapping types and proving fundamental properties about them,
culminating in the proof of uniqueness of overlapping
\ell
-types in
(\ell-1)
-independent
representations (Proposition
5.8
); this uniqueness will be inherited by
amalgamating
\ell
-types later.

In Section
6
, we address measurability within the space of dissociated/overlapping types
by constructing a Polish topology over these spaces, so that equipping the space with the Borel

\sigma
-algebra turns it into a standard Borel space (Proposition
6.6
). This allows
us to make sense of a “random type”.

Section
7
is devoted to proving the aforementioned “defective amalgamation”
(Proposition
7.4
), that is, that overlapping types amalgamate to
dissociated types; this will serve as a building block for the final construction of amalgamating
types in Section
9
.

However, before that, Section
8
proves a technical measure-theoretic lemma
(Lemma
8.3
) that will provide all the measurability and measure-preserving properties
required for the formalization of amalgamating types.

In Section
9
, we finally define amalgamating types (Definition
9.2
) and
prove that every
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
object has a
\ast
-
\ell
-independent representation
(Proposition
9.7
).

In Section
10
, we show that every
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
object is the reduct of an independent
coupling of an
\texttt{Independence}[\ell]
object with a quasirandom
(\ell+1)
-orientation
(Proposition
10.1
). This is the formalization of the statement that
“the gap between
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
and
\texttt{Independence}[\ell]
is a quasirandom orientation”.

We conclude the paper with some final remarks and open problems in Section
11
.

More details on the location of the proofs of our main theorems can be found in
Table
1
near the end of Section
2
.

2
Notation, basic definitions and main theorems

In this section, we establish basic notation and provide all basic definitions required to formally
state our main results.

We denote the set of non-negative integers by
\mathbb{N}
and the set of positive integers by
\mathbb{N}_{+}
. For

k\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
[k]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{1,\ldots,k\}
. The set of injective functions from
A
to
B
is denoted

(B)_{A}
and we use the shorthand
(B)_{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(B)_{[k]}
when
k\in\mathbb{N}
. The symmetric group on a set

B
is denoted
S_{B}
and we also use the shorthand
S_{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}S_{[k]}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
(we also identify

S_{k}=([k])_{k}
). For sets
A\subseteq B
, we let
\iota_{A,B}\colon A\to B
be the inclusion map.

For an injection
\alpha\colon A\to B
, we let
\overline{\alpha}\colon A\to\im(\alpha)
be the
bijection obtained by restricting the codomain of
\alpha
to its image
\im(\alpha)
. As usual, if

U\subseteq A
, we denote the restriction of
\alpha
to
U
by
\alpha\mathord{|}_{U}\colon U\to B
, but we
will also use the notation
\alpha\mathord{\downarrow}_{U}\colon U\to\alpha(U)
for the bijection obtained from

\alpha
by restricting its domain to
U
and its codomain to
\alpha(U)
(i.e.,

\alpha\mathord{\downarrow}_{U}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\overline{\alpha\mathord{|}_{U}}
). Furthermore, if
A
and
B
are both disjoint from a
set
V
, we let
\alpha\mathord{\uparrow}_{V}\colon A\cup V\to B\cup V
be the extension of
\alpha
that acts
identically on
V
.

For
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and a set
V
, we let
\binom{V}{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{A\subseteq V\mid\lvert A\rvert=\ell\}

be the set of subsets of
V
of size
\ell
, we let
\binom{V}{>\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{A\subseteq V\mid\lvert A\rvert>\ell\}
, we let
r(V,\ell)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\bigcup_{1\leq k\leq\ell}\binom{V}{k}
and we let

r(V)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\bigcup_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}\binom{V}{\ell}
be the set of
finite
non-empty subsets of
V
.

By Borel space, we mean a standard Borel space, that is, a measurable space
(X,{\mathcal{B}})
, where
X
can
be equipped with a Polish space structure such that
{\mathcal{B}}
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of the
corresponding topology; however, we will typically omit
{\mathcal{B}}
from the notation. The set of
probability measures on a Borel space
X
is denoted by
{\mathcal{P}}(X)
. By standard probability space, we
mean a probability space whose underlying measurable space is a (standard) Borel space. (Finite or)
countable products of Borel spaces will always be equipped with the product (Borel)

\sigma
-algebra. Random variables will always be typeset in bold font. We will denote the Lebesgue
measure by
\lambda
(with a small abuse of notation, we will use
\lambda
for the Lebesgue measure
in any number of dimensions and even for a countable product of one-dimensional Lebesgue measures).

For a relational language
{\mathcal{L}}
and a predicate symbol
P\in{\mathcal{L}}
, we denote the
arity
of
P

by
k(P)\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
. A structure
M
in
{\mathcal{L}}
is
canonical
if
P^{M}\subseteq(V(M))_{k(P)}
for
every
P\in{\mathcal{L}}
, that is, if all its predicates can only hold in injective tuples. A universal
theory is
canonical
if all of its models are canonical. Since every universal theory is
isomorphic (via a quantifier-free definition) to a canonical universal theory
(see
[
CR20
, Theorem 2.3]
, where quantifier-free definitions are called open interpretations),

all of our structures will be assumed to be canonical unless explicitly stated otherwise
. We
denote the set of (canonical) structures in
{\mathcal{L}}
over a set
V
by
{\mathcal{K}}_{V}[{\mathcal{L}}]
and for a canonical
theory
T
, the set of models of
T
over a set
V
is denoted
{\mathcal{K}}_{V}[T]
; we will omit
{\mathcal{L}}
and

T
from the notation when these are clear from the context. The
pure canonical theory
in

{\mathcal{L}}
is the universal theory
T_{\mathcal{L}}
whose models are all canonical structures in
{\mathcal{L}}
(i.e.,

{\mathcal{K}}_{V}[T_{\mathcal{L}}]={\mathcal{K}}_{V}[{\mathcal{L}}]
). When
V
is (finite or) countable, we equip
{\mathcal{K}}_{V}
with the

\sigma
-algebra generated by cylinder sets, that is, sets of the form

\displaystyle C(U,K)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{M\in{\mathcal{K}}_{V}\mid M\mathord{|}_{U}=K\}

(2.1)

for some finite set
U\subseteq V
and some
N\in{\mathcal{K}}_{U}
; this makes
{\mathcal{K}}_{V}
a Borel space.

2.1
Contra-variance and equivariance

Throughout this article, we will define several classes of objects
X_{A}
indexed by countable sets

A
and most of the time, whenever
\alpha\colon A\to B
is an injection, there will a
contra-variantly defined map
\alpha^{*}\colon X_{B}\to X_{A}
in the sense that
\id_{A}^{*}
is the identity
map on
X_{A}
and
(\beta\mathbin{\circ}\alpha)^{*}=\alpha^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\beta^{*}
whenever
\alpha\colon A\to B
and

\beta\colon B\to C
are injective.

Formally, what is going on here is that there is a subcategory
{\mathcal{X}}
of
Set
whose objects are
precisely the
X_{A}
and there is a contra-variant functor
F_{{\mathcal{X}}}\colon\textsc{Inj}\to{\mathcal{X}}
given by

F_{{\mathcal{X}}}(\alpha)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\alpha^{*}
, where
Inj
is the subcategory of
Set
whose objects are the
countable sets and whose morphisms are the injective functions. However, since what will be
important is the functor
F_{{\mathcal{X}}}
rather than the precise morphisms of
{\mathcal{X}}
, we will abbreviate
this abstract non-sense by simply saying that every
\alpha\colon A\to B

contra-variantly

defines the map
\alpha^{*}\colon X_{B}\to X_{A}
. Technically, we should add
{\mathcal{X}}
to the notation

\alpha^{*}
, but since
{\mathcal{X}}
will be indicated by the domain and co-domain of the function, we opt
for the simpler notation.

A few important examples are the following:

I.

If
Z
is a set, then every injection
\alpha\colon A\to B
between countable sets
contra-variantly induces a map
\alpha^{*}\colon Z^{B}\to Z^{A}
given by

\displaystyle\alpha^{*}(z)_{t}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}z_{\alpha(t)}\qquad(z\in Z^{B},t\in A).

Recalling that
\overline{\alpha}\colon A\to\im(\alpha)
is the restriction of the codomain of

\alpha
to its image and that
\iota_{\im(\alpha),B}\colon\im(\alpha)\to B
is the inclusion map,
note that
\alpha^{*}=\overline{\alpha}^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\iota_{\im(\alpha),B}^{*}
due to

\alpha=\iota_{\im(\alpha),B}\mathbin{\circ}\overline{\alpha}
and contra-variance.

II.

If
Z
is a set and for each countable set
A
, we have a collection

c(A)\subseteq 2^{A}
of subsets of
A
such that
\alpha(c(A))\subseteq c(B)
whenever

\alpha\colon A\to B
is an injection, then there is a contra-variantly defined
\alpha^{*}\colon Z^{c(B)}\to Z^{c(A)}
given by

\displaystyle\alpha^{*}(z)_{C}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}z_{\alpha(C)}\qquad(z\in Z^{c(B)},C\in c(A)).

Examples of
c(A)
with the required property include
r(A)
,
r(A,\ell)
for some fixed
\ell
,

\binom{A}{>\ell}
for some fixed
\ell
, etc.

III.

If
Z
is a Borel space, then every injection
\alpha\colon A\to B
between
countable sets contra-variantly induces a map
\alpha^{*}\colon{\mathcal{P}}(Z^{B})\to{\mathcal{P}}(Z^{A})
, where for

\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}(Z^{B})
,
\alpha^{*}(\mu)
is the pushforward measure under the map
\alpha^{*}\colon Z^{B}\to Z^{A}
of item
(I)
above:

\displaystyle\alpha^{*}(\mu)(U)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mu((\alpha^{*})^{-1}(U))=\mu(\{z\in Z^{B}\mid\alpha^{*}(z)\in U\}).

(Typically, the pushforward construction is co-variant, but since this is the pushforward of the
contra-variant
\alpha^{*}
of item
(I)
, the composed functor is contra-variant.)

IV.

For a fixed finite relational language
{\mathcal{L}}
, every injection
\alpha\colon A\to B
between countable sets contra-variantly induces a map
\alpha^{*}\colon{\mathcal{K}}_{B}\to{\mathcal{K}}_{A}
given
by

\displaystyle P^{\alpha^{*}(M)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{\beta\in A^{k(P)}\mid\alpha\mathbin{\circ}\beta\in P^{M}\}\qquad(P\in{\mathcal{L}}),

where
k(P)\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
is the arity of
P\in{\mathcal{L}}
, that is,
\alpha^{*}(M)
is the unique structure in

{\mathcal{K}}_{A}
such that
\alpha
is an embedding of
M
in
\alpha^{*}(M)
.

V.

Building on items
(III)
and
(IV)
, for a finite relational language

{\mathcal{L}}
, an injection
\alpha\colon A\to B
between countable sets contra-variantly induces a map

\alpha^{*}\colon{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{K}}_{B})\to{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{K}}_{A})
defined by letting
\alpha^{*}(\mu)
be the distribution of

\alpha^{*}(\bm{M})
for the
\alpha^{*}\colon{\mathcal{K}}_{B}\to{\mathcal{K}}_{A}
of item
(IV)
, where
\bm{M}

is sampled in
{\mathcal{K}}_{B}
according to
\mu
. Note that in this language, an exchangeable distribution
is simply a
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{K}}_{A})
such that
\sigma^{*}(\mu)=\mu
for every
\sigma\in S_{A}
.

VI.

In a similar fashion to item
(II)
, suppose we already have contra-variantly
defined
\alpha^{*}\colon X_{B}\to X_{A}
for every injection
\alpha\colon A\to B
and suppose for each
countable set
A
, we have a collection
c(A)\subseteq 2^{A}
of subsets of
A
such that

\alpha(c(A))\subseteq c(B)
whenever
\alpha\colon A\to B
is an injection.

Then we have a further contra-variantly defined

\displaystyle\alpha^{*}\colon\prod_{C\in c(B)}X_{C}\to\prod_{C\in c(A)}X_{C}

given by

\displaystyle\alpha^{*}(x)_{C}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\alpha\mathord{\downarrow}_{C}^{*}(x_{C})\qquad\left(x\in\prod_{D\in c(B)}X_{D},C\in c(A)\right).

(Recall that
\alpha\mathord{\downarrow}_{C}\colon C\to\alpha(C)
is the bijective restriction of
\alpha
to
U
.)

We will also perform several constructions
G_{A}\colon X_{A}\to X^{\prime}_{A}
, where
X_{A}
is an object of

{\mathcal{X}}
and
X^{\prime}_{A}
is an object of
{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}
and most of the time, these constructions will be

equivariant
in the sense that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}_{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{A}}

is commutative for every injection
\alpha\colon A\to B
. Formally, what is going on here is that
the collection of maps
(G_{A})_{A}
is a natural transformation between the contra-variant functors

F_{{\mathcal{X}}}\to F_{{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}}
, but we will abbreviate this abstract non-sense by simply saying that the
construction
G_{A}
is
equivariant
.

One example of equivariance already appeared in the introduction: for a
k
-hypergraphon

{\mathcal{N}}\subseteq[0,1]^{r(k)}
and a countable set
A
, we implicitly defined a function
M_{A}^{\mathcal{N}}\colon[0,1]^{r(A)}\to{\mathcal{K}}_{A}
that constructed a
k
-hypergraph
M_{A}^{\mathcal{N}}(x)
with vertex set
A
from

x\in[0,1]^{r(A)}
and
{\mathcal{N}}
by declaring
e\in\binom{A}{k}
to be an edge exactly when

\alpha^{*}(x)\in{\mathcal{N}}
for
\alpha^{*}\colon[0,1]^{r(A)}\to[0,1]^{r(k)}
given by item
(II)

for any enumeration
\alpha\in A^{k}
of
e
. It is straightforward to check that the construction

M_{A}^{\mathcal{N}}
is equivariant, that is, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[0,1]}^{r(B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}}_{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[0,1]}^{r(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{B}^{\mathcal{N}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{A}^{\mathcal{N}}}

is commutative.

2.2
d
-peons,
d
-Euclidean structures and
d
-theons

For a (finite or) countable set
V
, we let
{\mathcal{O}}_{V}
be the set of linear orders on
V
and if
V
is
finite, we equip
{\mathcal{O}}_{V}
with the uniform probability measure, which we denote by
\nu_{V}
. Note that
every injection
\alpha\colon U\to V
contra-variantly defines
\alpha^{*}\colon{\mathcal{O}}_{V}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{U}
by

\displaystyle u_{1}\mathbin{\alpha^{*}(\lhd)}u_{2}

\displaystyle\iff\alpha(u_{1})\lhd\alpha(u_{2})\qquad(u_{1},u_{2}\in U).

Given further an atomless probability space
\Omega=(X,\mu)
, a countable set
V
and

d,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, we let

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{V}^{(d)}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\prod_{A\in r(V)}(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{d}),

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{V,\ell}^{(d)}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\prod_{A\in r(V,\ell)}(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{d}),

be equipped with the product (Borel)
\sigma
-algebra and with the product measures

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{A\in r(V)}\left(\mu\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{d}\nu_{A}\right),

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{A\in r(V,\ell)}\left(\mu\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{d}\nu_{A}\right),

respectively, which we denote both by
\mu^{(d)}
by abuse of notation. We will omit
\Omega
from
the notation when it is clear from context and we will omit
(d)
from the notation when
d=0
(the
same convention will be used in terms defined later that use
\Omega
and
d
). We will also use the
shorthands
{\mathcal{O}}_{k}
,
{\mathcal{E}}_{k}^{(d)}
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{k,\ell}^{(d)}
when
V=[k]
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Finally, we
will naturally identify
{\mathcal{E}}_{V}^{(d)}
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{V,\ell}^{(d)}
with the spaces

\displaystyle\left(\prod_{A\in r(V)}X\right)\times\prod_{A\in r(V)}{\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{d},

\displaystyle\left(\prod_{A\in r(V,\ell)}X\right)\times\prod_{A\in r(V,\ell)}{\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{d},

respectively.

Note that every injection
\alpha\colon U\to V
contra-variantly defines

\alpha^{*}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{V}^{(d)}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{U}^{(d)}
by

\displaystyle\alpha^{*}(x,{\lhd})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(y,{\ll})\qquad\left(x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{V},{\lhd}\in\prod_{A\in r(V)}{\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{d}\right),

where

\displaystyle y_{A}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\alpha^{*}(x)_{A}=x_{\alpha(A)}\qquad(A\in r(V)),

\displaystyle{\ll^{i}_{A}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\alpha\mathord{\downarrow}_{A}^{*}({\lhd^{i}_{\im(\alpha)}})\qquad(i\in[d],A\in r(V)).

An analogous formula yields a contra-variantly defined

\alpha^{*}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{V,\ell}^{(d)}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{U,\ell}^{(d)}
.

For
d_{1},d_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
and two atomless standard probability spaces
\Omega_{1}
and
\Omega_{2}
, we will
naturally identify the spaces
{\mathcal{E}}_{V}^{(d_{1}+d_{2})}(\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2})
and

{\mathcal{E}}_{V}^{(d_{1})}(\Omega_{1})\times{\mathcal{E}}_{V}^{(d_{2})}(\Omega_{2})
via the natural bijection that maps

x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{V}^{(d_{1}+d_{2})}(\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2})
to

(y,z)\in{\mathcal{E}}_{V}^{(d_{1})}(\Omega_{1})\times{\mathcal{E}}_{V}^{(d_{2})}(\Omega_{2})
, where for every
A\in r(V)
,
y_{A}
is
the projection of
x_{A}
to
\Omega_{1}
along with the first
d_{1}
orders and
z_{A}
is the projection
of
x_{A}
to
\Omega_{2}
along with the last
d_{2}
orders.

The remainder of the section is devoted to generalizing the notions of theons of
[
CR20
]
and the
associated quasirandomness notions of
[
CR23
]
to include the extra order variables
of
[
CT18
]
.

Let
\Omega=(X,\mu)
be an atomless probability space, let
d,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, let
{\mathcal{L}}
be a relational
language and
P\in{\mathcal{L}}
.

A
(Borel)
d
-
P
-on
over
\Omega
is a measurable subset
{\mathcal{N}}
of

{\mathcal{E}}_{k(P)}^{(d)}(\Omega)
. We say that
{\mathcal{N}}
is
\ell
-independent
if

{\mathcal{N}}={\mathcal{E}}_{k(P),\ell}^{(d)}\times{\mathcal{H}}
for some
{\mathcal{H}}\subseteq\prod_{A\in\binom{[k(P)]}{>\ell}}(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{d})
. Dually, the
rank
of
{\mathcal{N}}
(denoted
\rk({\mathcal{N}})
) is the minimum
\ell\in\mathbb{N}

such that
{\mathcal{N}}={\mathcal{H}}\times\prod_{A\in\binom{[k(P)]}{>\ell}}(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{d})
for some

{\mathcal{H}}\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}_{k(P),\ell}^{(d)}
.

A
(Borel)
d
-Euclidean structure
in
{\mathcal{L}}
over
\Omega
is a function
{\mathcal{N}}
that maps each

P\in{\mathcal{L}}
to a
d
-
P
-on
{\mathcal{N}}_{P}\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}_{k(P)}^{(d)}(\Omega)
. We say that
{\mathcal{N}}
is

\ell
-independent
if all of its
d
-peons
{\mathcal{N}}_{P}
are
\ell
-independent. Dually, the

rank
of
{\mathcal{N}}
is
\rk({\mathcal{N}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\max\{\rk({\mathcal{N}}_{P})\mid P\in{\mathcal{L}}\}\cup\{0\}
.

For a
d
-Euclidean structure in
{\mathcal{L}}
over
\Omega
and a countable set
V
, we let

M^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{V}^{(d)}\to{\mathcal{K}}_{V}
be the (Borel) measurable function given by

\displaystyle P^{M^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}(x)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{\alpha\in(V)_{k(P)}\mid\alpha^{*}(x)\in{\mathcal{N}}_{P}\}\qquad(x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{V}^{(d)},P\in{\mathcal{L}}),

that is, the predicate
P
holds on an injective tuple
\alpha
in
M^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}(x)
exactly when the
point
\alpha^{*}(x)\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k(P)}^{(d)}
is an element of
{\mathcal{N}}_{P}
. It is straightforward to check that

M^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}
is equivariant in the sense of Section
2.1
.

We also let
\mu^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(M^{\mathcal{N}}_{V})_{*}(\mu^{(d)})
be the pushforward probability measure of

\mu^{(d)}
by
M^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}
, that is, if
\bm{x}\sim\mu^{(d)}
is picked in
{\mathcal{E}}_{V}^{(d)}
according
to
\mu^{(d)}
, then
\mu^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}
is the distribution of
M^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}(\bm{x})
. The fact that the
distribution of
\bm{x}
is
S_{V}
-invariant and
M^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}
is equivariant implies that the
distribution
\mu^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}
is
S_{V}
-invariant (i.e.,
exchangeable
). Obviously
\mu^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}
is
also
local
, that is, for
A,B\subseteq V
disjoint,
M^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}(\bm{x})\mathord{|}_{A}
is independent
from
M^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}(\bm{x})\mathord{|}_{B}
.

For a finite (canonical)
{\mathcal{L}}
-structure
K
, we let

\displaystyle T_{\operatorname{ind}}(K,{\mathcal{N}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(M^{\mathcal{N}}_{V(K)})^{-1}(K)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{V(K)}^{(d)}\mid M^{\mathcal{N}}_{V(K)}(x)=K\},

\displaystyle t_{\operatorname{ind}}(K,{\mathcal{N}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mu^{(d)}(T_{\operatorname{ind}}(K,{\mathcal{N}}))=\mu^{\mathcal{N}}_{V(K)}(\{K\}),

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathcal{N}}(K)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\frac{\lvert K\rvert!}{\lvert\Aut(K)\rvert}t_{\operatorname{ind}}(K,{\mathcal{N}})=\mu^{\mathcal{N}}_{V(K)}(\{K^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{K}}_{V(K)}\mid K^{\prime}\cong K\}).

Exchangeability implies that
t_{\operatorname{ind}}(K,{\mathcal{N}})
and
\phi_{\mathcal{N}}(K)
depends only on the isomorphism-type
of
K
.

For a canonical theory
T
, a
(Borel)
d
-
T
-on
is a
d
-Euclidean structure
{\mathcal{N}}
such that

t_{\operatorname{ind}}(K,{\mathcal{N}})=0
whenever
K
is
not
a model of
T
. Let
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
be a

d_{1}
-Euclidean structure over
\Omega_{1}
and a
d_{2}
-Euclidean structure over
\Omega_{2}
,
respectively, both in
{\mathcal{L}}
. We say that
{\mathcal{N}}_{1}
is
equivalent
to
{\mathcal{N}}_{2}
if

\mu^{{\mathcal{N}}_{1}}_{V}=\mu^{{\mathcal{N}}_{2}}_{V}
for every countable set
V
(this is easily summarized as

\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}_{1}}=\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}_{2}}
).

The set of functions

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}^{+}({\mathcal{A}}[T],\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{\phi_{\mathcal{N}}\mid{\mathcal{N}}\text{ is a $0$-$T$-on}\land d\in\mathbb{N}\}

was completely characterized in the theory of flag algebras
[
Raz07
]
(see also
[
CR20
]
for
the connection to theons). Since the precise characterization will not be important for us, the
unfamiliarized reader can think of this as a set of representatives of each equivalence class of

0
-
T
-ons. Furthermore, since elements
\operatorname{Hom}^{+}({\mathcal{A}}[T],\mathbb{R})
encode limits of convergent sequences of finite
models of
T
, we will refer to them as
limits
of
T
or as limits in
{\mathcal{L}}
when we do not
want to specify
T
. As one might expect, every
d
-
T
-on
{\mathcal{N}}
is equivalent to some
0
-
T
-on

{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}
, but it will be important to produce
{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}
from
{\mathcal{N}}
preserving as much independence as
possible.

We say that two
d
-Euclidean structures
{\mathcal{N}}^{1}
and
{\mathcal{N}}^{2}
in the same language
{\mathcal{L}}
over the
same space
\Omega=(X,\mu)
are
almost everywhere (a.e.) equal
if

\mu^{(d)}({\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{P}\mathbin{\triangle}{\mathcal{N}}^{2}_{P})=0
for every
P\in{\mathcal{L}}
; in particular, this implies that they
represent the same limit:
\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}^{1}}=\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}^{2}}
.

We say that a limit
\phi
is
\ell
-independent if there exists a
0
-Euclidean structure
{\mathcal{N}}

such that
\phi_{\mathcal{N}}=\phi
; this property is also denoted
\texttt{Independence}[\ell]
. We say that a limit

\phi
is
\ast
-
\ell
-independent if there exist
d\in\mathbb{N}
and a
d
-Euclidean structure
{\mathcal{N}}

such that
\phi_{\mathcal{N}}=\phi
. These are not equivalent: our main result Theorem
2.2
says
that
\ast
-
\ell
-independence is equivalent to the property
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
defined below, which
is strictly weaker than
\texttt{Independence}[\ell]
(see
[
CR23
]
).

Dually, the
rank
of a limit
\phi
is the minimum rank of a
0
-
T
-on
{\mathcal{N}}
such that

\phi=\phi_{\mathcal{N}}
. We could define the
\ast
-rank by allowing
d
-
T
-ons, but as we will see in
Corollary
3.9
, such a definition would be equivalent to the usual rank.

Recall that a
quantifier-free definition
(or
open interpretation
) from a canonical
theory
T_{1}
in
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
to a canonical theory
T_{2}
in
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
is a function
I
that maps predicate
symbols
P\in{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
to quantifier-free formulas
I(P)(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k(P)})
in
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
so that
axioms of
T_{1}
are mapped to theorems of
T_{2}
when we declare
I
to commute with logical
connectives; such a quantifier-free definition is denoted as
I\colon T_{1}\leadsto T_{2}
. For a set

V
, a quantifier-free definition naturally defines a map
I^{*}_{V}\colon{\mathcal{K}}_{V}[T_{2}]\to{\mathcal{K}}_{V}[T_{1}]
given
by
10
10

10

The
\ast
in
I^{*}_{V}
is to denote a different kind of contra-variance. Namely, this is
a contra-variant functor from the category
Int
of quantifier-free definitions to
Set
,
see
[
CR20
]
. The next two defined maps, which will be denoted by
I^{*}
also have similar
contra-variances as functors from
Int
.

\displaystyle P^{I^{*}_{V}(M)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}I(P)(M)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{\alpha\in(V)_{k(P)}\mid M\vDash I(P)(\alpha)\}\qquad(P\in{\mathcal{L}}),

that is, the predicate
P\in{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
holds on a tuple
\alpha
in
I^{*}_{V}(M)
exactly when the formula

I(P)
holds on
\alpha
in
M
. It is straightforward to check that
I^{*}_{V}
is equivariant in the
sense of Section
2.1
. Similarly,
I\colon T_{1}\leadsto T_{2}
also naturally defines
a map
I^{*}\colon\operatorname{Hom}^{+}({\mathcal{A}}[T_{2}],\mathbb{R})\to\operatorname{Hom}^{+}({\mathcal{A}}[T_{1}],\mathbb{R})
given by

\displaystyle I^{*}(\phi)(M)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\sum\{\phi(N)\mid N\in{\mathcal{M}}_{V(M)}[T_{2}]\land I^{*}_{V(M)}(N)\cong M\},

for every finite
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
-structure
M
, where
{\mathcal{M}}_{V(M)}[T_{2}]
is the set of models of
T_{2}
over

V(M)

up to isomorphism
(in
[
Raz07
,
CR20
]

I^{*}(\phi)
is denoted as
\phi^{I}
). From a
probabilistic view, we have
I^{*}(\phi_{\mathcal{N}})=\phi_{\mathcal{H}}
exactly when for every countable set
V
, if

\bm{M}
is sampled at random according to
\mu^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}
, then
I^{*}_{V}(\bm{M})\sim\mu^{\mathcal{H}}_{V}
.

In turn, there is also a naturally induced map over
d
-Euclidean structures
{\mathcal{N}}
over

\Omega=(X,\mu)
defined as follows. First, for a quantifier-free formula
F(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
, we
define the
truth set

T(F,{\mathcal{N}})\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{(d)}(\Omega)
as follows:

•

if
F
is
P(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{k}})
and
i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}
are not pairwise distinct or
F
is

x_{i}=x_{j}
for
i\neq j
, then
T(F,{\mathcal{N}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\varnothing
.

•

T(x_{i}=x_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{(d)}(\Omega)
.

•

if
F
is
P(x_{i_{1}},\ldots,x_{i_{k}})
and
i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}
are pairwise distinct, then

T(F,{\mathcal{N}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(i^{*})^{-1}({\mathcal{N}}_{P})
, where we view
i
as an injection
[k]\to[n]
.

•

T(\mathord{-},{\mathcal{N}})
commutes with propositional connectives (e.g., we have
T(F_{1}\lor F_{2},{\mathcal{N}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}T(F_{1},{\mathcal{N}})\cup T(F_{2},{\mathcal{N}})
and
T(\neg F,{\mathcal{N}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{(d)}(\Omega)\setminus T(F,{\mathcal{N}})
).

Then for a
d
-Euclidean structure
{\mathcal{N}}
over
\Omega=(X,\mu)
, we define the
d
-Euclidean structure

I^{*}({\mathcal{N}})
over
\Omega=(X,\mu)
by

\displaystyle I^{*}({\mathcal{N}})_{P}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}T(I(P),{\mathcal{N}})\qquad(P\in{\mathcal{L}}_{1}).

(2.2)

(In
[
CR20
, Remark 6]
, this was defined only for the
d=0
case and used the notation
I({\mathcal{N}})

instead.) These maps are connected to each other in the sense that
\phi_{I^{*}({\mathcal{N}})}=I^{*}(\phi_{\mathcal{N}})

(this is easily checked by noting that if
\bm{M}
is sampled at random according to
\mu^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}
,
then
I^{*}_{V}(\bm{M})\sim\mu^{I^{*}({\mathcal{N}})}_{V}
).

A particularly important case of quantifier-free definitions are

reducts
11
11

11

In
[
CR20
,
CR23
]
, these were called structure-erasing interpretations.
,
i.e., quantifier-free definitions of the form
I\colon T_{{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}}\leadsto T_{\mathcal{L}}
in which

{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{L}}
and
I
acts identically on predicate symbols of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
. In this case,

I^{*}_{V}\colon{\mathcal{K}}_{V}[{\mathcal{L}}]\to{\mathcal{K}}_{V}[{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}]
is simply the reduct map
M\mathord{|}_{{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}I^{*}_{V}(M)
and we
also abbreviate
\phi\mathord{|}_{{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}I^{*}(\phi)
. It is straightforward to check that if
{\mathcal{N}}
is a

d
-Euclidean structure in
{\mathcal{L}}
, then
\phi_{\mathcal{N}}\mathord{|}_{{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}}=\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}\mathord{|}_{{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}}}
, where

{\mathcal{N}}\mathord{|}_{{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}}
is the
d
-Euclidean structure in
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
obtained from
{\mathcal{N}}
by simply restricting
it to
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
(which is a.e. equal to
I^{*}({\mathcal{N}})
).

Given two theories
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
in
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
, respectively, the
disjoint union
theory
is the theory
T_{1}\cup T_{2}
in the disjoint union language
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cdot}}{{\cup}}}{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
that
contains both axioms of
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
(i.e., its models are models of
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
on the same
vertex set). With a small abuse of notation, for
i\in[2]
, we also call the quantifier-free
definitions of the form
I\colon T_{i}\leadsto T_{1}\cup T_{2}
that act identically on predicate symbols
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}

reducts
and use the notation
\phi\mathord{|}_{T_{i}}
for
\phi\mathord{|}_{{\mathcal{L}}_{i}}
when

\phi\in\operatorname{Hom}^{+}({\mathcal{A}}[T_{1}\cup T_{2}],\mathbb{R})
.

A
coupling
of two limits
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
in
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
, respectively, is a
limit
\psi
in the disjoint union language
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cdot}}{{\cup}}}{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
such that

\psi\mathord{|}_{{\mathcal{L}}_{1}}=\phi_{1}
and
\psi\mathord{|}_{{\mathcal{L}}_{2}}=\phi_{2}
. For example, if
{\mathcal{N}}^{1}
and
{\mathcal{N}}^{2}
are

d
-Euclidean structures in
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
, respectively, over the same
\Omega
with

\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}^{1}}=\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}^{2}}=\phi_{2}
, then the (disjoint) union function

{\mathcal{N}}^{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cdot}}{{\cup}}}{\mathcal{N}}^{2}
is a
d
-Euclidean structure in
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cdot}}{{\cup}}}{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
over
\Omega
such that

\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}^{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cdot}}{{\cup}}}{\mathcal{N}}^{2}}
is a coupling of
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
.

The
independent coupling
of two limits
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
in
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
,
respectively is the limit
\phi_{1}\otimes\phi_{2}
in
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cdot}}{{\cup}}}{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
given by

\displaystyle(\phi_{1}\otimes\phi_{2})(M)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\frac{\lvert\Aut(M\mathord{|}_{{\mathcal{L}}_{1}})\rvert\cdot\lvert\Aut(M\mathord{|}_{{\mathcal{L}}_{2}})\rvert}{\lvert\Aut(M)\rvert\cdot\lvert M\rvert!}\cdot\phi_{1}(M\mathord{|}_{{\mathcal{L}}_{1}})\cdot\phi_{2}(M\mathord{|}_{{\mathcal{L}}_{2}}).

A more geometric description of the independent coupling is as follows: let
{\mathcal{N}}^{1}
be a

d_{1}
-Euclidean structure in
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
over
\Omega_{1}
and
{\mathcal{N}}^{2}
be a

d_{2}
-Euclidean structure in
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
over
\Omega_{2}
. The
independent coupling
of
{\mathcal{N}}^{1}

and
{\mathcal{N}}^{2}
is the
(d_{1}+d_{2})
-Euclidean structure in
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cdot}}{{\cup}}}{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
over

\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2}
given by

\displaystyle({\mathcal{N}}^{1}\otimes{\mathcal{N}}^{2})_{P}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{(x^{1},x^{2})\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k(P)}^{(d_{1})}(\Omega_{1})\times{\mathcal{E}}_{k(P)}^{(d_{2})}(\Omega_{2})\mid x^{i}\in{\mathcal{N}}^{i}_{P}\}\qquad(P\in{\mathcal{L}}_{i},i\in[2]).

It is straightforward to check that for every finite
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cdot}}{{\cup}}}{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
-structure
M
, we have

\displaystyle t_{\operatorname{ind}}(M,{\mathcal{N}}^{1}\otimes{\mathcal{N}}^{2})

\displaystyle=t_{\operatorname{ind}}(M\mathord{|}_{{\mathcal{L}}_{1}},{\mathcal{N}}^{1})\cdot t_{\operatorname{ind}}(M\mathord{|}_{{\mathcal{L}}_{2}},{\mathcal{N}}^{2}),

from which one gets
\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}^{1}\otimes{\mathcal{N}}^{2}}=\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}^{1}}\otimes\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}^{2}}
.

A more probabilistic view of couplings and independent couplings is as follows:
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}
is a
coupling of
\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}^{1}}
and
\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}^{2}}
if for every countable set
V
, if
\bm{N}
is
sampled according to
\mu^{\mathcal{H}}_{V}
, then
\bm{N}\mathord{|}_{{\mathcal{L}}_{1}}\sim\mu^{{\mathcal{N}}^{1}}_{V}
and

\bm{N}\mathord{|}_{{\mathcal{L}}_{2}}\sim\mu^{{\mathcal{N}}^{2}}_{V}
; if further
\bm{N}\mathord{|}_{{\mathcal{L}}_{1}}\sim\mu^{{\mathcal{N}}^{1}}_{V}
and

\bm{N}\mathord{|}_{{\mathcal{L}}_{2}}\sim\mu^{{\mathcal{N}}^{2}}_{V}
are independent, then
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}
is the independent coupling
of
\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}^{1}}
and
\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}^{2}}
.

Two limits
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
are
uniquely coupleable
if there exists exactly one
coupling of
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
(which must be the independent coupling

\phi_{1}\otimes\phi_{2}
).

We say that a limit
\phi
is
uniquely
\ell
-coupleable
if for every limit
\psi
with

\rk(\psi)\leq\ell
,
\phi
and
\psi
are uniquely coupleable; this property is also denoted

\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
. It was shown in
[
CR23
, Theorem 3.10]
that
\phi\in\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
is
equivalent to the property that for some (equivalently, every)
0
-Euclidean structure
{\mathcal{N}}
over

\Omega=(X,\mu)
with
\phi_{\mathcal{N}}=\phi
, if
V
is a countable set and
\bm{x}
is picked at random in

{\mathcal{E}}_{V}
according to
\mu
, then
M^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}(\bm{x})
is independent (as a random variable) from

(\bm{x}_{A}\mid A\in r(V,\ell))
. As we will see in Proposition
4.4
, the
same characterization holds if we let
{\mathcal{N}}
be a
d
-Euclidean structure with
\phi_{\mathcal{N}}=\phi
for

d\in\mathbb{N}
. From this characterization, it is obvious that

\texttt{Independence}[\ell]\implies\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
(see
[
CR23
, Theorem 3.2]
)

Furthermore, it was shown in
[
CR23
, Theorem 3.3]
that both
\texttt{Independence}[\ell]
and

\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
are natural properties in the sense that if
\phi\in\operatorname{Hom}^{+}({\mathcal{A}}[T],\mathbb{R})
satisfies the property
and
I\colon T^{\prime}\leadsto T
is a quantifier-free definition, then
I^{*}(\phi)
also satisfies the
property. Moreover, these properties are also preserved under independent
couplings
[
CR23
, Theorem 3.4]
in the sense that if
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
both have the property
then
\phi_{1}\otimes\phi_{2}
also has the property.

We end this section with a high-arity generalization of the quasirandom tournament of
Example
1.4
.

Definition 2.1.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, the
theory of
k
-orientations
is the theory
T_{k\operatorname{-Orientation}}
in a
language
{\mathcal{L}}
containing a single predicate symbol
P
, whose arity is
k(P)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}k
, and with
axioms

\displaystyle\forall x_{1},\ldots,x_{k},(P(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\to\neg P(x_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,x_{\sigma(k)}))\qquad(\sigma\in S_{k}\setminus\{\id_{k}\}),

\displaystyle\forall x_{1},\ldots,x_{k},\left(\bigwedge_{i\neq j}x_{i}\neq x_{j}\to\bigvee_{\sigma\in S_{k}}P(x_{\sigma(1),\ldots,\sigma(k)})\right),

that is, for each
k
-set
A
of vertices, the predicate
P
holds in exactly one
of the (injective)
k
-tuples
\alpha
with
\im(\alpha)=A
.

The
quasirandom
k
-orientation
is the limit
\phi^{k\operatorname{-qrO}}\in\operatorname{Hom}^{+}({\mathcal{A}}[T_{k\operatorname{-Orientation}}],\mathbb{R})
is the limit
encoded by the (
(k-1)
-independent)
1
-
T_{k\operatorname{-Orientation}}
-on
{\mathcal{N}}^{k\operatorname{-qrO}}
over any space
\Omega=(X,\mu)

given by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}^{k\operatorname{-qrO}}_{P}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{(x,{\lhd})\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k}^{(1)}(\Omega)\mid{\lhd}_{[k]}={\leq}\},

(2.3)

where
{\leq}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{k}
is the usual order on
[k]
. Note that
\mu^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}
consists of the
distribution in which each
k
-set is given a uniformly at random orientation independently.

Alternatively, we will see in Remark
3.7
that

\phi^{k\operatorname{-qrO}}=\phi_{{\mathcal{H}}^{k\operatorname{-qrO}}}
for the
0
-
T_{k\operatorname{-Orientation}}
-on
{\mathcal{H}}^{k\operatorname{-qrO}}
over

\Omega=([0,1),\lambda)
given by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}^{k\operatorname{-qrO}}_{P}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k}^{(0)}(\Omega)\mid\sigma_{x}=\gamma_{\lfloor x_{[k]}\cdot k!\rfloor+1}\},

(2.4)

where
\sigma_{x}\in S_{k}
is defined when
x
has distinct coordinates as the unique permutation
such that

\displaystyle x_{[k]\setminus\{\sigma^{-1}_{x}(1)\}}<x_{[k]\setminus\{\sigma^{-1}_{x}(2)\}}<\cdots<x_{[k]\setminus\{\sigma^{-1}_{x}(k)\}},

and defined arbitrarily (but measurably) when
x
has repeated coordinates and

\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{k!}
is some fixed enumeration of
S_{k}
.

This latter representation shows that
\phi^{k\operatorname{-qrO}}
satisfies
\texttt{Independence}[k-2]
.

It is straightforward to check that the quasirandom
k
-orientation
\phi^{k\operatorname{-qrO}}
can be obtained
as a quantifier-free definition of the
(\Theta,p)
-quasirandom homomorphisms in
S_{k}
-action
theories defined in
[
CR23
, Definition 2.9]
, which in particular implies that it satisfies

\texttt{UCouple}[k-1]
(by
[
CR23
, Proposition 9.1]
). However, we will see that

\phi^{k\operatorname{-qrO}}\in\texttt{UCouple}[k-1]
follows from the more conceptual fact that it is represented by the

(k-1)
-independent
1
-
T_{k\operatorname{-Orientation}}
-on
{\mathcal{N}}^{k\operatorname{-qrO}}
(see Corollary
4.5
).

2.3
Main theorems

Our main theorem provides some more characterizations of
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
complementing the ones
of
[
CR23
, Theorem 3.10]
.

Theorem 2.2.

The following are equivalent for a limit
\phi\in\operatorname{Hom}^{+}({\mathcal{A}}[T],\mathbb{R})
and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.

i.

\phi\in\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
.

ii.

\phi
is
\ast
-
\ell
-independent.

iii.

There exists an
\ell
-independent
1
-Euclidean structure

{\mathcal{N}}
such that
\phi=\phi_{\mathcal{N}}
.

iv.

There exist a canonical theory
T^{\prime}
, an
\ell
-independent
limit
\psi\in\operatorname{Hom}^{+}({\mathcal{A}}[T^{\prime}],\mathbb{R})
and a quantifier-free definition
I\colon T\leadsto T^{\prime}\cup T_{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-Orientation}}
such that
\phi=I^{*}(\psi\otimes\phi^{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-qrO}})
.

The implication
(iii)
\implies
(ii)
follows trivially
from definitions.

As it was shown in
[
CR23
, Theorems 3.6 and 3.8]
(see also Example
1.4
),

\texttt{Independence}[\ell]
(i.e., existence of an
\ell
-independent
0
-Euclidean structure
{\mathcal{N}}
with

\phi=\phi_{\mathcal{N}}
) is strictly stronger than
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
(see Example
1.4
). The theorem
above says that not only is
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
(item
(i)
) equivalent to

\ast
-
\ell
-independence (item
(ii)
), but even adding extra order
variables does not increase the strength of quasirandomness: existence of an
\ell
-independent

1
-Euclidean structure representation (item
(iii)
) is still equivalent to

\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
. Finally, the fact that item
(iv)
is also equivalent to

\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
can be seen as a classification of all
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
limits as those that
essentially consist of an
\texttt{Independence}[\ell]
part independently coupled with a quasirandom

(\ell+1)
-orientation; in other words, the only difference between
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
and

\texttt{Independence}[\ell]
is the potential presence of a hidden quasirandom
(\ell+1)
-orientation.

As a consequence of Theorem
2.2
above, we settle the final missing implications
between the quasirandomness properties of
[
CR23
]
:

Theorem 2.3.

For every
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
with
\ell^{\prime}<\ell
, we have

\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]\implies\texttt{Independence}[\ell^{\prime}]
.

Since by
[
CR23
, Theorems 3.3 and 3.4]
independent couplings and quantifier-free definitions
preserve both
\texttt{Independence}[\ell]
and
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
, one can easily see how
Theorem
2.2
(i)
\implies
(iv)
implies
Theorem
2.3
as the representation
\phi^{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-qrO}}=\phi_{{\mathcal{H}}^{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-qrO}}}
shows

\phi^{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-qrO}}\in\texttt{Independence}[\ell-1]
. However, as it turns out, it will be easier to prove that
every
\ast
-
\ell
-independent limit satisfies
\texttt{Independence}[\ell-1]

(Corollary
3.8
), (so Theorem
2.3
will follow from the implication
Theorem
2.2
(i)
\implies
(ii)
instead,
see Corollary
9.8
).

Table
1
contains the location of the proofs of the implications of
Theorem
2.2
and the proof of Theorem
2.3
.

Result

Proof location

Theorem
2.2

(iii)
\implies
(ii)

Trivial from definitions

(ii)
\implies
(i)

Proposition
4.4

(iv)
\implies
(i)

Corollary
4.6

(i)
\implies
(iii)

Proposition
9.7

(iii)
\implies
(iv)

Proposition
10.1

Theorem
2.3

Corollaries
3.8
and
9.8

Table 1:
Proof locations for main theorems.

3
d
-Realizations

This section is dedicated to proving in Corollary
3.8
that every

\ast
-
\ell
-independent limit is
(\ell-1)
-independent, which in particular means that
Theorem
2.3
is a direct corollary of Theorem
2.2
(this will be put
together in Corollary
9.8
). We start by recalling a few concepts and basic results from
exchangeability theory.

Definition 3.1.

Let
\Omega_{1}=(X_{1},\mu_{1})
and
\Omega_{2}=(X_{2},\mu_{2})
be atomless probability spaces, let

d_{1},d_{2},n\in\mathbb{N}
and let
f\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{(d_{1})}(\Omega_{1})\to\Omega_{2}\times{\mathcal{O}}_{n}^{d_{2}}
be a function.

We say that
f
is
symmetric
if it is a.e.
S_{n}
-invariant in the first component and
a.e.
S_{n}
-equivariant in the order components in the sense that

\displaystyle f(\sigma^{*}(x))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(f_{0}(x),\sigma^{*}(f_{1}(x)),\sigma^{*}(f_{2}(x)),\ldots,\sigma^{*}(f_{d_{2}}(x)))

for
\mu_{1}^{(d_{1})}
-almost every
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{(d_{1})}(\Omega_{1})
and every
\sigma\in S_{n}
, where

f_{0}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{(d_{1})}(\Omega_{1})\to\Omega_{2}
and
f_{i}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{(d_{1})}(\Omega_{1})\to{\mathcal{O}}_{n}

(
i\in[d_{2}]
) denote the components of
f
.

We say that
f
is
measure-preserving on highest order argument
(h.o.a.) if it is
measurable and for almost every
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{n,n-1}^{(d_{1})}
, the function

\displaystyle f(x,\mathord{-})\colon\Omega_{1}\times{\mathcal{O}}_{n}^{d_{1}}\to\Omega_{2}\times{\mathcal{O}}_{n}^{d_{2}}

is measure-preserving (with respect to
\mu_{1}^{(d_{1})}
and
\mu_{2}^{(d_{2})}
).

Given a family
f=(f_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
of symmetric functions

f_{i}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{(d_{1})}(\Omega_{1})\to\Omega_{2}\times{\mathcal{O}}_{i}^{d_{2}}
and a countable set
V
, we define
the function
\widehat{f}_{V}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{V}^{(d_{1})}(\Omega_{1})\to{\mathcal{E}}_{V}^{(d_{2})}(\Omega_{2})
by

\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{V}(x)_{A}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(\overline{\alpha}_{A}^{-1})^{*}(f_{\lvert A\rvert}(\alpha_{A}^{*}(x)))\qquad(A\in r(V)),

where
\alpha_{A}\colon[\lvert A\rvert]\to V
is any injection with
\im(\alpha_{A})=A
(recall that

\overline{\alpha}_{A}\colon[\lvert A\rvert]\to A
is the restriction of the codomain of

\alpha_{A}
). Since the
f_{i}
are symmetric, this a.e. does not depend on the particular choice of

\alpha_{A}
.

Given further a
d_{2}
-Euclidean structure
{\mathcal{N}}
in
{\mathcal{L}}
over
\Omega_{2}
, we define the

d_{1}
-Euclidean structure
f^{*}({\mathcal{N}})
in
{\mathcal{L}}
over
\Omega_{1}
by

\displaystyle f^{*}({\mathcal{N}})_{P}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\widehat{f}_{k(P)}^{-1}({\mathcal{N}}_{P})\qquad(P\in{\mathcal{L}}).

The following lemma is an extension of a standard fact from exchangeability theory to account for
order variables.

Lemma 3.2.

Let
\Omega_{1}=(X_{1},\mu_{1})
and
\Omega_{2}=(X_{2},\mu_{2})
be atomless probability spaces, let

d_{1},d_{2},n\in\mathbb{N}
and let
f=(f_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
of symmetric functions

f_{i}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{(d_{1})}(\Omega_{1})\to\Omega_{2}\times{\mathcal{O}}_{i}^{d_{2}}
. Then the following hold.

i.

If each
f_{i}
is measure-preserving on h.o.a. and
V
is a
countable set, then
\widehat{f}_{V}
is measure-preserving.

ii.

\widehat{f}_{V}
is a.e. equivariant, that is, if

\beta\colon U\to V
is an injection between countable sets, then
\beta^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\widehat{f}_{V}=\widehat{f}_{U}\mathbin{\circ}\beta^{*}
holds
\mu_{1}^{(d_{1})}
