-almost everywhere.

Proof.

Item
(i)
is equivalent to proving that if
\bm{x}
is picked at random in

{\mathcal{E}}_{V}^{(d_{1})}(\Omega_{1})
according to
\mu_{1}^{(d_{1})}
, then
\bm{y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\widehat{f}_{V}(\bm{x})
is
distributed according to the measure
\mu_{2}^{(d_{2})}
of
{\mathcal{E}}_{V}^{(d_{2})}(\Omega_{2})
. The definition
of
\widehat{f}_{V}
implies that each coordinate
\bm{y}_{A}
is measurable with respect to the

\sigma
-algebra generated by
(\bm{x}_{B}\mid B\in r(A))
and since all
f_{i}
are
measure-preserving on h.o.a., the conditional distribution of the coordinate
\bm{y}_{A}
given

(\bm{x}_{B}\mid B\subsetneq A)
is
\mu_{2}^{(d)}
, hence a simple induction and the fact that
the coordinates of
\bm{x}
are mutually independent yields item
(i)
.

For item
(ii)
, note that for
\mu_{1}^{(d_{1})}
-almost every

x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{V}^{(d_{1})}(\Omega_{1})
and every
A\in r(U)
, we have

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!(\beta^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\widehat{f}_{V})(x)_{A}

\displaystyle=\Biggl(f_{\lvert\beta(A)\rvert,0}(\alpha_{\beta(A)}^{*}(x)),

\displaystyle\hphantom{{}={}}\beta\mathord{\downarrow}_{A}^{*}\biggl((\overline{\alpha}_{\beta(A)}^{-1})^{*}\Bigl(f_{\lvert\beta(A)\rvert,1}\bigl(\alpha_{\beta(A)}^{*}(x)\bigr)\Bigr)\biggr),\ldots,\beta\mathord{\downarrow}_{A}^{*}\biggl((\overline{\alpha}_{\beta(A)}^{-1})^{*}\Bigl(f_{\lvert\beta(A)\rvert,d_{2}}\bigl(\alpha_{\beta(A)}^{*}(x)\bigr)\Bigr)\biggr)\Biggr),

where
f_{i,0}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{(d_{1})}(\Omega_{1})\to\Omega_{2}
and

f_{i,j}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{(d_{1})}(\Omega_{1})\to{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
(
j\in[d_{2}]
) denote the components of
f_{i}

(
i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
).

Note that the composition
\beta\mathord{\downarrow}_{A}^{-1}\mathbin{\circ}\overline{\alpha}_{\beta(A)}
is a bijection

[\lvert A\rvert]\to A
. Let
\gamma_{A}\colon[\lvert A\rvert]\to U
be obtained from

\beta\mathord{\downarrow}_{A}^{-1}\mathbin{\circ}\overline{\alpha}_{\beta(A)}
by changing its codomain to
U
(i.e., we
have
\gamma_{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\iota_{A,V}\mathbin{\circ}\beta\mathord{\downarrow}_{A}^{-1}\mathbin{\circ}\overline{\alpha}_{\beta(A)}
). By
construction,
\gamma_{A}
is an injection with
\im(\gamma_{A})=A
and
\alpha_{\beta(A)}=\beta\mathbin{\circ}\gamma_{A}
, so we get

\displaystyle f_{\lvert\beta(A)\rvert,0}(\alpha_{\beta(A)}^{*}(x))

\displaystyle=f_{\lvert A\rvert,0}(\gamma_{A}^{*}(\beta^{*}(x))).

On the other hand, note that

\overline{\gamma}_{A}=\beta\mathord{\downarrow}_{A}^{-1}\mathbin{\circ}\overline{\alpha}_{\beta(A)}
, hence

\overline{\gamma}_{A}^{-1}=\overline{\alpha}_{\beta(A)}^{-1}\mathbin{\circ}\beta\mathord{\downarrow}_{A}
, so for every

j\in[d_{2}]
, we get

\displaystyle\beta\mathord{\downarrow}_{A}^{*}\bigl((\overline{\alpha}_{\beta(A)}^{-1})^{*}(f_{\lvert\beta(A)\rvert,j}(\alpha_{\beta(A)}^{*}(x)))\bigr)

\displaystyle=(\overline{\gamma}_{A}^{-1})^{*}(f_{\lvert A\rvert,j}(\gamma_{A}^{*}(\beta^{*}(x))).

Since
\im(\gamma_{A})=A
, we conclude that

\displaystyle(\beta^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\widehat{f}_{V})(x)_{A}

\displaystyle=(\widehat{f}_{U}\mathbin{\circ}\beta^{*})(x)_{A}

for
\mu_{1}^{(d_{1})}
-almost every
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{V}^{(d_{1})}(\Omega_{1})
and every
A\in r(U)
.
∎

In order to prove Theorem
2.3
, we need to find a way of simulating the order variables
of a
d
-Euclidean structure by the ordinary variables of a
0
-Euclidean structures in a way that
preserves as much independence as possible. This will be done by
d
-realizations defined below,
which are heavily inspired by the different representations
{\mathcal{N}}^{k\operatorname{-qrO}}
and
{\mathcal{H}}^{k\operatorname{-qrO}}
of the
quasirandom
k
-orientation (see Definition
2.1
).

Definition 3.3.

Let
\Omega_{1}=(X_{1},\mu_{1})
and
\Omega_{2}=(X_{2},\mu_{2})
be atomless probability spaces and let

d\in\mathbb{N}
.

A
d
-realization
from
\Omega_{1}
to
\Omega_{2}
is a family
f=(f_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
of
symmetric measure-preserving on h.o.a. functions

f_{i}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\Omega_{1})\to\Omega_{2}\times{\mathcal{O}}_{i}^{d}
satisfying the following properties:

i.

For every
i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, if the coordinate functions of
f_{i}
are

f_{i,0}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\Omega_{1})\to\Omega_{2}
and
f_{i,j}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\Omega_{1})\to{\mathcal{O}}_{i}

(
j\in[d]
), then, except for a zero
\mu_{1}
-measure set,
f_{i,0}
depends only on the top
coordinate (indexed by
[i]
) and for every
j\in[d]
, except for a zero
\mu_{1}
-measure set,

f_{i,j}
depends only on the coordinates indexed by sets in
\binom{[i]}{>i-2}
.

ii.

There exists a family
g=(g_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
of symmetric
measure-preserving on h.o.a. functions

g_{i}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{(d)}(\Omega_{2}\times\Omega_{2})\to\Omega_{1}
such that

\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{i}(\widehat{g}_{i}(x,y,{\lhd}))

\displaystyle=(x,{\lhd})

(3.1)

for every
i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
(\mu_{2}\otimes\mu_{2})^{(d)}
-almost every

(x,y,{\lhd})\in{\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{(d)}(\Omega_{2}\times\Omega_{2})
.

Any family of functions
g
as in item
(ii)
above is called an

almost inverse family
of
f
.

Remark 3.4.

Since both the
f_{i}
and
g_{i}
are symmetric, by
Lemma
3.2
(ii)
, equation (
3.1
)
implies

\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{V}(\widehat{g}_{V}(x,y,{\lhd}))

\displaystyle=(x,{\lhd})

for every countable
V
and
(\mu_{2}\otimes\mu_{2})^{(d)}
-almost every

(x,y,{\lhd})\in{\mathcal{E}}_{V}^{(d)}(\Omega_{2})
.

Our first order of business is to show that
d
-realizations exist.

Lemma 3.5.

For all
d\in\mathbb{N}
and all atomless standard probability spaces
\Omega_{1}=(X_{1},\mu_{1})
and

\Omega_{2}=(X_{2},\mu_{2})
, there exists a
d
-realization from
\Omega_{1}
to
\Omega_{2}
.

Proof.

First we claim that it is enough to show the result for only one fixed pair of spaces

(\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2})
. Indeed, if
f=(f_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
is a
d
-realization from
\Lambda_{1}
to

\Lambda_{2}
,
g=(g_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
is an almost inverse family of
f
and
\Omega_{1}
and

\Omega_{2}
are standard probability spaces, then we can let
F_{1}\colon\Omega_{1}\to\Lambda_{1}
and

F_{2}\colon\Omega_{2}\to\Lambda_{2}
be measure-isomorphisms modulo
0
and for every
i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
define
f^{\prime}_{i}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\Omega_{1})\to\Omega_{2}\times{\mathcal{O}}_{i}^{d}
and

g^{\prime}_{i}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{(d)}(\Omega_{2}\times\Omega_{2})\to\Omega_{1}
by

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(F_{2}^{-1}\otimes\id_{{\mathcal{O}}_{i}^{d}})\mathbin{\circ}f_{i}\mathbin{\circ}\bigotimes_{A\in r(i)}F_{1},

\displaystyle g^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}F_{1}^{-1}\mathbin{\circ}g_{i}\mathbin{\circ}\bigotimes_{A\in r(i)}(F_{2}\otimes F_{2}\otimes\id_{{\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{d}}).

(Note that the above only gives an almost everywhere definition as
F_{1}^{-1}
and
F_{2}^{-1}
are
only defined in a full measure set, but we can simply extend the definition arbitrarily but
measurably.)

It is straightforward to check that
f^{\prime}=(f^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
is a
d
-realization from
\Omega_{1}

to
\Omega_{2}
and
g^{\prime}=(g^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
is an almost inverse family of
f^{\prime}
.

We now note that the case
d=0
is trivial: by taking
\Omega_{1}=\Omega_{2}
, we can simply let

f_{i}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}x_{[i]}
for every
i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and every
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\Omega_{1})
. An almost inverse
family of
f
is then obtained by taking
g_{i}(x,y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}x_{[i]}
for every
i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and every

(x,y)\in{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\Omega_{2}\times\Omega_{2})
.

We now claim that the case
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
follows from the case
d=1
. Indeed, if

f=(f_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
is a
1
-realization from
\Omega_{1}
to
\Omega_{2}
and
g=(g_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}

is an almost inverse family of
f
, then letting

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\bigotimes_{i=1}^{d}f_{i},

\displaystyle g^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\bigotimes_{i=1}^{d}g^{\prime}_{i}

for every
i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, it is straightforward to check that
f^{\prime}=(f^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
is a

d
-realization from
\Omega_{1}^{d}
to
\Omega_{2}^{d}
and
g^{\prime}=(g^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
is an almost inverse
family of
f^{\prime}
.

Let us then prove the case
d=1
when
\Omega_{1}=([0,1)^{2},\lambda)
and
\Omega_{2}=([0,1),\lambda)
.

First, for every
i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, let

\displaystyle D_{i}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{(x,y)\in{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\Omega_{1})\mid\exists A,B\in r(i),(A\neq B\land y_{A}=y_{B})\}.

(Recall that we naturally identify
{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\Omega_{1})
with
{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\Omega_{2})\times{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\Omega_{2})
.)
Note that
D_{i}
is a Borel set with
\lambda
-measure zero, so it is sufficient to define
f_{i}

only outside of
D_{i}
.

For each
i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, let
\theta_{i}
be the uniform probability measure on
S_{i}
and let

\tau_{i}\colon[0,1)\to S_{i}
be defined as follows. We fix an enumeration

\gamma^{i}_{1},\ldots,\gamma^{i}_{i!}
of
S_{i}
and let

\displaystyle\tau_{i}(x)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\gamma^{i}_{\lfloor x\cdot i!\rfloor+1}.

(3.2)

Note that
\tau_{i}
is a measure-preserving with respect to
\lambda
and
\theta_{i}
.

Define the function
f_{i}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\Omega_{1})\to\Omega_{2}\times{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
by

\displaystyle f_{i}(x,y)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(x_{[i]},(\tau_{i}(y_{[i]})\mathbin{\circ}\sigma_{y})^{*}({\leq}))

for every
(x,y)\in{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\Omega_{1})\setminus D_{i}
, where
\leq
is the usual order on
[i]
and

\sigma_{y}\in S_{i}
is the unique permutation such that

\displaystyle y_{[i]\setminus\{\sigma_{y}^{-1}(1)\}}<y_{[i]\setminus\{\sigma_{y}^{-1}(2)\}}<\cdots<y_{[i]\setminus\{\sigma_{y}^{-1}(i)\}}.

(3.3)

We also define
f_{i}
arbitrarily but measurably in
D_{i}
.

Clearly, item
(i)
of Definition
3.3
holds for
f=(f_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
.

Let us now show that
f
is symmetric. Let
i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, let
(x,y)\in{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\Omega)\setminus D_{i}
,
let
\pi\in S_{i}
and note that

\displaystyle(f_{i}\mathbin{\circ}\pi^{*})(x,y)

\displaystyle=\bigl(x_{[i]},(\tau_{i}(y_{[i]})\mathbin{\circ}\sigma_{\pi^{*}(y)})^{*}({\leq})\bigr)

\displaystyle\bigl((\id_{\Omega_{2}}\otimes\pi^{*})\mathbin{\circ}f_{i}\bigr)(x,y)

\displaystyle=\Bigl(x_{[i]},\pi^{*}\bigl((\tau_{i}(y_{[i]})\mathbin{\circ}\sigma_{y})^{*}({\leq})\bigr)\Bigr)=\bigl(x_{[i]},(\tau_{i}(y_{[i]})\mathbin{\circ}\sigma_{y}\mathbin{\circ}\pi)^{*}({\leq})\bigr),

so it suffices to show that
\sigma_{y}\mathbin{\circ}\pi=\sigma_{\pi^{*}(y)}
.

But indeed, we know that
\sigma_{\pi^{*}(y)}
is the unique element of
S_{i}
such that

\displaystyle\pi^{*}(y)_{[i]\setminus\{\sigma_{\pi^{*}(y)}^{-1}(1)\}}<\pi^{*}(y)_{[i]\setminus\{\sigma_{\pi^{*}(y)}^{-1}(2)\}}<\cdots<\pi^{*}(y)_{[i]\setminus\{\sigma_{\pi^{*}(y)}^{-1}(i)\}}

and since for every
j\in[i]
, we have

\displaystyle\pi^{*}(y)_{[i]\setminus\{\sigma_{\pi^{*}(y)}^{-1}(j)\}}

\displaystyle=y_{[i]\setminus\{(\pi\mathbin{\circ}\sigma_{\pi^{*}(y)}^{-1})(j)\}}=y_{[i]\setminus\{(\sigma_{\pi^{*}(y)}\mathbin{\circ}\pi^{-1})^{-1}(j)\}},

it follows that
\sigma_{y}=\sigma_{\pi^{*}(y)}\mathbin{\circ}\pi^{-1}
, hence
\sigma_{y}\mathbin{\circ}\pi=\sigma_{\pi^{*}(y)}
as desired. Therefore
f_{i}
is symmetric.

Let us now show that
f_{i}
is measure-preserving on h.o.a. Fix
(x,y)\in{\mathcal{E}}_{i,i-1}(\Omega_{1})
and
note that showing that the function
f_{i}(x,y,\mathord{-})\colon\Omega_{1}\to\Omega_{2}\times{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
is
measure-preserving is equivalent to showing that if
\bm{w}
and
\bm{z}
are picked
i.i.d. uniformly at random in
[0,1)
, then
(\bm{u},{\bm{\lhd}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}f_{i}((x,\bm{w}),(y,\bm{z}))

is uniformly distributed in
[0,1)\times{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
.

The definition of
f_{i}
gives
\bm{u}=\bm{w}
and
\bm{\lhd}
is
\bm{z}
-measurable with

\displaystyle\bm{\lhd}

\displaystyle=(\tau_{i}(\bm{z})\mathbin{\circ}\sigma_{y,\bm{z}})^{*}({\leq}),

but note that
\sigma_{y,\bm{z}}
does not depend on
\bm{z}
at all and
\tau_{i}(\bm{z})
is
uniformly distributed in
S_{i}
, hence
\bm{\lhd}
is uniformly distributed in
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
and is
independent from
\bm{u}
. Therefore
(\bm{u},\bm{\lhd})
is uniformly distributed in

[0,1)\times{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
.

It remains to show that there exists an almost inverse family of
f
. For each
i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, let

\displaystyle D^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{(y,{\lhd})\in{\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{(1)}(\Omega_{2})\mid\exists A,B\in r(i),(A\neq B\land y_{A}=y_{B})\}.

Clearly
D^{\prime}_{i}
is an
S_{i}
-invariant Borel set with zero
\lambda
-measure.

We now define
S_{i}
-invariant functions

\displaystyle h_{i}\colon

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{(1)}(\Omega_{2})\setminus D^{\prime}_{i}\to[0,1),

\displaystyle k_{i}\colon

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{(1)}(\Omega_{2})\setminus D^{\prime}_{i}\to[i!]

inductively in
i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
as follows. (We will later use
h_{i}
in (
3.8
) to define
the almost inverse family
g
of
f
.)

To start the induction, we define
h_{0}
as the constant
0
function. Given
h_{i-1}
, for

(y,{\lhd})\in{\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{(1)}(\Omega_{2})\setminus D^{\prime}_{i}
, we let
k_{i}(y,{\lhd})\in[i!]
be the unique
element such that

\displaystyle(\gamma^{i}_{k_{i}(y,{\lhd})}\mathbin{\circ}\widetilde{\sigma}_{y,{\lhd}})^{*}({\leq})

\displaystyle={\lhd_{[i]}},

(3.4)

where
\leq
is the usual order on
[i]
,
\widetilde{\sigma}_{y,{\lhd}}\in S_{i}
is the unique
permutation such that

\displaystyle[i]\ni a

\displaystyle\longmapsto h_{i-1}(\alpha_{[i]\setminus\{\widetilde{\sigma}_{y,{\lhd}}^{-1}(a)\},i}^{*}(y,{\lhd}))\in[0,1)

(3.5)

is increasing and for each
A\subseteq[i]
,
\alpha_{A,i}\colon[\lvert A\rvert]\to[i]
is any
injection with
\im(\alpha_{A,i})=A
. Note that since
h_{i-1}
is inductively assumed to be

S_{i-1}
-invariant, the above does not depend on the choice of
\alpha_{A,i}
.

We then define

\displaystyle h_{i}(y,{\lhd})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\frac{y_{[i]}+k_{i}(y,{\lhd})-1}{i!}.

Clearly, if
k_{i}
is
S_{i}
-invariant, so is
h_{i}
.

Let us then show that
k_{i}
is
S_{i}
-invariant. Let
(y,{\lhd})\in{\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{(1)}(\Omega_{2})\setminus D^{\prime}_{i}
, let
\pi\in S_{i}
. Since

\displaystyle(\gamma^{i}_{k_{i}(\pi^{*}(y,{\lhd}))}\mathbin{\circ}\widetilde{\sigma}_{\pi^{*}(y,{\lhd})})^{*}({\leq})

\displaystyle=\pi^{*}({\lhd_{[i]}}),

we get

\displaystyle(\gamma^{i}_{k_{i}(\pi^{*}(y,{\lhd}))}\mathbin{\circ}\widetilde{\sigma}_{\pi^{*}(y,{\lhd})}\mathbin{\circ}\pi^{-1})^{*}({\leq})

\displaystyle={\lhd_{[i]}},

so it suffices to show that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\sigma}_{\pi^{*}(y,{\lhd})}\mathbin{\circ}\pi^{-1}=\widetilde{\sigma}_{y,{\lhd}}.

(3.6)

To do so, note first that since for every
a\in[i]
, we have

\displaystyle h_{i-1}(\alpha_{[i]\setminus\{\widetilde{\sigma}_{\pi^{*}(y,{\lhd})}^{-1}(a)\},i}^{*}(\pi^{*}(y,{\lhd})))

\displaystyle=h_{i-1}(\pi\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{[i]\setminus\{\widetilde{\sigma}_{\pi^{*}(y,{\lhd})}^{-1}(a)\},i})^{*}(y,{\lhd}))

(3.7)

\displaystyle=h_{i-1}(\alpha_{[i]\setminus\{(\widetilde{\sigma}_{\pi^{*}(y,{\lhd})}\mathbin{\circ}\pi^{-1})^{-1}(a)\},i})^{*}(y,{\lhd}),

where the second equality follows since
h_{i-1}
is inductively assumed to be

S_{i-1}
-invariant and

\displaystyle\im(\pi\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{[i]\setminus\{\widetilde{\sigma}_{\pi^{*}(y,{\lhd})}^{-1}(a)})

\displaystyle=\pi([i]\setminus\{\widetilde{\sigma}_{\pi^{*}(y,{\lhd})}^{-1}(a)\})

\displaystyle=[i]\setminus\{(\pi\mathbin{\circ}\widetilde{\sigma}_{\pi^{*}(y,{\lhd})}^{-1})(a)\}=\im(\alpha_{[i]\setminus\{(\widetilde{\sigma}_{\pi^{*}(y,{\lhd})}\mathbin{\circ}\pi^{-1})^{-1}(a)}).

Since
\widetilde{\sigma}_{y,{\lhd}}
and
\widetilde{\sigma}_{\pi^{*}(y,{\lhd})}
are the unique elements of

S_{i}
such that

\displaystyle[i]\ni a

\displaystyle\longmapsto h_{i-1}(\alpha_{[i]\setminus\{\widetilde{\sigma}_{y,{\lhd}}^{-1}(a)\}}^{*}(y,{\lhd}))\in[0,1)

\displaystyle[i]\ni a

\displaystyle\longmapsto h_{i-1}(\alpha_{[i]\setminus\{\widetilde{\sigma}_{\pi^{*}(y,{\lhd})}^{-1}(a)\}}^{*}(\pi^{*}(y,{\lhd})))\in[0,1)

are increasing, (
3.6
) follows from (
3.7
). Therefore
k_{i}
is

S_{i}
-invariant.

We now claim that
h_{i}
is measure-preserving on h.o.a. (or more precisely, any measurable
extension of
h_{i}
to
{\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{(1)}(\Omega_{2})
is measure-preserving on h.o.a.). This is equivalent
to saying that for
\lambda^{(1)}
-almost every
(y,{\lhd})\in{\mathcal{E}}_{i,i-1}^{(1)}(\Omega_{2})
such
that
y_{A}\neq y_{B}
whenever
A\neq B
, if
\bm{z}
and
\bm{\ll}
are sampled independently and
uniformly in
[0,1)
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
, respectively, then
\bm{w}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}h_{i}((y,\bm{z}),({\lhd},\bm{\ll}))

is uniformly distributed in
[0,1)
.

In turn, from the definition of
h_{i}
, the above is equivalent to showing that
\bm{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}k_{i}((y,\bm{z}),({\lhd},\bm{\ll}))
is uniformly distributed in
[i!]
. But this follows since

\displaystyle(\gamma^{i}_{\bm{j}}\mathbin{\circ}\widetilde{\sigma}_{(y,\bm{z}),({\lhd},\bm{\ll})})^{*}({\leq})

\displaystyle={\bm{\ll}},

since
\widetilde{\sigma}_{(y,\bm{z}),({\lhd},\bm{\ll})}
does not depend on
\bm{z}
or

\bm{\ll}
and since
\bm{\ll}
is uniformly distributed on
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
.

We now finally define
g_{i}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{(1)}(\Omega_{2}\times\Omega_{2})\to\Omega_{1}
(
i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
) as

\displaystyle g_{i}(x,y,{\lhd})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(x_{[i]},h_{i}(y,{\lhd})),

(3.8)

(using an arbitrary measurable extension of
h_{i}
).

It is straightforward to check that the fact that
h_{i}
is
S_{i}
-invariant implies that
g_{i}
is
symmetric and the fact that
h_{i}
is measure-preserving on h.o.a. implies that
g_{i}
is measure
preserving on h.o.a.

We now claim that
g=(g_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
is an almost inverse family of
f
. For this, we have to
show that for every
i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
\lambda^{(1)}
-almost every

(x,y,{\lhd})\in{\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{(1)}(\Omega_{2}\times\Omega_{2})
, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{i}(\widehat{g}_{i}(x,y,{\lhd}))

\displaystyle=(x,{\lhd}).

(3.9)

Since
D^{\prime}_{i}
has zero measure, we may suppose that
(y,{\lhd})\notin D^{\prime}_{i}
. Note that for
A\in r(i)
, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{i}(\widehat{g}_{i}(x,y,{\lhd}))_{A}

\displaystyle=(\overline{\alpha}_{A,i}^{-1})^{*}\biggl(f_{\lvert A\rvert}\Bigl(\alpha_{A,i}^{*}\bigl(\widehat{g}_{i}(x,y,{\lhd})\bigr)\Bigr)\biggr)=(\overline{\alpha}_{A,i}^{-1})^{*}\biggl(f_{\lvert A\rvert}\Bigl(\bigl(\widehat{g}_{\lvert A\rvert}(\alpha_{A,i}^{*}(x,y,{\lhd}))\bigr)\Bigr)\biggr),

almost everywhere, where the second equality follows from
Lemma
3.2
(ii)
.

For every
B\in r(\lvert A\rvert)
, let

\displaystyle z_{B}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\alpha_{B,\lvert A\rvert}^{*}\bigl(\alpha_{A,i}^{*}(x)\bigr)_{[\lvert B\rvert]}=\alpha_{A,i}^{*}(x)_{B}=x_{\alpha_{A,i}(B)},

\displaystyle w_{B}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}h_{\lvert B\rvert}\Bigl(\alpha_{B,\lvert A\rvert}^{*}\bigl(\alpha_{A,i}^{*}(y,{\lhd})\bigr)\Bigr)=\frac{y_{\alpha_{A,i}(B)}+k_{\lvert B\rvert}\bigl((\alpha_{A,i}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{B,\lvert A\rvert})^{*}(y,{\lhd})\bigr)-1}{\lvert B\rvert!},

(3.10)

so that
\widehat{g}_{\lvert A\rvert}(\alpha_{A,i}^{*}(x,y,{\lhd}))=(z,w)
.

We claim that

\displaystyle\sigma_{w}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\sigma}_{\alpha_{A,i}^{*}(y,{\lhd})}.

(3.11)

To see this, note first that for every
a\in[\lvert A\rvert]
, we have

\displaystyle w_{[\lvert A\rvert]\setminus\{\sigma_{w}^{-1}(a)\}}

\displaystyle=h_{\lvert A\rvert-1}\Bigl(\alpha_{[\lvert A\rvert]\setminus\{\sigma_{w}^{-1}(a)\},\lvert A\rvert}^{*}\bigl(\alpha_{A,i}^{*}(y,{\lhd})\bigr)\Bigr).

(3.12)

The definition of
\sigma_{w}
in (
3.3
) is as the unique permutation in
S_{\lvert A\rvert}
such that mapping
a\in[\lvert A\rvert]
to the left-hand side of (
3.12
)
yields an increasing function. On the other hand, the definition of

\widetilde{\sigma}_{\alpha_{A,i}^{*}(y,{\lhd})}
in (
3.5
) is as the
unique permutation in
S_{\lvert A\rvert}
such that mapping
a\in[\lvert A\rvert]
to

\displaystyle h_{\lvert A\rvert-1}\Bigl(\alpha_{[\lvert A\rvert]\setminus\{\widetilde{\sigma}_{\alpha_{A,i}^{*}(y,{\lhd})}(a)\}}^{*}\bigl(\alpha_{A,i}^{*}(y,{\lhd})\bigr)\Bigr)

yields an increasing function; comparing this to the right-hand side of (
3.12
), we
conclude that
\sigma_{w}=\widetilde{\sigma}_{\alpha_{A,i}^{*}(y,{\lhd})}
(i.e.,
(
3.11
) holds).

Note now that the definitions of
\tau_{\lvert A\rvert}
and
w
(in (
3.2
)
and (
3.10
), respectively) imply that

\displaystyle\tau_{\lvert A\rvert}(w_{[\lvert A\rvert]})

\displaystyle=\gamma^{\lvert A\rvert}_{\lfloor w_{[\lvert A\rvert]}\cdot\lvert A\rvert!\rfloor+1}=\gamma^{\lvert A\rvert}_{k_{\lvert A\rvert}(\alpha_{A,i}^{*}(y,{\lhd}))}

(3.13)

almost everywhere simply because
y_{A}\in[0,1)
.

Thus, almost everywhere we have

\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{i}(\widehat{g}_{i}(x,y,{\lhd}))_{A}

\displaystyle=(\overline{\alpha}_{A,i}^{-1})^{*}\bigl(f_{\lvert A\rvert}(z,w)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\Bigl(z_{[\lvert A\rvert]},(\overline{\alpha}_{A,i}^{-1})^{*}\bigl((\tau_{\lvert A\rvert}(w_{[\lvert A\rvert]})\mathbin{\circ}\sigma_{w})^{*}({\leq})\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\Bigl(x_{A},(\overline{\alpha}_{A,i}^{-1})^{*}\bigl((\gamma^{\lvert A\rvert}_{k_{\lvert A\rvert}(\alpha_{A,i}^{*}(y,{\lhd}))}\mathbin{\circ}\widetilde{\sigma}_{\alpha_{A,i}^{*}(x,y,{\lhd})})^{*}({\leq})\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\Bigl(x_{A},(\overline{\alpha}_{A,i}^{-1})^{*}\bigl(\alpha_{A,i}\mathord{\downarrow}_{[\lvert A\rvert]}^{*}({\lhd_{A}})\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=(x_{A},{\lhd_{A}}),

where the third equality follows from (
3.11
) and (
3.13
),
the fourth equality follows from (
3.4
) and the fifth equality follows since

\alpha_{A,i}\mathord{\downarrow}_{[\lvert A\rvert]}=\overline{\alpha}_{A,i}
. Thus (
3.9
)
holds almost everywhere, so
g
is an almost inverse family of
f
.
∎

The next lemma shows how to use families of symmetric measure-preserving on h.o.a. functions, and in
particular
d
-realizations to change representations of limits.

Lemma 3.6.

Let
\Omega_{1}=(X_{1},\mu_{1})
and
\Omega_{2}=(X_{2},\mu_{2})
be atomless standard probability spaces, let

f=(f_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
be a family of symmetric measure-preserving on h.o.a. functions

f_{i}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{(d_{1})}(\Omega_{1})\to\Omega_{2}\times{\mathcal{O}}_{i}^{d_{2}}
and let
{\mathcal{N}}
be a
d_{2}
-Euclidean
structure in
{\mathcal{L}}
over
\Omega_{2}
. Then the following hold.

i.

\rk(f^{*}({\mathcal{N}}))\leq\rk({\mathcal{N}})
.

ii.

\phi_{f^{*}({\mathcal{N}})}=\phi_{\mathcal{N}}
.

iii.

If
d_{1}=0
,
f
is a
d_{2}
-realization and
{\mathcal{N}}
is

\ell
-independent, then
f^{*}({\mathcal{N}})
is a.e. equal to an
(\ell-1)
-independent
0
-Euclidean
structure.

iv.

If
d_{1}=0
,
f
is a
d_{2}
-realization and
{\mathcal{N}}
is

\ell
-independent and does not depend on any coordinate in
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
with
\lvert A\rvert=\ell+1
, then
f^{*}({\mathcal{N}})
is a.e. equal to an
\ell
-independent
0
-Euclidean
structure.

Proof.

Item
(i)
follows directly from definitions.

For item
(ii)
, note that for every finite set
V
, for almost every

x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{V}^{(d_{1})}(\Omega_{1})
, every
P\in{\mathcal{L}}
and every
\alpha\in(V)_{k(P)}
, we have

\displaystyle(M^{f^{*}({\mathcal{N}})}_{V}(x)\vDash P(\alpha))

\displaystyle\iff\alpha^{*}(x)\in f^{*}({\mathcal{N}})_{P}\iff\alpha^{*}(x)\in\widehat{f}_{k(P)}^{-1}({\mathcal{N}}_{P})\iff(\widehat{f}_{k(P)}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha^{*})(x)\in{\mathcal{N}}_{P}

\displaystyle\iff\alpha^{*}(\widehat{f}_{V}(x))\in{\mathcal{N}}_{P}\iff(M^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}(\widehat{f}_{V}(x))\vDash P(\alpha)),

where the third equivalence follows from Lemma
3.2
(ii)
.

Since
\widehat{f}_{V}
is measure-preserving by Lemma
3.2
(i)
,
we get that for
K\in{\mathcal{K}}_{V}
, we have

\displaystyle t_{\operatorname{ind}}\bigl(K,f^{*}({\mathcal{N}})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mu^{(d_{1})}\Bigl(T_{\operatorname{ind}}\bigl(K,f^{*}({\mathcal{N}})\bigr)\Bigr)=\mu^{(d_{1})}\bigl((M_{V}^{f^{*}({\mathcal{N}})})^{-1}(\{K\})\bigr)=\mu^{(d_{1})}\Bigl(\widehat{f}_{V}^{-1}\bigr((M_{V}^{\mathcal{N}})^{-1}(\{K\})\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\mu^{(d_{2})}\bigl((M_{V}^{\mathcal{N}})^{-1}(\{K\})\bigr)=\mu^{(d_{2})}\bigl(T_{\operatorname{ind}}(K,{\mathcal{N}})\bigr)=t_{\operatorname{ind}}(K,{\mathcal{N}}).

Therefore
\phi_{f^{*}({\mathcal{N}})}=\phi_{\mathcal{N}}
.

For item
(iii)
, since each
d_{2}
-
P
-on
{\mathcal{N}}_{P}
is
\ell
-independent
and item
(i)
of Definition
3.3
says that, except for a zero

\mu_{1}
-measure set,
f_{i}
depends only on coordinates indexed by sets in
\binom{[i]}{>i-2}
, it
follows that, except for a zero
\mu_{1}
-measure set, each
P
-on
f^{*}({\mathcal{N}}_{P})
depends only on
coordinates indexed by sets in
\binom{[k(P)]}{>\ell-1}
.

Item
(iv)
follows with the same logic as
item
(iii)
, except that since
{\mathcal{N}}
also does not depend on coordinates
in
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
with
\lvert A\rvert=\ell+1
, it follows that, except for a zero
\mu_{1}
-measure
set, each
P
-on
f^{*}({\mathcal{N}}_{P})
depends only on coordinates indexed by sets in

\binom{[k(P)]}{>\ell}
as the order coordinates of size
\ell+1
are now irrelevant.
∎

Remark 3.7.

The alternative representation
{\mathcal{H}}^{k\operatorname{-qrO}}
in (
2.4
) of the quasirandom

k
-orientation is obtained from the standard representation
{\mathcal{N}}^{k\operatorname{-qrO}}
as
{\mathcal{H}}^{k\operatorname{-qrO}}=f^{*}({\mathcal{N}}^{k\operatorname{-qrO}})
(up to zero-measure) for the
d
-realization constructed in
Lemma
3.5
(when
\Omega=([0,1),\lambda)
).

As a direct corollary, we conclude that
\ast
-
\ell
-independence implies
\texttt{Independence}[\ell^{\prime}]

for every
\ell^{\prime}<\ell
:

Corollary 3.8.

If
\ell^{\prime},\ell\in\mathbb{N}
are such that
\ell^{\prime}<\ell
, then every
\ast
-
\ell
-independent limit

\phi
satisfies
\texttt{Independence}[\ell^{\prime}]
.

Proof.

It suffices to show the case
\ell^{\prime}=\ell-1
. If
\phi
is
\ast
-
\ell
-independent, then there
exist
d\in\mathbb{N}
and an
\ell
-independent
d
-Euclidean structure
{\mathcal{N}}
over some space

\Omega=(X,\mu)
such that
\phi=\phi_{\mathcal{N}}
. By Lemma
3.5
, there exists a

d
-realization
f
from
\Omega
to
\Omega
and by Lemma
3.6
,
items
(ii)
and
(iii)
, we have

\phi=\phi_{f^{*}({\mathcal{N}})}
and
f^{*}({\mathcal{N}})
is a
0
-Euclidean structure that is a.e. equal to some

(\ell-1)
-independent
0
-Euclidean structure
{\mathcal{H}}
. In particular, this implies that for the

(\ell-1)
-independent
0
-Euclidean structure
{\mathcal{H}}
, we have
\phi=\phi_{\mathcal{H}}
, so
\phi
satisfies

\texttt{Independence}[\ell-1]
.
∎

Another direct corollary is that the rank of a limit can be alternatively defined as the minimum
rank of any
d
-Euclidean structure representing it (instead taking the minimum over only

0
-Euclidean structures representing it):

Corollary 3.9.

For every limit
\phi
, we have

\displaystyle\rk(\phi)

\displaystyle=\min_{\mathcal{N}}\rk({\mathcal{N}}),

where the minimum is over all
d
-Euclidean structures
{\mathcal{N}}
over some space
\Omega
and for some

d\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\phi=\phi_{\mathcal{N}}
.

Proof.

Let
m
be the minimum on the right-hand side. From the definition of rank, it is clear that

\rk(\phi)\geq m
. On the other hand, if
{\mathcal{N}}
is a
d
-Euclidean structure over some space

\Omega
with
\phi=\phi_{\mathcal{N}}
and
\rk({\mathcal{N}})=m
, then by Lemma
3.5
, there exists a

d
-realization
f
from
\Omega
to
\Omega
and by Lemma
3.6
,
items
(ii)
and
(i)
, we have

\phi=\phi_{f^{*}({\mathcal{N}})}
and
\rk(\phi)\leq\rk(f^{*}({\mathcal{N}}))\leq\rk({\mathcal{N}})=m
(as
f^{*}({\mathcal{N}})
is a

0
-Euclidean structure).
∎

4
Uniqueness for
d
-theons

This section is devoted to proving the two easiest non-trivial implications of
Theorem
2.2
, namely, the
implications
(ii)
\implies
(i)
, which says that any

\ast
-
\ell
-independent limit satisfies
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]

(Proposition
4.4
) and the
implication
(iv)
\implies
(i)
, which says that any
limit obtained as a quantifier-free definition of an independent coupling of an

(\ell+1)
-quasirandom orientation with an
\ell
-independent limit satisfies
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]

(Corollary
4.6
).

The proof idea is the same as the proof that
\texttt{Independence}[\ell]\implies\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]

from
[
CR23
, Theorem 3.2]
: we first show that if
\rk(\psi)\leq\ell
, then every
d
-Euclidean
structure
{\mathcal{N}}
representing
\psi
must be a.e. equal to a
d
-Euclidean structure of rank at most

\ell
(Lemma
4.3
). This means that for a coupling
\xi
of a
\ast
-
\ell
-independent
limit
\phi
with
\psi
, we can represent
\xi
by some
d
-Euclidean structure
{\mathcal{H}}
such that the

\phi
part is
\ell
-independent and the
\psi
part has “almost everywhere” rank at most
\ell
;
thus
t_{\operatorname{ind}}(\mathord{-},{\mathcal{H}})
behaves multiplicatively so
\xi
must be the independent coupling

\phi\otimes\psi
.

The main ingredient for such a proof is
d
-theon uniqueness that characterizes when two
d
-theons
represent the same limit. We start by stating the
0
-theon uniqueness proved
in
[
CR20
]
12
12

12

Let us note that the statement in
[
CR20
]
is slightly different from the
below in two aspects: First, the measurability is with respect to the completion of the spaces

{\mathcal{E}}_{k}(\Omega)
on the domains of the functions (i.e., the analogue of Lebesgue measurability as
opposed to Borel measurability); however, one can get Borel measurability by simply changing the
functions on a zero measure set. Second, the notions of symmetric and measure-preserving are not
a.e. but everywhere notions in
[
CR20
]
, but this is clearly not an issue (see
Lemma
3.6
(ii)
).
.

Theorem 4.1
(Theon Uniqueness
[
CR20
, Theorems 3.9 and 3.11, Proposition 7.7]
)
.

The following are equivalent for
T
-ons
{\mathcal{N}}^{1}
and
{\mathcal{N}}^{2}
over spaces
\Omega_{1}
and

\Omega_{2}
, respectively.

i.

We have
\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}^{1}}=\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}^{2}}
(i.e.,
{\mathcal{N}}^{1}
and
{\mathcal{N}}^{2}
represent the same limit).

ii.

For some (equivalently, every) atomless standard probability space
\Omega
, there exist
families
f=(f_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
and
g=(g_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
of symmetric measure-preserving on
h.o.a. functions
f_{i}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\Omega)\to\Omega_{1}
and
g_{i}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\Omega)\to\Omega_{2}

such that
f^{*}({\mathcal{N}}^{1})
is a.e. equal to
g^{*}({\mathcal{N}}^{2})
.

iii.

There exists a family
h=(h_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
of symmetric measure-preserving on
h.o.a. functions (
h_{i}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{1})\to\Omega_{2}
) such that
h^{*}({\mathcal{N}}^{2})

is a.e. equal to the the
T
-on
{\mathcal{H}}
over
\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{1}
given by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{P}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{(x,y)\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k(P)}(\Omega\times\Omega)\mid x\in{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{P}\}\qquad(P\in{\mathcal{L}}),

where
{\mathcal{L}}
is the language of
T
.

The avid reader might have noticed that we did not use almost inverse families of
d
-realizations
in any of the applications in Section
3
; this is where they will become useful: we
will reduce
d
-theon uniqueness to theon uniqueness via
d
-realizations and their almost inverse
families.

Theorem 4.2
(
d
-Theon Uniqueness)
.

The following are equivalent for a
d_{1}
-
T
-on
{\mathcal{N}}^{1}
over
\Omega_{1}
and a
d_{2}
-
T
-on
{\mathcal{N}}^{2}

over
\Omega_{2}
.

i.

We have
\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}^{1}}=\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}^{2}}
(i.e.,
{\mathcal{N}}^{1}
and
{\mathcal{N}}^{2}

represent the same limit).

ii.

For every
d\in\mathbb{N}
and every atomless standard probability space

\Omega
, there exist families
f=(f_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
and
g=(g_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
of symmetric
measure-preserving on h.o.a. functions

f_{i}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{(d)}(\Omega)\to\Omega_{1}\times{\mathcal{O}}_{i}^{d_{1}}
and

g_{i}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{(d)}(\Omega)\to\Omega_{2}\times{\mathcal{O}}_{i}^{d_{2}}
such that
f^{*}({\mathcal{N}}^{1})
is a.e. equal
to
g^{*}({\mathcal{N}}^{2})
.

iii.

Item
(iii)
holds for some
d
and
\Omega
.

iv.

There exists a family
h=(h_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
of symmetric measure-preserving
on h.o.a. functions
(
h_{i}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{(d_{1})}(\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{1})\to\Omega_{2}\times{\mathcal{O}}_{i}^{d_{2}}
) such that

h^{*}({\mathcal{N}}^{2})
is a.e. equal to the
d_{1}
-
T
-on
{\mathcal{H}}
over
\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{1}
given by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{P}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{(x,y,{\lhd})\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k(P)}^{(d_{1})}(\Omega\times\Omega)\mid(x,{\lhd})\in{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{P}\}\qquad(P\in{\mathcal{L}}),

where
{\mathcal{L}}
is the language of
T
.

Proof.

The implication
(ii)
\implies
(iii)
is trivial.

The implications
(iii)
\implies
(i)

and
(iv)
\implies
(i)
follow from
Lemma
3.6
(ii)
.

Let us now show the implication
(i)
\implies
(ii)
.

By Lemma
3.5
, for
j\in[2]
, there exists a
d_{j}
-realization
F_{j}
from
\Omega_{j}
to

\Omega_{j}
. Since by Lemma
3.6
(ii)
we have

\displaystyle\phi_{F_{1}^{*}({\mathcal{N}}^{1})}

\displaystyle=\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}^{1}}=\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}^{2}}=\phi_{F_{2}^{*}({\mathcal{N}}^{2})}

for the
0
-
T
-ons
F_{1}^{*}({\mathcal{N}}^{1})
and
F_{2}^{*}({\mathcal{N}}^{2})
, by Theorem
4.1
, there exist
families
f^{\prime}=(f^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
and
g^{\prime}=(g^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
of symmetric measure-preserving on
h.o.a. functions
f^{\prime}_{i}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\Omega)\to\Omega_{1}
and
g^{\prime}_{i}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\Omega)\to\Omega_{2}

such that
(f^{\prime})^{*}(F_{1}^{*}({\mathcal{N}}^{1}))
is a.e. equal to
(g^{\prime})^{*}(F_{2}^{*}({\mathcal{N}}^{2}))
.

Define
f_{i}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{(d)}(\Omega)\to\Omega_{1}\times{\mathcal{O}}_{i}^{d_{1}}
and

g_{i}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{(d)}(\Omega)\to\Omega_{2}\times{\mathcal{O}}^{d_{2}}
by

\displaystyle f_{i}(x,{\leq})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}((F_{1})_{i}\mathbin{\circ}\widehat{f^{\prime}}_{i})(x),

\displaystyle g_{i}(x,{\leq})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}((F_{2})_{i}\mathbin{\circ}\widehat{g^{\prime}}_{i})(x).

(Note that both
f_{i}
and
g_{i}
completely ignore the order variables.) It is straightforward to
check that
f_{i}
and
g_{i}
are symmetric and measure-preserving on h.o.a. and that
f^{*}({\mathcal{N}}^{1})

is a.e. equal to
g^{*}({\mathcal{N}}^{2})
.

It remains to show the implication
(i)
\implies
(iv)
.

Let
F_{1}
be a
d_{1}
-realization from
\Omega_{1}
to
\Omega_{1}
and let
G_{1}
be an almost inverse
family of
F
. Let also
F_{2}
be a
d_{2}
-realization from
\Omega_{2}
to
\Omega_{2}
. Since by
Lemma
3.6
(ii)
we have

\displaystyle\phi_{F_{1}^{*}({\mathcal{N}}^{1})}

\displaystyle=\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}^{1}}=\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}^{2}}=\phi_{F_{2}^{*}({\mathcal{N}}^{2})}

for the
0
-
T
-ons
F_{1}^{*}({\mathcal{N}}^{1})
and
F_{2}^{*}({\mathcal{N}}^{2})
, by Theorem
4.2
, there exists a
family
h^{\prime}=(h^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
of symmetric measure-preserving on h.o.a. functions

h^{\prime}_{i}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{1})\to\Omega_{2}
such that
(h^{\prime})^{*}(F_{2}^{*}({\mathcal{N}}^{2}))
is
a.e. equal to the
T
-on
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
over
\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{1}
given by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}_{P}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{(x,y)\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k(P)}(\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{1})\mid x\in F_{1}^{*}({\mathcal{N}}^{1})_{P}\}\qquad(P\in{\mathcal{L}}).

Let now
H\colon\Omega_{1}\to\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{1}
be a measure-isomorphism modulo
0
and let

H^{1},H^{2}\colon\Omega_{1}\to\Omega_{1}
be its coordinate functions (which are measure-preserving) and
define functions
\widetilde{H}^{1}_{i},\widetilde{H}^{2}_{i}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\Omega_{1})\to{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\Omega_{1})
by

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}^{j}_{i}(y)_{A}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}H^{j}(y_{A})\qquad(j\in[2],y\in{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\Omega_{1}),A\in r(i)).

Clearly
\widetilde{H}^{j}_{i}
is measure-preserving.

Finally, define
h_{i}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{(d)}(\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{1})\to\Omega_{2}\times{\mathcal{O}}_{i}^{d_{2}}
by

\displaystyle h_{i}(x,y,{\leq})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(F_{2})_{i}\Bigl(\widehat{h^{\prime}}_{i}\bigl(\widehat{(G_{1})}_{i}(x,\widetilde{H}^{1}_{i}(y),{\leq}),\widetilde{H}^{2}_{i}(y)\bigr)\Bigr).

It is straightforward to check that
h_{i}
is symmetric and measure-preserving on h.o.a.

Let us now show that
h^{*}({\mathcal{N}}^{2})
is a.e. equal to the
d_{1}
-
T
-on
{\mathcal{H}}
over

\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{1}
given by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{P}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{(x,y,{\lhd})\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k(P)}^{(d_{1})}(\Omega\times\Omega)\mid(x,{\lhd})\in{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{P}\}\qquad(P\in{\mathcal{L}}).

Fix
P\in{\mathcal{L}}
and note that for
(\mu_{1}\otimes\mu_{1})^{(d_{1})}
-almost every

(x,y,{\leq})\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k(P)}^{(d_{1})}(\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{1})
, we have

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!(x,y,{\leq})\in{\mathcal{H}}_{P}

\displaystyle\iff(x,{\leq})\in{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{P}

\displaystyle\iff\widehat{(F_{1})}_{k(P)}\Bigl(\widehat{(G_{1})}_{k(P)}\bigl(x,\widetilde{H}_{k(P)}^{1}(y),{\leq}\bigr)\Bigr)\in{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{P}

\displaystyle\iff\widehat{(G_{1})}_{k(P)}\bigl(x,\widetilde{H}_{k(P)}^{1}(y),{\leq}\bigr)\in F_{1}^{*}({\mathcal{N}}^{1})_{P}

\displaystyle\iff\Bigl(\widehat{(G_{1})}_{k(P)}\bigl(x,\widetilde{H}_{k(P)}^{1}(y),{\leq}\bigr),\widetilde{H}^{2}_{k(P)}(y)\Bigr)\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}_{P}

\displaystyle\iff\Bigl(\widehat{(G_{1})}_{k(P)}\bigl(x,\widetilde{H}_{k(P)}^{1}(y),{\leq}\bigr),\widetilde{H}^{2}_{k(P)}(y)\Bigr)\in(h^{\prime})^{*}(F_{2}^{*}({\mathcal{N}}^{2}))_{P}

\displaystyle\iff(\widehat{F_{2}})_{k(P)}\Biggl(\biggl(\widehat{h^{\prime}}_{k(P)}\Bigl(\widehat{(G_{1})}_{k(P)}\bigl(x,\widetilde{H}_{k(P)}^{1}(y),{\leq}\bigr),\widetilde{H}^{2}_{k(P)}(y)\Bigr)\biggr)\Biggr)\in{\mathcal{N}}^{2}_{P}

\displaystyle\iff\widehat{h}_{k(P)}(x,y,{\leq})\in{\mathcal{N}}^{2}_{P}

\displaystyle\iff(x,y,{\leq})\in h^{*}({\mathcal{N}}^{2})_{P},

as desired, where the second equivalence follows since
G
is an almost inverse family to
F
and

\widetilde{H}^{1}_{k(P)}
is measure-preserving, the fourth equivalence follows by the definition
of
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
, the fifth equivalence follows since
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
is a.e. equal to
(h^{\prime})^{*}(F_{2}^{*}({\mathcal{N}}^{2}))
and
the seventh equivalence follows by the definition of
h
.
∎

The next lemma shows that except for zero measure changes, the notion of rank does not depend on the
representation.

Lemma 4.3.

If
{\mathcal{N}}
is a
d
-Euclidean structure over
\Omega
, then it is a.e. equal to a

d
-Euclidean structure
{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}
over
\Omega
with
\rk({\mathcal{N}}^{\prime})=\rk(\phi_{\mathcal{N}})
. Moreover, if
{\mathcal{N}}
is

\ell
-independent from some
\ell
, then
{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}
can be taken to also be
\ell
-independent.

Proof.

Write
\Omega=(X,\mu)
and let
r\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\rk(\phi_{\mathcal{N}})
. Note that it suffices to prove the case when

{\mathcal{L}}
has a single predicate symbol
P
.

Let
k\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}k(P)
and define
W\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{k,r}^{(d)}(\Omega)\to[0,1]
by

\displaystyle W(x)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mu^{(d)}\left(\left\{y\in\prod_{A\in\binom{[k]}{>r}}(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{d})\;\middle|\;(x,y)\in{\mathcal{N}}_{P}\right\}\right)

and let

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{P}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}W^{-1}(1)\times\prod_{A\in\binom{[k]}{>r}}(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{d}).

Clearly
\rk({\mathcal{H}})\leq r
. Also note that if
{\mathcal{N}}
is
\ell
-independent, then so is
{\mathcal{H}}
.

Thus, it suffices to show that
{\mathcal{N}}
is a.e. equal to
{\mathcal{H}}
. In turn, this is equivalent to
showing that
W
is
\{0,1\}
-valued
\mu^{(d)}
-a.e.

Since
\rk(\phi_{\mathcal{N}})\leq r
, we know that there exists a
0
-Euclidean structure
{\mathcal{G}}
over some
space
\Omega^{\prime}=(X^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
such that
\phi_{\mathcal{G}}=\phi_{\mathcal{N}}
and
\rk({\mathcal{G}})=r
. By
Theorem
4.2
, there exist families
f=(f_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
and
g=(g_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
of
symmetric measure-preserving on h.o.a. functions

f_{i}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\Omega)\to\Omega\times{\mathcal{O}}_{i}^{(d)}
and
g_{i}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}(\Omega)\to\Omega^{\prime}
such
that
f^{*}({\mathcal{N}})
is a.e. equal to
g^{*}({\mathcal{G}})
.

From the structure of the functions
\widehat{f}_{k}
and
\widehat{g}_{k}
, we can decompose them as

\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{k}(x,y)

\displaystyle=(F_{1}(x),F_{2}(x,y)),

\displaystyle\widehat{g}_{k}(x,y)

\displaystyle=(G_{1}(x),G_{2}(x,y)),

where
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k,r}(\Omega)
,
y\in X^{\binom{[k]}{>r}}
and

\displaystyle F_{1}\colon

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{k,r}(\Omega)\to{\mathcal{E}}_{k,r}^{(d)}(\Omega),

\displaystyle F_{2}\colon

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{k}(\Omega)\to\prod_{A\in\binom{[k]}{>r}}(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{d}),

\displaystyle G_{1}\colon

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{k,r}(\Omega)\to{\mathcal{E}}_{k,r}(\Omega^{\prime}),

\displaystyle G_{2}\colon

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{k}(\Omega)\to X^{\binom{[k]}{>r}}.

The fact that the
f_{i}
and
g_{i}
are measure-preserving on h.o.a. implies that
F_{1}
and
G_{1}

are measure-preserving and that for almost every
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k,r}(\Omega)
, the restrictions

F_{2}(x,\mathord{-})
and
G_{2}(x,\mathord{-})
are also measure-preserving.

Since
f^{*}({\mathcal{N}})
is a.e. equal to
g^{*}({\mathcal{G}})
, by Fubini’s Theorem, we get

\displaystyle W(F_{1}(x))

\displaystyle=\mu\left(\left\{y\in X^{\binom{[k]}{>r}}\;\middle|\;(G_{1}(x),G_{2}(x,y))\in{\mathcal{G}}_{P}\right\}\right)

for
\mu
-almost every
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k,r}(\Omega)
. Since
\rk({\mathcal{G}})=r
, the measure above can only be

0
or
1
(as
G_{2}(x,y)
only contains coordinates indexed by sets in
\binom{[k]}{>r}
). Using
again that
F_{1}
is measure-preserving, we conclude that
W(z)\in\{0,1\}
for
\mu^{(d)}
-almost
every
z\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k,r}^{(d)}(\Omega)
, as desired.
∎

We can finally prove the
implication
(ii)
\implies
(i)
of
Theorem
2.2
in the proposition below.

Proposition 4.4
(Theorem
2.2
(ii)
\implies
(i)
)
.

If
\phi\in\operatorname{Hom}^{+}({\mathcal{A}}[T],\mathbb{R})
is
\ast
-
\ell
-independent, then
\phi
satisfies
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
.

Proof.

Let
{\mathcal{L}}
be the language of
T
. Since
\phi
is
\ast
-
\ell
-independent, there exist
d\in\mathbb{N}

and an
\ell
-independent
d
-
T
-on over some space
\Omega
such that
\phi=\phi_{\mathcal{N}}
.

Let now
\psi
be any limit in some language
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
with
\rk(\psi)\leq\ell
, let
\xi
be a
coupling of
\phi
and
\psi
and let
{\mathcal{G}}
be any
0
-Euclidean structure in
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
over some
space
\Omega^{\prime}
such that
\xi=\phi_{\mathcal{G}}
.

Since
\xi\mathord{|}_{\mathcal{L}}=\phi
, we have
\phi_{{\mathcal{G}}\mathord{|}_{\mathcal{L}}}=\phi_{\mathcal{N}}
, so by Theorem
4.2
,
there exists a family
h=(h_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
of symmetric measure-preserving on h.o.a. functions

h_{i}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{(d)}(\Omega\times\Omega)\to\Omega^{\prime}
such that
h^{*}({\mathcal{G}}\mathord{|}_{\mathcal{L}})
is a.e. equal
to the
d
-
T
-on
{\mathcal{H}}
over
\Omega\times\Omega
given by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{P}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{(x,y,{\lhd})\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k(P)}^{(d)}(\Omega\times\Omega)\mid(x,{\lhd})\in{\mathcal{N}}_{P}\}\qquad(P\in{\mathcal{L}}).

We now extend
{\mathcal{H}}
to a
d
-Euclidean structure
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
in
{\mathcal{L}}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cdot}}{{\cup}}}{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
over

\Omega\times\Omega
by defining for every
P\in{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
the
P
-on

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}_{P}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\widehat{h}_{k(P)}^{-1}({\mathcal{G}}_{P}).

Note that since
h^{*}({\mathcal{G}}\mathord{|}_{\mathcal{L}})
is a.e. equal to
{\mathcal{H}}
, it follows that
h^{*}({\mathcal{G}})
is
a.e. equal to
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
, hence by Lemma
3.6
(ii)
, we
get
\phi_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}=\phi_{\mathcal{G}}=\xi
.

Note now that, since
{\mathcal{N}}
is
\ell
-independent, it follows that
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\mathord{|}_{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{H}}
is

\ell
-independent and since

\rk(\phi_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\mathord{|}_{{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}}})=\rk(\xi\mathord{|}_{{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}})=\rk(\psi)\leq\ell
, by Lemma
4.3
, by
possibly changing only a zero measure set, we may suppose that
\rk({\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\mathord{|}_{{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}})\leq\ell
.

Let now
V
be a finite set and note that if
z\in{\mathcal{E}}_{V}^{(d)}(\Omega\times\Omega)
, then since

{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\mathord{|}_{\mathcal{L}}
is
\ell
-independent, it follows that
M^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}(z)\mathord{|}_{\mathcal{L}}
only depends on the
coordinates of
z
that are indexed by sets in
\binom{V}{>\ell}
. Similarly, since

\rk({\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\mathord{|}_{{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}})\leq\ell
,
M^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}(z)\mathord{|}_{{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}}
only depends on the coordinates of
z

that are indexed by sets in
r(V,\ell)
.

We conclude that if
\bm{z}
is picked at random in
{\mathcal{E}}_{V}^{(d)}(\Omega\times\Omega)
according to

(\mu\otimes\mu)^{(d)}
, then
M^{{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}_{V}(\bm{z})\mathord{|}_{\mathcal{L}}
is independent from

M^{{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}_{V}(\bm{z})\mathord{|}_{{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}}
, so for every
K\in{\mathcal{K}}_{V}[{\mathcal{L}}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cdot}}{{\cup}}}{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}]
, we have

\displaystyle t_{\operatorname{ind}}(K,{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=t_{\operatorname{ind}}(K\mathord{|}_{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\mathord{|}_{\mathcal{L}})\cdot t_{\operatorname{ind}}(K\mathord{|}_{{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}},{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\mathord{|}_{{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}}),

that is,
\phi_{{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}=\xi
is the independent coupling
\phi\otimes\psi
. Therefore
\phi

satisfies
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
.
∎

As a corollary, we get that the quasirandom
k
-orientation (see Definition
2.1
)
satisfies
\texttt{UCouple}[k-1]
:

Corollary 4.5.

The quasirandom
k
-orientation
\phi^{k\operatorname{-qrO}}
satisfies
\texttt{UCouple}[k-1]
.

Proof.

Since
\phi^{k\operatorname{-qrO}}=\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}^{k\operatorname{-qrO}}}
for the
(k-1)
-independent
1
-
T_{k\operatorname{-Orientation}}
-on
{\mathcal{N}}^{k\operatorname{-qrO}}
,
it is
\ast
-
(k-1)
-independent, so it satisfies
\texttt{UCouple}[k-1]
by
Proposition
4.4
.
∎

As a further consequence, we can also obtain the
implication
(iv)
\implies
(i)
of
Theorem
2.2
from the facts that
\texttt{Independence}[\ell]\implies\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
and that

\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
is preserved by independent couplings and quantifier-free definitions.

Corollary 4.6
(Theorem
2.2
(iv)
\implies
(i)
)
.

If
I\colon T\leadsto T^{\prime}\cup T_{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-Orientation}}
is a quantifier-free definition and
\psi\in\operatorname{Hom}^{+}({\mathcal{A}}[T^{\prime}],\mathbb{R})

satisfies
\texttt{Independence}[\ell]
, then
I^{*}(\psi\otimes\phi^{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-qrO}})
satisfies

\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
.

Proof.

By
[
CR23
, Theorem 3.2]
and Corollary
4.5
, both
\psi
and
\phi^{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-qrO}}

satisfy
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
, hence by
[
CR23
, Theorem 3.4]
so does their independent coupling

\psi\otimes\phi^{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-qrO}}
and by
[
CR23
, Theorem 3.3]
so does

I^{*}(\psi\otimes\phi^{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-qrO}})
.
∎

5
Types

We now move to arguably the hardest implication of Theorem
2.2
,

(i)
\implies
(iii)
, whose full argument will only be
concluded in Section
9
.

Recall from the introduction that the main idea is to show that a limit satisfying
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]

has a different representation in which we sample “amalgamating types” of points instead of the
points directly. For this, we will need these “amalgamating types” to have the following
properties:

i.

(Uniqueness for
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
) If
{\mathcal{N}}
is a
d
-Euclidean structure that is

(\ell-1)
-independent and satisfies
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
, then
{\mathcal{N}}
has a unique amalgamating

\ell
-type.

ii.

(Strong amalgamation:) If
A
is a finite set then the distribution of
the random amalgamating
A
-type is completely determined when we are given the amalgamating
B
-types for
all
B\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)
.

As we have previously mentioned, amalgamating types will be obtained as something intermediate
between what we call “dissociated types” and “overlapping types”. The objective of this section
is to define these latter concepts and prove basic properties about them, culminating in the proof
that they satisfy the uniqueness of item
(i)
above
(Proposition
5.8
). We will see in Section
7
that they also
satisfy a weak version of item
(ii)
(Proposition
7.4
).

Definition 5.1.

Given a finite set
A
and a countable set
V
disjoint from
A
, we define the sets

\displaystyle r^{\dsct}(A,V)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}r(V),

\displaystyle\overline{r}^{\dsct}(A,V)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}r(A\cup V)\setminus(r(A)\cup r^{\dsct}(A,V))=\{B\in r(A\cup V)\mid B\cap A\neq\varnothing\land B\cap V\neq\varnothing\},

\displaystyle r^{\ovlp}(A,V)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{B\in r(A\cup V)\mid A=\varnothing\lor(B\cap V\neq\varnothing\land A\not\subseteq B)\},

\displaystyle\overline{r}^{\ovlp}(A,V)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}r(A\cup V)\setminus(r(A)\cup r^{\ovlp}(A,V))=\{B\in r(A\cup V)\mid\varnothing\neq A\subsetneq B\}.

(Note that when
A=\varnothing
, we have
r^{\dsct}(\varnothing,V)=r^{\ovlp}(\varnothing,V)=r(V)

and
\overline{r}^{\dsct}(\varnothing,V)=\overline{r}^{\ovlp}(\varnothing,V)=\varnothing
.)

The dissociated type of some
d
-Euclidean structure
\mathcal{N}
will be determined by the
information contained in the coordinates whose indices are in
r(A)\cup r^{\dsct}(A,V)
. The
overlapping type will contain slightly more information, since it will instead use the coordinates
whose indices are in
r(A)\cup r^{\ovlp}(A,V)
, a slightly larger set. (The sets

\overline{r}^{\dsct}
and
\overline{r}^{\ovlp}
are simply the complementary information.)

Definition 5.2.

Given further an atomless standard probability space
\Omega=(X,\mu)
and
d\in\mathbb{N}
, we let

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\prod_{B\in r^{\dsct}(A,V)}(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{B}^{d}),

\displaystyle\overline{{\mathcal{E}}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\prod_{B\in\overline{r}^{\dsct}(A,V)}(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{B}^{d}),

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\prod_{B\in r^{\ovlp}(A,V)}(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{B}^{d}),

\displaystyle\overline{{\mathcal{E}}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\prod_{B\in\overline{r}^{\ovlp}(A,V)}(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{B}^{d}),

and we equip these spaces with the product measures

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{B\in r^{\dsct}(A,V)}\left(\mu\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{d}\nu_{B}\right),

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{B\in\overline{r}^{\dsct}(A,V)}\left(\mu\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{d}\nu_{B}\right),

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{B\in r^{\ovlp}(A,V)}\left(\mu\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{d}\nu_{B}\right),

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{B\in\overline{r}^{\ovlp}(A,V)}\left(\mu\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{d}\nu_{B}\right),

which by abuse of notation, we denote all by
\mu^{(d)}
(which is also the notation for the
measure on
{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{A,\ell}^{(d)}
).

Note that an injection
\alpha\colon A\cup V\to U
contra-variantly induces maps

\displaystyle\alpha^{*}\colon

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha(A),U\setminus\alpha(A)}^{(d),\dsct}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct},

\displaystyle\alpha^{*}\colon

\displaystyle\overline{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\alpha(A),U\setminus\alpha(A)}^{(d),\dsct}\to\overline{{\mathcal{E}}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct},

\displaystyle\alpha^{*}\colon

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha(A),U\setminus\alpha(A)}^{(d),\ovlp}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp},

\displaystyle\alpha^{*}\colon

\displaystyle\overline{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\alpha(A),U\setminus\alpha(A)}^{(d),\ovlp}\to\overline{{\mathcal{E}}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp},

all given by the formula
\alpha^{*}(x)_{B}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}x_{\alpha(B)}
. With even more abuse of notation, we
also use
\alpha^{*}
for the product maps of the first two or the last two functions above (which
are also contra-variant definitions).

Given further a
d
-Euclidean structure
{\mathcal{N}}
in
{\mathcal{L}}
over
\Omega
, we define the functions

\displaystyle f^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}\colon

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{A}\times{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}\to{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V}),

\displaystyle f^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}\colon

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{A}\times{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}\to{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V})

as follows: for
(x,z)\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}\times{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
, we let
\bm{y}
be picked at
random in
\overline{{\mathcal{E}}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
according to
\mu^{(d)}
and let

f^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x,z)
be the distribution of the random
{\mathcal{L}}
-structure
M^{\mathcal{N}}_{A\cup V}(x,\bm{y},z)
over
A\cup V
. We define
f^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}
analogously by replacing
\dsct

with
\ovlp
throughout.

For a point
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}
, the
dissociated
(A,V)
-type
of
x
in
{\mathcal{N}}
, denoted by

\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x)
, is the function
{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}\to{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V})
obtained
from
f^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}
by fixing its
{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}
argument to be
x
, up to

\mu^{(d)}
-almost everywhere equivalence (in the argument in
{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
).

We also let
{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}(\Omega,{\mathcal{L}})
be the space of all functions

p\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}\to{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V})
up to
\mu^{(d)}
-almost everywhere
equivalence such that:

•

For every cylinder set
C(U,K)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{M\in{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V}\mid M\mathord{|}_{U}=K\}
of
{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V}

(see (
2.1
)), the map
x\mapsto p(x)(B)
is measurable.

•

There exists
M^{\dsct}_{A,V}(p)\in{\mathcal{K}}_{A}
such that for every
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
, we have

\displaystyle p(x)(\{K\in{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V}\mid K\mathord{|}_{A}=M^{\dsct}_{A,V}(p)\})=1.

Clearly such
M^{\dsct}_{A,V}(p)
is unique and is called the
underlying model
of
p
(and we
view
M^{\dsct}_{A,V}
as a function
{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}\to{\mathcal{K}}_{A}
). Note also that we can view

\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}
as the unique function
{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x)(z)

\displaystyle=f^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x,z)\qquad(x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)},z\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}).

We define
overlapping
(A,V)
-types
and

\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}
analogously by replacing
\dsct

with
\ovlp
throughout.

Our first goal is to show in the lemma below that the definitions of
f^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}
and

f^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}
are equivariant. An important consequence is that the dissociated (overlapping,
respectively)
(A,V)
-type of
x
is completely determined by the dissociated (overlapping,
respectively)
(A,U)
-type of
x
for any fixed countably infinite set
U
(disjoint from

A
). Thus, we will use the term dissociated (overlapping, respectively)
A
-type when we do not
want to specify
V
.

Lemma 5.3
(Equivariance of
f^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}
and
f^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{N}}
be a
d
-Euclidean structure in
{\mathcal{L}}
over
\Omega
, let
A
be a finite set, let
V
be
a countable set disjoint from
A
. Then the definition of
f^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}
is equivariant in
the sense that for every injection
\alpha\colon A\cup V\to U
, the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha(A)}^{(d)}\times{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha(A),U\setminus\alpha(A)}^{(d),\dsct}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{K}}_{U})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}\times{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{\alpha(A),U\setminus\alpha(A)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}}

(5.1)

In particular, if
V
is infinite, then for every set
A^{\prime}
with
\lvert A^{\prime}\rvert=\lvert A\rvert

and every
V^{\prime}
countable and disjoint from
A^{\prime}
, the
function
\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A^{\prime},V^{\prime}}
is completely determined by
\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}
via

\displaystyle\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A^{\prime},V^{\prime}}(\alpha\mathord{\downarrow}_{A^{\prime}}^{*}(x))\mathbin{\circ}\alpha^{*}

\displaystyle=\alpha^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x)\qquad(x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}),

(5.2)

where
\alpha\colon A^{\prime}\cup V^{\prime}\to A\cup V
is any injection with
\alpha(A^{\prime})=A
.

The analogous statements hold for
f^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}
and overlapping types.

Proof.

The proofs for the dissociated and overlapping cases are analogous to each other, so we prove only
the dissociated case.

Let
\alpha\colon A\cup V\to U
be an injection, let
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha(A)}^{(d)}
and

z\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha(A),U\setminus\alpha(A)}^{(d),\dsct}
. Our objective is to show that

\displaystyle\alpha^{*}(f^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{\alpha(A),U\setminus\alpha(A)}(x,z))

\displaystyle=f^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(\alpha^{*}(x,z)).

For this, we let
\bm{y}
and
\bm{w}
be picked at random in

\overline{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\alpha(A),U\setminus\alpha(A)}^{(d),\dsct}
and

\overline{{\mathcal{E}}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
, respectively according to
\mu^{(d)}
and we need to show that

\displaystyle\alpha^{*}(M^{\mathcal{N}}_{U}(x,\bm{y},z))

\displaystyle\sim M^{\mathcal{N}}_{A\cup V}(\alpha^{*}(x),\bm{w},\alpha^{*}(z)).

(5.3)

But note that since
M^{\mathcal{N}}
is equivariant, we have

\displaystyle\alpha^{*}(M^{\mathcal{N}}_{U}(x,\bm{y},z))

\displaystyle=M^{\mathcal{N}}_{U}(\alpha^{*}(x),\alpha^{*}(\bm{y}),\alpha^{*}(z))

and since the middle
\alpha^{*}
is measure-preserving (with respect to both versions of

\mu^{(d)}
), we have
\alpha^{*}(\bm{y})\sim\bm{w}
, so (
5.3
) follows.

For the final assertion, first note that since
V
is (countably) infinite and
V^{\prime}
is countable,
an injection
\alpha\colon A^{\prime}\cup V^{\prime}\to A\cup V
is guaranteed to exist.

We claim that
\alpha\mathord{\downarrow}_{A^{\prime}}^{*}
and
\alpha^{*}
that appear on the left-hand side
of (
5.2
) are surjective (which implies that (
5.2
) indeed completely
determines
\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A^{\prime},V^{\prime}}
).

For
\alpha\mathord{\downarrow}_{A^{\prime}}^{*}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{A^{\prime}}^{(d)}
, since
\lvert A^{\prime}\rvert=\lvert A\rvert
,
\alpha\mathord{\downarrow}_{A^{\prime}}
is bijective so if
x^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A^{\prime}}^{(d)}
, then
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}

defined by
x_{B}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}x_{\alpha^{-1}(B)}
(
B\in r(A)
) satisfies
\alpha\mathord{\downarrow}_{A^{\prime}}^{*}(x)=x^{\prime}
.

For
\alpha^{*}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{A^{\prime},V^{\prime}}^{(d),\dsct}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
, if

z^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A^{\prime},V^{\prime}}^{(d),\dsct}
, then
z\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
defined by
z_{B}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}z_{\alpha^{-1}(B)}
when
B\subseteq\im(\alpha)
and defined arbitrarily for
B\in r^{\dsct}(A,V)\setminus\im(\alpha)
satisfies
\alpha^{*}(z)=z^{\prime}
.

Thus the claim is proved.

Let us now show that (
5.2
) holds: for
z\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A^{\prime},V^{\prime}}^{(d),\dsct}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A^{\prime},V^{\prime}}(\alpha\mathord{\downarrow}_{A^{\prime}}^{*}(x))(\alpha^{*}(z))

\displaystyle=f^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A^{\prime},V^{\prime}}(\alpha^{*}(x,z))=\alpha^{*}(f^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x,z))=\alpha^{*}(\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x)(z)),

where the second equality follows from the fact that (
5.1
) commutes and
\alpha(A)=A^{\prime}

since
\lvert A\rvert=\lvert A^{\prime}\rvert<\infty
and
\alpha
is injective.
∎

Our next objective is to show that injective functions also induce contra-variant maps between the
spaces of types (both dissociated and overlapping).

Definition 5.4.

Let
\Omega=(X,\mu)
be an atomless standard probability space, let
d\in\mathbb{N}
, let
{\mathcal{L}}
be a
finite relational language, let
\alpha\colon A\to B
be an injection between finite sets and let

V
be a countable set disjoint from both
A
and
B
.

We define the map
\alpha^{*}\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\dsct}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
as follows: recall
that
\overline{\alpha}
denotes the bijection
A\to\alpha(A)
obtained from
\alpha
by
restricting its codomain. In turn, this contra-variantly induces the map

(\overline{\alpha}^{-1})^{*}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha(A),V}^{(d),\dsct}
via

(\overline{\alpha}^{-1})^{*}(x)_{C}=x_{\overline{\alpha}^{-1}(C)}
.

For
p\in{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\dsct}
, that is,
p\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{B,V}^{(d),\dsct}\to{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{K}}_{B\cup V})
we
need to define
\alpha^{*}(p)\in{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
, that is,

\alpha^{*}(p)\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}\to{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V})
. For this, given

x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
, we sample
\bm{y}
at random in

\displaystyle\prod_{C\in r^{\dsct}(B,V)\setminus r^{\dsct}(\alpha(A),V)}(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d})

according to
\bigotimes_{C\in r^{\dsct}(B,V)\setminus r^{\dsct}(\alpha(A),V)}(\mu\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{d}\nu_{C})
, we sample
\bm{M}
at random in

{\mathcal{K}}_{B\cup V}
according to

\displaystyle p((\overline{\alpha}^{-1})^{*}(x),\bm{y})

(conditionally on
\bm{y}
) and we let
\alpha^{*}(p)(x)\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V})
be the distribution of

\displaystyle\alpha\mathord{\uparrow}_{V}^{*}(\bm{M})

(recall that
\alpha\mathord{\uparrow}_{V}\colon A\cup V\to B\cup V
is the extension of
\alpha
that acts identically on

V
, which gives a contra-variantly induced map
\alpha\mathord{\uparrow}_{V}^{*}\colon{\mathcal{K}}_{B\cup V}\to{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V}
).

Note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\alpha\mathord{\uparrow}_{V}^{*}(\bm{M})\mathord{|}_{A}=\alpha^{*}(M^{\dsct}_{B,V}(p))]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[\alpha^{*}(\bm{M}\mathord{|}_{B})=\alpha^{*}(M^{\dsct}_{B,V}(p))]\geq\mathbb{P}[\bm{M}\mathord{|}_{B}=M^{\dsct}_{B,V}(p)]=1,

so indeed
\alpha^{*}(p)
is an element of
{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
and we have

\displaystyle M^{\dsct}_{A,V}(\alpha^{*}(p))

\displaystyle=\alpha^{*}(M^{\dsct}_{B,V}(p)),

(5.4)

that is,
M^{\dsct}
is equivariant.

We define
\alpha^{*}\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\ovlp}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}
analogously by replacing

\dsct
with
\ovlp
throughout.

Lemma 5.5.

The definitions of the maps
\alpha^{*}
of Definition
5.4
are contra-variant,
that is, if
\alpha\colon A\to B
and
\beta\colon B\to C
are injections and
V
is a countable
set disjoint from
A
,
B
and
C
, then
(\beta\mathbin{\circ}\alpha)^{*}=\alpha^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\beta^{*}
.

Proof.

We prove only the overlapping case as the dissociated case has an analogous proof.

Let
p\in{\mathcal{S}}_{C,V}^{(d),\ovlp}
and
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}
and let
\bm{y}
and
\bm{z}
be
picked at random in

\displaystyle\prod_{D\in r^{\ovlp}(B,V)\setminus r^{\ovlp}(\alpha(A),V)}(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{D}^{d}),

\displaystyle\prod_{D\in r^{\ovlp}(C,V)\setminus r^{\ovlp}(\beta(B),V)}(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{D}^{d}),

respectively, according to

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{D\in r^{\ovlp}(B,V)\setminus r^{\ovlp}(\alpha(A),V)}\left(\mu\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{d}\nu_{D}\right),

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{D\in r^{\ovlp}(C,V)\setminus r^{\ovlp}(\beta(B),V)}\left(\mu\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{d}\nu_{D}\right)

and independently from each other.

By sampling
\bm{M}
in
{\mathcal{K}}_{C\cup V}
according to

\displaystyle p\Bigl((\overline{\beta}^{-1})^{*}\bigl((\overline{\alpha}^{-1})^{*}(x),\bm{y}\bigr),\bm{z}\Bigr)

(conditionally on
(\bm{y},\bm{z})
), we have that the conditional distributions given
\bm{y}
of

\beta\mathord{\uparrow}_{V}^{*}(\bm{M})
and
\beta^{*}(p)((\overline{\alpha}^{-1})^{*}(x),\bm{y})
are the same. In turn, this
means that
\alpha\mathord{\uparrow}_{V}^{*}(\beta\mathord{\uparrow}_{V}^{*}(\bm{M}))\sim\alpha^{*}(\beta^{*}(p))(x)
.

Now let
\bm{w}
be picked at random in

\displaystyle\prod_{D\in r^{\ovlp}(C,V)\setminus r^{\ovlp}((\beta\mathbin{\circ}\alpha)(A),V)}(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{D}^{d})

according to

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{D\in r^{\ovlp}(C,V)\setminus r^{\ovlp}((\beta\mathbin{\circ}\alpha)(A),V)}\left(\mu\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{d}\nu_{D}\right)

and note that

\displaystyle\bm{w}

\displaystyle\sim\bigl((\overline{\beta}^{-1})^{*}(\bm{y}),\bm{z}\bigr),

so if we sample
\bm{N}
in
{\mathcal{K}}_{C\cup V}
according to

\displaystyle p\Bigl((\overline{\beta}^{-1})^{*}\bigl((\overline{\alpha}^{-1})^{*}(x)\bigr),\bm{w}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=p\bigl((\overline{\beta\mathbin{\circ}\alpha}^{-1})^{*}(x),\bm{w}\bigr)

(conditionally on
\bm{w}
), then we have
\bm{N}\sim\bm{M}
.

Thus we get

\displaystyle(\beta\mathbin{\circ}\alpha)^{*}(p)(x)

\displaystyle\sim(\beta\mathbin{\circ}\alpha)\mathord{\uparrow}_{V}^{*}(\bm{N})\sim(\beta\mathbin{\circ}\alpha)\mathord{\uparrow}_{V}^{*}(\bm{M})=\alpha\mathord{\uparrow}_{V}^{*}(\beta\mathord{\uparrow}_{V}^{*}(\bm{M}))\sim\alpha^{*}(\beta^{*}(p))(x),

so we conclude that
(\beta\mathbin{\circ}\alpha)^{*}=\alpha^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\beta^{*}
.
∎

We also define natural maps that move between overlapping types and dissociated types.

Definition 5.6.

Let
\Omega=(X,\mu)
be an atomless standard probability space, let
d\in\mathbb{N}
, let
{\mathcal{L}}
be a
finite relational language, let
A
be a finite set and let
V
be a countable set disjoint from

A
.

We define the map
\pi^{\ovlp}_{A,V}\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
as follows: for

p\in{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}
, that is,
p\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}\to{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V})
and

x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
, we sample
\bm{y}
at random in
\prod_{B\in r^{\ovlp}(A,V)\setminus r^{\dsct}(A,V)}(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{B}^{d})
according to
\bigotimes_{B\in r^{\ovlp}(A,V)\setminus r^{\dsct}(A,V)}(\mu\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{d}\nu_{B})
, we sample
\bm{M}
at random in
{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V}
according to

\displaystyle p(x,\bm{y})

(conditionally on
\bm{y}
) and we let
\pi^{\ovlp}_{A,V}(p)(x)
be the distribution of

\bm{M}
. Lemma
5.7
below shows that
\pi^{\ovlp}_{A,V}(p)
is indeed an element of

{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
by showing that
\mathbb{P}[\bm{M}\mathord{|}_{A}=M^{\ovlp}_{A,V}(p)]=1
.

The next lemma shows that both
\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}
and
\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}
have the expected
equivariances and appropriately commute with the maps
M^{\dsct}
,
M^{\ovlp}
and
\pi^{\ovlp}
.

Lemma 5.7.

Let
{\mathcal{N}}
be a
d
-Euclidean structure in
{\mathcal{L}}
over
\Omega
, let
A
and
B
be finite sets, let
V
be
a countable set disjoint from
A
and
B
and let
\alpha\colon A\to B
be an injection. Then the
following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}_{B}^{(d)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\ovlp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\dsct}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}}_{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{B,V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{B,V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\mathcal{N}}_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\ovlp}_{B,V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\ovlp}_{B,V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\dsct}_{B,V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\mathcal{N}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\ovlp}_{A,V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\ovlp}_{A,V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\dsct}_{A,V}}

Proof.

Let us first prove the commutative properties involving only one of the rows, which we can
assume without loss of generality to be the second row.

The equalities

\displaystyle M^{\dsct}_{A,V}\mathbin{\circ}\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}

\displaystyle=M^{\mathcal{N}}_{A},

\displaystyle M^{\ovlp}_{A,V}\mathbin{\circ}\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}

\displaystyle=M^{\mathcal{N}}_{A}

follow straightforwardly from definitions.

We now prove that

\displaystyle\pi^{\ovlp}_{A,V}\mathbin{\circ}\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}.

(5.5)

Let
z\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}
, let
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
, sample
\bm{y}
at random in
\prod_{C\in r^{\ovlp}(A,V)\setminus r^{\dsct}(A,V)}(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d})
according to
\bigotimes_{C\in r^{\ovlp}(A,V)\setminus r^{\dsct}(A,V)}(\mu\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{d}\nu_{C})
and sample
\bm{M}

at random in
{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V}
according to

\displaystyle\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(z)(x,\bm{y})

(conditionally on
\bm{y}
) so that
\pi^{\ovlp}_{A,V}(\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(z))(x)
is the distribution
of
\bm{M}
.

By the definition of
\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}
, if we sample
\bm{w}
in

\overline{{\mathcal{E}}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}
according to
\mu^{(d)}
, then
\bm{M}
has the same
distribution as
M^{\mathcal{N}}_{A\cup V}(z,x,\bm{y},\bm{w})
. Since the distribution of
(\bm{y},\bm{w})

as an element of
\overline{{\mathcal{E}}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
is exactly
\mu^{(d)}
, by the definition of

\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}
, it follows that
\bm{M}
has the same distribution as

\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(z)(x)
, so we conclude that

\pi^{\ovlp}_{A,V}(\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(z))(x)=\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(z)(x)
, from which (
5.5
)
follows.

To conclude the second row, it remains to show that

\displaystyle M^{\dsct}_{A,V}\mathbin{\circ}\pi^{\ovlp}_{A,V}

\displaystyle=M^{\ovlp}_{A,V}.

(5.6)

Let
p\in{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}
, let
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
, sample
\bm{y}
at random in

\prod_{C\in r^{\ovlp}(A,V)\setminus r^{\dsct}(A,V)}(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d})
according to

\bigotimes_{C\in r^{\ovlp}(A,V)\setminus r^{\dsct}(A,V)}(\mu\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{d}\nu_{C})
and
sample
\bm{M}
at random in
{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V}
according to

\displaystyle p(x,\bm{y})

(conditionally on
\bm{y}
) so that
\pi^{\ovlp}_{A,V}(p)(x)
is the distribution of
\bm{M}
.

Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\bm{M}\mathord{|}_{A}=M^{\ovlp}_{A,V}(p)]

\displaystyle=1,

so we have
M^{\dsct}_{A,V}(\pi^{\ovlp}_{A,V}(p))=M^{\ovlp}_{A,V}(p)
, from which (
5.6
) follows.

It remains to show that all squares involving exactly two elements of each row commute, that is,
we need to show equivariance of
\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}
,
\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}
,
M^{\mathcal{N}}
,
\pi^{\ovlp}_{A,V}
,

M^{\ovlp}
and
M^{\dsct}
.

The fact that
\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}
and
\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}
are equivariant follows by applying
Lemma
5.3
with
\alpha\mathord{\uparrow}_{V}
.

Equivariance of
M^{\mathcal{N}}
is straightforward.

Equivariance of
M^{\dsct}
was observed in (
5.4
) and equivariance of

M^{\ovlp}
is analogous.

It remains to show equivariance of
\pi^{\ovlp}
.

Let
p\in{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\ovlp}
, let
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
and let us describe the
distributions of
(\pi^{\ovlp}_{A,V}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha^{*})(p)(x)
and

(\alpha^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\pi^{\ovlp}_{B,V})(p)(x)
. Throughout this, all measures will be product measures of
copies of
\mu
with appropriate copies of
\nu_{C}
, which can be deduced from the underlying space
of the random variables, so we will omit the measures for simplicity.

First, we sample
\bm{y}
and
\bm{z}
independently in

\displaystyle\prod_{C\in r^{\ovlp}(A,V)\setminus r^{\dsct}(A,V)}(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d}),

\displaystyle\prod_{C\in r^{\ovlp}(B,V)\setminus r^{\ovlp}(\alpha(A),V)}(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d}),

respectively and we sample
\bm{M}
according to

\displaystyle p((\overline{\alpha}^{-1})^{*}(x,\bm{y}),\bm{z})

(conditionally on
\bm{y}
and
\bm{z}
) so that the conditional distribution of

\alpha\mathord{\uparrow}_{V}^{*}(\bm{M})
given
\bm{y}
is
\alpha^{*}(p)(x,\bm{y})
, which in turn means that
the unconditional distribution of
\alpha\mathord{\uparrow}_{V}^{*}(\bm{M})
is
(\pi^{\ovlp}_{A,V}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha^{*})(p)(x)
.

For the other distribution, we sample
\bm{u}
and
\bm{v}
independently in

\displaystyle\prod_{C\in r^{\dsct}(B,V)\setminus r^{\dsct}(\alpha(A),V)}(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d}),

\displaystyle\prod_{C\in r^{\ovlp}(B,V)\setminus r^{\dsct}(B,V)}(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d}),

respectively and we sample
\bm{N}
according to

\displaystyle p((\overline{\alpha}^{-1})^{*}(x),\bm{u},\bm{v})

(conditionally on
\bm{u}
and
\bm{v}
) so that the conditional distribution of
\bm{N}
given

\bm{u}
is
\pi^{\ovlp}_{B,V}(p)((\overline{\alpha}^{-1})^{*}(x),\bm{u})
, which in turn means that the
unconditional distribution of
\alpha\mathord{\uparrow}_{V}^{*}(\bm{N})
is
(\alpha^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\pi^{\ovlp}_{B,V})(p)(x)
.

Now we note that since all distributions are products of copies of
\mu
with appropriate copies
of
\nu_{C}
and we have appropriate independences between random elements, it follows that

\displaystyle((\overline{\alpha}^{-1})^{*}(\bm{y}),\bm{z})

\displaystyle\sim(\bm{u},\bm{v})

as random elements of

\displaystyle\prod_{C\in r^{\ovlp}(B,V)\setminus r^{\dsct}(\alpha(A),V)}(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d}),

(5.7)

so both distributions of
\bm{M}
and
\bm{N}
are equal to the distribution of
\bm{K}
defined
as follows: we sample
\bm{w}
in the space of (
5.7
) above and sample
\bm{K}

according to

\displaystyle p((\overline{\alpha}^{-1})^{*}(x),\bm{w})

(conditionally on
\bm{w}
). Clearly
\bm{M}\sim\bm{K}\sim\bm{N}
, from which we get

\alpha\mathord{\uparrow}_{V}^{*}(\bm{M})\sim\alpha\mathord{\uparrow}_{V}^{*}(\bm{K})\sim\alpha\mathord{\uparrow}_{V}^{*}(\bm{N})
, hence

(\pi^{\ovlp}_{A,V}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha^{*})(p)(x)=(\alpha^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\pi^{\ovlp}_{B,V})(p)(x)
, concluding the
proof.
∎

We conclude this section by showing uniqueness of types of size
\ell
for
(\ell-1)
-independent

d
-Euclidean structures satisfying
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
.

Proposition 5.8.

If
{\mathcal{N}}
is a
d
-Euclidean structure in
{\mathcal{L}}
that is
(\ell-1)
-independent and satisfies

\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
,
A
is a set of size at most
\ell
and
V
is a countable set disjoint from

A
, then for almost every
(x,x^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}\times{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}
, we have

\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x)=\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x^{\prime})
.

Before we start the proof, let us point out that the analogous result for
\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}
can be
derived from the above and the fact that

\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}=\pi^{\ovlp}_{A,V}\mathbin{\circ}\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}
from Lemma
5.7
.

Proof.

By equivariance of
\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}
(Lemma
5.7
), it suffices to show only the case
when
A=[\ell]
. Furthermore, by using measure-isomorphisms modulo
0
, it suffices to show the
case when
\Omega
is the interval
[0,1]
and
\mu
is the Lebesgue measure
\lambda
.

In turn, it suffices to show that for every finite
U\subseteq[\ell]\cup V
, every
K\in{\mathcal{K}}_{U}
,
and for the function
g\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{\ell}^{(d)}(\Omega)\times{\mathcal{E}}_{\ell,V}^{(d),\ovlp}(\Omega)\to[0,1]

given by

\displaystyle g(x,y)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{\ell,V}(x)(y)(\{M\in{\mathcal{K}}_{[\ell]\cup V}\mid M\mathord{|}_{U}=K\})\qquad(x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\ell}^{(d)}(\Omega),y\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\ell,V}^{(d),\ovlp}(\Omega)),

the function
x\mapsto g(x,y)
is a.e. constant for a.e.
y\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\ell,V}^{(d),\ovlp}(\Omega)
.

Without loss of generality, we may suppose that
[\ell]\subseteq U
. Furthermore, since the
definition of
\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{\ell,V}
implies that
g
only depends on the coordinates of
y
that
are indexed by sets in
r(U)
, we may assume without loss of generality that
V=U\setminus[\ell]
.

Recalling that overlapping types are factored up to almost everywhere equivalence, by Fubini’s
Theorem and Lebesgue’s Differentiation Theorem, it suffices to show that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda^{(d)}(B^{\prime}_{\ell}\times B_{\ell})}\cdot\int_{B^{\prime}_{\ell}\times B_{\ell}}\int_{B^{\prime}_{V}\times B_{V}}g(x,y)\ d\lambda^{(d)}(y)\ d\lambda^{(d)}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathcal{E}}_{\ell}^{(d)}(\Omega)}\int_{B^{\prime}_{V}\times B_{V}}g(x,y)\ d\lambda^{(d)}(y)\ d\lambda^{(d)}(x)

for all positive measure sets

\displaystyle B^{\prime}_{\ell}

\displaystyle\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}_{\ell,\ell-1}^{(d)}(\Omega),

\displaystyle B_{\ell}

\displaystyle\subseteq[0,1]\times{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell}^{d},

\displaystyle B^{\prime}_{V}

\displaystyle\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}_{\ell,V,\ell-1}^{(d),\ovlp}(\Omega)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\in r^{\ovlp}(\ell,V)\\
\lvert C\rvert\leq\ell-1\end{subarray}}(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d}),

\displaystyle B_{V}

\displaystyle\subseteq\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\in r^{\ovlp}(\ell,V)\\
\lvert C\rvert\geq\ell\end{subarray}}(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d}).

Since
{\mathcal{N}}
is
(\ell-1)
-independent, it follows that
g
does not depend on the coordinates
indexed by sets of size at most
\ell-1
, so it suffices to show only the case when

B^{\prime}_{\ell}={\mathcal{E}}_{\ell,\ell-1}^{(d)}(\Omega)
and
B^{\prime}_{V}={\mathcal{E}}_{\ell,V,\ell-1}^{(d)}(\Omega)
:

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\frac{1}{\lambda^{(d)}(B_{\ell})}\cdot\int_{{\mathcal{E}}_{\ell,\ell-1}^{(d)}(\Omega)\times B_{\ell}}\int_{{\mathcal{E}}_{\ell,V,\ell-1}^{(d),\ovlp}(\Omega)\times B_{V}}g(x,y)\ d\lambda^{(d)}(y)\ d\lambda^{(d)}(x)
