\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathcal{E}}_{\ell}^{(d)}(\Omega)}\int_{{\mathcal{E}}_{\ell,V,\ell-1}^{(d),\ovlp}(\Omega)\times B_{V}}g(x,y)\ d\lambda^{(d)}(y)\ d\lambda^{(d)}(x),

which in turn is equivalent to

\displaystyle\lambda^{(d)}(\{w\in T_{\operatorname{ind}}(K,{\mathcal{N}})\mid\pi_{\ell}(w)\in B_{\ell}\land\pi_{V}(w)\in B_{V}\})

(5.8)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{(d)}(\{w\in T_{\operatorname{ind}}(K,{\mathcal{N}})\mid\pi_{V}(w)\in B_{V}\})\cdot\lambda^{(d)}(B_{\ell}),

where
\pi_{\ell}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{U}^{(d)}(\Omega)\to[0,1]\times{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell}^{d}
is the projection onto the coordinate
indexed by
[\ell]
and
\pi_{V}
is the projection onto the coordinates indexed by sets
C
in

r^{\ovlp}(\ell,V)
with
\lvert C\rvert\geq\ell
.

Let
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
be obtained from
{\mathcal{L}}
by adding a predicate symbol
Q
of arity
\ell
and let
{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}

be the
d
-Euclidean structure in
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
extending
{\mathcal{N}}
by letting

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}_{Q}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}{\mathcal{E}}_{\ell,\ell-1}^{(d)}(\Omega)\times B_{\ell}.

Clearly
\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}}
is a coupling of
\phi_{\mathcal{N}}
with the
(\ell-1)
-independent
d
-peon

{\mathcal{H}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}_{Q}
of arity
\ell
, so
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
gives
\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}}=\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}}
.

By Theorem
4.2
, there exists a family
h=(h_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
of symmetric
measure-preserving on h.o.a. functions
h_{i}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{(2d)}(\Omega^{4})\to[0,1]\times{\mathcal{O}}_{i}^{d}

such that
h^{*}({\mathcal{N}}^{\prime})
is a.e. equal to the
2d
-
T
-on
{\mathcal{G}}
over
\Omega^{4}
given by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}_{P}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{(x,y,z)\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k(P)}^{(d)}(\Omega)\times{\mathcal{E}}_{k(P)}^{(d)}(\Omega)\times{\mathcal{E}}_{k(P)}(\Omega^{2})\mid(x,y)\in({\mathcal{N}}\otimes{\mathcal{H}})_{P}\}\qquad(P\in{\mathcal{L}}).

From the product structure of
{\mathcal{N}}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}
and the definition of
{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle x\in{\mathcal{N}}_{P}

\displaystyle\iff\widehat{h}_{k(P)}(x,y,z)\in{\mathcal{N}}_{P}

(5.9)

for every
P\in{\mathcal{L}}
and almost every

(x,y,z)\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k(P)}^{(d)}(\Omega)\times{\mathcal{E}}_{k(P)}^{(d)}(\Omega)\times{\mathcal{E}}_{k(P)}(\Omega^{2})
and

\displaystyle y\in{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\iff\widehat{h}_{\ell}(x,y,z)\in{\mathcal{H}}

(5.10)

for almost every
(x,y,z)\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\ell}^{(d)}(\Omega)\times{\mathcal{E}}_{\ell}^{(d)}(\Omega)\times{\mathcal{E}}_{\ell}(\Omega^{2})
.

To simplify notation, in all expressions from now on, we will take
x
and
y
in

{\mathcal{E}}_{U}^{(d)}(\Omega)
and take
z
in
{\mathcal{E}}_{U}(\Omega^{2})
.

By Lemma
3.2
(i)
,
\widehat{h}_{U}
is measure-preserving, so we
get

\displaystyle\lambda^{(d)}\Bigl(\bigl\{w\in T_{\operatorname{ind}}(K,{\mathcal{N}})\mid\pi_{\ell}(w)\in B_{\ell}\land\pi_{V}(w)\in B_{V}\bigr\}\Bigr)

(5.11)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{(2d)}\biggl(\Bigl\{(x,y,z)\;\Big|\;\widehat{h}_{U}(x,y,z)\in T_{\operatorname{ind}}(K,{\mathcal{N}})\land\pi_{\ell}\bigl(\widehat{h}_{U}(x,y,z)\bigr)\in B_{\ell}\land\pi_{V}\bigl(\widehat{h}_{U}(x,y,z)\bigr)\in B_{V}\Bigr\}\biggr)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{(2d)}\biggl(\Bigl\{(x,y,z)\;\Big|\;\widehat{h}_{U}(x,y,z)\in T_{\operatorname{ind}}(K,{\mathcal{N}})\land\iota_{[\ell],U}^{*}\bigl(\widehat{h}_{U}(x,y,z)\bigr)\in{\mathcal{H}}\land\pi_{V}\bigl(\widehat{h}_{U}(x,y,z)\bigr)\in B_{V}\Bigr\}\biggr)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{(2d)}\biggl(\Bigl\{(x,y,z)\;\Big|\;x\in T_{\operatorname{ind}}(K,{\mathcal{N}})\land\iota_{[\ell],U}^{*}(y)\in{\mathcal{H}}\land\pi_{V}\bigl(\widehat{h}_{U}(x,y,z)\bigr)\in B_{V}\Bigr\}\biggr),

where the last equality follows from (
5.9
)
and (
5.10
) (recall that
\iota_{[\ell],U}\colon[\ell]\to U
is the
inclusion map).

Note now that since
{\mathcal{H}}
is
(\ell-1)
-independent, the condition
\iota_{[\ell],U}^{*}(y)\in{\mathcal{H}}
depends
only on the coordinate of
y
indexed by
[\ell]
. On the other hand, the definition of
\pi_{V}

implies that the condition
\pi_{V}(\widehat{h}_{U}(x,y,z))\in B_{V}
depends only on coordinates of

\widehat{h}_{U}(x,y,z)
indexed by sets in
r^{\ovlp}(\ell,V)
, which by the definition of

\widehat{h}_{U}
means that the condition depends only on coordinates of
(x,y,z)
indexed by sets
in
r^{\ovlp}(\ell,V)\cup r(\ell,\ell-1)
, which does not contain the set
[\ell]
. Finally, since
the condition
x\in T_{\operatorname{ind}}(K,{\mathcal{N}})
clearly does not depend on
y
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\lambda^{(2d)}(\{(x,y,z)\mid x\in T_{\operatorname{ind}}(K,{\mathcal{N}})\land\iota_{[\ell],U}^{*}(y)\in B_{\ell}\land\pi_{V}(\widehat{h}_{U}(x,y,z))\in B_{V}\}),

\displaystyle=\lambda^{(2d)}(\{(x,y,z)\mid x\in T_{\operatorname{ind}}(K,{\mathcal{N}})\land\pi_{V}(\widehat{h}_{U}(x,y,z))\in B_{V}\})\cdot\lambda^{(2d)}(\{(x,y,z)\mid\iota_{[\ell],U}^{*}(y)\in B_{\ell}\}).

Plugging this in (
5.11
) and using the fact that
\widehat{h}_{U}

is measure-preserving again, we get (
5.8
), concluding the proof.
∎

6
Topologies of Markov kernels and of types

Recall that our plan is to provide a way of sampling models from a
d
-Euclidean structure by
instead sampling types. This means that we need to make sense of the notion of a “random type”,
which in turn requires putting a
\sigma
-algebra on the spaces
{\mathcal{S}}^{(d),\ovlp}
and

{\mathcal{S}}^{(d),\dsct}
. Furthermore, to finally produce an equivalent
d
-Euclidean structure that
corresponds to sampling types, we will need the resulting measurable space to be a standard Borel
space. With this in mind, the objective of this section is to construct Polish topologies on the
spaces of dissociated and overlapping types that have enough measurable sets to measure all that we
will need for the notion of “random types”.

Recall that a Markov kernel from a measurable space
X
to a measurable space
Y
is a map

\rho\colon X\to{\mathcal{P}}(Y)
such that for every measurable
B\subseteq Y
, the map
X\ni x\mapsto\rho(x)(B)\in[0,1]
is measurable. Note that both dissociated and overlapping types are
Markov kernels, so it will suffice to produce a Polish topology on the space of Markov kernels.

Given a Markov kernel
\rho\colon X\to{\mathcal{P}}(Y)
and a probability measure
\mu
on
X
,
Carathéodory’s Extension Theorem implies that there exists a unique probability measure
\rho[\mu]

on
X\times Y
such that

\displaystyle\rho[\mu](A\times B)

\displaystyle=\int_{A}\rho(x)(B)\ d\mu(x)

(6.1)

for all measurable sets
A\subseteq X
and
B\subseteq Y
. In particular, if

(\bm{x},\bm{y})\sim\rho[\mu]
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\bm{y}\in B\mathbin{|}\bm{x}\in A]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mu(A)}\cdot\int_{A}\rho(x)(B)\ d\mu(x)

and more generally, if
h\colon Y\to\mathbb{R}
is bounded and measurable, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[h(\bm{y})\mathbin{|}\bm{x}]

\displaystyle=\int_{Y}h(\bm{y})\ d\rho(\bm{x})(y)

with probability
1
(over
\bm{x}
).

Informally,
\rho(x)
can be interpreted as the conditional probability of
\bm{y}
given the
potentially zero measure event
\bm{x}=x
. This informal intuition is made formal by the
Disintegration Theorem below (for a proof, see e.g.
[
Bog07
, Theorem 10.6.6 and Corollary 10.6.7]
).

Theorem 6.1
(Disintegration Theorem)
.

Let
X
and
Y
be Borel spaces, let
\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}(Y)
, let
\pi\colon Y\to X
be a measurable
function and let
\mu\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\pi_{*}(\nu)\in{\mathcal{P}}(X)
be the pushforward measure (i.e.,

\pi_{*}(\nu)(A)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\nu(\pi^{-1}(A))
). Then there exists a Markov kernel
\rho\colon X\to{\mathcal{P}}(Y)

such that the set

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{x\in X\mid\rho(x)(\pi^{-1}(x))=1\}

has
\mu
-measure
1
and for every measurable function
h\colon Y\to[0,\infty]
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{Y}h(y)\ d\nu(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{R}\int_{\pi^{-1}(x)}h(y)\ d\rho(x)(y)\ d\mu(x).

In particular, the marginal of
\rho[\mu]
on
Y
is
\nu
, that is, we have

\displaystyle\nu(B)

\displaystyle=\rho[\mu](X\times B)=\int_{X}\rho(x)(B)\ d\mu(x)

for every measurable
B\subseteq Y
.

When
Y
is a product space and
\pi
is a projection, the Disintegration Theorem takes the
following form:

Theorem 6.2
(Disintegration Theorem, projection version)
.

Let
X
and
Y
be Borel spaces, let
\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}(X\times Y)
, let
\pi\colon X\times Y\to X
be the
projection function and
\mu\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\pi_{*}(\nu)
be the marginal of
\nu
on
X
. Then there exists a
Markov kernel
\rho\colon X\to{\mathcal{P}}(Y)
such that for every measurable function
h\colon X\times Y\to[0,\infty]
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{X\times Y}h(x,y)\ d\nu(x,y)

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\int_{Y}h(x,y)\ d\rho(x)(y)\ d\mu(x).

In particular,
\rho[\mu]=\nu
.

Proof.

Let
\rho^{\prime}\colon X\to{\mathcal{P}}(X\times Y)
be provided by Theorem
6.1
. Define
\rho\colon X\to{\mathcal{P}}(Y)
by

\displaystyle\rho(x)(B)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\rho^{\prime}(x)(\{x\}\times B)

whenever
x\in R
and define
\rho(x)
to be an arbitrary fixed probability measure on
Y
when

x\notin R
. The definition of
R
ensures that
\rho
is indeed a Markov kernel.

Note now that if
h\colon X\times Y\to[0,\infty]
is a measurable function, then

\displaystyle\int_{X\times Y}h(x,y)\ d\nu(x,y)

\displaystyle=\int_{R}\int_{\pi^{-1}(x)}h(w,y)\ d\rho^{\prime}(x)(w,y)\ d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{R}\int_{\pi^{-1}(x)}h(x,y)\ d\rho(x)(y)\ d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\int_{Y}h(x,y)\ d\rho(x)(y)\ d\mu(x),

where the second equality follows since when
x\in R
, then every
(w,y)\in\pi^{-1}(x)
satisfies

x=w
and
\rho(x)(B)=\rho^{\prime}(x)(\{x\}\times B)
for every
B\subseteq Y
measurable and the third
equality follows since when
x\in R
, the set
\pi^{-1}(x)
has
\rho(x)
-measure
1
and the set

R
itself has
\mu
-measure
1
.

Finally, note that for all measurable
A\subseteq X
and
B\subseteq Y
, we have

\displaystyle\rho[\mu](A\times B)

\displaystyle=\int_{A}\rho(x)(B)\ d\mu(x)=\int_{X}\mathbbm{1}[x\in A]\int_{Y}\mathbbm{1}[y\in B]\ d\rho(x)(y)\ d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\int_{Y}\mathbbm{1}[(x,y)\in A\times B]\ d\rho(x)(y)\ d\mu(x)=\int_{X\times Y}\mathbbm{1}[(x,y)\in A\times B]\ d\nu(x,y)

\displaystyle=\nu(A\times B),

so
\rho[\mu]=\nu
.
∎

Definition 6.3.

Let
\Omega=(X,\mu)
be a standard probability space and let
Y
be a Borel space. We denote by

M(\Omega,Y)
the space of all Markov kernels from
\Omega
to
Y
up to almost everywhere
equivalence.

For measurable sets
A\subseteq X
and
B\subseteq Y
and a real
r\in\mathbb{R}
, we let

\displaystyle U(A,B,r)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{\rho\in M(\Omega,Y)\mid I_{A,B}(\rho)<r\},

\displaystyle L(A,B,r)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{\rho\in M(\Omega,Y)\mid I_{A,B}(\rho)>r\},

where

\displaystyle I_{A,B}(\rho)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\int_{A}\rho(x)(B)\ d\mu(x).

Let
{\mathcal{B}}
be a collection of measurable sets of
Y
. We let
\tau(\Omega,Y,{\mathcal{B}})
be the topology
on
M(\Omega,Y)
generated by all sets of the form
U(A,B,r)
or
L(A,B,r)
for some measurable

A\subseteq X
, some real
r\in\mathbb{R}
and some
B\in{\mathcal{B}}
.

The next proposition proves basic properties about the topology on the space of Markov kernels.

Proposition 6.4.

Let
\Omega=(X,\mu)
be a standard probability space, let
Y
be a Polish space and let
{\mathcal{B}}_{X}

and
{\mathcal{B}}_{Y}
be countable Boolean algebras of measurable sets of
X
and
Y
, respectively that
generate their
\sigma
-algebras. Let further
{\mathcal{C}}
be the collection of all sets of the form

U(A,B,r)
or
L(A,B,r)
for some
A\in{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
, some
B\in{\mathcal{B}}_{Y}
and some rational
r\in\mathbb{Q}
. Then
the following hold.

i.

{\mathcal{C}}
generates the topology
\tau(\Omega,Y,{\mathcal{B}}_{Y})
.

ii.

\tau(\Omega,Y,{\mathcal{B}}_{Y})
is separable.

iii.

\tau(\Omega,Y,{\mathcal{B}}_{Y})
is metrizable.

iv.

If
Y
is compact, all sets in
{\mathcal{B}}_{Y}
are clopen and
X
is either
finite or uncountable, then
(M(\Omega,Y),\tau(\Omega,Y,{\mathcal{B}}_{Y}))
is a Polish space.

Proof.

To prove item
(i)
, it suffices to show that for all measurable sets
A\subseteq X

and
B\subseteq Y
and all real numbers
r\in\mathbb{R}
, the following hold:

•

If
\rho\in U(A,B,r)
, then there exists
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
with
\rho\in C\subseteq U(A,B,r)
.

•

If
\rho\in L(A,B,r)
, then there exists
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
with
\rho\in C\subseteq L(A,B,r)
.

We prove only the first item as the second has an analogous proof.

Since
\rho\in U(A,B,r)
, we can let
r^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}
be such that
I_{A,B}(\rho)<r^{\prime}<r
and let

\varepsilon\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\min\{r^{\prime}-I_{A,B}(\rho),r-r^{\prime}\}>0
.

Since
{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
is a Boolean algebra that generates the
\sigma
-algebra of
X
, there exists

A^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
such that
\mu(A\mathbin{\triangle}A^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon/2
. We claim that
C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}U(A^{\prime},B,r^{\prime})

satisfies the required property
\rho\in C\subseteq U(A,B,r)
. First note that since

\mu(A\mathbin{\triangle}A^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon/2
and
0\leq\rho(x)(B)\leq 1
for every
x\in X
, we get

\displaystyle I_{A^{\prime},B}(\rho)

\displaystyle\leq I_{A,B}(\rho)+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}<r^{\prime},

hence
\rho\in C
.

On the other hand, if
\rho^{\prime}\in C
, then

\displaystyle I_{A,B}(\rho^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq I_{A^{\prime},B}(\rho^{\prime})+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}<r^{\prime}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}<r,

so
\rho^{\prime}\in U(A,B,r)
, hence
C\subseteq U(A,B,r)
as desired.

Therefore,
{\mathcal{C}}
generates the topology
\tau(\Omega,Y,{\mathcal{B}}_{Y})
.

For item
(ii)
, since
{\mathcal{C}}
is clearly countable, it follows that

\tau(\Omega,Y,{\mathcal{B}}_{Y})
is second-countable, which in particular implies it is separable.

We now prove item
(iii)
by constructing an explicit metric that generates the
topology
\tau(\Omega,Y,{\mathcal{B}}_{Y})
. For this, let
(A_{n},B_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be an enumeration of

{\mathcal{B}}_{X}\times{\mathcal{B}}_{Y}
and for
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\in M(\Omega,Y)
, we let

\displaystyle\Delta(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{\lvert I_{A_{n},B_{n}}(\rho_{1})-I_{A_{n},B_{n}}(\rho_{2})\rvert}{2^{n}}.

(Note that the sum above is convergent as
0\leq I_{A_{n},B_{n}}(\rho_{i})\leq\mu(A_{n})\leq 1
.)

Obviously
\Delta
is a non-negative symmetric function and it is straightforward to check that

\Delta
satisfies triangle inequality.

Let us now show that if
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\in M(\Omega,Y)
are such that
\Delta(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})=0
,
then we must have
\rho_{1}=\rho_{2}
a.e. Since
{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
is a Boolean algebra that generates the

\sigma
-algebra of
X
, the set

\displaystyle\{x\in X\mid\forall B\in{\mathcal{B}}_{Y},\rho_{1}(x)(B)=\rho_{2}(x)(B)\}

must have
\mu
-measure
1
by the uniqueness of Carathéodory’s Extension Theorem. In turn, by
the same theorem but applied to
{\mathcal{B}}_{Y}
, the set above is equal to

\displaystyle\{x\in X\mid\rho_{1}(x)=\rho_{2}(x)\},

hence
\rho_{1}=\rho_{2}
almost everywhere.

Therefore
\Delta
is a metric on
M(\Omega,Y)
.

Let us now show that
\Delta
generates the topology
\tau(\Omega,Y,{\mathcal{B}}_{Y})
. To show this, it
suffices to show that the following hold:

a.

For every
C\in\tau(\Omega,Y,{\mathcal{B}}_{Y})
and every
\rho\in C
,
there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
B_{\varepsilon}(\rho)\subseteq C
, where
B_{\varepsilon}(C)
is
the ball of radius
\varepsilon
centered at
\rho
(with respect to
\Delta
).

b.

For every
\rho\in M(\Omega,Y)
and every
\varepsilon>0
,
there exists
C\in\tau(\Omega,Y,{\mathcal{B}}_{Y})
such that
\rho\in C\subseteq B_{\varepsilon}(\rho)
.

We start with item
(a)
. Since
{\mathcal{C}}
generates the topology

\tau(\Omega,Y,{\mathcal{B}}_{Y})
, it suffices to show only the case when
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, that is, it suffices to
show only the case when
C
is of the form
U(A,B,r)
or
L(A,B,r)
for some
A\in{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
, some

B\in{\mathcal{B}}_{Y}
and some rational
r\in\mathbb{Q}
. We show only the case
C=U(A,B,r)
as the other case
has an analogous proof.

Since
(A,B)\in{\mathcal{B}}_{X}\times{\mathcal{B}}_{Y}
, we know that
(A,B)=(A_{n},B_{n})
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
and since

\rho\in U(A,B,r)
, we have
I_{A,B}(\rho)<r
. Let then
\varepsilon\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}2^{-n}(r-I_{A,B}(\rho))>0
and note that if
\rho^{\prime}\in B_{\varepsilon}(\rho)
, then

\displaystyle I_{A,B}(\rho^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq I_{A,B}(\rho)+2^{n}\cdot\Delta(\rho,\rho^{\prime})<r,

so
\rho^{\prime}\in U(A,B,r)
, hence
B_{\varepsilon}(\rho)\subseteq U(A,B,r)
as desired.

Let us now show item
(b)
. Let
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
be large enough so that

\sum_{n>n_{0}}2^{-n}<\varepsilon/2
and consider the set

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\bigcap_{n=0}^{n_{0}}U\left(A_{n},B_{n},I_{A_{n},B_{n}}(\rho)+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\right)\cap\bigcap_{n=0}^{n_{0}}L\left(A_{n},B_{n},I_{A_{n},B_{n}}(\rho)-\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\right).

Clearly
C\in\tau(\Omega,Y,{\mathcal{B}}_{Y})
and
\rho\in C
.

On the other hand, if
\rho^{\prime}\in C
, then

\displaystyle\Delta(\rho,\rho^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{\lvert I_{A_{n},B_{n}}(\rho)-I_{A_{n},B_{n}}(\rho^{\prime})\rvert}{2^{n}}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\cdot\sum_{n=0}^{n_{0}}2^{-n}+\sum_{n>n_{0}}2^{-n}<\varepsilon,

hence
\rho^{\prime}\in B_{\varepsilon}(\rho)
, thus
C\subseteq B_{\varepsilon}(\rho)
as desired.

Therefore
\Delta
generates the topology
\tau(\Omega,Y,{\mathcal{B}}_{Y})
.

It remains to show item
(iv)
. Since
X
is either finite or uncountable and is a
Borel space, we may put a Polish topology on
X
that both generates the
\sigma
-algebra of

\Omega
and is
compact
. Furthermore, we may assume that
{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
is a countable Boolean
algebra of
clopen
sets of
X
that generates the
\sigma
-algebra of
\Omega
(in the
finite case, any choice works as all subsets are clopen; in the uncountable case, this can be done
as
\Omega
is Borel-isomorphic to the Cantor space). Note that even though this changes the
metric
\Delta
, it does not change the topology
\tau(\Omega,Y,{\mathcal{B}}_{Y})
as it does not depend on

{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
nor on the topology on
X
(and the new metric
\Delta
still generates

\tau(\Omega,Y,{\mathcal{B}}_{Y})
by our proof of item
(iii)
). With this choice, it now suffices
to show that
\Delta
is complete.

Let
(\rho_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a Cauchy sequence with respect to
\Delta
. It is straightforward to
check that this implies that for every
A\in{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
and every
B\in{\mathcal{B}}_{Y}
, the limit

\lim_{n\to\infty}I_{A,B}(\rho_{n})
exists. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\theta_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\rho_{n}[\mu]
so
that for every
A\in{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
and every
B\in{\mathcal{B}}_{Y}
, we have

\displaystyle\theta_{n}(A\times B)

\displaystyle=\int_{A}\rho_{n}(x)(B)\ d\mu(x).

Let
{\mathcal{D}}
be the (countable) Boolean algebra generated by sets of the form
A\times B
for

A\in{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
and
B\in{\mathcal{B}}_{Y}
. Clearly,
{\mathcal{D}}
generates the product
\sigma
-algebra on
X\times Y
. Furthermore, the product topology on
X\times Y
is compact and all elements of
{\mathcal{D}}
are
clopen.

Since every set
C
in
{\mathcal{D}}
can be written as a finite disjoint union
\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}A_{i}\times B_{i}
with
A\in{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
and
B\in{\mathcal{B}}_{Y}
, we can define

\displaystyle\theta(C)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lim_{n\to\infty}I_{A_{i},B_{i}}(\rho_{n})

(it is straightforward to check that this does not depend on the particular choice of sets

(A_{i},B_{i})_{i=1}^{m}
).

We claim that
\theta
is a pre-measure on
{\mathcal{D}}
. It is clear that
\theta
is finitely additive and
assigns measure
0
to the empty set. To see that
\theta
is also
\sigma
-additive, note that since
elements of
{\mathcal{C}}
are both compact and open, any countably infinite disjoint union of elements of

{\mathcal{C}}
that is in
{\mathcal{C}}
must necessarily contain only finitely many non-empty sets, so

\sigma
-additivity follows from finite additivity.

By Carathéodory’s Extension Theorem, we can uniquely extend
\theta
to a measure on
X\times Y

(which we denote by
\theta
as well). Note that for every
A\in{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
, we have

\displaystyle\theta(A\times Y)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\theta_{n}(A\times Y)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{A}\rho_{n}(x)(Y)\ d\mu(x)=\mu(A),

which in particular implies that the marginal of
\theta
on
X
is
\mu
and that
\theta
is a
probability measure on
X\times Y
.

By the Disintegration Theorem, Theorem
6.2
, there exists a Markov kernel
\rho\in M(\Omega,Y)
such that

\displaystyle\theta(A\times B)

\displaystyle=\int_{A}\rho(x)(B)\ d\mu(x)

for every
A\in{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
and every
B\in{\mathcal{B}}_{Y}
, which in particular implies that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\Delta(\rho_{n},\rho)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{\lvert I_{A_{m},B_{m}}(\rho_{n})-I_{A_{m},B_{m}}(\rho)\rvert}{2^{m}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{\lvert\theta_{n}(A_{m}\times B_{m})-\theta(A_{m}\times B_{m})\rvert}{2^{m}}=0,

where the last equality follows since the
m
th term in the sum is bounded by
2^{-m}
and

\lim_{n\to\infty}\theta_{n}(A\times B)=\theta(A\times B)
for every
A\in{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
and every

B\in{\mathcal{B}}_{Y}
. Thus,
\Delta
is complete as desired.
∎

We are now ready to describe the topology and
\sigma
-algebra of the spaces of dissociated and
overlapping types, which in turn allows us to make sense of the notion of “random type”.

Definition 6.5.

Let
\Omega=(X,\mu)
be an atomless standard probability space, let
d\in\mathbb{N}
, let
{\mathcal{L}}
be a
finite relational language, let
V
be a countable set, let
A
be a finite set disjoint from
V
.

Consider the space
M({\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct},{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V})
of Markov kernels from

{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
to
{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V}
equipped with the topology

\tau({\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct},{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V},{\mathcal{B}}_{A\cup V})
(see Definition
6.3
), where

{\mathcal{B}}_{A\cup V}
is the (countable) Boolean algebra generated by all cylinder sets of
{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V}
(see (
2.1
)). We equip the set of dissociated types

{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}\subseteq M({\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct},{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V})
with the subspace topology
and with the Borel
\sigma
-algebra.

Given a random element
\bm{p}
of
{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
, a measurable set

E\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
, a finite set
U\subseteq A\cup V
and
K\in{\mathcal{K}}_{U}
, we define
the shorthand notation

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\bm{p}[E]\mathord{|}_{U}=K]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{E}\bm{p}(x)(C(U,K))\ d\mu^{(d)}(x)\right],

where
C(U,K)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{M\in{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V}\mid M\mathord{|}_{U}=K\}
is the cylinder set as
in (
2.1
) (note that this is well-defined as the function

{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}\ni p\mapsto\int_{E}p(x)(C(U,K))\ d\mu^{(d)}(x)\in\mathbb{R}
is continuous, hence
measurable).

We define the analogous notions for overlapping types similarly.

The next proposition collects all topological properties of dissociated and overlapping types that
we will need. A direct consequence of it is that all maps of Lemma
5.7
are measurable.

Proposition 6.6.

Let
\Omega=(X,\mu)
be an atomless standard probability space, let
d\in\mathbb{N}
, let
{\mathcal{L}}
be a
finite relational language, let
V
be a countable set, let
A
be a finite set disjoint from

V
. Then the following hold.

i.

The sets
{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}
are
closed subsets of
M({\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct},{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V})
and

M({\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp},{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V})
in the topologies

\tau({\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct},{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V},{\mathcal{B}}_{A\cup V})
and

\tau({\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp},{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V},{\mathcal{B}}_{A\cup V})
, respectively.

ii.

{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}
are Polish
spaces, hence standard Borel spaces.

iii.

The maps
M^{\dsct}_{A,V}\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}\to{\mathcal{K}}_{A}
and

M^{\ovlp}_{A,V}\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}\to{\mathcal{K}}_{A}
are continuous (when
{\mathcal{K}}_{A}
is equipped
with discrete topology).

iv.

The map

\pi^{\ovlp}_{A,V}\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
is continuous.

v.

If
B
is a finite set disjoint from
V
and
\alpha\colon A\to B
is an injection, then the maps
\alpha^{*}\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\dsct}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}

and
\alpha^{*}\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\ovlp}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}
are continuous.

vi.

If
{\mathcal{N}}
is a
d
-Euclidean structure in
{\mathcal{L}}
over
\Omega
, then
the maps
\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}
and
\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}
are measurable.

Proof.

For item
(i)
, note that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}

\displaystyle=\bigl\{p\in M({\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct},{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V})\mathrel{\big|}\exists K\in{\mathcal{K}}_{A},\forall x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct},p(x)(\{K^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V}\mid K^{\prime}\mathord{|}_{A}=K\})=1\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\left.M({\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct},{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V})\middle\backslash\bigcap_{K\in{\mathcal{K}}_{A}}U\bigl({\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct},C(A,K),1\bigr)\right.

and since the last intersection is finite (as
{\mathcal{K}}_{A}
is finite since
{\mathcal{L}}
is finite), the set
above is closed.

The proof that
{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}
is also closed is analogous.

For item
(ii)
, first we equip
{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V}
with the topology generated by
cylinder sets so that its
\sigma
-algebra is simply the Borel
\sigma
-algebra. Note that since

{\mathcal{L}}
is finite, all cylinder sets are clopen and
{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V}
is compact. Also,

{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
is either finite (e.g., when
V=\varnothing
) or uncountable, so
Proposition
6.4
(iv)
gives that
(M({\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct},{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V}),\tau({\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct},{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V},{\mathcal{B}}_{A\cup V}))
is a Polish space. Since

{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
is a closed subspace (with subspace topology), it follows that it is also
a Polish space.

The proof that
{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}
is also a Polish space is analogous.

For item
(iii)
, continuity of the map

M^{\dsct}_{A,V}\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}\to{\mathcal{K}}_{A}
follows since for every
K\in{\mathcal{K}}_{A}
, the
preimage

\displaystyle(M^{\dsct}_{A,V})^{-1}(\{K\})

\displaystyle=\bigl\{p\in M({\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct},{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V})\mathrel{\big|}\forall x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d)},p(x)(\{K^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V}\mid K^{\prime}\mathord{|}_{A}=K\})=1\bigr\}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}\setminus U({\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct},C(A,K),1)

is a closed set. The proof of continuity of
M^{\ovlp}_{A,V}
is analogous.

For item
(iv)
, continuity of the map

\pi^{\ovlp}_{A,V}\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
follows since for every
measurable
E\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
, every finite set
W\subseteq A\cup V
, every

K\in{\mathcal{K}}_{W}
and every
r\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle(\pi^{\ovlp}_{A,V})^{-1}\Bigl(U\bigl(E,C(W,K),r\bigr)\cap{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=U\bigl(E\times{\mathcal{R}},C(W,K),r\bigr)\cap{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp},

\displaystyle(\pi^{\ovlp}_{A,V})^{-1}\Bigl(L\bigl(E,C(W,K),r\bigr)\cap{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=L\bigl(E\times{\mathcal{R}},C(W,K),r\bigr)\cap{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp},

where

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\prod_{C\in r^{\ovlp}(A,V)\setminus r^{\dsct}(A,V)}(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d}).

Thus, the pre-images above are open (relative to
{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}
).

For item
(v)
, to show continuity of the map

\alpha^{*}\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\dsct}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
, let

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\prod_{C\in r^{\dsct}(B,V)\setminus r^{\dsct}(\alpha(A),V)}(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d})

and with a small abuse of notation, let us denote by
\mu^{(d)}
the measure on
Y
that is the
product of several copies of
\mu
with the appropriate copies of
\nu_{C}
.

Note that for every measurable
E\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
, every finite set
W\subseteq A\cup V
, every
K\in{\mathcal{K}}_{W}
and every
r\in\mathbb{R}
, the preimage

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!(\alpha^{*})^{-1}(U(E,C(W,K),r)\cap{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct})

\displaystyle=\left\{p\in{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\dsct}\;\middle|\;\int_{E}\int_{Y}p((\overline{\alpha}^{-1})^{*}(x),y)(\{M\in{\mathcal{K}}_{B\cup V}\mid\alpha\mathord{\uparrow}_{V}^{*}(M)\mathord{|}_{W}=K\})\ d\mu^{(d)}(y)\ d\mu^{(d)}(x)<r\right\}

\displaystyle=U\bigl((\overline{\alpha}^{-1})^{*}(E)\times Y,C(\alpha\mathord{\uparrow}_{V}(W),(\alpha\mathord{\uparrow}_{V}\mathord{\downarrow}_{W}^{-1})^{*}(K)),r\bigr)\cap{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}

is open (relative to
{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
).

A similar calculation shows that
(\alpha^{*})^{-1}(L(E,C(W,K),r)\cap{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct})
is
also open (relative to
{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
).

The proof of continuity of
\alpha^{*}\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\ovlp}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}
is analogous.

For the final item
(vi)
, to show measurability of the map

\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}
, we note that for every measurable
E\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
,
every finite set
W\subseteq A\cup V
, every
K\in{\mathcal{K}}_{W}
and every
r\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle(\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V})^{-1}({\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}\cap U(E,C(W,K),r))

\displaystyle=\left\{x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}\;\middle|\;\int_{E}f^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}(x,y)(C(W,K))\ d\mu^{(d)}(y)<r\right\},

which is measurable by Fubini’s Theorem. A similar calculation shows that

(\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V})^{-1}({\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}\cap L(E,C(W,K),r))
is measurable.

The proof of measurability of
\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}
is analogous.
∎

7
Weak amalgamation of dissociated and overlapping types

The objective of this section is to prove in Proposition
7.4
a weak
amalgamation property of dissociated and overlapping types that (in particular) says that the
distribution of a dissociated
A
-type is completely determined by all overlapping
B
-types when

B
ranges in
r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)
.

The next lemma provides a formula for the “expected value” of a random dissociated
A
-type in
terms of a Lebesgue Differentiation expression involving the overlapping
B
-type for a fixed

B\subsetneq A
. The lemma below as stated only works when
\Omega=([0,1],\lambda)
(but as
expected, we will be able to transfer its consequence to arbitrary
\Omega
via a
measure-isomorphism modulo
0
).

Lemma 7.1.

Let
{\mathcal{N}}
be a
d
-Euclidean structure in
{\mathcal{L}}
over
\Omega=([0,1],\lambda)
, let
A
be a finite
set, let
B\subsetneq A
, let
k\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\lvert B\rvert
, let
V
be a countable set disjoint from
A
,
let
U\subseteq A\cup V
be a finite set, let
K\in{\mathcal{K}}_{U}
and let

E\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
be measurable.

Let also
\bm{y}
be picked at random in
\prod_{C\in\binom{A}{>k}}([0,1]\times{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d})

according to
\bigotimes_{C\in\binom{A}{>k}}(\lambda\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{d}\nu_{C})
.

For every
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,k}^{(d)}
, decompose its coordinates as
x=(x^{B},\widehat{x},{\lhd})
, where

\displaystyle x^{B}

\displaystyle\in{\mathcal{E}}_{B}^{(d)},

\displaystyle\widehat{x}

\displaystyle\in[0,1]^{r(A,k)\setminus r(B)},

\displaystyle{\lhd}

\displaystyle\in\prod_{C\in r(A,k)\setminus r(B)}{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d}.

Define also

\displaystyle\bm{p}_{x}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x,\bm{y}).

Then for
\lambda^{(d)}
-almost every
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,k}^{(d)}
, we have

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathbb{E}[\bm{p}_{x}[E]\mathord{|}_{U}=K]

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{1}{\lambda(B_{r}(\widehat{x}))}\cdot\int_{B_{r}(\widehat{x})}\int_{E_{B}}\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{B,V_{B}}(x^{B})(z,t,{\lhd})(\{M\in{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V}\mid M\mathord{|}_{U}=K\})\ d\lambda^{(d)}(z)\ d\lambda(t),

where

\displaystyle V_{B}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}V\cup(A\setminus B),

\displaystyle E_{B}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}E\times\prod_{C\in r^{\ovlp}(B,V_{B})\setminus(r(A,k)\cup r^{\dsct}(A,V))}([0,1]\times{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d})

and
B_{r}(\widehat{x})
is the
\ell^{\infty}
-ball of radius
r
centered at
\widehat{x}
(and by
abuse of notation, the measure
\lambda^{(d)}
over
E_{B}
denotes the product of several copies of

\lambda
with the appropriate copies of
\nu_{C}
).

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}r(A\cup V)\setminus(r(A,k)\cup r^{\dsct}(A,V))

\displaystyle=\{C\subseteq A\cup V\mid A\cap C\neq\varnothing\land(C\subseteq A\to\lvert C\rvert>k)\},

\displaystyle R_{B}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}r^{\ovlp}(B,V_{B})\setminus(r(A,k)\cup r^{\dsct}(A,V))

\displaystyle=\{C\in A\cup V\mid(B=\varnothing\lor(C\cap V_{B}\neq\varnothing\land B\not\subseteq C))\land A\cap C\neq\varnothing\land(C\subseteq A\to\lvert C\rvert>k)\},

so that
E_{B}=E\times\prod_{C\in R_{B}}([0,1]\times{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d})
. Note also that
R_{B}\subseteq R
and

\displaystyle R\setminus R_{B}

\displaystyle=\{C\subseteq A\cup V\mid A\cap C\neq\varnothing\land(C\subseteq A\to\lvert C\rvert>k)\land B\neq\varnothing\land(C\cap V_{B}=\varnothing\lor B\subseteq C)\}

\displaystyle=\{C\subseteq A\cup V\mid A\cap C\neq\varnothing\land(C\subseteq A\to\lvert C\rvert>k)\land\varnothing\neq B\subseteq C\}

\displaystyle=\{C\in r(A\cup V)\mid\varnothing\neq B\subsetneq C\}=\overline{r}^{\ovlp}(B,V_{B}),

where the second equality follows since
\lvert B\rvert=k
makes the condition
C\cap V_{B}=\varnothing

incompatible with
C\subseteq A\to\lvert C\rvert<k
, so it can be dropped.

With a small abuse of notation, whenever we have a product of several copies of
[0,1]
with
several copies of
{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d}
, let us also denote by
\lambda^{(d)}
the measure that is the product
of the appropriate number of copies of
\lambda
with the appropriate copies of
\nu_{C}
.

Then by Fubini’s Theorem, we have

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathbb{E}[\bm{p}_{x}[E]\mathord{|}_{U}=K]

\displaystyle=\int_{\prod_{C\in\binom{A}{>k}}([0,1]\times{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d})}\int_{E}\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x,y)(u)(\{M\in{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V}\mid M\mathord{|}_{U}=K\})\ d\lambda^{(d)}(u)\ d\lambda^{(d)}(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{E}\int_{\prod_{C\in R}([0,1]\times{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d})}\mathbbm{1}[M^{\mathcal{N}}_{A\cup V}(x,u,v)\mathord{|}_{U}=K]\ d\lambda^{(d)}(v)\ d\lambda^{(d)}(u)

\displaystyle=\int_{E_{B}}\int_{\prod_{C\in R\setminus R_{B}}([0,1]\times{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d})}\mathbbm{1}[M^{\mathcal{N}}_{A\cup V}(x,z,w)\mathord{|}_{U}=K]\ d\lambda^{(d)}(w)\ d\lambda^{(d)}(z).

Recalling now the decomposition
x=(x^{B},\widehat{x},{\lhd})
, where
x^{B}
contains the
coordinates indexed by sets in
r(B)
and
(\widehat{x},{\lhd})
the coordinates indexed by sets
in
r(A,k)\setminus r(B)
, since the latter set is a subset of
r^{\ovlp}(B,V_{B})
and
R\setminus R_{B}=\overline{r}^{\ovlp}(B,V_{B})
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\bm{p}_{x}[E]\mathord{|}_{U}=K]

\displaystyle=\int_{E_{B}}\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{B,V_{B}}(x^{B})(z,\widehat{x},{\lhd})\ d\lambda^{(d)}(z).

By the Lebesgue Differentiation Theorem (and Fubini’s Theorem), we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\bm{p}_{x}[E]\mathord{|}_{U}=K]

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{1}{\lambda(B_{r}(\widehat{x}))}\cdot\int_{B_{r}(\widehat{x})}\int_{E_{B}}\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{B,V_{B}}(x^{B})(z,t,{\lhd})\ d\lambda^{(d)}(z)\ d\lambda(t),

for
\lambda^{(d)}
-almost every
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,k}^{(d)}
as desired.
∎

The next lemma is a stepping stone of the weak amalgamation property we aim to prove in
Proposition
7.4
.

Lemma 7.2.

Let
{\mathcal{N}}
be a
d
-Euclidean structure in
{\mathcal{L}}
over
\Omega=(X,\mu)
, let
A
be a finite set,
let
B\subsetneq A
, let
k\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\lvert B\rvert
and let
V
be a countably infinite set disjoint
from
A
.

Let also
\bm{y}
be picked at random in
\prod_{C\in\binom{A}{>k}}([0,1]\times{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d})

according to
\bigotimes_{C\in\binom{A}{>k}}(\lambda\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{d}\nu_{C})
and let

\displaystyle R_{B}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}r(A,k)\setminus r(B).

Then there exists a Markov kernel

\displaystyle\rho\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\ovlp}\times\prod_{C\in R_{B}}(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d})\to{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct})

such that for
\mu^{(d)}
-almost every
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,k}^{(d)}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x,\bm{y})

\displaystyle\sim\rho(\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{B,V}(x^{B}),x^{R_{B}}),

(7.1)

where
x^{B}
and
x^{R_{B}}
are the projections of
x
onto the coordinates indexed by
r(B)
and

R_{B}
, respectively.

In particular, if
\bm{x}
is picked at random in
{\mathcal{E}}_{A,k}^{(d)}
according to

\mu^{(d)}
independently from
\bm{y}
, then
\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(\bm{x},\bm{y})
is
conditionally independent from
\bm{x}^{B}
given
\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{B,V}(\bm{x}^{B})
and
\bm{x}^{R_{B}}
.

Proof.

First note that the conditional independence statement follows from (
7.1
) as
its left-hand side is precisely the conditional distribution of

\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(\bm{x},\bm{y})
given
\bm{x}
and the right-hand side gives an expression
for the same conditional distribution depending only on
\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{B,V}(\bm{x}^{B})
and

\bm{x}^{R_{B}}
.

Now we claim that it suffices to show the result when
\Omega=([0,1],\lambda)
. To see this how
the result for an arbitrary
\Omega=(X,\mu)
follows from the result for

\Omega^{\prime}=([0,1],\lambda)
, let
G\colon\Omega\to\Omega^{\prime}
be a measure-isomorphism modulo
0
and
for every countable set
C
, let

\widetilde{G}_{C}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{C}^{(d)}(\Omega)\to{\mathcal{E}}_{C}^{(d)}(\Omega^{\prime})
be the function that acts
as
G
in all coordinates that have the space
X
and acts identically in all other
coordinates. We define

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{A,V}^{\dsct}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\widetilde{G}_{V}\colon

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}(\Omega)\to{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}(\Omega^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{B,V}^{\ovlp}\colon

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{B,V}^{(d),\ovlp}(\Omega)\to{\mathcal{E}}_{B,V}^{(d),\ovlp}(\Omega^{\prime}),

similarly.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}
be the
d
-Euclidean structure in
{\mathcal{L}}
over
\Omega^{\prime}
given by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{P}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\widetilde{G}_{k(P)}^{-1}({\mathcal{N}}_{P})

and note that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(u)(v)

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{H}}}_{A,V}(\widetilde{G}_{A}(u))(\widetilde{G}_{A,V}^{\dsct}(v)),

\displaystyle\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{B,V}(u)(v)

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{H}}}_{B,V}(\widetilde{G}_{B}(u))(\widetilde{G}_{B,V}^{\ovlp}(v)),

so the result for
{\mathcal{N}}
follows from the result from
{\mathcal{H}}
and the fact that all
\widetilde{G}

are measure-preserving and bijective (except for a zero measure set).

Let us now prove the case
\Omega=([0,1],\lambda)
. Fix a countable Boolean algebra
{\mathcal{B}}
that
generates the
\sigma
-algebra of
{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d)}
and let
{\mathcal{C}}
be the Boolean algebra of
subsets of
{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
generated by all sets of the form
{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}\cap U(E,C(W,K),r)
or
{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}\cap L(E,C(W,K),r)
for some
E\in{\mathcal{B}}
, some
W\subseteq A\cup V
finite, some
K\in{\mathcal{K}}_{W}
and some
r\in\mathbb{Q}
, where
C(W,K)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{M\in{\mathcal{K}}_{A\cup V}\mid M\mathord{|}_{W}=K\}
are the cylinder sets of (
2.1
) and
U
and
L
are as
Definition
6.3
of the topology of
{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
.

Clearly
{\mathcal{C}}
is countable and by Proposition
6.4
(i)
, we know that
{\mathcal{C}}

generates the
\sigma
-algebra of
{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
. Since
{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d)}
is a Polish
space (by Proposition
6.6
(ii)
), it follows that distributions
on
{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d)}
are completely determined by their values on
{\mathcal{C}}
.

For every
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,k}^{(d)}
, let
\bm{p}_{x}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x,\bm{y})
. Given further

E\in{\mathcal{B}}
, a finite set
W\subseteq A\cup V
and
K\in{\mathcal{K}}_{W}
, let

\displaystyle\bm{Z}_{x,E,W,K}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}I_{E,C(W,K)}(\bm{p}_{x})=\int_{E}\bm{p}_{x}(z)(C(W,K))\ d\lambda^{(d)}(z)

and note that

\displaystyle\bm{p}_{x}\in U(E,C(W,K),r)

\displaystyle\iff\bm{Z}_{x,E,W,K}<r,

\displaystyle\bm{p}_{x}\in L(E,C(W,K),r)

\displaystyle\iff\bm{Z}_{x,E,W,K}>r.

Thus, the distribution of
\bm{p}_{x}
is completely determined by the joint distribution of the
random variables
(\bm{Z}_{x,E,W,K})_{E,W,K}
. In turn, since the
\bm{Z}_{x,E,W,K}
have ranges
contained in
[0,1]
(and there are countably many triples
(E,W,K)
), their joint distribution is
completely determined by their joint moments.

Let then
E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}\in{\mathcal{B}}
,
W_{1},\ldots,W_{n}\subseteq A\cup V
be finite and
K_{i}\in{\mathcal{K}}_{W_{i}}

(
i\in[n]
) and let us compute the moment

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bm{Z}_{x,E_{i},W_{i},K_{i}}\right].

For each
i\in[n]
, let
\pi_{i}\colon A\cup V\to A\cup(V\times[n])
be given by

\displaystyle\pi_{i}(a)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\begin{cases}a,&if $a\in A$,\\
(a,i),&if $a\in V$,\end{cases}

and let

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}(\pi_{i}^{*})^{-1}(E_{i})\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V\times[n]}^{(d)}.

For each
i\in[n]
, we also let
W^{\prime}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\pi_{i}(W_{i})
and let

K^{\prime}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(\pi_{i}\mathord{\downarrow}_{W_{i}}^{-1})^{*}(K_{i})
, that is,
K^{\prime}_{i}
is the unique
{\mathcal{L}}
-structure on
W^{\prime}_{i}

such that
\pi_{i}\mathord{\downarrow}_{W_{i}}
is an isomorphism from
K_{i}
to
K^{\prime}_{i}
(recall that

\pi_{i}\mathord{\downarrow}_{W_{i}}
is the bijection obtained from
\pi_{i}
by restricting its domain to
W_{i}
and
its codomain to
\pi_{i}(W_{i})
). We also define

\displaystyle\bm{q}_{x}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V\times[n]}(x,\bm{y}).

By (
5.2
) in Lemma
5.3
applied to
\pi_{i}
, since
\pi_{i}\mathord{\downarrow}_{A}=\id_{A}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{E_{i}}\bm{p}_{x}(z)(C(W_{i},K_{i}))\ d\lambda^{(d)}(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{(\pi_{i}^{*})^{-1}(E_{i})}\bm{p}_{x}(\pi_{i}^{*}(w))(C(W_{i},K_{i}))\ d\lambda^{(d)}(w)

(7.2)

\displaystyle=\int_{(\pi_{i}^{*})^{-1}(E_{i})}\pi_{i}^{*}(\bm{q}_{x}(w))(C(W_{i},K_{i}))\ d\lambda^{(d)}(w)

\displaystyle=\int_{(\pi_{i}^{*})^{-1}(E_{i})}\bm{q}_{x}(w)(C(W^{\prime}_{i},K^{\prime}_{i}))\ d\lambda^{(d)}(w).

We now let

\displaystyle\widehat{W}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}W^{\prime}_{i},

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{K}}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{K\in{\mathcal{K}}_{\widehat{W}}\mid\forall i\in[n],K\mathord{|}_{W^{\prime}_{i}}=K^{\prime}_{i}\},

and note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bm{Z}_{x,E_{i},W_{i},K_{i}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\int_{E_{i}}\bm{p}_{x}(z)(C(W_{i},K_{i}))\ d\lambda^{(d)}(z)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\int_{(\pi_{i}^{*})^{-1}(E_{i})}\bm{q}_{x}(w)(C(W^{\prime}_{i},K^{\prime}_{i}))\ d\lambda^{(d)}(w)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{K\in\widehat{{\mathcal{K}}}}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{E}\bm{q}_{x}(w)(C(\widehat{W},K))\ d\lambda^{(d)}(w)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{K\in\widehat{{\mathcal{K}}}}\mathbb{E}[\bm{q}_{x}[E]\mathord{|}_{\widehat{W}}=K]

where the second equality follows from (
7.2
) and the third equality follows from
linearity of expectation.

Let us decompose
x^{R_{B}}=(\widehat{x},{\lhd})
, where all order variables are collected in
{\lhd}
,
let
V_{B}
and
E_{B}
be as in Lemma
7.1
using
V\times[n]
in place of
V
, so
that applying the lemma to the above yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bm{Z}_{x,E_{i},W_{i},K_{i}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{K\in\widehat{{\mathcal{K}}}}\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{1}{\lambda(B_{r}(\widehat{x}))}\cdot\int_{B_{r}(\widehat{x})}\int_{E_{B}}\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{B,V_{B}}(x^{B})(z,t,{\lhd})(C(\widehat{W},K))\ d\lambda^{(d)}(z)\ d\lambda(t)

for almost every
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,k}^{(d)}
.

Note now that the expression above only depends on
x
via the overlapping
(B,V_{B})
-type

\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{B,V_{B}}(x^{B})
and the projection
x^{R_{B}}=(\widehat{x},{\lhd})
. Since
Lemma
5.3
says that the overlapping
(B,V_{B})
-type
\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{B,V_{B}}(x^{B})
is
completely determined by the overlapping
(B,V)
-type
\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{B,V}(x^{B})
(and there are
only countably many joint moments of
(\bm{Z}_{x,E,W,K})_{E,W,K}
), it follows that there exists a
Markov kernel

\displaystyle\rho^{\prime}\colon\im(\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{B,V})\times\prod_{C\in R_{B}}([0,1]\times{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d})\to{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct})

such that (
7.1
) holds for
\lambda^{(d)}
-almost every
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,k}^{(d)}

with
\rho^{\prime}
in place of
\rho
.

One final technicality is that
\rho^{\prime}
is not defined when its first argument is not in

\im(\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{B,V})
and we need to define the Markov kernel
\rho
measurably. This can
be done as follows: since by Proposition
6.6
(vi)

\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{B,V}
is measurable, its image is universally measurable (as it is an analytic
set); in particular, it is measurable with respect to the completion of the pushforward measure

\theta\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{B,V})_{*}(\lambda^{(d)})
, so we can find a Borel set

D\subseteq\im(\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{B,V})
that differs from
\im(\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{B,V})
only by a
zero
\theta
-measure set. We can then let
\rho
act as
\rho^{\prime}
in
D\times\prod_{C\in R_{B}}([0,1]\times{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d})
and to be constant equal to any fixed distribution in its complement
and (
7.1
) follows.
∎

Before we proceed, let us recall the following standard lemma from probability theory:

Lemma 7.3.

Let
\Omega_{X}
,
\Omega_{Y}
and
\Omega_{Z}
be Borel spaces, let
\bm{X}
,
\bm{Y}
and
\bm{Z}
be
random variables with values in
\Omega_{X}
,
\Omega_{Y}
and
\Omega_{Z}
, respectively. Then the
following are equivalent:

i.

\bm{X}
is conditionally independent from
\bm{Y}
given
\bm{Z}
.

ii.

There exists a Markov kernel
\rho\colon\Omega_{Z}\to{\mathcal{P}}(\Omega_{X})
such
that if
\bm{\widetilde{X}}
is picked at random according to
\rho(\bm{Z})
conditionally on

\bm{Z}
and conditionally independently from
\bm{Y}
, then

(\bm{\widetilde{X}},\bm{Y},\bm{Z})\sim(\bm{X},\bm{Y},\bm{Z})
.

Proof.

The implication
(ii)
\implies
(i)
is obvious as
\bm{\widetilde{X}}

and
\bm{Y}
are conditionally independent given
\bm{Z}
.

For the implication
(i)
\implies
(ii)
, by the Disintegration Theorem,
Theorem
6.2
, there exists a Markov kernel
\rho\colon\Omega_{Z}\to{\mathcal{P}}(\Omega_{X})
such
that for all measurable
A\subseteq\Omega_{X}
and measurable
C\subseteq\Omega_{Z}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\bm{X}\in A,\bm{Z}\in C]

\displaystyle=\int_{C}\rho(z)(A)\ d\mu_{Z}(z),

where
\mu_{Z}\in{\mathcal{P}}(\Omega_{Z})
is the distribution of
\bm{Z}
. Thus, with probability
1
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\bm{X}\in A\mathbin{|}\bm{Y},\bm{Z}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[\bm{X}\in A\mathbin{|}\bm{Z}]=\rho(\bm{Z})(A)=\mathbb{P}[\bm{\widetilde{X}}\in A\mathbin{|}\bm{Z}]=\mathbb{P}[\bm{\widetilde{X}}\in A\mathbin{|}\bm{Y},\bm{Z}],

where the first equality follows since
\bm{X}
is conditionally independent from
\bm{Y}
given

\bm{Z}
and the last equality follows since
\bm{\widetilde{X}}
is conditionally independent
from
\bm{Y}
given
\bm{Z}
. Thus we get

(\bm{\widetilde{X}},\bm{Y},\bm{Z})\sim(\bm{X},\bm{Y},\bm{Z})
as desired.
∎

We can now prove the weak amalgamation property of dissociated and overlapping types.

Proposition 7.4.

Let
{\mathcal{N}}
be a
d
-Euclidean structure in
{\mathcal{L}}
over
\Omega=(X,\mu)
, let
A
be a finite set and
let
V
be a countably infinite set disjoint from
A
. Let also
k\in\{0,\ldots,\lvert A\rvert-1\}
and let
\bm{y}
be picked at random in
\prod_{B\in\binom{A}{>k}}(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{d})

according to
\bigotimes_{B\in\binom{A}{>k}}(\mu\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{d}\nu_{B})
.

Then there exists a Markov kernel

\displaystyle\rho\colon\prod_{B\in r(A,k)}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\ovlp}\to{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct})

such that for
\mu^{(d)}
-almost every
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,k}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x,\bm{y})

\displaystyle\sim\rho\biggl(\Bigl(\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{B,V}\bigl(\iota_{B,A}^{*}(x)\bigr)\mathrel{\Big|}B\in r(A,k)\Bigr)\biggr),

(7.3)

(recall that
\iota_{B,A}\colon B\to A
is the inclusion map).

In particular, if
\bm{x}
is picked at random in
{\mathcal{E}}_{A,k}^{(d)}
according to

\mu^{(d)}
independently from
\bm{y}
, then
\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(\bm{x},\bm{y})
is
conditionally independent from
\bm{x}
given

(\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{B,V}(\iota_{B,A}(\bm{x}))\mid B\in r(A,k))
.

Proof.

First, let us show how (
7.3
) follows from the conditional independence
statement and Lemma
7.3
.

Let
\bm{p}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(\bm{x},\bm{y})
and let
\bm{q}
be the random element of

\prod_{B\in\binom{A}{k}}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\ovlp}
given by

\displaystyle\bm{q}_{B}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{B,V}\bigl(\iota_{B,A}(\bm{x})\bigr)\qquad\left(B\in\binom{A}{k}\right).

Since
\bm{p}
is conditionally independent from
\bm{x}
given
\bm{q}
, by
Lemma
(i)
, there exists a Markov kernel

\displaystyle\rho\colon\prod_{B\in\binom{A}{k}}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\ovlp}\to{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct})

such that if
\bm{\widetilde{p}}
is picked at random according to
\rho(\bm{q})
conditionally on

\bm{q}
and conditionally independently from
\bm{x}
, then

(\bm{\widetilde{p}},\bm{x},\bm{q})\sim(\bm{p},\bm{x},\bm{q})
. In particular this implies that
with probability
1
, for every measurable
P\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(\bm{x},\bm{y})\in A\mathbin{|}\bm{x}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[\bm{p}\in P\mathbin{|}\bm{x}]=\mathbb{P}[\bm{\widetilde{p}}\in P\mathbin{|}\bm{x}]

\displaystyle=\rho(\bm{q})(P)=\rho\left(\left(\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{B,V}\bigl(\iota_{B,A}(\bm{x})\bigr)\;\middle|\;B\in\binom{A}{k}\right)\right)(P),

from which (
7.3
) follows.

Let us now prove that
\bm{p}
is conditionally independent from
\bm{x}
given
\bm{q}
.

For this, it suffices to show that for all measurable
P\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
and all
measurable
E_{C}\subseteq X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d}
(
C\in r(A,k)
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\bm{p}\in P\land\bm{x}\in E_{r(A,k)}\mathbin{|}\bm{q}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[\bm{p}\in P\mathbin{|}\bm{q}]\cdot\mathbb{P}[\bm{x}\in E_{r(A,k)}\mathbin{|}\bm{q}],

where
E_{r(A,k)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\prod_{C\in r(A,k)}E_{C}
.

For every
W\subseteq r(A,k)
, let

\displaystyle E_{W}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\prod_{C\in W}E_{C}

(note that this is compatible with the notation
E_{r(A,k)}
) and let
\bm{x}_{W}
be the projection
of
\bm{x}
onto the coordinates indexed by sets in
W
.

Let us enumerate the elements of
\binom{A}{k}
as
B_{1},\ldots,B_{m}
where
m\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\binom{\lvert A\rvert}{k}
and for each
n\in[m]
, define inductively

\displaystyle W_{n}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\left.r(B_{n})\middle\backslash\bigcup_{i=1}^{n-1}W_{i}\right..

Note that the
W_{n}
form a partition of
r(A,k)
. We also let
R_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}r(A,k)\setminus r(B_{n})
for
every
n\in[m]
and note that for every
i\in\{n+1,\ldots,m\}
, we have
W_{i}\subseteq R_{n}
.

Note also that for every
n\in[m]
, we have

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathbb{P}[\bm{p}\in P\land\forall i\in\{n,\ldots,m\},\bm{x}_{W_{i}}\in E_{W_{i}}\mathbin{|}\bm{q}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathbb{P}[\bm{p}\in P\land\bm{x}_{W_{n}}\in E_{W_{n}}\mathbin{|}\bm{q}_{B_{n}},\bm{x}_{R_{n}}]\cdot\mathbbm{1}[\forall i\in\{n+1,\ldots,m\},\bm{x}_{W_{i}}\in E_{W_{i}}]\mathbin{\big|}\bm{q}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathbb{P}[\bm{p}\in P\mathbin{|}\bm{q}_{B_{n}},\bm{x}_{R_{n}}]\cdot\mathbb{P}[\bm{x}_{W_{n}}\in E_{W_{n}}\mathbin{|}\bm{q}_{B_{n}},\bm{x}_{R_{n}}]\cdot\mathbbm{1}[\forall i\in\{n+1,\ldots,m\},\bm{x}_{W_{i}}\in E_{W_{i}}]\mathbin{\big|}\bm{q}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathbb{P}[\bm{p}\in P\land\forall i\in\{n+1,\ldots,m\},\bm{x}_{W_{i}}\in E_{W_{i}}\mathbin{|}\bm{q}_{B_{n}},\bm{x}_{R_{n}}]\cdot\mathbb{P}[\bm{x}_{W_{n}}\in E_{W_{n}}\mathbin{|}\bm{q}_{B_{n}}]\mathbin{\big|}\bm{q}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[\bm{p}\in P\land\forall i\in\{n+1,\ldots,m\},\bm{x}_{W_{i}}\in E_{W_{i}}\mathbin{|}\bm{q}]\cdot\mathbb{P}[\bm{x}_{W_{n}}\mathbin{|}\bm{q}_{B_{n}}],

where the first equality follows since for each
i\in\{n+1,\ldots,m\}
,

\bm{x}_{W_{i}}
is
\bm{x}_{R_{n}}
-measurable (as
W_{i}\subseteq R_{n}
), the second equality follows
since Lemma
7.2
says that
\bm{p}
is conditionally independent from

\bm{x}^{B_{n}}
(hence also from
\bm{x}_{W_{n}}
) given
(\bm{q}_{B_{n}},\bm{x}_{R_{n}})
, the third
equality follows since for each
i\in\{n+1,\ldots,m\}
,
\bm{x}_{W_{i}}
is

\bm{x}_{R_{n}}
-measurable and since
\bm{x}_{W_{n}}
is conditionally independent from

\bm{x}_{R_{n}}
given
\bm{q}_{B_{n}}
(this is because both
\bm{x}_{W_{n}}
and
\bm{q}_{B_{n}}
are

\bm{x}^{B_{n}}
-measurable and
\bm{x}^{B_{n}}
is independent from
\bm{x}_{R_{n}}
).

With a simple inductive argument, we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\bm{p}\in P\land\forall i\in[m],\bm{x}_{W_{i}}\in E_{W_{i}}\mathbin{|}\bm{q}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[\bm{p}\in P\mathbin{|}\bm{q}]\cdot\prod_{n=1}^{m}\mathbb{P}[\bm{x}_{W_{n}}\mathbin{|}\bm{q}_{B_{n}}]

Applying the above with both
P=P
and with
P={\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
then yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\bm{p}\in P\land\forall i\in[m],\bm{x}_{W_{i}}\in E_{W_{i}}\mathbin{|}\bm{q}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[\bm{p}\in P\mathbin{|}\bm{q}]\cdot\mathbb{P}[\forall i\in[m],\bm{x}_{W_{i}}\in E_{W_{i}}\mathbin{|}\bm{q}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[\bm{p}\in P\mathbin{|}\bm{q}]\cdot\mathbb{P}[\bm{x}\in E_{r(A)}\mathbin{|}\bm{q}],

concluding the proof.
∎

8
Measure theory interlude

Recall that our goal is to provide a way of sampling
M^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}(\bm{x})\sim\mu^{\mathcal{N}}_{V}
by inductively
sampling types of larger and larger finite sets instead of sampling points in
{\mathcal{E}}_{V}^{(d)}
. This
means that we would have liked to have a version of Proposition
7.4
that
either allowed us to correctly sample the dissociated
A
-type given the dissociated
B
-types for
all
B\in\binom{A}{\lvert A\rvert-1}
, or that allowed us to sample the overlapping
A
-type given
the overlapping
B
-types for all
B\in\binom{A}{\lvert A\rvert-1}
. However,
Proposition
7.4
is an awkward in-between: to correctly sample the
dissociated
A
-type, we need to know the overlapping
B
-types for all
B\in\binom{A}{\lvert A\rvert-1}
, which means that we cannot use Proposition
7.4
inductively.

Instead, as mentioned in the introduction, we will define the notion of “amalgamating type” which
is something between the dissociated and overlapping type and that has the correct amalgamation
property: to correctly sample the amalgamating
A
-type, it suffices to know the amalgamating

B
-types for all
B\in\binom{A}{\lvert A\rvert-1}
.

Intuitively, we want the amalgamating
A
-type
p
to know the dissociated
A
-type and know a
recipe that is able to retrieve the overlapping
A
-type if it is given a point
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,\lvert A\rvert-1}^{(d)}
whose amalgamating
B
-types are consistent with
p
. However, it is important
that the amalgamating
A
-type does not store too much extra information; namely, it is obvious that
the overlapping
A
-type has the property above (as
\pi^{\ovlp}_{A,V}
recovers the dissociated

A
-type), but it has too much information as it fails to amalgamate.

To make sense out of this, the definition of amalgamating
A
-types needs to be inductive and must
itself require a weak amalgamation property: to correctly sample the dissociated
A
-type, it
suffices to know the amalgamating
B
-types for all
B\in\binom{A}{\lvert A\rvert-1}
.

Let us assume for a second that we have an even stronger property: assume that if

x,x^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,\lvert A\rvert-1}^{(d)}
have the same amalgamating
B
-types for all

B\in\binom{A}{\lvert A\rvert-1}
, then there exists a measure-preserving function
f_{x,x^{\prime}}\colon X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{d}\to X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{d}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x^{\prime},f_{x,x^{\prime}}(y))

for almost every
y\in X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{d}
. Intuitively, this means that we would like the amalgamating

A
-type of some
(x,y)\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,\lvert A\rvert-1}^{(d)}\times(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{d})
to be

\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x,y)
along with the function

\displaystyle x^{\prime}\mapsto\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}(x^{\prime},f_{x,x^{\prime}}(y))

defined only when
x^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,\lvert A\rvert-1}^{(d)}
has the same amalgamating
B
-types for all

B\in\binom{A}{\lvert A\rvert-1}
.

Here is where the measure-theoretic technicalities start creeping in:

1.

To ensure consistency of the definition, we would like the functions above to satisfy a
cocycle condition of the form
f_{x,x^{\prime}}\mathbin{\circ}f_{x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}}=f_{x,x^{\prime\prime}}
.

2.

The weak amalgamation only ensures that the existence of measure-preserving functions

f_{x,x^{\prime}}\colon X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{d}\to(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{d})\times(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{d})
such that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x^{\prime},f_{x,x^{\prime}}(y,y^{\prime}))

for almost every
(y,y^{\prime})\in(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{d})\times(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{d})
, that is, we might actually need
some dummy variable
y^{\prime}
to properly change one function into the other.

In particular, this makes the cocycle condition of the previous item even more complicated.

3.

When we eventually define the space
{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg}
of amalgamating
A
-types, we will
also define a function
\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg}
that maps
a point to its amalgamating
A
-type, a function

\pi^{\amlg}_{A,V}\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
that retrieves the dissociated

A
-type from the amalgamating
A
-type and a function
\mathbbm{q}^{\amlg,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{A,\lvert A\rvert-1}^{(d)}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}
that retrieves the
overlapping
A
-type when given lower pointwise information in the sense

\displaystyle\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{q}^{\amlg,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x,\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x,y))\qquad(x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,\lvert A\rvert-1}^{(d)},y\in X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{d}).

Then we want to equip
{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg}
with a
\sigma
-algebra that turns it into a Borel
space such that the functions
\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}
,
\pi^{\amlg}_{A,V}
and
\mathbbm{q}^{\amlg,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}

are measurable.

In fact, we will also want to include the
\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}
and
\pi^{\amlg}_{A,V}
in the
commutative diagram of Lemma
5.7
. This means that will also need to define
measurable contra-variant maps
\alpha^{*}\colon{\mathcal{S}}^{(d),\amlg}_{B,V}\to{\mathcal{S}}^{(d),\amlg}_{A,V}
for
all injections
\alpha\colon A\to B
, which in particular means that the amalgamating
A
-type
must know all amalgamating
C
-types for
C\subseteq A
.

To avoid all technicalities of having to make sense of a Polish topology over a space of functions

f_{x,x^{\prime}}
satisfying a complicated cocycle condition, we will instead define the space of
amalgamating
A
-types
{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg}
in a more pragmatic way: intuitively, since

{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}
is a Borel space, any sort of information that the amalgamating
A
-type
needs to know about the overlapping
A
-type that is not already present in the dissociated
A
-type
can be encoded by a point of
[0,1]
, so we can define inductively

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\left(\prod_{B\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg}\right)\times{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}\times[0,1].

In this way,
{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg}
has the natural product topology (which is Polish), we can
retrieve all amalgamating
B
-types for
B\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)
measurably by simply projecting
to the first coordinate and we can retrieve the dissociated
A
-type by projecting to the second
coordinate (this gives us
\pi^{\amlg}_{A,V}
). We only need to be careful in defining the functions

\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}
and
\mathbbm{q}^{\amlg,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}
so that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{q}^{\amlg,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x,\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x,y)),

\displaystyle\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\pi^{\amlg}_{A,V}(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x,y))

for almost every
(x,y)\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,\lvert A\rvert-1}^{(d)}\times(X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{d})
and so that weak
amalgamation holds.

The product definition of
{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg}
is also very convenient to understand how

\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}
has to be defined in order for the (strong) amalgamation property to
hold. To elaborate, fix
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,\lvert A\rvert-1}^{(d)}
, let
\bm{y}
be picked at random in

X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{d}
according to
\mu\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{d}\nu_{A}
and write

\displaystyle\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x,\bm{y})

\displaystyle=(q_{x},\bm{p}_{x},\bm{t}_{x}),

where
q_{x}
contains the lower amalgamating types (which is not random as it is completely
determined by
x
),
\bm{p}_{x}
is the dissociated
A
-type and
\bm{t}_{x}
is the extra
information. Amalgamation then reduces to saying that if
x
and
x^{\prime}
satisfy
q_{x}=q_{x^{\prime}}
, then

(\bm{p}_{x},\bm{t}_{x})\sim(\bm{p}_{x^{\prime}},\bm{t}_{x^{\prime}})
(and weak amalgamation amounts only to the
marginal conclusion
\bm{p}_{x}\sim\bm{p}_{x^{\prime}}
).

Since we have the freedom of choosing how the extra information is encoded by points of
[0,1]
, we
can be pragmatic again and insist that
\bm{p}_{x}
is independent from
\bm{t}_{x}
and
\bm{t}_{x}
is
distributed uniformly in
[0,1]
. If we indeed can define
\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}
so as to ensure
that, then amalgamation will immediately follow from weak amalgamation, which in turn should follow
from the dissociated vs. overlapping amalgamation of Proposition
7.4
.

Finally, we point out that to properly ensure weak amalgamation, we will still need to add “dummy
variables”. This is because for amalgamation to hold, then it must be true that, except for a zero
measure change, if
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,\lvert A\rvert-1}^{(d)}
and
p\in{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
, then the
cardinality of the set

\displaystyle\{\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x,y)\mid y\in X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{d}\land\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x,y)=p\}

can only depend on
x
via the amalgamating
B
-types for
B\in\binom{A}{\lvert A\rvert-1}
. If we
do not add dummy variables, this can fail terribly as Example
8.1
below shows.

Example 8.1.

Inspired by
{\mathcal{N}}^{\qr}
and
{\mathcal{N}}^{\twist}
from Examples
1.1
and
1.2
, consider
the
2
-hypergraphon
{\mathcal{N}}^{\semitwist}
given by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}^{\semitwist}_{E}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\Biggl\{x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{2}\;\Bigg|\;

\displaystyle\left(\min\{x_{\{1\}},x_{\{2\}}\}<\frac{1}{2}\land x_{\{1,2\}}<\frac{1}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\lor\left(\min\{x_{\{1\}},x_{\{2\}}\}\geq\frac{1}{2}\land(x_{\{1\}}+x_{\{2\}}+x_{\{1,2\}})\bmod 1<\frac{1}{2}\right)\Biggr\}.

It is straightforward to check that
{\mathcal{N}}^{\semitwist}
has a unique overlapping/dissociated

1
-type. This can be done directly, or one can note that

\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}^{\semitwist}}=\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}^{\qr}}
, so
{\mathcal{N}}^{\qr}
satisfies
\texttt{UCouple}[1]
(and is
obviously
0
-independent) hence uniqueness of overlapping/dissociated
1
-types follows from
Proposition
5.8
.

We now consider
x,x^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{2,1}
given by

\displaystyle x_{\{1\}}

\displaystyle=x_{\{2\}}=\frac{1}{4},

\displaystyle x^{\prime}_{\{1\}}

\displaystyle=x^{\prime}_{\{2\}}=\frac{3}{4}.

Note that for every
y\in[0,1]
and every
w\in{\mathcal{E}}_{2,V}^{(d),\ovlp}
, we have that

\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}^{\semitwist}}_{2,V}(x,y)(w)
is
the distribution of the random graph
\bm{G}
on
[2]\cup V
such that:

•

We have
\{1,2\}\in E(\bm{G})
if and only if
y<1/2
.

•

If
\{u,v\}\in\binom{[2]\cup V}{2}
is such that
\{u,v\}\cap V\neq\varnothing
and

[2]\not\subseteq\{u,v\}
, then
\{u,v\}\in E(\bm{G})
if and only if
w_{\{u,v\}}<1/2
.

•

All other pairs are declared to be edges independently at random with
probability
1/2
.

This in particular implies that the set

\displaystyle\{\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}^{\semitwist}}_{2,V}(x,y)\mid y\in[0,1]\}

has size
2
: one of the overlapping
2
-types happens when
y<1/2
and the other when
y\geq 1/2
.

On the other hand, note that if
y\in[0,1]
,
u\in V
and
w\in{\mathcal{E}}_{2,V}^{(d),\ovlp}
is such that

w_{\{u\}}\geq 1/2
, then by sampling
\bm{G}
according to

\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}^{\semitwist}}_{2,V}(x^{\prime},y)(w)
, we see that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\{1,u\}\in E(\bm{G})]

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1,&if $(x^{\prime}_{\{1\}}+w_{\{u\}}+w_{\{1,u\}})\bmod 1<1/2$,\\
0,&otherwise,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\{2,u\}\in E(\bm{G})]

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1,&if $(x^{\prime}_{\{2\}}+w_{\{u\}}+w_{\{2,u\}})\bmod 1<1/2$,\\
0,&otherwise,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\{1,2\}\in E(\bm{G})]

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1,&if $(x^{\prime}_{\{1\}}+x^{\prime}_{\{2\}}+y)\bmod 1<1/2$,\\
0,&otherwise,\end{cases}

which means that whenever
y\neq y^{\prime}
, we have

\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}^{\semitwist}}_{2,V}(x^{\prime},y)\neq\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}^{\semitwist}}_{2,V}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
. In
particular, the set

\displaystyle\{\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}^{\semitwist}}_{2,V}(x^{\prime},y)\mid y\in[0,1]\}

has size continuum.

The measure-theoretic formalization of the ideas necessary to make the construction of the
amalgamating types possible is done in Lemma
8.3
below. To ease notation, all lemmas
in this section are proved with single letters for the different objects, but for the reader’s
convenience, we point out now how each of these will be used:

•

(X,\mu)
will be the set of all lower pointwise data along with dummy variables:

{\mathcal{E}}_{A,\lvert A\rvert-1}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
along with the measure
(\mu^{2})^{(d)}
.

•

Y
will be the set of overlapping
A
-types:
{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}
.

•

(Z,\nu)
will be an extra dummy variable (that is being added indexed by
A
):
(X,\mu)
.

•

U
will be the set of all lower amalgamating types:
\prod_{B\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg}
.

•

V
will be the set of dissociated
A
-types:
{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
.

•

W
will be the set of extra information that the amalgamating
A
-type carries besides the
dissociated
A
-type information:
[0,1]
.

•

\gamma\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{A,\lvert A\rvert-1}^{(d)}\to{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp})
will be the Markov
kernel that gives the conditional distribution of
\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(\bm{x},\bm{y})
given the
lower pointwise data
\bm{x}
.

•

\theta\colon\prod_{B\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg}\to{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct})
will be the Markov kernel that
gives the conditional distribution of
\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(\bm{x},\bm{y})
given the lower
amalgamating types
(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,{\mathcal{N}}}_{B,V}(\iota_{B,A}^{*}(\bm{x})))_{B\in\binom{A}{\lvert A\rvert-1}}
(recall that
\iota_{B,A}\colon B\to A
is the inclusion map); the existence of
this is weak amalgamation and will be proven in Lemma
9.5
(i)
.

•

f
will be the function that maps a point
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,\lvert A\rvert-1}^{(d)}
to all the
lower amalgamating types:
f(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,{\mathcal{N}}}_{B,V}(\iota_{B,A}^{*}(x)))_{B\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)}
.

•

g
will be the function that takes the lower pointwise data along with the overlapping

A
-type
(x,p)\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,\lvert A\rvert-1}^{(d)}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
and returns the
dissociated
A
-type (ignoring the pointwise data):
g(x,p)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\pi^{\ovlp}_{A,V}(p)
.

Lemma
8.3
will then provide us objects that will be used as follows:

•

G
will be used to construct the function
\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}
that maps a point of

{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}
to its amalgamating
A
-type.

•

G^{\prime}
will be the function
\mathbbm{q}^{\amlg,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}
that retrieves the overlapping
A
-type from
the amalgamating
A
-type plus the lower pointwise data.

•

\Theta
will be the Markov kernel that gives the conditional distribution of the random
amalgamating
A
-type
\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(\bm{x},\bm{y})
given the lower amalgamating types

(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,{\mathcal{N}}}_{B,V}(\iota_{B,A}^{*}(\bm{x})))_{B\in\binom{A}{\lvert A\rvert-1}}
(the fact that this
exists gives our desired (strong) amalgamation property).

Finally, we point out that for technical reasons, it will be convenient to work with partial
functions that are defined almost everywhere. We use the notation
f\colon A\rightharpoonup B
for such partial
functions and
\dom(f)\subseteq A
for their domain. As usual, composition of partial functions

f\colon A\rightharpoonup B
and
g\colon B\rightharpoonup C
is the partial function
g\mathbin{\circ}f\colon A\rightharpoonup C
defined
only for
x\in f^{-1}(\dom(g))
as
(g\mathbin{\circ}f)(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}g(f(x))
.

By a measurable partial function, we mean a partial function
f
such that
\dom(f)
is measurable
and the restriction
f\mathord{|}_{\dom(f)}
(which is a total function) is measurable. It is
straightforward to check that the Disintegration Theorem, Theorem
6.1
still holds if
\pi

is a partial function as long as it is defined almost everywhere (i.e.,
\nu(\dom(\pi))=1
): this is
done by extending
\pi
arbitrarily (but measurably) to be defined everywhere.

By a partial Markov kernel from a Borel space
X
to a Borel space
Y
, we mean a partial function

\rho\colon X\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{P}}(Y)
such that for every measurable
B\subseteq Y
, the partial function
X\ni x\mapsto\rho(x)(B)\in[0,1]
is measurable. If we are given further a probability measure
\mu
on

X
such that
\mu(\dom(\rho))=1
, then it remains true that there is a unique probability measure

\rho[\mu]
such that

\displaystyle\rho[\mu](A\times B)

\displaystyle=\int_{A}\rho(x)(B)\ d\mu(x)

for all measurable sets
A\subseteq X
and
B\subseteq Y
(this is because the expression under the
integral is defined
\mu
-almost everywhere).

The next lemma will serve as an inductive step for the end goal of this section, Lemma
8.3
.

Lemma 8.2.

Let
(X,\mu)
,
(Z,\nu)
and
(W,\eta)
be atomless standard probability spaces, let
Y
and
U

be Borel spaces with
Y
uncountable, let
f\colon X\rightharpoonup U
and
g\colon X\times Y\rightharpoonup V
be
measurable partial functions, let
\gamma\colon X\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{P}}(Y)
and
\theta\colon U\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{P}}(V)
be
partial Markov kernels with
\mu(\dom(\gamma))=1
, let
A\subseteq\dom(f)\subseteq X
and

B\subseteq\dom(g)\subseteq X\times Y
be measurable sets with
\mu(A)=\gamma[\mu](B)=1

(see (
6.1
)). Suppose further that
A\subseteq f^{-1}(\dom(\theta))\cap\dom(\gamma)
and
for every
x\in A
, we have

\displaystyle g(x,\mathord{-})_{*}(\gamma(x))

\displaystyle=\theta(f(x)).

Finally, let
\pi\colon V\times W\to V
be the natural projection.

Then there exist partial functions
G\colon X\times Y\times Z\rightharpoonup V\times W
and
G^{\prime}\colon X\times V\times W\rightharpoonup Y
and measurable sets
A^{\prime}\subseteq X
and
B^{\prime}\subseteq X\times Y\times Z
such that
letting
\Theta\colon U\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{P}}(V\times W)
be the partial Markov kernel given by

\displaystyle\Theta(u)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\theta(u)\otimes\eta\qquad(u\in U),

the following hold:

i.

A^{\prime}\subseteq A
.

ii.

B^{\prime}\subseteq((A^{\prime}\times Y)\cap B)\times Z
.

iii.

B^{\prime}\subseteq\dom(G)
.

iv.

For every
(x,y,z)\in B^{\prime}
, we have
G^{\prime}(x,G(x,y,z))=y
.

v.

For every
(x,y,z)\in B^{\prime}
, we have
\pi(G(x,y,z))=g(x,y)
.

vi.

(\gamma[\mu]\otimes\nu)(B^{\prime})=1
.

vii.

\mu(A^{\prime})=1
.

viii.

For every
x\in A^{\prime}
, we have

G(x,\mathord{-},\mathord{-})_{*}(\gamma(x)\otimes\nu)=\Theta(f(x))
.

Proof.

First we prove the case when
(X,\mu)=(Z,\nu)=(W,\eta)=([0,1],\lambda)
, where
\lambda
is the Lebesgue
measure and
Y=[0,1]
.

Let
\phi\colon X\times Y\times Z\rightharpoonup X\times V
be given by

\displaystyle\phi(x,y,z)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(x,g(x,y))\qquad(x\in X,y\in Y,z\in Z).

(note that
\phi
does not actually depend on the last coordinate
z\in Z
). Since
\dom(\phi)=\dom(g)\times Z\supseteq B
and
\gamma[\mu](B)=1
, it follows that
\phi
is defined

\gamma[\mu]\otimes\nu
-almost everywhere.

We now apply Theorem
6.1
to
\phi
to obtain a Markov kernel
\rho\colon X\times V\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{P}}(X\times Y\times Z)
so that the set

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{x\in X\mid\rho(x)(\phi^{-1}(x))=1\},

has
\sigma(R)=1
, where
\sigma=\phi_{*}(\gamma[\mu]\otimes\nu)
and for every measurable
h\colon X\times Y\times Z\to[0,\infty]
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{X\times Y\times Z}h(s)\ d(\gamma[\mu]\otimes\nu)(s)

\displaystyle=\int_{R}\int_{\phi^{-1}(r)}h(s)\ d\rho(r)(s)\ d\sigma(r).

(8.1)

Define the partial function
G\colon X\times Y\times Z\rightharpoonup V\times W
by

\displaystyle G(x,y,z)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\Bigl(g(x,y),\rho\bigl(\phi(x,y,z)\bigr)\bigl(L(x,y,z)\bigr)\Bigr)\qquad(x\in X,y\in Y,z\in Z),

(8.2)

where

\displaystyle L(x,y,z)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime})\in X\times Y\times Z\mid\phi(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime})=\phi(x,y,z)\land(y^{\prime},z^{\prime})\leq_{L}(y,z)\},

and
\leq_{L}
is the lexicographic order on
Y\times Z=[0,1]^{2}
(note that the definition of
\phi

forces
x^{\prime}=x
in the above). It is obvious that
G
is measurable and

\dom(G)=\dom(\phi)=\dom(g)\times Z\supseteq B\times Z
. Note also that for
(x,y,z)\in\dom(G)
,
we have
\pi(G(x,y,z))=g(x,y)
(recall
\pi\colon V\times W\to V
is the natural projection).

We now define a partial function
G^{\prime}\colon X\times V\times W\rightharpoonup Y
by

\displaystyle G^{\prime}(x,v,w)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\inf\{y\in Y\mid\phi(x,y,1)=(x,v)\land\rho(x,v)(L(x,y,1))\geq w\}\qquad(x\in X,v\in V,w\in W),

(8.3)

leaving
G^{\prime}
undefined if the set above is empty.

Let us now prove some basic properties about these definitions.

Claim 8.2.1.

We have
R\times[0,1)\subseteq\dom(G^{\prime})
.

Proof of Claim
8.2.1
.

For
(x,v,w)\in R\times[0,1)
, the definition of
R
ensures that

\rho(x,v)(\phi^{-1}(x,v))=1
. Let

\displaystyle\widetilde{y}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\sup\{y\in Y\mid\phi(x,y,1)=(x,v)\}.

We claim that there exists a non-decreasing sequence
(y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in the set above such
that
\phi^{-1}(x,v)=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}L(x,y_{n},1)
. Indeed, since
\phi
does not actually
depend on its last coordinate, if the supremum
\widetilde{y}
is actually attained by some
y
,
then we can simply take
y_{n}=y
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, otherwise, we can simply take a
non-decreasing sequence
(y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in the set converging to
\widetilde{y}
.

Since
(y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is non-decreasing, we have
L(x,y_{n},1)\subseteq L(x,y_{n+1},1)
and
since
\rho(x,v)(\phi^{-1}(x,v))=1>w
, there must exist
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\rho(x,y)(L(x,y_{n},1))\geq w
, so the set in (
8.3
) is non-empty, hence

(x,v,w)\in\dom(G^{\prime})
.
∎

Claim 8.2.2.

For every
(x,v)\in R
and every
(x^{\prime},y,z)\in\phi^{-1}(x,v)
, we have

\displaystyle G^{\prime}\Bigl(x,v,\rho(x,v)\bigl(L(x^{\prime},y,z)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq y.

Proof of Claim
8.2.2
.

Let
w\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\rho(x,v)(L(x^{\prime},y,z))
so that the claim amounts to
G^{\prime}(x,v,w)\leq y
.

Since the definition of
\phi
forces
x^{\prime}=x
and
\phi
does not depend on its last coordinate,
it follows that
L(x,y,1)\supseteq L(x^{\prime},y,z)
, which in particular implies

\rho(x,v)(L(x,y,1))\geq\rho(x,v)(L(x^{\prime},y,z))=w
.

Since
\phi(x,y,1)=(x,v)=\phi(x,y,z)
, we get that
y
is in the set on (
8.3
), hence

\displaystyle G^{\prime}\Bigl(x,v,\rho(x,v)\bigl(L(x^{\prime},y,z)\bigr)\Bigr)=G^{\prime}(x,v,w)

\displaystyle\leq y

as desired.
∎

We now define the set

\displaystyle B^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{(x,y,z)\in X\times Y\times Z\mid(x,y)\in B\land\phi(x,y,z)\in R\land G^{\prime}(x,G(x,y,z))=y\}.

(8.4)

After we define the set
A^{\prime}
, we will let
