B^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}B^{\prime\prime}\cap(A^{\prime}\times Y\times Z)
, which will ensure
that
B^{\prime}\subseteq((A^{\prime}\times Y)\cap B)\times Z
yielding item
(ii)
. Note also
that once we show item
(vii)
(i.e.,
\mu(A^{\prime})=1)
, then
items
(iii)
,
(iv)
,
(v)

and
(vi)
will follow from their counterparts for
B^{\prime\prime}
, so let us prove that
these items do hold for
B^{\prime\prime}
.

We have already observed that
\dom(G)=\dom(\phi)=\dom(g)\times Z\supseteq B\times Z
and since

B^{\prime\prime}\subseteq B\times Z
, item
(iii)
holds for
B^{\prime\prime}
(
B^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\dom(G)
). It
is also clear from the definition of
B^{\prime\prime}
that it satisfies item
(iv)

(
G^{\prime}(x,G(x,y,z))=y
for every
(x,y,z)\in B^{\prime\prime}
). Item
(v)
for
B^{\prime\prime}
(
\pi(G(x,y,z))=g(x,y)
for every
(x,y,z)\in B^{\prime\prime}
) follows from the definition of
G
and the already proved

B^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\dom(G)
.

Let us now prove item
(vi)
for
B^{\prime\prime}

(
(\gamma[\mu]\otimes\nu)(B^{\prime\prime})=1
). By (
8.1
) applied to
\mathbbm{1}_{B^{\prime\prime}}
, we have

\displaystyle(\gamma[\mu]\otimes\nu)(B^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{R}\int_{\phi^{-1}(r)}\mathbbm{1}_{B^{\prime\prime}}(s)\ d\rho(r)(s)\ d\sigma(r).

Since for every
r\in R
we have
\rho(r)(\phi^{-1}(r))=1
and using Claim
8.2.2
,
we get that to show
(\gamma[\mu]\otimes\nu)(B^{\prime\prime})=1
it suffices to show that for every
(x,v)\in R
and
for the set

\displaystyle U(x,v)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\left\{(x^{\prime},y,z)\in\phi^{-1}(x,v)\;\middle|\;G^{\prime}\Bigl(x,v,\rho(x,v)\bigl(L(x,y,z)\bigr)\Bigr)<y\right\},

we have
\rho(x,v)(U(x,v))=0
.

Fix
(x,v)\in R
and since we will be working with points of the form
(x^{\prime},y,z)\in\phi^{-1}(x,v)
,
which forces
x^{\prime}=x
, we will simplify notation by writing
(x,y,z)\in\phi^{-1}(x,v)
. We will also
use the shorthand
U\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}U(x,v)
.

Let us define by transfinite induction sequences
(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\alpha_{0}}
,

(y^{\prime}_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\alpha_{0}}
,
(z_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\alpha_{0}}
and
(M_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\alpha_{0}}

in
[0,1]
with the following properties:

a.

y_{\alpha}<y^{\prime}_{\alpha}
.

b.

If
(x,y^{\prime}_{\alpha},z_{\alpha})\in U
, then
(x,y^{\prime}_{\alpha},z_{\alpha})
is

not
an atom of the measure
\rho(x,v)
, that is, we have

\rho(x,v)(\{(x,y^{\prime}_{\alpha},z_{\alpha})\})=0
.

c.

For the set

\displaystyle U_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{(x,y,z)\in U\mid(y_{\alpha},1)<_{L}(y,z)\leq_{L}(y^{\prime}_{\alpha},z_{\alpha})\},

(8.5)

we have
\rho(x,v)(L(x,y,z))=M_{\alpha}
for every
(x,y,z)\in U_{\alpha}
.

d.

\rho(x,v)(U_{\alpha})=0
for
U_{\alpha}
given by (
8.5
).

e.

For every
\beta<\alpha
, we have
(y_{\beta},y^{\prime}_{\beta})\not\supseteq(y_{\alpha},y^{\prime}_{\alpha})
(as open intervals in
[0,1]
).

Given
(y_{\beta})_{\beta<\alpha}
,
(y^{\prime}_{\beta})_{\beta<\alpha}
and
(z_{\beta})_{\beta<\alpha}
, if

U\subseteq\bigcup_{\beta<\alpha}U_{\beta}
, then stop the construction setting

\alpha_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\alpha
; otherwise, let
(x,y^{\prime}_{\alpha},\widetilde{z}_{\alpha})\in U\setminus\bigcup_{\beta<\alpha}U_{\beta}
and define

\displaystyle M_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\rho(x,v)\bigl(L(x,y^{\prime}_{\alpha},\widetilde{z}_{\alpha})\bigr),

\displaystyle y_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}G^{\prime}(x,v,M_{\alpha}),

\displaystyle z_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\sup\left\{z\in Z\;\middle|\;\rho(x,v)\bigl(L(x,y^{\prime}_{\alpha},z)\bigr)=M_{\alpha}\right\}.

(Note that in the definition of
z_{\alpha}
, all points
z
in the set satisfy

\phi(x,y^{\prime}_{\alpha},z)=(x,v)
since
(x,y^{\prime}_{\alpha},\widetilde{z}_{\alpha})\in U
.)

Let us check that all promised items are inductively satisfied.

First note that since
(x,y^{\prime}_{\alpha},\widetilde{z}_{\alpha})\in U
, we must have

\displaystyle y_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=G^{\prime}\Bigl(x,v,\rho(x,v)\bigl(L(x,y,z)\bigr)\Bigr)<y^{\prime}_{\alpha},

so item
(a)
holds.

We prove item
(b)
by the contra-positive. Suppose that
(x,y^{\prime}_{\alpha},z_{\alpha})
is
an atom of
\rho(x,v)
and note that the definition of
G^{\prime}
in (
8.3
) implies that

\displaystyle G^{\prime}\Bigl(x,v,\rho(x,v)\bigl(L(x,y^{\prime}_{\alpha},z_{\alpha})\bigr)\Bigr)=y^{\prime}_{\alpha}

as for any
y<y^{\prime}_{\alpha}
with
\phi(x,y,1)=(x,v)
, the set
L(x,y,1)
has strictly less

\rho(x,v)
-measure than
L(x,y^{\prime}_{\alpha},1)
as it excludes the atom

(x,y^{\prime}_{\alpha},z_{\alpha}
). Thus, we get
(x,y^{\prime}_{\alpha},z_{\alpha})\notin U
.

To show item
(c)
, first note that if
(x,y,z)\in U_{\alpha}
, then we must have
y>y_{\alpha}
, so there exists
y^{\prime}
with
y_{\alpha}\leq y^{\prime}<y
,
\phi(x,y^{\prime},1)=(x,v)
and

\rho(x,v)(L(x,y^{\prime},1))\geq M_{\alpha}
and since
L(x,y,z)\supseteq L(x,y^{\prime},1)
, we conclude

\displaystyle\rho(x,v)\bigl(L(x,y,z)\bigr)

\displaystyle\geq\rho(x,v)\bigl(L(x,y^{\prime},1)\bigr)\geq M_{\alpha}.

To prove the other inequality, first suppose
(y,z)<_{L}(y^{\prime}_{\alpha},z_{\alpha})
and note that the
definition of
z_{\alpha}
implies that there exists
z^{\prime}\in Z
such that
(y,z)<_{L}(y^{\prime}_{\alpha},z^{\prime})

and
\rho(x,v)(L(x,y^{\prime}_{\alpha},z^{\prime}))=M_{\alpha}
. Since
L(x,y,z)\subseteq L(x,y^{\prime}_{\alpha},z^{\prime})
, we get

\rho(x,v)(L(x,y,z))\leq M_{\alpha}
.

Suppose now that
(y,z)=(y^{\prime}_{\alpha},z_{\alpha})
and note that the definition of
z_{\alpha}
and the
facts that
\phi
does not depend on its last coordinate and that

(x,y^{\prime}_{\alpha},\widetilde{z}_{\alpha})\in U
implies
\phi(x,y^{\prime}_{\alpha},\widetilde{z}_{\alpha})=(x,v)

give us that for every
z^{\prime}<z_{\alpha}
, we have
\rho(x,v)(L(x,y^{\prime}_{\alpha},z^{\prime}))\leq M_{\alpha}
. Finally, note that

\displaystyle\rho(x,v)\bigl(L(x,y,z)\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\rho(x,v)(\{(x,y,z)\})+\sup_{z^{\prime}<z_{\alpha}}\rho(x,v)\bigl(L(x,y^{\prime}_{\alpha},z^{\prime})\bigr)\leq M_{\alpha}

since
(x,y,z)=(x,y^{\prime}_{\alpha},z_{\alpha})\in U
and item
(b)
says that
(x,y,z)
is not
an atom of
\rho(x,v)
. This concludes the proof of item
(c)
.

Let us now show item
(d)
. The definition of
y_{\alpha}
and
z_{\alpha}
as infimum and
supremum, respectively, ensure the existence of a non-increasing sequence
(y_{\alpha}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

and a non-decreasing sequence
(z_{\alpha}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converging to
y_{\alpha}
and
z_{\alpha}
,
respectively, such that

\displaystyle\phi(x,y_{\alpha}^{n},1)=\phi(y^{\prime}_{\alpha},z_{\alpha}^{n})=(x,v),

\displaystyle\rho(x,v)\bigl(L(x,y_{\alpha}^{n},1)\bigr)=\rho(x,v)\bigl(L(x,y^{\prime}_{\alpha},z_{\alpha}^{n})\bigr)=M_{\alpha},

from which it follows that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, letting

\displaystyle U_{\alpha}^{n}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}L(x,y^{\prime}_{\alpha},z_{\alpha}^{n})\setminus L(x,y_{\alpha}^{n},1)

\displaystyle=\{(x,y,z)\in X\times Y\times Z\mid\phi(x,y,z)=(x,v)\land(y_{\alpha}^{n},1)<_{L}(y,z)\leq_{L}(y^{\prime}_{\alpha},z_{\alpha}^{n})\},

we get
\rho(x,v)(U_{\alpha}^{n})=0
.

Note now that

\displaystyle U_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\subseteq\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}U_{\alpha}^{n}\cup\{(x,y^{\prime}_{\alpha},z_{\alpha})\mid\text{ if }(x,y^{\prime}_{\alpha},z_{\alpha})\in U\}.

Since each
U_{\alpha}^{n}
has zero
\rho(x,v)
-measure and
(x,y^{\prime}_{\alpha},z_{\alpha})\in U
implies
that it is not an atom of
\rho(x,v)
by the already proved item
(b)
, it follows
that
\rho(x,v)(U_{\alpha})=0
, concluding the proof of item
(d)
.

Finally, to show item
(e)
, it suffices to show that if the intervals

(y_{\beta},y^{\prime}_{\beta})
and
(y_{\alpha},y^{\prime}_{\alpha})
have non-empty intersection, then we have

y^{\prime}_{\alpha}>y^{\prime}_{\beta}
.

Suppose for a contradiction that
y^{\prime}_{\alpha}\leq y^{\prime}_{\beta}
and that
(y_{\beta},y^{\prime}_{\beta})\cap(y_{\alpha},y^{\prime}_{\alpha})\neq\varnothing
. Note that
y^{\prime}_{\alpha}>y_{\beta}
and since the construction
of
y^{\prime}_{\alpha}
ensures
(x,y^{\prime}_{\alpha},\widetilde{z}_{\alpha})\in U\setminus U_{\beta}
, we must have

(y^{\prime}_{\alpha},\widetilde{z}_{\alpha})>_{L}(y^{\prime}_{\beta},z_{\beta})
. Since
y^{\prime}_{\alpha}\leq y^{\prime}_{\beta}
, we get

y^{\prime}_{\alpha}=y^{\prime}_{\beta}
and
\widetilde{z}_{\alpha}>z_{\beta}
.

Since
\widetilde{z}_{\beta}\leq z_{\beta}<\widetilde{z}_{\alpha}\leq z_{\alpha}
and
y_{\alpha}<y^{\prime}_{\alpha}=y^{\prime}_{\beta}
from item
(a)
, we get
(y^{\prime}_{\beta},\widetilde{z}_{\beta})\in U_{\alpha}
,
so item
(c)
gives

\displaystyle M_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\rho(x,v)\bigl(L(x,y^{\prime}_{\beta},\widetilde{z}_{\beta})\bigr)=M_{\alpha},

which in turn implies
y_{\alpha}=y_{\beta}
. Finally, since
y^{\prime}_{\alpha}=y^{\prime}_{\beta}
and

M_{\alpha}=M_{\beta}
, we also get
z_{\beta}=z_{\alpha}\geq\widetilde{z}_{\alpha}
, contradicting

\widetilde{z}_{\alpha}>z_{\beta}
.

This concludes the inductive construction.

Note now that item
(e)
implies that the construction must stop (as the union of the
intervals
(y_{\alpha},y^{\prime}_{\alpha})
is getting strictly larger and is always a subset of
[0,1]
), so

\alpha_{0}
gets defined. We claim that
\alpha_{0}
is countable. Indeed, otherwise, for the first
uncountable ordinal
\omega_{1}
, the union
\bigcup_{\alpha<\omega_{1}}(y_{\alpha},y^{\prime}_{\alpha})\subseteq[0,1]
has a cover by open sets that does not contain a
countable subcover, contradicting the fact that the usual topology on
[0,1]
is second-countable.

By construction, we know that
U=\bigcup_{\alpha<\alpha_{0}}U_{\alpha}
, so item
(d)

along with the fact that
\alpha_{0}
is countable implies
\rho(x,v)(U)=0
. Thus we have proven
that
\rho(r)(U)=0
for every
r\in R
so item
(vi)
for
B^{\prime\prime}

(
(\gamma[\mu]\otimes\nu)(B^{\prime\prime})=0
) holds.

Our next objective is to construct
A^{\prime}
so that the remaining items (items
(i)
,

(vii)
and
(viii)
) hold.

To do so, let us first define for every
(x,v)\in R
and every
a\in(0,1)
the set

\displaystyle C_{a}(x,v)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\left\{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime})\in\phi^{-1}(x,v)\;\middle|\;\rho(x,v)\bigl(L(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime})\bigr)\leq a\right\}.

We want to show that for
\sigma
-almost every
(x,v)\in R
we have
\rho(x,v)(C_{a}(x,v))=a
. For
this, we define the following points:

\displaystyle y_{x,v,a}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\sup\{y\in[0,1]\mid(x,y,0)\in C_{a}(x,v)\}\cup\{0\},

\displaystyle z_{x,v,a}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\sup\{z\in[0,1]\mid(x,y_{x,v,a},z)\in C_{a}(x,v)\}\cup\{0\}

\displaystyle y^{\prime}_{x,v,a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}G^{\prime}(x,v,a)

\displaystyle=\inf\left\{y\in[0,1]\;\middle|\;\phi(x,v,1)=(x,v)\land\rho(x,v)\bigl(L(x,y,1)\bigr)\geq a\right\}

\displaystyle z^{\prime}_{x,v,a}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\inf\left\{z\in[0,1]\;\middle|\;\phi(x,y^{\prime}_{x,v,a},z)=(x,v)\land\rho(x,v)\bigl(L(x,y,1)\bigr)\geq a\right\}\cup\{1\}

(Note that Claim
8.2.1
along with the fact that
a<1
ensure that

y^{\prime}_{x,v,a}
is actually defined, i.e., that
(x,v,a)\in\dom(G^{\prime})
.)

We first prove the following weaker claim about the
\rho(x,v)
-measure of
C_{a}(x,v)
.

Claim 8.2.3.

Let
(x,v)\in R
. Then
\rho(x,v)(C_{a}(x,v))\leq a
. Furthermore, if
\rho(x,v)(C_{a}(x,v))<a
,
then
(x,y^{\prime}_{x,v,a},z^{\prime}_{x,v,a})
is an atom of
\rho(x,v)
.

Proof of Claim
8.2.3
.

If
C_{a}(x,v)
is empty, the we clearly have
\rho(x,v)(C_{a}(x,v))\leq a
. Suppose then that

C_{a}(x,v)
is non-empty and note that the definitions of
y_{x,v,a}
and
z_{x,v,a}
as suprema
ensure the existence of non-decreasing sequences
(y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(z_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

such that
(x,y_{n},z_{n})\in C_{a}(x,v)
and
C_{a}(x,v)=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}L(x,y_{n},z_{n})
(when any of
the suprema is actually a maximum, the corresponding sequence should be taken constant equal to
the maximum; otherwise, the corresponding sequence should approximate the supremum).

Since both sequences are non-decreasing, we get
L(x,y_{n},z_{n})\subseteq L(x,y_{n+1},z_{n+1})
for
every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, so we conclude

\displaystyle\rho(x,v)\bigl(C_{a}(x,v)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\rho(x,v)\left(\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}L(x,y_{n},z_{n})\right)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\rho(x,v)(L(x,y_{n},z_{n}))\leq a,

where the last inequality follows since
(x,y_{n},z_{n})\in C_{a}(x,v)
.

Suppose now that
\rho(x,v)(C_{a}(x,v))<a
and note that the definitions of
y^{\prime}_{x,v,a}
and

z^{\prime}_{x,v,a}
as infima ensure the existence of non-increasing sequences
(y^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and

(z^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converging to
y^{\prime}_{x,v,a}
and
z^{\prime}_{x,v,a}
, respectively and such that

\phi(x,y^{\prime}_{n},z^{\prime}_{n})=(x,v)
,
\rho(x,v)(L(x,y^{\prime}_{n},z^{\prime}_{n}))\geq a
and

\displaystyle\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}L(x,y^{\prime}_{n},z^{\prime}_{n})

\displaystyle=\left\{(x,y,z)\;\middle|\;\phi(x,y,z)=(x,v)\land\rho(x,v)\bigl(L(x,y,z)\bigr)\geq a\right\}.

(Again, whenever any of the infima is actually a minimum, the corresponding sequence should be
taken constant equal to the minimum.)

Since both sequences are non-increasing, we get
L(x,y^{\prime}_{n},z^{\prime}_{n})\supseteq L(x,y^{\prime}_{n+1},z^{\prime}_{n+1})
, from which we conclude

\displaystyle\rho(x,v)\left(\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}L(x,y^{\prime}_{n},z^{\prime}_{n})\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\rho(x,v)\bigl(L(x,y^{\prime}_{n},z^{\prime}_{n})\bigr)\geq a.

On the other hand, since
y^{\prime}_{n}
and
z^{\prime}_{n}
converge to
y^{\prime}_{x,v,a}
and
z^{\prime}_{x,v,a}

respectively, we have

\displaystyle\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}L(x,y^{\prime}_{n},z^{\prime}_{n})

\displaystyle=\{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime})\in\phi^{-1}(x,v)\mid(y^{\prime},z^{\prime})\leq_{L}(y^{\prime}_{x,v,a},z^{\prime}_{x,v,a})\}.

Let
C^{\prime}_{a}
be the set above and note that since
\rho(x,v)(C_{a}(x,v))<a\leq\rho(x,v)(C^{\prime}_{a})
,
we must have
C_{a}(x,v)\subsetneq C^{\prime}_{a}
(since both these sets are subsets of
\phi^{-1}(x,v)

that are closed downward with respect to
\leq_{L}
within
\phi^{-1}(x,v)
).

We claim that
C^{\prime}_{a}\setminus C_{a}(x,v)=\{(x,y^{\prime}_{x,v,a},z^{\prime}_{x,v,a})\}
. To see this, let

(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime})\in C^{\prime}_{a}\setminus C_{a}(x,v)
and note that the fact that
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime})\notin C_{a}(x,v)

gives
\rho(x,v)(L(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime}))>a
. From
\phi(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime})=(x,v)
along with the definitions of

y^{\prime}_{x,v,a}
and
z^{\prime}_{x,v,a}
as infima, we get
x^{\prime}=x
and

(y^{\prime},z^{\prime})\geq_{L}(y^{\prime}_{x,v,a},z^{\prime}_{x,v,a})
. In turn, since
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime})\in C^{\prime}_{a}
, we conclude

(y^{\prime},z^{\prime})\leq_{L}(y^{\prime}_{x,v,a},z^{\prime}_{x,v,a})
. Thus,
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime})=(x,y^{\prime}_{x,v,a},z^{\prime}_{x,v,a})
, hence

C^{\prime}_{a}\setminus C_{a}(x,v)=\{(x,y^{\prime}_{x,v,a},z^{\prime}_{x,v,a})\}
.

Finally, since

\displaystyle\rho(x,v)(\{x,y^{\prime}_{x,v,a},z^{\prime}_{x,v,a}\})

\displaystyle=\rho(x,v)(C^{\prime}_{a}\setminus C_{a}(x,v))>0,

we conclude that
(x,y^{\prime}_{x,v,a},z^{\prime}_{x,v,a})
is an atom of
\rho(x,v)
.
∎

For every
a\in(0,1)
, let

\displaystyle S_{a}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{(x,y,z)\in B^{\prime\prime}\mid y=y^{\prime}_{\phi(x,y,z),a}\land z=z^{\prime}_{\phi(x,y,z),a}\}.

Since
\nu=\lambda
is atomless and for each
(x,y)
there exists at most one
z
such that

(x,y,z)\in S_{a}
, by Fubini’s Theorem, the set
S_{a}
must have zero
\gamma[\mu]\otimes\nu
-measure. On
the other hand, by (
8.1
) with
h\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbbm{1}_{S_{a}}
, we have

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(\gamma[\mu]\otimes\nu)(S_{a})=\int_{R}\int_{\phi^{-1}(x,v)}\mathbbm{1}_{S_{a}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime})\ d\rho(x,v)(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime})\ d\sigma(x,v)

\displaystyle=\int_{R}\rho(x,v)(\{(x,y^{\prime}_{x,v,a},z^{\prime}_{x,v,a})\})\ d\sigma(x,v),

from which we conclude that for
\sigma
-almost every
(x,v)\in R
, we have

\rho(x,v)(\{x,y^{\prime}_{x,v,a},z^{\prime}_{x,v,a}\})=0
, which along with Claim
8.2.3

allows us to conclude that

\displaystyle\rho(x,v)(C_{a}(x,v))=a

for
\sigma
-almost every
(x,v)\in R
.

We now define

\displaystyle R^{\prime}_{a}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\left\{(x,v)\in R\;\middle|\;\rho(x,v)\bigl(C_{a}(x,v)\bigr)=a\right\}\qquad\bigl(a\in(0,1)\bigr)

\displaystyle R^{\prime}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\bigcap_{a\in(0,1)\cap\mathbb{Q}}R^{\prime}_{a}.

We have just proved that
\sigma(R^{\prime}_{a})=1
for all
a\in(0,1)
, which also implies
\sigma(R^{\prime})=1
.

In particular, we get

\displaystyle 1=\sigma(R^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{R}\mathbbm{1}_{R^{\prime}}(r)\ d\sigma(r)

\displaystyle=\int_{R}\int_{\phi^{-1}(r)}\mathbbm{1}_{\phi^{-1}(R^{\prime})}(s)\ d\rho(r)(s)\ d\sigma(r)

\displaystyle=\int_{X\times Y\times Z}\mathbbm{1}_{\phi^{-1}(R^{\prime})}(s)\ d(\gamma[\mu]\otimes\nu)(s)

\displaystyle=\int_{X}(\gamma(x)\otimes\nu)(\{(y,z)\in Y\times Z\mid\phi(x,y,z)\in R^{\prime}\})\ d\mu(x)

where the second equality follows since
\sigma(R)=1
, the third equality follows since when
r\in R
, we have
\rho(r)(\phi^{-1}(r))=1
, the third equality follows from (
8.1
) with

h\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbbm{1}_{\phi^{-1}(R^{\prime})}
and the last equality follows from Fubini’s Theorem.

Thus, we conclude that defining

\displaystyle A^{\prime}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\left\{x\in A\;\middle|\;(\gamma(x)\otimes\nu)\bigl(\{(y,z)\in Y\times Z\mid\phi(x,y,z)\in R^{\prime}\}\bigr)=1\right\},

we have
\mu(A^{\prime})=1
, that is, item
(vii)
is satisfied. It is also obvious from
the definition of
A^{\prime}
that item
(i)
(
A^{\prime}\subseteq A
) holds.

It remains to show item
(viii)
, that is, we want to show that for every
x\in A^{\prime}
, we have
G(x,\mathord{-},\mathord{-})_{*}(\gamma(x)\otimes\nu)=\Theta(f(x))
. To do so, we pick

(\bm{y},\bm{z})
according to
\gamma(x)\otimes\nu
, we let
(\bm{v},\bm{w})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}G(x,\bm{y},\bm{z})
and we need to show that
(\bm{v},\bm{w})
is distributed according to

\Theta(f(x))=\theta(f(x))\otimes\eta
(recall
\eta=\lambda
).

The hypothesis
g(x,\mathord{-})_{*}(\gamma(x))=\theta(f(x))
for every
x\in A
along with the already
proved items
(i)
and
(v)
(
A^{\prime}\subseteq A
and

\pi(G(x,y,z))=g(x,y)
) implies that
\bm{v}
is distributed according to
\theta(f(x))
. On the
other hand, the definition of
G
in (
8.2
) implies that for every
a\in[0,1]
, we
have the following conditional probability equality:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\bm{w}\leq a\mathbin{|}\bm{v}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\bigl[\rho(x,\bm{v})(L(x,\bm{y},\bm{z}))\leq a\mathbin{\big|}\bm{v}\bigr]=\rho(x,\bm{v})\bigl(C_{a}(x,\bm{v})\bigr).

On the other hand, by the definition of
A^{\prime}
, we know that for every
a\in(0,1)\cap\mathbb{Q}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl[\rho(x,\bm{v})\bigl(C_{a}(x,\bm{v})\bigr)=a\Bigr],

from which we get that the conditional distribution of
\bm{w}
given
\bm{v}
is
\lambda
(with
probability
1
over
\bm{v}
), so
(\bm{w},\bm{v})
has distribution
\theta(f(x))\otimes\lambda=\Theta(f(x))
, as desired.

Finally, as mentioned after the definition of
B^{\prime\prime}
in (
8.4
), letting
B^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}B^{\prime\prime}\cap(A^{\prime}\times Y\times Z)
concludes the proof in the case when

(X,\mu)=(Z,\nu)=([0,1],\lambda)
and
W=Y=[0,1]
.

We now prove that the general case reduces to the previous case. Let
I_{X}\colon[0,1]\to X
,

I_{Z}\colon[0,1]\to Z
and
I_{W}\colon[0,1]\to W
be measure-isomorphisms modulo
0
from

([0,1],\lambda)
to
(X,\mu)
,
(Z,\nu)
and
(W,\eta)
, respectively. Since
Y
is uncountable,
there exists a Borel-isomorphism
J_{Y}\colon[0,1]\to Y
.

Let now
\widetilde{f}\colon[0,1]\rightharpoonup U
,
\widetilde{g}\colon[0,1]\times[0,1]\rightharpoonup V
,

\widetilde{\gamma}\colon[0,1]\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{P}}([0,1])
,
\widetilde{A}\subseteq[0,1]
and

\widetilde{B}\subseteq[0,1]\times[0,1]
be given by

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \widetilde{f}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}f\mathbin{\circ}I_{X},&\widetilde{g}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}g\mathbin{\circ}(I_{X}\otimes J_{Y}),\\
\widetilde{A}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}I_{X}^{-1}(A),&\widetilde{B}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(I_{X}\otimes J_{Y})^{-1}(B),\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\gamma}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(J_{Y}^{-1})_{*}\Bigl(\gamma\bigl(I_{X}(x)\bigr)\Bigr).

Let then
\widetilde{G}\colon[0,1]\times[0,1]\times[0,1]\rightharpoonup V\times[0,1]
,
\widetilde{G}^{\prime}\colon[0,1]\times V\times[0,1]\rightharpoonup[0,1]
,
\widetilde{A}^{\prime}\subseteq\widetilde{A}
and

\widetilde{B}^{\prime}\subseteq\widetilde{B}\times[0,1]
be the
(G,G^{\prime},A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
provided by the
lemma for
(\widetilde{f},\widetilde{g},\widetilde{\gamma},\widetilde{A},\widetilde{B})
(it is
straightforward to check that the hypotheses are satisfied and this is the already proved case).

Then define

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(\id_{V}\otimes I_{W})\mathbin{\circ}\widetilde{G}\mathbin{\circ}(I_{X}^{-1}\otimes J_{Y}^{-1}\otimes I_{Z}^{-1}),

\displaystyle G^{\prime}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}J_{Y}\mathbin{\circ}\widetilde{G}^{\prime}\mathbin{\circ}(I_{X}^{-1}\otimes\id_{V}\otimes I_{W}^{-1}),

\displaystyle A^{\prime}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}I_{X}(\widetilde{A}^{\prime})\cap\dom(I_{X}^{-1}),

\displaystyle B^{\prime}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(I_{X}\otimes J_{Y}\otimes I_{Z})(\widetilde{B}^{\prime})\cap(\dom(I_{X}^{-1})\times Y\times\dom(I_{Z}^{-1})),

It is straightforward to check that the conclusions of the lemma for
(G,G^{\prime},A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
follow from
the conclusions of the lemma for
(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{G}^{\prime},\widetilde{A}^{\prime},\widetilde{B}^{\prime})
.
∎

The next lemma is the key lemma of this section that will be used to formalize the construction of
the amalgamating types. It upgrades Lemma
8.2
in two ways: the first is so that the
resulting
G^{\prime}(x,\mathord{-},\mathord{-})
is an almost everywhere left-inverse of
G(x,\mathord{-},\mathord{-})
(i.e.,
it is able to retrieve both the
Y
and
Z
coordinates instead of only the former); the second is
an added equivariant version of the construction.

Lemma 8.3.

Let
(X,\mu)
,
(Z,\nu)
and
(W,\eta)
be atomless standard probability spaces, let
Y
and
U

be Borel spaces with
Y
uncountable, let
f\colon X\rightharpoonup U
and
g\colon X\times Y\rightharpoonup V
be
measurable partial functions, let
\gamma\colon X\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{P}}(Y)
and
\theta\colon U\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{P}}(V)
be
partial Markov kernels with
\mu(\dom(\gamma))=1
and let
A\subseteq\dom(f)\subseteq X
and

B\subseteq\dom(g)\subseteq X\times Y
be measurable sets with
\mu(A)=\gamma[\mu](B)=1

(see (
6.1
)). Suppose further that
A\subseteq f^{-1}(\dom(\theta))\cap\dom(\gamma)
and
for every
x\in A
, we have

\displaystyle g(x,\mathord{-})_{*}(\gamma(x))

\displaystyle=\theta(f(x)).

Finally, let
\pi\colon V\times W\to V
be the natural projection.

Then there exist partial functions
G\colon X\times Y\times Z\rightharpoonup V\times W
and
G^{\prime}\colon X\times V\times W\rightharpoonup Y\times Z
and measurable sets
A^{\prime}\subseteq X
and
B^{\prime}\subseteq X\times Y\times Z

such that letting
\Theta\colon U\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{P}}(V\times W)
be the partial Markov kernel given by

\displaystyle\Theta(u)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\theta(u)\otimes\eta\qquad(u\in U),

the following hold:

i.

A^{\prime}\subseteq A
.

ii.

B^{\prime}\subseteq((A^{\prime}\times Y)\cap B)\times Z
.

iii.

B^{\prime}\subseteq\dom(G)
.

iv.

For every
(x,y,z)\in B^{\prime}
, we have
G^{\prime}(x,G(x,y,z))=(y,z)
.

v.

For every
(x,y,z)\in B^{\prime}
, we have
\pi(G(x,y,z))=g(x,y)
.

vi.

(\gamma[\mu]\otimes\nu)(B^{\prime})=1
.

vii.

\mu(A^{\prime})=1
.

viii.

For every
x\in A^{\prime}
, we have

G(x,\mathord{-},\mathord{-})_{*}(\gamma(x)\otimes\nu)=\Theta(f(x))
.

Furthermore, if
H
is a finite group such that the following hold:

•

H
acts measurably on
X
,
Y
,
Z
,
U
,
V
and
W
.

•

There exists a measurable set
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
such that
H
the orbits
(\sigma\cdot X^{\prime})_{\sigma\in H}
of
X^{\prime}
are pairwise disjoint and
\mu(\bigcup_{\sigma\in H}\sigma\cdot X^{\prime})=1
.

•

The
H
-actions on
Z
and
W
are trivial.

•

\mu
is
H
-invariant.

•

f
,
g
,
\gamma
and
\theta
are
H
-equivariant (when we equip product spaces with the
diagonal action and
{\mathcal{P}}(V)
and
{\mathcal{P}}(Y)
with the induced actions).

Then
G
,
G^{\prime}
,
\Theta
,
A^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}
can be taken to further satisfy:

ix.

A^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}
are
H
-invariant (with respect to the diagonal
actions).

x.

G
,
G^{\prime}
and
\Theta
are
H
-equivariant (with respect to
the diagonal actions and the induced action on
{\mathcal{P}}(V\times W)
).

Proof.

We will first show all the statements that do not involve the group
H
.

We first prove the case when
(Z,\nu)=(\widetilde{Z}^{\mathbb{N}_{+}},\widetilde{\nu}^{\mathbb{N}_{+}})
and

(W,\eta)=(\widetilde{W}^{\mathbb{N}_{+}},\widetilde{\eta}^{\mathbb{N}_{+}})
for some atomless standard
probability spaces
(\widetilde{Z},\widetilde{\nu})
and
(\widetilde{W},\widetilde{\eta})
.

For every
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
with
m\geq n
, let
\tau_{m,n}\colon\widetilde{Z}^{m}\to\widetilde{Z}^{n}
,

\tau_{\mathbb{N}_{+},n}\colon\widetilde{Z}^{\mathbb{N}_{+}}\to\widetilde{Z}^{n}
,

\pi_{m,n}\colon\widetilde{W}^{m}\to\widetilde{W}^{n}
and

\pi_{\mathbb{N}_{+},n}\colon\widetilde{W}^{\mathbb{N}_{+}}\to\widetilde{W}^{n}
be the projections onto the first
n

coordinates.

Define

\displaystyle G_{-1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}g,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{-1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\gamma,

\displaystyle\Theta_{-1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\theta,

\displaystyle A_{-1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}A,

\displaystyle B_{-1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}B

and let us construct inductively in
n\in\mathbb{N}
sequences of measurable partial functions
G_{n}\colon X\times Y\times\widetilde{Z}^{n+1}\rightharpoonup V\times\widetilde{W}^{n+1}
and
G^{\prime}_{n}\colon X\times V\times\widetilde{W}^{n+1}\rightharpoonup X\times Y\times\widetilde{Z}^{n}
, sequences of partial Markov
kernels
\Gamma_{n}\colon X\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{P}}(Y\times\widetilde{Z}^{n+1})
and
\Theta_{n}\colon U\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{P}}(V\times\widetilde{W}^{n+1})
and sequences of measurable sets
A_{n}\subseteq X
and

B_{n}\subseteq X\times Y\times\widetilde{Z}^{n+1}
such that the following hold:

I.

A_{n}\subseteq A_{n-1}
.

II.

B_{n}\subseteq((A_{n}\times Y\times\widetilde{Z}^{n})\cap B_{n-1})\times\widetilde{Z}
.

III.

B_{n}\subseteq\dom(G_{n})
.

IV.

For every
(x,y,z)\in B_{n}
, we have
G^{\prime}_{n}(x,G_{n}(x,y,z))=(y,\tau_{n+1,n}(z))
.

V.

For every
(x,y,z)\in B_{n}
, we have

(\id_{V}\otimes\pi_{n+1,n})(G_{n}(x,y,z))=G_{n-1}(x,y,\tau_{n+1,n}(z))
.

VI.

\Gamma_{n}[\mu](B_{n})=1
.

VII.

\mu(A_{n})=1
.

VIII.

For every
x\in A_{n}
, we have

G_{n}(x,\mathord{-},\mathord{-})_{*}(\Gamma_{n}(x))=\Theta_{n}(f(x))
.

IX.

A_{n}\subseteq\dom(f)
.

X.

\mu(\dom(\Gamma_{n}))=1
.

Assume that we are given
(G_{n-1},\Gamma_{n-1},\Theta_{n-1},A_{n-1},B_{n-1})
satisfying
items
(III)
,
(VI)
,
(VII)
,

(VIII)
,
(IX)
and
(X)
(note
that these indeed hold for
(G_{-1},\Gamma_{-1},\Theta_{-1},A_{-1},B_{-1})
) and note that the
hypotheses of Lemma
8.2
are satisfied with the following choice of parameters:

\displaystyle(X,\mu)

\displaystyle\leftarrow(X,\mu),

\displaystyle(Z,\nu)

\displaystyle\leftarrow(\widetilde{Z},\widetilde{\nu}),

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle\leftarrow Y\times\widetilde{Z}^{n},

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle\leftarrow U,

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle\leftarrow V\times\widetilde{W}^{n},

\displaystyle(W,\eta)

\displaystyle\leftarrow(\widetilde{W},\widetilde{\eta}),

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\leftarrow f,

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle\leftarrow G_{n-1},

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle\leftarrow\Gamma_{n-1},

\displaystyle\theta

\displaystyle\leftarrow\Theta_{n-1},

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\leftarrow A_{n-1},

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle\leftarrow B_{n-1}.

Let then
(G_{n},G^{\prime}_{n},\Theta_{n},A_{n},B_{n})
be the
(G,G^{\prime},\Theta,A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
provided by
Lemma
8.2
with the choice of parameters above. Define also the partial Markov
kernel
\Gamma_{n}\colon X\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{P}}(Y\times\widetilde{Z}^{n+1})
by

\displaystyle\Gamma_{n}(x)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\Gamma_{n-1}(x)\otimes\widetilde{\nu}\qquad(x\in X)

(which by induction is equivalent to
\Gamma_{n}(x)=\gamma(x)\otimes\widetilde{\nu}^{n+1}
). Note
that we have the following implications between the items of Lemma
8.2
and the
items required by our inductive construction:

(i)
\implies

(I)
,

(ii)
\implies

(II)
,

(iii)
\implies

(III)
,

(iv)
\implies

(IV)
,

(v)
\implies

(V)
,

(vi)
\implies

(VI)
,

(vii)
\implies

(VII)
,

(viii)
\implies

(VIII)
.

Note also that item
(IX)
follows from the same item for
A_{n-1}
and the
already proven item
(I)
and item
(X)
follows from the
same item for
\Gamma_{n-1}
.

Define then the sets

\displaystyle A^{\prime}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}A_{n},

\displaystyle B^{\prime}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}(\id_{X}\otimes\id_{Y}\otimes\tau_{\mathbb{N}_{+},n})^{-1}(B_{n-1}).

Since item
(I)
implies
A_{n}\subseteq A_{-1}=A
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
item
(i)
(
A^{\prime}\subseteq A)
holds.

An inductive application of item
(II)
shows that item
(ii)

(
B^{\prime}\subseteq((A^{\prime}\times Y)\cap B)\times Z
) holds.

Define the measurable partial function
G\colon X\times Y\times Z\rightharpoonup V\times W
by letting

\displaystyle G(x,y,z)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\bigl(g(x,y),w\bigr)\qquad(x\in X,y\in Y,z\in Z),

\displaystyle w_{n}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}G_{n-1}(x,y,\tau_{\mathbb{N}_{+},n}(z))_{n}\qquad(n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}).

Note that item
(III)
implies item
(iii)
(
B^{\prime}\subseteq\dom(G)
)
holds.

Define now the measurable partial function
G^{\prime}\colon X\times V\times W\rightharpoonup Y\times Z
by letting

\displaystyle G^{\prime}(x,v,w)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\Bigl(G^{\prime}_{0}\bigl(x,v,\pi_{\mathbb{N}_{+},1}(w)\bigr),z)\qquad(x\in X,v\in V,w\in W),

\displaystyle z_{n}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\widetilde{\tau}_{n}\Bigl(G^{\prime}_{n}\bigl(x,v,\pi_{\mathbb{N}_{+},n+1}(w)\bigr)\Bigr)\qquad(n\in\mathbb{N}),

where
\widetilde{\tau}_{n}\colon Y\times\widetilde{Z}^{n}\to\widetilde{Z}
is the projection onto the
last coordinate of
\widetilde{Z}^{n}
. A simple induction shows that item
(IV)

implies item
(iv)
(
G^{\prime}(x,(G(x,y,z)))=(y,z)
whenever
(x,y,z)\in B^{\prime}
) holds.

It is obvious from the definition of
G
that item
(v)
(
\pi(G(x,y,z))=g(x,y)

whenever
(x,y,z)\in B^{\prime}
) holds.

Since
\Gamma_{n}(x)=\gamma(x)\otimes\widetilde{\nu}^{n+1}
, an inductive application of
item
(VI)
implies item
(vi)

(
(\gamma[\mu]\otimes\nu)(B^{\prime})=1
) holds.

It is also clear that item
(VII)
implies that item
(vii)

(
\mu(A^{\prime})=1
) holds.

Recalling now that the partial Markov kernel
\Theta\colon U\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{P}}(V\times W)
is given by

\displaystyle\Theta(u)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\theta(u)\otimes\eta=\theta(u)\otimes\widetilde{\eta}^{\mathbb{N}_{+}}\qquad(u\in U),

by item
(VIII)
, it follows that item
(viii)

(
G(x,\mathord{-},\mathord{-})_{*}(\gamma(x)\otimes\nu)=\Theta(f(x))
) holds.

Let us now prove that the general case reduces to the previous case. Let
I_{Z}\colon Z^{\mathbb{N}_{+}}\to Z
and
I_{W}\colon W^{\mathbb{N}_{+}}\to W
be measure-isomorphisms modulo
0
from

(Z^{\mathbb{N}_{+}},\nu^{\mathbb{N}_{+}})
to
(Z,\nu)
and from
(W^{\mathbb{N}_{+}},\eta^{\mathbb{N}_{+}})
to
(W,\eta)
.

Let
\widetilde{G}\colon X\times Y\times Z^{\mathbb{N}_{+}}\rightharpoonup V\times W^{\mathbb{N}_{+}}
,
\widetilde{G}^{\prime}\colon X\times V\times W^{\mathbb{N}+}\rightharpoonup Y\times Z^{\mathbb{N}_{+}}
,
\widetilde{A}^{\prime}\subseteq A
,

\widetilde{B}^{\prime}\subseteq B\times Z^{\mathbb{N}_{+}}
be the
(G,G^{\prime},A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
provided by the lemma in the
previous case.

It is then straightforward to check that setting

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} G&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(\id_{V}\otimes I_{W})\mathbin{\circ}\widetilde{G}\mathbin{\circ}(\id_{X}\otimes\id_{Y}\otimes I_{Z}^{-1}),&G^{\prime}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(\id_{Y}\otimes I_{Z})\mathbin{\circ}\widetilde{G}^{\prime}\mathbin{\circ}(\id_{X}\otimes\id_{V}\otimes I_{W}^{-1}),\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} A^{\prime}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\widetilde{A}^{\prime},&B^{\prime}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(\id_{X}\otimes\id_{Y}\otimes I_{Z})(\widetilde{B}^{\prime})\cap(X\times Y\times\dom(I_{Z}^{-1}))\end{aligned}

gives the desired result.

We now prove that the equivariant version of the lemma involving the group
H
reduces to the
non-equivariant version.

First let

\displaystyle A_{H}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\bigcap_{\sigma\in H}(\sigma\cdot A)\cap X^{\prime},

\displaystyle B_{H}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\bigcap_{\sigma\in H}(\sigma\cdot B)\cap(X^{\prime}\times Y).

It will also be convenient to set
X_{H}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\bigcup_{\sigma\in H}\sigma\cdot X^{\prime}
.

Note that since the orbits
(\sigma\cdot X^{\prime})_{\sigma\in H}
of
X^{\prime}
are pairwise disjoint and

\mu(X_{H})=1
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mu(A_{H})=\gamma[\mu](B_{H})=\mu(X^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lvert H\rvert}.

Note that for all measurable
C\subseteq X
and all measurable
D\subseteq Y
, we have

\displaystyle\gamma[\mu](C\times D)

\displaystyle=\int_{C}\gamma(x)(D)\ d\mu(x)=\int_{C}\gamma(\sigma\cdot x)(\sigma\cdot D)\ d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\sigma\cdot C}\gamma(z)(\sigma\cdot D)\ d\mu(z)=\gamma[\mu](\sigma\cdot(C\times D)),

where the second equality follows since
\gamma
is
H
-equivariant and the third equality follows
since
\mu
is
H
-invariant. Thus
\gamma[\mu]
is
H
-invariant. In particular, since

\gamma[\mu](B)=1
, it follows that
\gamma[\mu](\bigcap_{\sigma\in H}\sigma\cdot B)=1
.

We now let
\mu_{H}
be the probability measure on
X^{\prime}
that is the rescaled restriction of
\mu
,
that is,
\mu_{H}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\lvert H\rvert\cdot\mu\mathord{|}_{X^{\prime}}
. It is also clear from definitions that

\gamma\mathord{|}_{X^{\prime}}[\mu_{H}]=\lvert H\rvert\cdot\gamma[\mu]\mathord{|}_{X^{\prime}\times Y}
. In particular, we have

\displaystyle\gamma\mathord{|}_{X^{\prime}}[\mu_{H}](B_{H})

\displaystyle=\lvert H\rvert\cdot\gamma[\mu]\left(\bigcap_{\sigma\in H}(\sigma\cdot B)\cap(X^{\prime}\times Y)\right)=\lvert H\rvert\cdot\gamma[\mu](X^{\prime}\times Y)=1,

where the second equality follows since
\gamma[\mu](\bigcap_{\sigma\in H}\sigma\cdot B)=1
as

\gamma[\mu](B)=1
and
\gamma[\mu]
is
H
-invariant.

This means that we can apply the current lemma without the equivariance/invariance conclusions
with the following choice of parameters:

\displaystyle(X,\mu)

\displaystyle\leftarrow(X^{\prime},\mu_{H}),

\displaystyle(Z,\nu)

\displaystyle\leftarrow(Z,\nu),

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle\leftarrow Y,

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle\leftarrow U,

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle\leftarrow V,

\displaystyle(W,\eta)

\displaystyle\leftarrow(W,\eta),

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\leftarrow f\mathord{|}_{X^{\prime}},

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle\leftarrow g\mathord{|}_{X^{\prime}\times Y},

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle\leftarrow\gamma\mathord{|}_{X^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\theta

\displaystyle\leftarrow\theta,

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\leftarrow A_{H},

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle\leftarrow B_{H}.

Let then
(G_{H},G^{\prime}_{H},A^{\prime}_{H},B^{\prime}_{H})
be the
(G,G^{\prime},A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
provided by the lemma with
the choice of parameters above.

We then define
(G,G^{\prime},A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
by

\displaystyle G(x,y,z)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\tau_{x}^{-1}\cdot G_{H}\bigl(\tau_{x}\cdot(x,y,z)\bigr)\qquad(x\in X_{H},y\in Y,z\in Z)

\displaystyle G^{\prime}(x,v,w)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\tau_{x}^{-1}\cdot G^{\prime}_{H}\bigl(\tau_{x}\cdot(x,v,w)\bigr),\qquad(x\in X_{H},v\in V,w\in W),

\displaystyle A^{\prime}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\bigcup_{\sigma\in H}\sigma\cdot A^{\prime}_{H},

\displaystyle B^{\prime}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\bigcup_{\sigma\in H}\sigma\cdot B^{\prime}_{H},

where
\tau_{x}\in H
is the unique group element such that
\tau_{x}\cdot x\in X^{\prime}
(recall that the
orbits
(\sigma\cdot X^{\prime})_{\sigma\in H}
of
X^{\prime}
are pairwise disjoint).

It is obvious that
A^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}
are
H
-invariant (i.e., item
(ix)

holds). Note also that for
x\in X_{H}
and
\sigma\in H
, we have
\tau_{\sigma\cdot x}=\tau_{x}\mathbin{\circ}\sigma^{-1}
, from which it follows that

\displaystyle G\bigl(\sigma\cdot(x,y,z)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\tau_{\sigma\cdot x}^{-1}\cdot G_{H}\bigl((\tau_{\sigma\cdot x}\mathbin{\circ}\sigma)\cdot(x,y,z)\bigr)=\sigma\cdot\Bigl(\tau_{x}^{-1}\cdot G_{H}\bigl(\tau_{x}\cdot(x,y,z)\bigr)\Bigr)=\sigma\cdot G(x,y,z),

\displaystyle G^{\prime}\bigl(\sigma\cdot(x,v,w)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\tau_{\sigma\cdot x}^{-1}\cdot G^{\prime}_{H}\bigl((\tau_{\sigma\cdot x}\mathbin{\circ}\sigma)\cdot(x,v,w)\bigr)=\sigma\cdot\Bigl(\tau_{x}^{-1}\cdot G^{\prime}_{H}\bigl(\tau_{x}\cdot(x,v,w)\bigr)\Bigr)=\sigma\cdot G^{\prime}(x,v,w),

for every
y\in Y
, every
z\in Z
, every
v\in V
and every
w\in W
. Thus,
G
and
G^{\prime}
are

H
-equivariant. Finally, the definition of
\Theta
along with the fact that
\theta
is

H
-equivariant and
\eta
is
H
-invariant (as the
H
-action on
W
is trivial) implies that

\Theta
is
H
-equivariant. Thus, item
(x)
holds.

Let us now check that all items not concerning invariance/equivariance follow from their
counterparts for
(G_{H},G^{\prime}_{H},A^{\prime}_{H},B^{\prime}_{H})
.

Item
(i)
is straightforward:

\displaystyle A^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\sigma\in H}\sigma\cdot A^{\prime}_{H}\subseteq\bigcup_{\sigma\in H}\sigma\cdot A_{H}=\left(\bigcap_{\sigma\in H}\sigma\cdot A\right)\cap X_{H}\subseteq A.

Item
(ii)
is also straightforward:

\displaystyle B^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\sigma\in H}\sigma\cdot B^{\prime}_{H}\subseteq\bigcup_{\sigma\in H}\sigma\cdot\Bigl(\bigl((A^{\prime}_{H}\times Y)\cap B_{H}\bigr)\times Z\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\left(\left(\left(\bigcup_{\sigma\in H}\sigma\cdot A^{\prime}_{H}\right)\times Y\right)\cap B_{H}\right)\times Z

\displaystyle=\left((A^{\prime}\times Y)\cap\left(\bigcap_{\sigma\in H}\sigma\cdot B\right)\cap(X_{H}\times Y)\right)\times Z

\displaystyle\subseteq\bigl((A^{\prime}\times Y)\cap B\bigr)\times Z.

Item
(iii)
is straightforward too:

\displaystyle\dom(G)

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\sigma\in H}\sigma\cdot\dom(G_{H})\supseteq\bigcup_{\sigma\in H}\sigma\cdot B^{\prime}_{H}=B^{\prime}.

For item
(iv)
, if
(x,y,z)\in B^{\prime}
, then we have
\tau_{x}\cdot(x,y,z)\in B^{\prime}_{H}
,
so we get

\displaystyle G^{\prime}\bigl(x,G(x,y,z)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\tau_{x}^{-1}\cdot\Biggl(G^{\prime}\biggl(\tau_{x}\cdot\Bigl(x,\tau_{x}^{-1}\cdot G\bigl(\tau_{x}\cdot(x,y,z)\bigr)\Bigr)\biggr)\Biggr)

\displaystyle=\tau_{x}^{-1}\cdot\biggl(G^{\prime}\Bigl(\tau_{x}\cdot x,G\bigl(\tau_{x}\cdot x,\tau_{x}\cdot(y,z)\bigr)\Bigr)\biggr)

\displaystyle=\tau_{x}^{-1}\cdot\bigl(\tau_{x}\cdot(y,z)\bigr)=(y,z).

For item
(v)
, if
(x,y,z)\in B^{\prime}
, then (since
\tau_{x}\cdot(x,y,z)\in B^{\prime}_{H}
)
we have

\displaystyle\pi\bigl(G(x,y,z)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\pi\biggl(\tau_{x}^{-1}\cdot\Bigl(G\bigl(\tau_{x}\cdot(x,y,z)\bigr)\Bigr)\biggr)=\tau_{x}^{-1}\cdot\pi\Bigl(G\bigl(\tau_{x}\cdot(x,y),\tau_{x}\cdot z\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\tau_{x}^{-1}\cdot g\bigl(\tau_{x}\cdot(x,y)\bigr)=g(x,y),

where the last equality follows since
g
is
H
-equivariant.

For item
(vi)
, we have

\displaystyle(\gamma[\mu]\otimes\nu)(B^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(\gamma[\mu]\otimes\nu)\left(\bigcup_{\sigma\in H}\sigma\cdot B^{\prime}_{H}\right)=\lvert H\rvert\cdot(\gamma[\mu]\otimes\nu)(B^{\prime}_{H})=(\gamma\mathord{|}_{X^{\prime}}[\mu_{H}]\otimes\nu)(B^{\prime}_{H})=1,

where the second equality follows since
\gamma[\mu]
is
H
-invariant, the
H
-action on
Z
is
trivial and the orbits
(\sigma\cdot X^{\prime})_{\sigma\in H}
of
X^{\prime}
are pairwise disjoint.

For item
(vii)
, we have

\displaystyle\mu(A^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mu\left(\bigcup_{\sigma\in H}\sigma\cdot A^{\prime}_{H}\right)=\lvert H\rvert\cdot\mu(A^{\prime}_{H})=\mu_{H}(A^{\prime}_{H})=1,

where the second equality follows since
\mu
is
H
-invariant and
A^{\prime}_{H}\subseteq A_{H}\subseteq X^{\prime}

and the orbits
(\sigma\cdot X^{\prime})_{\sigma\in H}
of
X^{\prime}
are pairwise disjoint.

Finally, for item
(viii)

(
G(x,\mathord{-},\mathord{-})_{*}(\gamma(x)\otimes\nu)=\Theta(f(x))
whenever
x\in A^{\prime}
), we need to show
that if
x\in A^{\prime}
and
(\bm{y},\bm{z})\sim(\gamma(x)\otimes\nu)
then

G(x,\bm{y},\bm{z})\sim\Theta(f(x))
.

Indeed, first note that since
\gamma
is
H
-equivariant and
\nu
is
H
-invariant (as the

H
-action on
Z
is trivial), it follows that

\displaystyle\tau_{x}\cdot(\bm{y},\bm{z})

\displaystyle\sim\tau_{x}\cdot(\gamma(x)\otimes\nu)=\gamma(\tau_{x}\cdot x)\otimes\nu

so we get

\displaystyle G(x,\bm{y},\bm{z})

\displaystyle=\tau_{x}^{-1}\cdot G_{H}\bigl(\tau_{x}\cdot(x,\bm{y},\bm{z})\bigr)\sim\tau_{x}^{-1}\cdot\Theta\bigl(f\mathord{|}_{X^{\prime}}(\tau_{x}\cdot x)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\tau_{x}^{-1}\cdot(\theta\bigl(f(\tau_{x}\cdot x)\bigr)\otimes\eta)=\theta(f(x))\otimes\eta=\Theta(f(x)),

where the distributional equality follows from item
(viii)
applied to

(G_{H},\gamma\mathord{|}_{X^{\prime}})
as
\tau_{x}\cdot x\in X^{\prime}
, the second equality follows from the definition
of
\Theta
, the third equality follows since
f
and
\theta
are
H
-equivariant and
\eta
is

H
-invariant (as the
H
-action on
W
is trivial).
∎

9
Amalgamating types

In this section we finally define amalgamating types and prove its amalgamation property. Before we
give the definition, let us point out that one more technical detail was not covered in
Section
8
. Recall that the whole purpose of sampling via amalgamating types is that we
want
(\ell-1)
-independent
d
-Euclidean structures
{\mathcal{N}}
satisfying
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
to have a
unique amalgamating
\ell
-type. We have seen in Proposition
5.8
that such

{\mathcal{N}}
have a unique overlapping
\ell
-type, which in particular implies that they also have a
unique dissociated
\ell
-type; since our intuition is that amalgamating
\ell
-types carry an
intermediate amount of information between their dissociated and overlapping counterparts, we would
expect that they are also unique. However, since to make Lemma
8.3
go through we had
encoded (potential) extra information within the set
[0,1]
, it is impossible to get uniqueness
from the output of Lemma
8.3
.

We solve this issue by being pragmatic again: since Proposition
5.8
says that
an
(\ell-1)
-independent
d
-Euclidean structure
{\mathcal{N}}
satisfying
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
has a unique
overlapping
\ell
-type, we can define amalgamating
A
-types in
{\mathcal{N}}
by splitting into cases: if

\lvert A\rvert\leq\ell
, then we simply let the amalgamating
A
-type be the overlapping
A
-type
and if
\lvert A\rvert>\ell
, we actually apply Lemma
8.3
(in both cases, we also
add information about lower amalgamating types). Since we also have to keep track of the additional
countable set
V
of the dissociated and overlapping types and the same
{\mathcal{N}}
can satisfy

(\ell-1)
-independence and
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
for multiple different
\ell
, we call this
construction the amalgamating
(A,V,\ell)
-type.

Finally, we point out that since Lemma
8.3
requires the addition of dummy variables,
our definition of
(A,V,\ell)
-type requires us to go from
{\mathcal{N}}
to an auxiliary
\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}

that has extra dummy variables.

Before we formalize this construction, let us introduce some terminology regarding partial
functions: we say that a diagram of partial functions is
weakly commutative
if for any two
composition paths
(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})
and
(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{m})
between from the same

A
to the same
B
, we have

\displaystyle(\alpha_{n}\mathbin{\circ}\cdots\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{1})\mathord{|}_{D}

\displaystyle=(\beta_{m}\mathbin{\circ}\cdots\mathbin{\circ}\beta_{1})_{D},

where

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dom(\alpha_{n}\mathbin{\circ}\cdots\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{1})\cap\dom(\beta_{m}\mathbin{\circ}\cdots\mathbin{\circ}\beta_{1})_{D},

that is, the two compositions match in their common domain (but might not match as partial functions
because of points in which only one of them is defined); weak equivariance is defined
similarly. Since in all of our weakly commutative diagrams the partial functions will be defined
almost everywhere, these can be thought of “almost everywhere commutative diagrams”.

We will also need the following invariant/equivariant version of Lemma
7.3
:

Lemma 9.1.

Let
\Omega_{X}
,
\Omega_{Y}
and
\Omega_{Z}
be Borel spaces, let
\bm{X}
,
\bm{Y}
and
\bm{Z}
be
random variables with values in
\Omega_{X}
,
\Omega_{Y}
and
\Omega_{Z}
, respectively. Suppose also
that
H
is a countable group acting measurably on
\Omega_{X}
,
\Omega_{Y}
and
\Omega_{Z}
and that
the distribution
\mu_{Z}
of
\bm{Z}
is
H
-invariant. Then the following are equivalent:

i.

\bm{X}
is conditionally independent from
\bm{Y}
given
\bm{Z}
and the
distribution of
(\bm{X},\bm{Z})
is
H
-invariant.

ii.

There exists an
H
-equivariant partial Markov kernel

\rho\colon\Omega_{Z}\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{P}}(\Omega_{X})
with
\mu_{Z}(\dom(\rho))=1
such that if

\bm{\widetilde{X}}
is picked at random according to
\rho(\bm{Z})
conditionally on
\bm{Z}

and conditionally independently from
\bm{Y}
, then

(\bm{\widetilde{X}},\bm{Y},\bm{Z})\sim(\bm{X},\bm{Y},\bm{Z})
.

Proof.

The implication
(ii)
\implies
(i)
is obvious as

\bm{\widetilde{X}}
and
\bm{Y}
are conditionally independent given
\bm{Z}
and the facts that

\rho
is
H
-equivariant and that
\mu_{Z}
is
H
-invariant imply that the distribution of

(\bm{\widetilde{X}},\bm{Z})
is
H
-invariant.

For the implication
(i)
\implies
(ii)
, we apply
Lemma
7.3
to obtain a (not necessarily
H
-equivariant) Markov kernel

\rho^{\prime}\colon\Omega_{Z}\to{\mathcal{P}}(\Omega_{X})
such that if
\bm{\widetilde{X}}
is picked at random
according to
\rho^{\prime}(\bm{Z})
conditionally on
\bm{Z}
and conditionally independently from

\bm{Y}
, then
(\bm{\widetilde{X}},\bm{Y},\bm{Z})\sim(\bm{X},\bm{Y},\bm{Z})
.

We now argue that
\rho^{\prime}
is
\mu_{Z}
-almost everywhere
H
-equivariant, that is, we argue that for

\mu_{Z}
-almost every
z\in\Omega_{Z}
and every
\tau\in H
, we have
\rho^{\prime}(\tau\cdot z)=\tau\cdot\rho^{\prime}(z)
. For this, it suffices to show that for every measurable
A\subseteq\Omega_{Z}

and every measurable
B\subseteq\Omega_{X}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{A}\rho^{\prime}(\tau\cdot z)(B)\ d\mu_{Z}(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{A}(\tau\cdot\rho^{\prime}(z))(B)\ d\mu_{Z}(z).

But indeed, note that since
\mu_{Z}
is
H
-invariant, we have

\displaystyle\int_{A}\rho^{\prime}(\tau\cdot z)(B)\ d\mu_{Z}(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{\tau^{-1}(A)}\rho^{\prime}(z)(B)\ d\mu_{Z}(z)=\mathbb{P}[\bm{Z}\in\tau^{-1}(A),\bm{\widetilde{X}}\in B]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[\bm{Z}\in A,\bm{\widetilde{X}}\in\tau(B)]=\int_{A}\rho^{\prime}(z)(\tau(B))\ d\mu_{Z}(z)=\int_{A}(\tau\cdot\rho^{\prime}(z))(B)\ d\mu_{Z}(z),

where the third equality follows since
(\bm{\widetilde{X}},\bm{Z})\sim(\bm{X},\bm{Z})
and this
latter distribution is
H
-invariant.

Thus
\rho^{\prime}
is
\mu_{Z}
-almost everywhere
H
-equivariant. Since
H
is countable, this means that
the set

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{z\in\Omega_{Z}\mid\forall\tau\in H,\rho^{\prime}(\tau\cdot z)=\tau\cdot\rho^{\prime}(z)\}

has
\mu_{Z}
-measure
1
, so letting
\rho\colon\Omega_{Z}\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{P}}(\Omega_{X})
be the partial Markov
kernel defined to be equal to
\rho
on
D
and not defined anywhere else yields the result.
∎

We are now finally ready to define amalgamating types.

Definition 9.2.

Let
A
be a finite set, let
V
be a countably infinite
13
13

13

Differently from dissociated or
overlapping types, amalgamating types do not make sense when
V
is finite. This is because for
the definition to go through (see Lemma
9.5
below), we make use of
Proposition
7.4
, which requires
V
to be infinite.
set disjoint from

A
and let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. Let also
\Omega=(X,\mu)
be an atomless standard probability space and

d\in\mathbb{N}
. The space of
amalgamating
(A,V,\ell)
-types
is defined inductively as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\begin{cases}\left(\prod_{B\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg}(\Omega)\right)\times{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}(\Omega),&if $\lvert A\rvert\leq\ell$,\\
\left(\prod_{B\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg}(\Omega)\right)\times{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}(\Omega)\times[0,1],&if $\lvert A\rvert>\ell$,\end{cases}

equipped with the product topology (which makes it a Polish space, hence a Borel space, see
Proposition
6.6
(ii)
). We think of

{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}(\Omega)
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}(\Omega)\times[0,1]
in the above as the
coordinate indexed by
A
.

We also define
\pi^{\amlg,\ell}_{A,V}\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
by
letting it be the natural projection when
\lvert A\rvert>\ell
and letting it be the
composition of the natural projection onto
{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}
with

\pi^{\ovlp}_{A,V}\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
when
\lvert A\rvert\leq\ell
.

We further define
M^{\amlg,\ell}_{A,V}\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}\to{\mathcal{K}}_{A}
as the
composition
M^{\amlg,\ell}_{A,V}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}M^{\dsct}_{A,V}\mathbin{\circ}\pi^{\amlg,\ell}_{A,V}
; as before, for
an amalgamating type
p\in{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}
, we call
M^{\amlg,\ell}_{A,V}(p)
the

underlying model
of
p
.

For an injection
\alpha\colon A\to B
with
B
disjoint from
V
we define a map

\alpha^{*}\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}
inductively on the size of

A
by

\displaystyle\alpha^{*}(p)_{C}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\begin{cases}\alpha\mathord{\downarrow}_{C}^{*}(p_{\alpha(C)}),&if $\lvert C\rvert<\lvert A\rvert$,\\
\alpha^{*}(p_{B}),&if $C=A$ and $\lvert A\rvert=\lvert B\rvert$,\\
\alpha\mathord{\downarrow}_{A}^{*}((p_{\alpha(A)})_{\alpha(A)}),&if $C=A$ and $\lvert A\rvert<\lvert B\rvert$,\end{cases}

for every
p\in{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}
and every
C\in r(A)
, where in the second case,

\alpha^{*}
is either the map
{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\ovlp}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}
defined
contra-variantly in Definition
5.4
when
\lvert B\rvert\leq\ell
or the
extension of the map
{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\dsct}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}
to a map

{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\dsct}\times[0,1]\to{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}\times[0,1]
by acting identically on the
second coordinate when
\lvert B\rvert>\ell
; in the third case,
\alpha\mathord{\downarrow}_{A}^{*}
is defined
similarly and we take the
\alpha(A)
th coordinate twice, the first time corresponds to the
projection
{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{\alpha(A),V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}
and the second time
corresponds to the projection of
{\mathcal{S}}_{\alpha(A),V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}
onto either

{\mathcal{S}}_{\alpha(A),V}^{(d),\ovlp}
when
\lvert A\rvert\leq\ell
or

{\mathcal{S}}_{\alpha(A),V}^{(d),\dsct}\times[0,1]
when
\lvert A\rvert>\ell
.

It is straightforward to check that the fact that the maps of Definition
5.4

are contra-variant (see Lemma
5.5
) implies that the maps defined above are
also contra-variant.

In turn, recall that this also contra-variantly induces maps

\displaystyle\alpha^{*}\colon\prod_{C\in r(B,\lvert B\rvert-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{C,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}\to\prod_{C\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{C,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}

given by

\displaystyle\alpha^{*}(p)_{C}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(\alpha\mathord{\downarrow}_{C})^{*}(p_{\alpha(C)})\qquad(p\in{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell},C\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)).

Finally, it is obvious that the maps
\pi^{\amlg,\ell}
and
M^{\amlg,\ell}
are equivariant, that
is, we have

\displaystyle\pi^{\amlg,\ell}_{A,V}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha^{*}

\displaystyle=\alpha^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\pi^{\amlg,\ell}_{B,V},

\displaystyle M^{\amlg,\ell}_{A,V}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha^{*}

\displaystyle=\alpha^{*}\mathbin{\circ}M^{\amlg,\ell}_{B,V},

(9.1)

Let now
{\mathcal{N}}
be a
d
-Euclidean structure in
{\mathcal{L}}
over
\Omega
and let
\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}
be the

d
-Euclidean structure in
{\mathcal{L}}
over
\Omega^{2}
given by

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}_{P}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{(x,x^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k(P)}^{(d)}(\Omega)\times{\mathcal{E}}_{k(P)}(\Omega)\mid x\in{\mathcal{N}}_{P}\}\qquad(P\in{\mathcal{L}}).

(9.2)

(In the above, all order variables are contained within
x
.)

Clearly
\phi_{\mathcal{N}}=\phi_{\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}
. In fact, note that for every

(x,x^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}(\Omega)\times{\mathcal{E}}_{A}(\Omega)
we have

\displaystyle\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(x,x^{\prime})(y,y^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x)(y)\qquad((y,y^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}(\Omega)\times{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(0),\dsct}(\Omega)),

(9.3)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(x,x^{\prime})(y,y^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,{\mathcal{N}}}_{A,V}(x)(y)\qquad((y,y^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}(\Omega)\times{\mathcal{E}}_{A,V}^{(0),\ovlp}(\Omega)).

Suppose now that
{\mathcal{N}}
is
(\ell-1)
-independent and satisfies
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
and note that

\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}
inherits the same properties. We will define

\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}
,
\mathbbm{q}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}
and

\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}
by induction on the size of
A
so that as to satisfy
the following properties:

a.

\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})

is a measurable partial function that is defined
\mu^{(d)}
-almost everywhere. Furthermore, if

\lvert A\rvert\leq\ell
, then
\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}
is constant on its
domain. (
\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}
gives the amalgamating
(A,V,\ell)
-type of

x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
.)

b.

When the amalgamating
(A,V,\ell)
-type is defined, then all lower
amalgamating types are defined: if
x\in\dom(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V})
, then for
every
B\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)
, we have

\iota_{B,A}^{*}(x)\in\dom(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{B,V})
(recall that
\iota_{B,A}\colon B\to A
is the inclusion map).

c.

\mathbbm{q}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{A,\lvert A\rvert-1}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})\times{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}(\Omega^{2})

is a measurable partial function such that for every

x\in\dom(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbbm{q}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}\bigl(\tau_{A}(x),\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(x)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(x),

where
\tau_{A}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})\to{\mathcal{E}}_{A,\lvert A\rvert-1}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
is the
natural projection (
\mathbbm{q}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}
is the function that retrieves the
overlapping type from the amalgamating type along with lower pointwise data).

d.

\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}\colon\prod_{B\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2}))
is
a partial Markov kernel that is
S_{A}
-equivariant and is such that for the natural projection

\tau_{A}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})\to{\mathcal{E}}_{A,\lvert A\rvert-1}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
, if
\bm{x}
is
sampled in
{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
according to
\mu^{(d)}
, then the conditional distribution
of
\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(\bm{x})
given
\tau_{A}(\bm{x})
is

\displaystyle\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}\Bigl(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{B,V}\bigl(\iota_{B,A}^{*}(\bm{x})\bigr)\mathrel{\Big|}B\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)\Bigr).

In particular,
\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(\bm{x})
is conditionally independent from

\tau_{A}(\bm{x})
given
(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{B,V}(\iota_{B,A}^{*}(\bm{x}))\mid B\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1))
(
\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}
then gives how to sample the
amalgamating
(A,V,\ell)
-type from the lower amalgamating types).

Furthermore, if
\lvert A\rvert\leq\ell
, then
\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}
is the
constant function that maps every point to the distribution concentrated on the unique point in
the image of
\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}
(see item
(a)
above).

e.

For every injection
\beta\colon A^{\prime}\to A
with
A^{\prime}
also
disjoint from
V
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct,\ell}(\Omega^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}}_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}_{A^{\prime}}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}_{A^{\prime},V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}_{A^{\prime},V}^{(d),\dsct,\ell}(\Omega^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}}_{A^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\amlg,\ell}_{A,V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\amlg,\ell}_{A,V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\dsct}_{A,V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\amlg,\ell}_{A^{\prime},V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\amlg,\ell}_{A^{\prime},V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\dsct}_{A^{\prime},V}}

is weakly commutative.

The final property is weak amalgamation, which is a joint property of all sets of size
\lvert A\rvert
:

vi.

Weak amalgamation: if
B
is a set with
\lvert B\rvert=\lvert A\rvert+1
and
B\cap V=\varnothing
and
\bm{x}
is sampled in
{\mathcal{E}}_{B}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})

according to
\mu^{(d)}
, then
\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{B,V}(\bm{x})
is conditionally
independent from
\tau_{B}(\bm{x})
given

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V}(\iota_{A^{\prime},B}(\bm{x}))\mathrel{\big|}A^{\prime}\in r(B,\lvert B\rvert-1)\bigr),

where
\tau_{B}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{B}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})\to{\mathcal{E}}_{B,\lvert B\rvert-1}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
is the
natural projection.

In this definition, we focus on describing the construction, deferring the proof of some of the
claimed properties to Lemmas
9.4
and
9.5
.

First we consider the case when
\lvert A\rvert\leq\ell
. Since
\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}
is

(\ell-1)
-independent and satisfies
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
, Proposition
5.8
says
that there exists an overlapping
(A,V)
-type
q_{A}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}(\Omega^{2})
such that
for
\mu^{(d)}
-almost every
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
, we have

\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(x)=q_{A}
. Note also that by inductive hypothesis, we know that
for every
B\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)
, for almost every
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
, we have

\iota_{B,A}^{*}(x)\in\dom(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{B,V})
. Thus, the set

\displaystyle D\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})

\displaystyle\mid\forall\beta\in S_{A},\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(\beta^{*}(x))=q_{A}

\displaystyle\land\forall B\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1),\iota_{B}^{*}(x)\in\dom(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{B,V})\}

has
\mu^{(d)}
-measure
1
, so we define

\displaystyle\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(x)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\biggl(\Bigl(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{B,V}\bigl(\iota_{B,A}^{*}(x)\bigr)\mathrel{\Big|}B\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)\Bigr),q_{A}\biggr)

whenever
x\in D
and leave
\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(x)
undefined when
x\notin D
.

Since inductively all
\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{B,V}
for
B\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)
are
constant on their domains, it follows that
\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}
is also constant
on its domain
D
(i.e., item
(a)
holds).

By definition it is also clear that item
(b)
holds (i.e., if

\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}
is defined, then all lower amalgamating types are also
defined).

We now define
\mathbbm{q}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{A,\lvert A\rvert-1}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})\times{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}(\Omega^{2})

by

\displaystyle\mathbbm{q}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(x,p)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\rho_{A}(p),\qquad(x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,\lvert A\rvert-1}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2}),p\in{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})),

where
\rho_{A}\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})\to{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}(\Omega^{2})
is the
natural projection.

Item
(c)

(
\mathbbm{q}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(\tau_{A}(x),\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(x))=\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(x)
whenever
x\in\dom(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V})
)
follows immediately from definitions.

Finally, define the partial Markov kernel

\displaystyle\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}\colon\prod_{B\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})

to be the constant function whose value is the distribution on

{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})
that is concentrated on the unique point in the image of

\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}
. Obviously, item
(d)
holds (i.e.,

\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}
correctly computes the conditional distribution of the
amalgamating
(A,V,\ell)
-type given the lower amalgamating types).

We defer the proofs of items
(e)
and
(vi)
to
Lemmas
9.4
and
9.5
, respectively.

We now consider the case when
A=[a]
for some
a\in\mathbb{N}
with
a>\ell
. Our objective is to
setup for applying Lemma
8.3
. First, we take the following choice of parameters for
the spaces:

\displaystyle(X,\mu)

\displaystyle\leftarrow({\mathcal{E}}_{a,a-1}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2}),\mu^{(d)}),

\displaystyle(Z,\nu)

\displaystyle\leftarrow(\Omega,\mu),

\displaystyle(W,\eta)

\displaystyle\leftarrow([0,1],\lambda),

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle\leftarrow{\mathcal{S}}_{a,V}^{(d),\ovlp}(\Omega^{2}),

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle\leftarrow\prod_{B\in r(a,a-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2}),

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle\leftarrow{\mathcal{S}}_{a,V}^{(d),\dsct}(\Omega^{2}).

Note that when we consider any of the contra-variant definitions only for members of
S_{a}
(i.e.,
injections
\beta\colon[a]\to[a]
, which are necessarily bijections), contra-variance translates
into a right action of
S_{a}
on the corresponding space.

It is obvious that the action of
S_{a}
on
{\mathcal{E}}_{a,a-1}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
is measurable and

\mu^{(d)}
is
S_{a}
-invariant. We claim that there exists a measurable set

X^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}_{a,a-1}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
such that the orbits
(\sigma\cdot X^{\prime})_{\sigma\in S_{a}}

of
X^{\prime}
under the action of
S_{a}
are pairwise disjoint and
\mu(\bigcup_{\sigma\in S_{a}}\sigma\cdot X^{\prime})=1
. Indeed, if we fix a measurable order
\leq
on
\Omega^{2}
(this can be
done by noting that
\Omega^{2}
is Borel-isomorphic to
[0,1]
and pulling back the usual order of

[0,1]
through the Borel-isomorphism), then we can take

\displaystyle X^{\prime}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{a,a-1}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})\mid x_{\{1\}}<x_{\{2\}}<\cdots<x_{\{a\}}\}.

The fact that
\mu^{(d)}(\bigcup_{\sigma\in S_{a}}\sigma\cdot X^{\prime})=1
follows since
\mu
is
atomless.

By Proposition
6.6
(v)
, the actions of
S_{a}
on

{\mathcal{S}}_{a,V}^{(d),\ovlp}(\Omega^{2})
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{a,V}^{(d),\dsct}(\Omega^{2})
are continuous, hence
measurable. Similarly, the same proposition (along with the fact that

{\mathcal{S}}_{a,V}^{(d),\amlg}(\Omega^{2})
is equipped with product topology) implies that the action of

S_{a}
on
\prod_{B\in r(a,a-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})
is continuous, hence
measurable.

Also, obviously all trivial actions are measurable. Thus, we have covered all hypotheses of
Lemma
8.3
concerning action measurability and invariance (but not yet equivariance
of
f
,
g
,
\gamma
and
\theta
).

Let us now setup the final objects
f
,
g
,
\gamma
and
\theta
of Lemma
8.3
.

Note that if
\bm{x}
is picked at random in
{\mathcal{E}}_{a}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
according to
\mu^{(d)}
,
then Lemma
5.7
implies that the distribution of

\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}(\bm{x})
is
S_{a}
-invariant (as
\mu^{(d)}
is

S_{a}
-invariant). Since inductively all lower sized amalgamating types satisfy the weak
amalgamation property of item
(vi)
, Lemma
9.1
gives us an

S_{a}
-equivariant partial Markov kernel

\displaystyle\theta_{a}\colon\prod_{B\in r(a,a-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{S}}_{a,V}^{(d),\dsct}(\Omega^{2}))

such that the conditional distribution of
\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}(\bm{x})
given

\tau_{a}(\bm{x})
is

\displaystyle\theta_{a}\Bigl(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{B,V}\bigl(\iota_{B,[a]}^{*}(\bm{x})\bigr)\mathrel{\Big|}B\in r(a,a-1)\Bigr).

On the other hand, Lemma
5.7
also implies that the distribution of

\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(\bm{x})
is
S_{a}
-invariant as well, so Lemma
9.1

(with
\bm{Y}
trivial) further gives us an
S_{a}
-equivariant partial Markov kernel

\displaystyle\gamma_{a}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{a,a-1}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{S}}_{a,V}^{(d),\ovlp}(\Omega^{2}))

such the conditional distribution of
\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}(\bm{x})
given

\tau_{a}(\bm{x})
is
\gamma_{a}(\tau_{a}(\bm{x}))
(in particular, we have

\mu^{(d)}(\dom(\gamma_{a}))=1
).

We then take the following parameters for Lemma
8.3
:

\displaystyle\theta

\displaystyle\leftarrow\theta_{a},

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle\leftarrow\gamma_{a},

we define the partial functions

\displaystyle f\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{a,a-1}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup\prod_{B\in r(a,a-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2}),

\displaystyle g\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{a,a-1}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})\times{\mathcal{S}}_{a,V}^{(d),\ovlp}(\Omega^{2})

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{S}}_{a,V}^{(d),\dsct}(\Omega^{2})

by

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{B,V}(\iota_{B,[a]}^{*}(x))\mid B\in r(a,a-1)),

\displaystyle g(x,p)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\pi^{\ovlp}_{a,V}(p),

(9.4)

for every
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{a,a-1}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
and every
p\in{\mathcal{S}}_{a,V}^{(d),\ovlp}(\Omega^{2})
and we
set

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dom(f)\cap f^{-1}(\dom(\theta_{a}))\cap\dom(\gamma_{a}),

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dom(g)={\mathcal{E}}_{a,a-1}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})\times{\mathcal{S}}_{a,V}^{(d),\ovlp}(\Omega^{2})

(
g
is actually a total function). Our inductive hypothesis of item
(a)
implies
that
\mu^{(d)}(A)=\gamma_{a}[\mu^{(d)}](B)=1
.

Let us now check that for every
x\in A
, we have

\displaystyle g(x,\mathord{-})_{*}(\gamma_{a}(x))

\displaystyle=\theta_{a}(f(x)).

Since
\mu^{(d)}(A)=1
and
A\subseteq f^{-1}(\dom(\theta_{a}))\cap\dom(\gamma_{a})
, this is of
course equivalent to showing that for
\bm{x}
picked at random according to
\mu^{(d)}
, we have

\displaystyle g(\bm{x},\mathord{-})_{*}(\gamma_{a}(\bm{x}))

\displaystyle=\theta_{a}(f(\bm{x})).

By construction, the right-hand side of the above is the conditional distribution of

\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}(\bm{x})
given
\tau_{a}(\bm{x})
and the left-hand side is the
conditional distribution of
\pi^{\ovlp}_{a,V}(\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}(\bm{x}))
given

\tau_{a}(\bm{x})
, so their equality follows from

\pi^{\ovlp}_{a,V}\mathbin{\circ}\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}=\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}
of
Lemma
5.7
.

Note also that Lemma
5.7
implies that
g
is
S_{a}
-equivariant and our inductive
hypothesis of item
(e)
implies that
f
is
S_{a}
-equivariant.

Thus, all hypotheses of Lemma
8.3
are satisfied. Let then
(G,G^{\prime},A^{\prime},B^{\prime},\Theta)
be
given by the lemma and define

\displaystyle\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{a}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{S}}_{a,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2}),

\displaystyle\mathbbm{q}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{a,a-1}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})\times{\mathcal{S}}_{a,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{S}}_{a,V}^{(d),\ovlp}(\Omega^{2}),

\displaystyle\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}\colon\prod_{B\in r(a,a-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2}))

by

\displaystyle\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}(x,(y,z))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\biggl(\Bigl(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{B,V}\bigl(\iota_{B,[a]}^{*}(x)\bigr)\mid B\in r(a,a-1)\Bigr),G\Bigl(x,\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}\bigl(x,(y,z)\bigr),z\Bigr)\biggr)

for every
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{a,a-1}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
, every
y\in\Omega\times{\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{d}
and every

z\in\Omega
(so that
(x,y,z)\in{\mathcal{E}}_{a}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
, with
(y,z)
containing the coordinate
indexed by
A
) such that

\displaystyle\Bigr(x,\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}\bigl(x,(y,z)\bigr),z\Bigr)

\displaystyle\in B^{\prime},

otherwise, we leave
\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}(x,(y,z))
undefined;

\displaystyle\mathbbm{q}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}(x,p)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\rho^{\prime}_{a}(G^{\prime}(x,\rho_{a}(p))),

for every
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{a,a-1}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
and every

p\in{\mathcal{S}}_{a,a-1}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})
, where

\rho_{a}\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})\to{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}(\Omega^{2})\times[0,1]

and
\rho^{\prime}_{a}\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}(\Omega^{2})\times\Omega\to{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\ovlp}(\Omega^{2})

are the natural projections; and

\displaystyle\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}(q)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\delta_{q}\otimes\Theta(q),

for every
q\in\prod_{B\in r(a,a-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})
, where
\delta_{q}
is the
Dirac measure concentrated on
q
.

We now argue why Lemma
8.3
gives all required properties to continue the inductive
construction, with the exception of properties
(e)

and
(vi)
whose proofs are deferred to Lemmas
9.4

and
9.5
, respectively.

Since
G
is measurable and is defined
\gamma_{a}[\mu^{(d)}]
-almost everywhere (by
items
(iii)
and
(vi)
of Lemma
8.3
), it
follows that
\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}
is measurable and is defined almost everywhere, so
item
(a)
holds.

Items
(i)
and
(ii)
of Lemma
8.3
imply that when
the amalgamating
(a,V,\ell)
-type is defined, then all lower amalgamating types are also defined,
that is, item
(b)
holds.

The fact that
\mathbbm{q}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}
correctly retrieves the overlapping type from
the amalgamating type along with lower pointwise data (item
(c)
) follows from
item
(iv)
in Lemma
8.3
: for every

(x,y,z)\in\dom(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V})
with
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{a,a-1}^{(d)}(\Omega)
,

y\in\Omega\times{\mathcal{O}}_{a}^{d}
and
z\in\Omega
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbbm{q}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}\Bigl(x,\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}\bigl(x,(y,z)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\rho^{\prime}_{a}\Biggl(G^{\prime}\biggl(x,G\Bigl(x,\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}\bigl(x,(y,z)\bigr),z\Bigr)\biggr)\Biggr)

\displaystyle=\rho^{\prime}_{a}\Bigl(\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}\bigl(x,(y,z)\bigr),z\Bigr)=\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}\bigl(x,(y,z)\bigr).

For item
(d)
, we have to show that if
\bm{x}
is sampled in

{\mathcal{E}}_{a}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
according to
\mu^{(d)}
, then the conditional distribution of

\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}(\bm{x})
given
\tau_{a}(\bm{x})
is

\displaystyle\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}\Bigl(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{B,V}\bigl(\iota_{B,[a]}^{*}(\bm{x})\bigr)\mathrel{\Big|}B\in r(a,a-1)\Bigr).

Note that the argument of
\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}
is precisely
f(\bm{x})
.

Since
\mu^{(d)}(A^{\prime})=1
, it suffices to show that for every
x\in A^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}_{a,a-1}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
, if
\bm{y}
and
\bm{z}
are picked independently at
random in
\Omega\times{\mathcal{O}}_{a}^{d}
and
\Omega
with respect to
\mu\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{d}\nu_{a}

and
\mu
, respectively, then

\displaystyle\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}(x,(\bm{y},\bm{z}))

\displaystyle\sim\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}\bigl(f(\bm{x})\bigr).

By the definition of
\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}
and

\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}
, it is clear that all coordinates corresponding to lower
amalgamating types correctly match, so the above is equivalent to

\displaystyle G\Bigl(x,\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}\bigl(x,(\bm{y},\bm{z})\bigr),\bm{z}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\sim\Theta\bigl(f(x)\bigr).

Recalling that
\bm{z}
is a dummy variable for
\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}
(more specifically,
using (
9.3
)), we note that
\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}\bigl(x,(\bm{y},\bm{z})\bigr)
is independent from
\bm{z}
, so their joint distribution is precisely

\gamma_{a}(x)\otimes\mu
, so the above is equivalent to

\displaystyle G(x,\mathord{-},\mathord{-})_{*}(\gamma_{a}(x)\otimes\mu)

\displaystyle=\Theta\bigl(f(x)\bigr),

which is precisely item
(viii)
of Lemma
8.3
.

Finally, we consider the case when
\lvert A\rvert>\ell
and
A
is not
[a]
for some

a\in\mathbb{N}
. We fix a bijection
\alpha_{A}\colon[\lvert A\rvert]\to A
and define

\displaystyle\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(\alpha_{A}^{-1})^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{\lvert A\rvert,V}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{A}^{*},

(9.5)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{q}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(\alpha_{A}^{-1})^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\mathbbm{q}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{\lvert A\rvert,V}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{A}^{*},

\displaystyle\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(\alpha_{A}^{-1})^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{\lvert A\rvert,V}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{A}^{*}.

It will be convenient to let
\alpha_{[a]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\id_{[a]}
for every
a\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
so that the
equalities in the above remain true even in the case
A=[a]
, in which we define the partial
functions above directly. It is also clear that the equalities above also hold when
\lvert A\rvert\leq\ell
.

It is straightforward to check that the properties required by the construction for
A
follow as
a consequence of the properties for
[\lvert A\rvert]
, except for the two joint properties of
items
(e)
and
(vi)
that are being deferred.

Note also that once we show that item
(e)
holds in
Lemma
9.4
, a direct consequence is that the definition above does not
depend on the choice of
\alpha_{A}
, except for a zero-measure set.

Remark 9.3.

A direct consequence of the item
(a)
of Definition
9.2
is that if
{\mathcal{N}}

is an
(\ell-1)
-independent
d
-Euclidean structure over
\Omega
satisfying
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
,
then for the associated structure
\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}
over
\Omega^{2}
given
by (
9.2
), whenever
\lvert A\rvert\leq\ell
, there exists a unique amalgamating

(A,V,\ell)
