-type. In turn, this uniqueness was obtained as a consequence of uniqueness of
overlapping
(A,V)
-types given by Proposition
5.8
.

Let us now prove the deferred property
(e)
.

Lemma 9.4.

Within Definition
9.2
, property
(e)
holds, that is, for every
injection
\beta\colon A^{\prime}\to A
between finite sets
A^{\prime}
and
A
, both disjoint from a countably
infinite set
V
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct,\ell}(\Omega^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}}_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}_{A^{\prime}}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}_{A^{\prime},V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}_{A^{\prime},V}^{(d),\dsct,\ell}(\Omega^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}}_{A^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\amlg,\ell}_{A,V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\amlg,\ell}_{A,V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\dsct}_{A,V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\amlg,\ell}_{A^{\prime},V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\amlg,\ell}_{A^{\prime},V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\dsct}_{A^{\prime},V}}

is weakly commutative.

Proof.

To completely prove the lemma, it suffices to show all weakly commutative properties involving
only the first row and to show weak equivariance of
\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}
,

\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}
,
M^{\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}
,
\pi^{\amlg,\ell}
,
M^{\amlg,\ell}
and

M^{\dsct}
.

Equivariance of
\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}
,
M^{\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}
and
M^{\dsct}
was shown in
Lemma
5.7
and equivariance of
\pi^{\amlg,\ell}
and
M^{\amlg,\ell}
was observed
in (
9.1
). The final weak equivariance of
\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}

will be the last thing we prove.

Let us prove the weakly commutative properties involving the first row. The equalities

\displaystyle M^{\dsct}_{A,V}\mathbin{\circ}\pi^{\amlg,\ell}_{A,V}

\displaystyle=M^{\amlg,\ell}_{A,V},

\displaystyle M^{\dsct}_{A,V}\mathbin{\circ}\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}

\displaystyle=M^{\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A},

follow straightforwardly from definitions (the latter already appeared in
Lemma
5.7
).

We now show

\displaystyle\pi^{\amlg,\ell}_{A,V}\mathbin{\circ}\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}

within the domain of the left-hand side.

When
\lvert A\rvert\leq\ell
, then by definition both sides evaluate to the unique dissociated

(A,V)
-type
q_{A}
within the domain of the left-hand side.

When
\lvert A\rvert>\ell
, for
(x,(y,z))\in\dom(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V})
where

x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
,
y\in\Omega\times{\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{d}
and
z\in\Omega
, we have

\displaystyle\pi^{\amlg,\ell}_{A,V}\bigl(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(x,(y,z))\bigr)

\displaystyle=\pi^{\amlg,\ell}_{A,V}\biggl((\alpha_{A}^{-1})^{*}\Bigl(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{\lvert A\rvert,V}\bigl(\alpha_{A}^{*}(x,(y,z))\bigr)\Bigr)\biggr)

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{A}^{-1})^{*}\Biggl(\pi^{\amlg,\ell}_{\lvert A\rvert,V}\biggl(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{\lvert A\rvert,V}\Bigl(\alpha_{A}^{*}(x),\bigl(\alpha_{A}^{*}(y),z\bigr)\Bigr)\biggr)\Biggr)

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{A}^{-1})^{*}\Biggl(\pi\Biggl(G\biggl(x,\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{\lvert A\rvert,V}\Bigl(\alpha_{A}^{*}(x),\bigl(\alpha_{A}^{*}(y),z\bigr)\Bigr),z\biggr)\Biggr)\Biggr)

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{A}^{-1})^{*}\Biggl(g\biggl(x,\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{\lvert A\rvert,V}\Bigl(\alpha_{A}^{*}(x),\bigl(\alpha_{A}^{*}(y),z\bigr)\Bigr)\biggr)\Biggr)

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{A}^{-1})^{*}\Biggl(\pi^{\ovlp}_{\lvert A\rvert,V}\biggl(\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{\lvert A\rvert,V}\Bigl(\alpha_{A}^{*}(x),\bigl(\alpha_{A}^{*}(y),z\bigr)\Bigr)\biggr)\Biggr)

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{A}^{-1})^{*}\biggl(\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{\lvert A\rvert,V}\Bigl(\alpha_{A}^{*}(x),\bigl(\alpha_{A}^{*}(y),z\bigr)\Bigr)\biggr)

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}\bigl(x,(y,z)\bigr),

where the second equality follows from the already shown equivariance of
\pi^{\amlg,\ell}
, in
the third equality, the undecorated
\pi
is the projection
\pi\colon V\times W\to V
of
Lemma
8.3
(i.e.,
\pi\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{\lvert A\rvert,V}^{(d),\dsct}\times[0,1]\to{\mathcal{S}}_{\lvert A\rvert,V}^{(d),\dsct}
), the fourth equality
follows from Lemma
8.3
(v)
, the fifth equality follows from the
definition of
g
in (
9.4
), the sixth equality follows from Lemma
5.7

and the final equality follows from the already shown equivariance of
\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}

(also shown in Lemma
5.7
).

To conclude the weak commutativity of the first row, it remains to show

\displaystyle M^{\amlg,\ell}_{A,V}\mathbin{\circ}\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}

\displaystyle=M^{\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}

within the domain of the left-hand side. This follows from the other already shown weak
commutativities of the first row:

\displaystyle M^{\amlg,\ell}_{A,V}\mathbin{\circ}\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}

\displaystyle=M^{\dsct,\ell}_{A,V}\mathbin{\circ}\pi^{\amlg,\ell}_{A,V}\mathbin{\circ}\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}=M^{\dsct,\ell}_{A,V}\mathbin{\circ}\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}=M^{\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}.

It remains to show weak equivariance of
\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}
, that is, we need to show that
if
\beta\colon A^{\prime}\to A
is an injection, then

\displaystyle\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V}\mathbin{\circ}\beta^{*}

\displaystyle=\beta^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}

within the common domain of the two sides.

Let
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A^{\prime}}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
be a point in the common domain of the two sides.

We first consider the case when
A^{\prime}
and
A
have the same cardinality, say
a
, and we prove the
weak equivariance by induction in
a
.

If
a\leq\ell
(and assuming inductively that weak equivariance holds for smaller sizes), we note
that

\displaystyle(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V}\mathbin{\circ}\beta^{*})(x)

\displaystyle=\Biggl(\biggl(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{B,V}\Bigl(\iota_{B,A^{\prime}}^{*}\bigl(\beta^{*}(x)\bigr)\Bigr)\mathrel{\bigg|}B\in r(A^{\prime},a-1)\biggr),q_{A^{\prime}}\Biggr)

\displaystyle=\Biggl(\biggl(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{B,V}\Bigl(\beta\mathord{\downarrow}_{B}^{*}\bigl(\iota_{\beta(B),A}^{*}(x)\bigr)\Bigr)\mathrel{\bigg|}B\in r(A^{\prime},a-1)\biggr),q_{A^{\prime}}\Biggr)

\displaystyle=\Biggl(\biggl(\beta\mathord{\downarrow}_{B}^{*}\Bigl(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{\beta(B),V}\bigl(\iota_{\beta(B),A}^{*}(x)\bigr)\Bigr)\mathrel{\bigg|}B\in r(A^{\prime},a-1)\biggr),\beta^{*}(q_{A})\Biggr)

\displaystyle=\beta^{*}\biggl(\Bigl(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{B,V}\bigl(\iota_{B,A}^{*}(x)\bigr)\mathrel{\Big|}B\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)\Bigr),q_{A}\biggr)

\displaystyle=\beta^{*}\bigl(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(x)\bigr),

where the second equality follows from contra-variance and the fact that

\beta\mathbin{\circ}\iota_{B,A^{\prime}}=\iota_{\beta(B),A}\mathbin{\circ}\beta\mathord{\downarrow}_{B}
and the third equality follows from
inductive hypothesis along with
\beta^{*}(q_{A})=q_{A^{\prime}}
(as
q_{A}
and
q_{A^{\prime}}
are the unique up to zero measure
overlapping types of
\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}
over their respective sets).

Suppose now that
a>\ell
and consider first the case
A=A^{\prime}=[a]
. Then a similar derivation
holds using
S_{a}
-equivariance of
G
instead: writing
x=(w,(y,z))
, where

w\in{\mathcal{E}}_{a,a-1}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
,
y\in\Omega\times{\mathcal{O}}_{a}^{d}
and
z\in\Omega
, we have

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V}\mathbin{\circ}\beta^{*})(x)

\displaystyle=\Biggl(\biggl(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{B,V}\Bigl(\iota_{B,a}^{*}\bigl(\beta^{*}(x)\bigr)\Bigr)\mathrel{\bigg|}B\in r(a,a-1)\biggr),G\Bigl(\beta^{*}(x),\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}\bigl(\beta^{*}(w),(\beta^{*}(y),z)\bigr),z\Bigr)\Biggr)

\displaystyle=\Biggl(\biggl(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{B,V}\Bigl(\beta\mathord{\downarrow}_{B}^{*}\bigl(\iota_{\beta(B),a}^{*}(x)\bigr)\Bigr)\mathrel{\bigg|}B\in r(a,a-1)\biggr),\beta^{*}\biggl(G\Bigl(x,\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}\bigl(w,(y,z)\bigr),z\Bigr)\biggr)\Biggr)

\displaystyle=\Biggl(\beta^{*}\biggl(\Bigl(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{B,V}\bigl(\iota_{\beta(B),a}^{*}(x)\bigr)\Bigr)\biggr)\mathrel{\bigg|}B\in r(a^{\prime},a-1)\biggr),\beta^{*}\biggl(G\Bigl(x,\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}\bigl(w,(y,z)\bigr),z\Bigr)\biggr)\Biggr)

\displaystyle=(\beta^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V})(x),

where the second equality follows from
S_{a}
-equivariance of
G
and
\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}

(from Lemma
5.7
) and the fact that

\beta\mathbin{\circ}\iota_{B,a}=\iota_{\beta(B),a}\mathbin{\circ}\beta\mathord{\downarrow}_{B}
and the third equality follows from
inductive hypothesis.

We now consider the case when
\lvert A\rvert=\lvert A^{\prime}\rvert>\ell
(but are not necessarily
of the form
[a]
). Letting
a\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\lvert A\rvert
and

\gamma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\alpha_{A}^{-1}\mathbin{\circ}\beta\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{A^{\prime}}\in S_{a}
, we have

\displaystyle(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V}\mathbin{\circ}\beta^{*})(x)

\displaystyle=\bigl((\alpha_{A^{\prime}}^{-1})^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\mathbbm{p}_{a,V}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{A^{\prime}}^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\beta^{*}\bigr)(x)=\bigl((\alpha_{A^{\prime}}^{-1})^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\mathbbm{p}_{a,V}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}\mathbin{\circ}\gamma^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{A}^{*}\bigr)(x)

\displaystyle=\bigl((\alpha_{A^{\prime}}^{-1})^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\gamma^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\mathbbm{p}_{a,V}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{A}^{*}\bigr)(x)=\bigl(\beta^{*}\mathbin{\circ}(\alpha_{A}^{-1})^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\mathbbm{p}_{a,V}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{A}^{*}\bigr)(x)

\displaystyle=(\beta^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V})(x),

where the third equality follows from the previous case (for
[a]
).

This concludes all cases in which
\lvert A^{\prime}\rvert=\lvert A\rvert
(i.e., cases in which
\beta

is a bijection).

We now prove the case when
A^{\prime}=[a^{\prime}]
and
A=[a]
for some
a^{\prime},a\in\mathbb{N}
with
a^{\prime}<a
. We need to
show that for every
C\in r(a^{\prime})
we have

\displaystyle(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V}\mathbin{\circ}\beta^{*})(x)_{C}

\displaystyle=(\beta^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V})(x)_{C}.

If
\lvert C\rvert<a^{\prime}
, then we have

\displaystyle(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V}\mathbin{\circ}\beta^{*})(x)_{C}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{C,V}\Bigl(\iota_{C,[a^{\prime}]}^{*}\bigl(\beta^{*}(x)\bigr)\Bigr)=\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{C,V}\Bigl(\beta\mathord{\downarrow}_{C}^{*}\bigl(\iota_{\beta(C),[a]}^{*}(x)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\beta\mathord{\downarrow}_{C}^{*}\Bigl(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{\beta(C),V}\bigl(\iota_{\beta(C),[a]}^{*}(x)\bigr)\Bigr)=\beta\mathord{\downarrow}_{C}^{*}\bigl(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}(x)_{\beta(C)}\bigr)=\beta^{*}\bigl(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}(x)\bigr)_{C},

where the third equality follows from the already proved case as
\beta\mathord{\downarrow}_{C}
is bijective.

If
C=[a^{\prime}]
, then we have

\displaystyle(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V}\mathbin{\circ}\beta^{*})(x)_{C}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a^{\prime},V}\bigl(\beta^{*}(x)\bigr)_{[a^{\prime}]}=\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a^{\prime},V}\Bigl(\beta\mathord{\downarrow}_{[a^{\prime}]}^{*}\bigl(\iota_{\beta([a^{\prime}]),[a]}^{*}(x)\bigr)\Bigr)_{[a^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\beta\mathord{\downarrow}_{[a^{\prime}]}^{*}\Bigl(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{\beta([a^{\prime}]),V}\bigl(\iota_{\beta([a^{\prime}]),[a]}^{*}(x)\bigr)\Bigr)_{[a^{\prime}]}=\beta\mathord{\downarrow}_{[a^{\prime}]}^{*}\bigl(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}(x)_{\beta([a^{\prime}])}\bigr)_{[a^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\beta\mathord{\downarrow}_{[a^{\prime}]}^{*}\Bigl(\bigl(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}(x)_{\beta([a^{\prime}])}\bigr)_{\beta([a^{\prime}])}\Bigr)=\beta^{*}\bigl(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(x)\bigr)_{C},

where the third equality follows from the already proved case as
\beta\mathord{\downarrow}_{[a^{\prime}]}
is
bijective, the fifth equality follows from the contra-variant definition of

\beta\mathord{\downarrow}_{[a^{\prime}]}^{*}
and the sixth equality follows from the contra-variant definition of

\beta^{*}
.

It remains to show the case when
A^{\prime}
and
A
are such that
\lvert A^{\prime}\rvert<\lvert A\rvert
and
are not necessarily of the form
[a^{\prime}]
and
[a]
, respectively. Let
a^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\lvert A^{\prime}\rvert
and

a\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\lvert A\rvert
, consider the injection

\gamma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\alpha_{A}^{-1}\mathbin{\circ}\beta\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{A^{\prime}}\colon[a^{\prime}]\to[a]
and note that

\displaystyle(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V}\mathbin{\circ}\beta^{*})(x)

\displaystyle=\bigl((\alpha_{A^{\prime}}^{-1})^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a^{\prime},V}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{A^{\prime}}^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\beta^{*}\bigr)(x)=\bigl((\alpha_{A^{\prime}}^{-1})^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a^{\prime},V}\mathbin{\circ}\gamma^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{A}^{*}\bigr)(x)

\displaystyle=\bigl((\alpha_{A^{\prime}}^{-1})^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\gamma^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a^{\prime},V}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{A}^{*}\bigr)(x)=\bigl(\beta^{*}\mathbin{\circ}(\alpha_{A}^{-1})^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a^{\prime},V}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{A}^{*}\bigr)(x)

\displaystyle=\beta^{*}\bigl(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(x)\bigr),

where the third equality follows from the already proved case as
\gamma\colon[a^{\prime}]\to[a]
.

This concludes weak equivariance of
\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}
.
∎

The next lemma proves the weak amalgamation property (item
(vi)
of
Definition
9.2
), completing Definition
9.2
, and proves the strong amalgamation
that we have been promising all along.

Lemma 9.5.

Let
{\mathcal{N}}
be an
(\ell-1)
-independent
d
-Euclidean structure in
{\mathcal{L}}
over
\Omega
satisfying

\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
, let
V
be a countably infinite set and let
A
be a finite set disjoint from

V
. Let also
\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}
be the
d
-Euclidean structure in
{\mathcal{L}}
over
\Omega^{2}
as in
Definition
9.2
, i.e.,
\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}
is given by

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}_{P}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{(x,x^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k(P)}^{(d)}(\Omega)\times{\mathcal{E}}_{k(P)}(\Omega)\mid x\in{\mathcal{N}}_{P}\}\qquad(P\in{\mathcal{L}}).

Finally, let
k\in\{0,\ldots,\lvert A\rvert-1\}
.

Then the following hold.

i.

Weak amalgamation: if
\bm{x}
is sampled in

{\mathcal{E}}_{A,k}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
according to
\mu^{(d)}
, then

\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(\bm{x})
is conditionally independent from
\tau_{A,k}(\bm{x})

given

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V}(\iota_{A^{\prime},A}(\bm{x}))\mathrel{\big|}A^{\prime}\in r(A,k)\bigr),

where
\tau_{A,k}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})\to{\mathcal{E}}_{A,k}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
is the natural
projection.

ii.

Amalgamating types are well-defined:

\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}
,
\mathbbm{q}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}
and

\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}
are well-defined.

iii.

Strong amalgamation: if
\bm{x}
is sampled in

{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
according to
\mu^{(d)}
, then

\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(\bm{x})
is conditionally independent from

\tau_{A,k}(\bm{x})
given

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V}(\iota_{A^{\prime},A}(\bm{x}))\mathrel{\big|}A^{\prime}\in r(A,k)\bigr),

where
\tau_{A,k}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})\to{\mathcal{E}}_{A,k}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
is the
natural projection.

Proof.

We will prove all assertions by induction on
\lvert A\rvert
and the logic of the inductive step
is:

A.

Item
(i)
for a set
A
and
k=\lvert A\rvert-1
implies item
(ii)
for
A
.

B.

Item
(ii)
for all sets of size at most

\lvert A\rvert
implies item
(iii)
for
A
.

C.

Item
(iii)
for all sets of size at most
\lvert A\rvert-1
implies item
(i)
for
A
(and every
k<\lvert A\rvert
).

Item
(A)
is trivial as item
(i)
when
k=\lvert A\rvert-1

is exactly item
(vi)
of Definition
9.2
.

For item
(B)
, we consider three random variables
\bm{p}
,
\bm{q}
and

\bm{r}
in
\prod_{A^{\prime}\in r(A)}{\mathcal{S}}_{A^{\prime},V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})
.

For
\bm{p}
, we simply let

\displaystyle\bm{p}_{A^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V}\bigl(\iota_{A^{\prime},A}^{*}(\bm{x})\bigr)\qquad(A^{\prime}\in r(A)).

For
\bm{q}
, we inductively define

\displaystyle\bm{q}_{A^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V}\bigl(\bm{q}_{C}\mid C\in r(A^{\prime},\lvert A^{\prime}\rvert-1)\bigr)\qquad(A^{\prime}\in r(A)).

An inductive application of items
(d)
and
(e)
of
Definition
9.2
shows that
\bm{p}
and
\bm{q}
are equidistributed.

Finally, for
\bm{r}
, we inductively define

\displaystyle\bm{r}_{A^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\begin{cases}\bm{p}_{A^{\prime}},&if $\lvert A^{\prime}\rvert\leq k$,\\
\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V}\bigl((\bm{r}_{C}\mid C\in r(A^{\prime},\lvert A^{\prime}\rvert-1)\bigr),&if $\lvert A^{\prime}\rvert>k$,\end{cases}\qquad(A^{\prime}\in r(A)).

Since
\bm{p}
and
\bm{q}
are equidistributed, it follows that
\bm{r}
also has the same
distribution as
\bm{p}
and
\bm{q}
.

Since by construction we have
(\bm{r}_{A^{\prime}}\mid A^{\prime}\in r(A,k))=(\bm{p}_{A^{\prime}}\mid A^{\prime}\in r(A,k))
, it
follows that the
\bm{p}
and
\bm{r}
have the same conditional distribution given

(\bm{p}_{A^{\prime}}\mid A^{\prime}\in r(A,k))
. On the other hand, by construction of
\bm{r}
, it is obvious
that the conditional distribution of
\bm{r}
given
(\bm{p}_{A^{\prime}}\mid A^{\prime}\in r(A,k))
is
(conditionally) independent from
(\bm{x}_{A^{\prime}}\mid A^{\prime}\in r(A,k))
, concluding the proof of
item
(B)
.

It remains to prove item
(C)
. We prove item
(i)
by
induction in
k<\lvert A\rvert
.

Let
\bm{z}
be picked at random in
\prod_{C\in\binom{A}{>k}}(X\times X\times{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d})
according
to
\bigotimes_{C\in\binom{A}{>k}}(\mu\otimes\mu\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{d}\nu_{C})
and for each

y\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,k}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
, let

\displaystyle\bm{p}^{y}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(y,\bm{z})

and note that it suffices to show that there exists a
\mu^{(d)}
-measure
1
subset
G
of

{\mathcal{E}}_{A,k}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
such that if
y,y^{\prime}\in G
are such that

\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V}(\iota_{A^{\prime},A}(y))=\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V}(\iota_{A^{\prime},A}(y^{\prime}))
for every
A^{\prime}\in r(A,k)
, then
\bm{p}^{y}

and
\bm{p}^{y^{\prime}}
are equidistributed. By our inductive hypothesis in
k
, we may further suppose
that
y_{A^{\prime}}=y^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}}
for every
A^{\prime}\in r(A,k-1)
.

We now introduce new random variables
\bm{r}^{y}
in
{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}(\Omega^{2})
for each

y\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,k}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
: by Proposition
7.4
, there exists a
Markov kernel

\displaystyle\rho\colon\prod_{A^{\prime}\in\binom{A}{k}}{\mathcal{S}}_{A^{\prime},V}^{(d),\ovlp}(\Omega^{2})\to{\mathcal{P}}\bigl({\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\dsct}(\Omega^{2})\bigr)

that allows us to correctly sample the conditional distribution of the dissociated
(A,V)
-type in

\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}
given the overlapping
(A^{\prime},V)
-types for every
A^{\prime}\in\binom{A}{k}
. For each

A^{\prime}\in\binom{A}{k}
, we let

\tau_{A^{\prime}}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{A^{\prime}}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})\to{\mathcal{E}}_{A^{\prime},k-1}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
be the natural projection
and

\displaystyle q^{y}_{A^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbbm{q}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V}\Bigl(\tau_{A^{\prime}}(y),\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V}\bigr(\iota_{A^{\prime},A}^{*}(y)\bigr)\Bigr)

be the overlapping
(A^{\prime},V)
-type recovered via
\mathbbm{q}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V}
from the
amalgamating
(A^{\prime},V,\ell)
-type of
\iota_{A^{\prime},A}^{*}(y)
along with the pointwise information

\tau_{A^{\prime}}(y)
of coordinates indexed by sets in
r(A^{\prime},k-1)
and finally let
\bm{r}^{y}
be sampled
according to
\rho((q^{y}_{A^{\prime}}\mid A^{\prime}\in\binom{A}{k}))
.

By item
(c)
in Definition
9.2
, we know that

\displaystyle q^{y}_{A^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{p}^{\ovlp,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V}(\iota_{A^{\prime},A}^{*}(y))

whenever
\iota_{A^{\prime},A}^{*}(y)\in\dom(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V})
. In turn, since our

\rho
was provided by Proposition
7.4
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\bm{r}^{y}

\displaystyle\sim\mathbbm{p}^{\dsct,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(y,\bm{z})=\bm{p}^{y}

(9.6)

for
\mu^{(d)}
-almost every
y
in the set

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\left\{y\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,k}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})\;\middle|\;\forall A^{\prime}\in\binom{A}{k},\iota_{A^{\prime},A}^{*}(y)\in\dom(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V})\right\}.

Clearly
\mu^{(d)}(G)=1
, so if
\widetilde{G}
is the set of all
y\in G
such that (
9.6
)
holds, then
\mu^{(d)}(\widetilde{G})=1
.

Now let
y,y^{\prime}\in\widetilde{G}
be such that

\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V}(\iota_{A^{\prime},A}(y))=\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V}(\iota_{A^{\prime},A}(y^{\prime}))
for every
A^{\prime}\in r(A,k)
and
y_{C}=y^{\prime}_{C}

for every
C\in r(A,k-1)
. The latter condition implies that for every
A^{\prime}\in\binom{A}{k}
, we get

\tau_{A^{\prime}}(y)=\tau_{A^{\prime}}(y^{\prime})
, so we get
q^{y}_{A^{\prime}}=q^{y^{\prime}}_{A^{\prime}}
, which in turn implies

\displaystyle\bm{p}^{y}

\displaystyle\sim\bm{r}^{y}=\bm{r}^{y^{\prime}}\sim\bm{p}^{y^{\prime}},

as desired.
∎

Before we proceed, let us show equivariance of the definition of

\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}
with respect to bijections.

Lemma 9.6.

Within Definition
9.2
, if
\beta\colon A^{\prime}\to A
is a bijection between finite sets
A^{\prime}

and
A
, both disjoint from a countably infinite set
V
, then the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{B\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{B\in r(A^{\prime},\lvert A^{\prime}\rvert-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{S}}_{A^{\prime},V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V}}

is weakly commutative.

Proof.

Let
a\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\lvert A\rvert=\lvert A^{\prime}\rvert
and
\gamma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\alpha_{A}^{-1}\mathbin{\circ}\beta\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{A^{\prime}}\in S_{a}
and note that

\displaystyle\beta^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}

\displaystyle=\beta^{*}\mathbin{\circ}(\alpha_{A}^{-1})^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{A}^{*}=(\alpha_{A^{\prime}}^{-1})^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\gamma^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{A}^{*}

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{A^{\prime}}^{-1})^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}\mathbin{\circ}\gamma^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{A}^{*}=(\alpha_{A^{\prime}}^{-1})^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{A^{\prime}}^{*}\mathbin{\circ}\beta^{*}

\displaystyle=\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A^{\prime},V}\mathbin{\circ}\beta^{*},

where the third equality follows since
\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}
is
S_{a}
-equivariant.
∎

We can finally prove the implication
(i)
\implies
(iii)

of Theorem
2.2
.

Proposition 9.7
(Theorem
2.2
(i)
\implies
(iii)
)
.

If
\phi\in\operatorname{Hom}^{+}({\mathcal{A}}[T],\mathbb{R})
satisfies
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
, then there exists an
\ell
-independent

1
-Euclidean structure
{\mathcal{N}}
such that
\phi=\phi_{\mathcal{N}}
.

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{\Omega}=([0,1],\lambda)
, where
\lambda
is the Lebesgue measure. Since

\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
implies
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell-1]
and since every
d
-Euclidean structure is

0
-independent, it suffices to show that if
{\mathcal{N}}
is an
(\ell-1)
-independent
d
-Euclidean
structure over some
\Omega=(X,\lambda)
, then there exists an
\ell
-independent
1
-Euclidean
structure
{\mathcal{H}}
over
\widetilde{\Omega}
such that
\phi_{\mathcal{N}}=\phi_{\mathcal{H}}
(in fact, it would even
suffice to show only the case
d=1
and
\Omega=\widetilde{\Omega}
, but the general
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
arbitrary
\Omega
case is not any harder and the different notation will help keep track of which
points are in which spaces).

Let
\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}
be the
d
-Euclidean structure in
{\mathcal{L}}
over
\Omega^{2}
as in
Definition
9.2
:

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}_{P}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{(x,x^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k(P)}^{(d)}(\Omega)\times{\mathcal{E}}_{k(P)}(\Omega)\mid x\in{\mathcal{N}}_{P}\}\qquad(P\in{\mathcal{L}}).

We will first provide an alternative way of sampling amalgamating
(A,V,\ell)
-types of

\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}
for every finite
A\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{+}
using i.i.d. variables distributed uniformly
in
[0,1]
(note that by equivariance of Lemma
9.4
, this also provides a way of
sampling the amalgamating
(A,V,\ell)
-types even when
A
is not a subset of
\mathbb{N}_{+}
). Throughout
this proof, we always assume
V
is a countably infinite set disjoint from
\mathbb{N}_{+}
(which ensures
it is disjoint from any
A
we consider) and we also follow the notation of
Definition
9.2
.

First, since all spaces of amalgamating types are Borel spaces (see
Proposition
6.6
(ii)
), for each
a\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, we can associate to
each Markov kernel

\displaystyle\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}\colon\prod_{B\in r(a,a-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{S}}_{a,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2}))

of Definition
9.2
a measurable partial function

\displaystyle F_{a,V}\colon\prod_{B\in r(a,a-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})\times[0,1]

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{S}}_{a,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})

such that

\displaystyle F_{a,V}(p,\mathord{-})_{*}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}(p)

(9.7)

for every
p\in\prod_{B\in r(a,a-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})
, that is,

\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}(p)
is the distribution of
F_{a,V}(p,\bm{t})
where

\bm{t}
is sampled uniformly at random in
[0,1]
.

Item
(d)
of Definition
9.2
says that when
a\leq\ell
, then

\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}
is the constant function that maps all points to the
distribution concentrated on the unique point
q_{a}
in the image of
\mathbbm{p}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}_{a,V}
,
so when
a\leq\ell
, we can further require that
F_{a,V}
is the constant function that maps
every point to
q_{a}
.

It is worth noting that even though
\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{a,V}
is

S_{a}
-equivariant, we cannot require
F_{a,V}
to be
S_{a}
-equivariant (as there are

S_{a}
-invariant distributions that cannot be generated by
S_{a}
-equivariant functions; e.g., the
quasirandom tournament, see Example
1.4
).

For
A\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{+}
finite non-empty that is not of the form
[a]
for some
a\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, we also
define

\displaystyle F_{A,V}\colon\prod_{B\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})\times[0,1]

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})

in a similar fashion to (
9.5
):

\displaystyle F_{A,V}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(\alpha_{A}^{-1})^{*}\mathbin{\circ}F_{\lvert A\rvert,V}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{A}^{*},

(9.8)

where
\alpha_{A}\colon[\lvert A\rvert]\to A
is the same bijection used in (
9.5
) in
Definition
9.2
and we declare the rightmost
\alpha_{A}^{*}
in the above to act trivially
on the coordinate in
[0,1]
. Note also that since we set
\alpha_{[a]}=\id_{a}
, it follows
that (
9.8
) for every
A\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{+}
regardless of whether it is of the form
[a]
or
not.

Note that Lemma
9.6
along with (
9.7
) implies that

\displaystyle F_{A,V}(p,\mathord{-})_{*}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(p)

(9.9)

for every
p\in\prod_{B\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})
.

Note also that equivariance of
\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}
(Lemma
9.4
) implies that

F_{A,V}
also has the property that if
\lvert A\rvert\leq\ell
, then
F_{A,V}
is the constant
function that maps every point to the unique point in the image of

\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}
.

Again,
F_{A,V}
is not necessarily
S_{A}
-equivariant. To address this, we will use order
variables to define a partial function

\displaystyle G_{A,V}\colon\prod_{B\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})\times[0,1]\times{\mathcal{O}}_{A}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})

by

\displaystyle G_{A,V}(p,t,{\lhd})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(\sigma_{\lhd}^{-1})^{*}\Bigl(F_{A,V}\bigl(\sigma_{\lhd}^{*}(p,t)\bigr)\Bigr)\qquad\left(p\in\prod_{B\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2}),t\in[0,1],{\lhd}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{A}\right),

where
\sigma_{\lhd}\in S_{A}
is the unique permutation such that

\displaystyle(\sigma_{\lhd}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{A})^{*}({\lhd})

\displaystyle={\leq_{\lvert A\rvert}},

where
\leq_{\lvert A\rvert}
is the usual order on
[\lvert A\rvert]
(recalling that

\alpha_{A}\colon[\lvert A\rvert]\to A
is the bijection used in (
9.5
) in
Definition
9.2
). Again, Lemma
9.4
implies that
G_{A,V}
also has the
property that if
\lvert A\rvert\leq\ell
, then
G_{A,V}
is the constant function that maps every
point to the unique point in the image of
\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}
.

The next claim gives a form of equivariance of
\sigma_{\lhd}
.

Claim 9.7.1.

If
\beta\colon A\to B
is a bijection and
{\lhd}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
, then

\displaystyle\sigma_{\beta^{*}({\lhd})}

\displaystyle=\beta^{-1}\mathbin{\circ}\sigma_{\lhd}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{B}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{A}^{-1}.

Proof of Claim
9.7.1
.

Letting
a\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\lvert A\rvert=\lvert B\rvert
, the claim follows from:

\displaystyle(\beta^{-1}\mathbin{\circ}\sigma_{\lhd}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{B}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{A}^{-1}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{A})^{*}\bigl(\beta^{*}({\lhd})\bigr)

\displaystyle=(\sigma_{\lhd}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{B})^{*}({\lhd})={\leq_{a}}.\qed

The next claim says that the partial functions
G_{A,V}
are equivariant under bijections.

Claim 9.7.2.

If
\beta\colon A\to B
is a bijection, then the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{C\in r(B,\lvert B\rvert-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{C,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})\times{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{C\in r(B,\lvert B\rvert-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{C,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})\times{\mathcal{O}}_{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{B,V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{B,V}}

is weakly commutative.

Proof of Claim
9.7.2
.

Let
a\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\lvert A\rvert=\lvert B\rvert
and let

(p,t,{\lhd})\in\dom(G_{A,V}\mathbin{\circ}\beta^{*})\cap\dom(G_{B,V})
and note that

\displaystyle(G_{A,V}\mathbin{\circ}\beta^{*})(p,t,{\lhd})

\displaystyle=(\sigma^{-1}_{\beta^{*}({\lhd})})^{*}\biggl(F_{A,V}\Bigl(\sigma_{\beta^{*}({\lhd})}\bigl(\beta^{*}(p,t)\bigr)\Bigr)\biggr)

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{A}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{B}^{-1}\mathbin{\circ}\sigma_{\lhd}^{-1}\mathbin{\circ}\beta)^{*}\biggl(F_{A,V}\Bigl((\beta^{-1}\mathbin{\circ}\sigma_{\lhd}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{B}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{A}^{-1})^{*}\bigl(\beta^{*}(p,t)\bigr)\Bigr)\biggr)

\displaystyle=\beta^{*}\biggl((\alpha_{A}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{B}^{-1}\mathbin{\circ}\sigma_{\lhd}^{-1})^{*}\Bigl(F_{A,V}\bigl((\sigma_{\lhd}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{B}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{A}^{-1})^{*}(p,t)\bigr)\Bigr)\biggr)

\displaystyle=\beta^{*}\biggl((\alpha_{B}^{-1}\mathbin{\circ}\sigma_{\lhd}^{-1})^{*}\Bigl(F_{a,V}\bigl((\sigma_{\lhd}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{B})^{*}(p,t)\bigr)\Bigr)\biggr)

\displaystyle=\beta^{*}\biggl((\sigma_{\lhd}^{-1})^{*}\Bigl(F_{B,V}\bigl(\sigma_{\lhd}^{*}(p,t)\bigr)\Bigr)\biggr)

\displaystyle=(\beta^{*}\mathbin{\circ}G_{B,V})(p,t,{\lhd}),

where the second equality follows from Claim
9.7.1
and the fourth and fifth
equalities follow from (
9.8
).
∎

We proceed to show that the partial function
G_{A,V}
can be used to correctly sample the
conditional distribution of the amalgamating
(A,V,\ell)
-types given the lower amalgamating
types.

Claim 9.7.3.

For
\mu^{(d)}
-almost every
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,\lvert A\rvert-1}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
and every

{\lhd}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
, we have

\displaystyle G_{A,V}(p_{x},\mathord{-},{\lhd})_{*}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(p_{x}),

where
p_{x}\in\prod_{B\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})
is given by

\displaystyle(p_{x})_{B}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}\bigl(\iota_{B,A}^{*}(x)\bigr)\qquad(B\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)).

In particular, we have

\displaystyle G_{A,V}(p_{x},\mathord{-})_{*}(\lambda\otimes\nu_{A})

\displaystyle=\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(p_{x})

for
\mu^{(d)}
-almost every
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,\lvert A\rvert-1}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
.

Proof of Claim
9.7.3
.

It is clear that the second assertion follows from the first as
\nu_{A}
is the uniform measure
on
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
.

In turn, to prove the first assertion, it suffices to show that if
\bm{x}
is sampled in

{\mathcal{E}}_{A,\lvert A\rvert-1}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
according to
\mu^{(d)}
, then

\displaystyle G_{A,V}(p_{\bm{x}},\mathord{-},{\lhd})_{*}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(p_{\bm{x}})

with probability
1
.

By item
(d)
in Definition
9.2
, we know that if we sample
\bm{y}
in

X^{2}\times{\mathcal{O}}_{A}^{d}
according to
\mu^{2}\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{d}\nu_{A}
independently from

\bm{x}
, then
\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(p_{\bm{x}})
is the conditional
distribution of
\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(\bm{x},\bm{y})
given
\bm{x}
.

By Lemma
9.4
, we also have

\displaystyle\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(\bm{x},\bm{y})

\displaystyle=(\sigma_{\lhd}^{-1})^{*}\Bigl(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}\bigl(\sigma_{\lhd}^{*}(\bm{x},\bm{y})\bigr)\Bigr).

By considering conditional distributions given
\bm{x}
of the above and applying
item
(d)
in Definition
9.2
again, we get

\displaystyle\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(p_{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=(\sigma_{\lhd}^{-1})^{*}\bigl(\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(p_{\sigma_{\lhd}^{*}(\bm{x})})\bigr).

In turn, applying (
9.9
) to the right-hand side of the above, we get

\displaystyle\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(p_{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=(\sigma_{\lhd}^{-1})^{*}\Bigl(F_{A,V}(p_{\sigma_{\lhd}^{*}(\bm{x})},\mathord{-})_{*}(\lambda)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=((\sigma_{\lhd}^{-1})^{*}\mathbin{\circ}F_{A,V}\mathbin{\circ}\sigma_{\lhd}^{*})(p_{\bm{x}},\mathord{-})_{*}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=G_{A,V}(p_{\bm{x}},\mathord{-},{\lhd})_{*}(\lambda),

where the second equality follows from equivariance of
\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}
(see
Lemma
9.4
) and since the action on
[0,1]
is trivial.
∎

Our goal is now to upgrade the correctness of the sampling via
G_{A,V}
to joint
distributions. To do so, for
A\in r(\mathbb{N}_{+})
and
c\leq\lvert A\rvert
,
let us define the natural “product” partial function

\displaystyle\widehat{G}_{A,c,V}\colon\prod_{B\in r(A,c-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})\times\prod_{C\in\binom{A}{c}}([0,1]\times{\mathcal{O}}_{C})

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup\prod_{C\in\binom{A}{c}}{\mathcal{S}}_{C,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})

by

\displaystyle\widehat{G}_{A,c,V}(p,u)_{C}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}G_{C,V}\bigl(\iota_{C,A}^{*}(p),u_{C}\bigr)

for every
C\in\binom{A}{c}
, every
p\in\prod_{B\in r(A,c-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})
and every
u\in\prod_{C^{\prime}\in\binom{A}{c}}([0,1]\times{\mathcal{O}}_{C^{\prime}})
.

The next claim is the desired upgrade of Claim
9.7.3
for
\widehat{G}_{A,c,V}
.

Claim 9.7.4.

Let
A\in r(\mathbb{N}_{+})
and let
c\leq\lvert A\rvert
. For every
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{A,c-1}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})

and every
C\in\binom{A}{c}
, let
p_{x}\in\prod_{B\in r(A,c-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})
and
p_{x,C}\in\prod_{B\in r(C,c-1)}{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})
be given by

\displaystyle(p_{x})_{B}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{B,V}\bigl(\iota_{B,A}^{*}(x)\bigr)\qquad(B\in r(A,c-1)),

\displaystyle(p_{x,C})_{B}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{B,V}\bigl(\iota_{B,C}^{*}(x)\bigr)\qquad(B\in r(C,c-1)).

Let further
\bm{x}
be picked in
{\mathcal{E}}_{A,c-1}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
according to
\mu^{(d)}
,
\bm{y}

be picked in
\prod_{C\in\binom{A}{c}}(X^{2}\times{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d})
according to

\bigotimes_{C\in\binom{A}{c}}(\mu^{2}\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{d}\nu_{C})
independently from
\bm{x}

and
\bm{u}
be picked in
\prod_{C\in\binom{A}{c}}([0,1]\times{\mathcal{O}}_{C})
according to

\bigotimes_{C\in\binom{A}{c}}(\lambda\otimes\nu_{C})
independently from
\bm{x}
.

Finally let

\displaystyle\bm{\widehat{G}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\widehat{G}_{A,c,V}(p_{\bm{x}},\bm{u}),

\displaystyle\bm{q}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\left(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{C,V}(\iota_{C,A}^{*}(\bm{x}),\bm{y}_{C})\;\middle|\;C\in\binom{A}{c}\right).

Then the following conditional distributions are the same:

(i)

\bm{\widehat{G}}
given
p_{\bm{x}}
,

(ii)

\bm{\widehat{G}}
given
\bm{x}
,

(iii)

\bm{q}
given
p_{\bm{x}}
,

(iv)

\bm{q}
given
\bm{x}
.

Proof of Claim
9.7.4
.

The fact that the conditional distributions of
\bm{\widehat{G}}
given
p_{\bm{x}}
or given

\bm{x}
are the same follows since the definition of
\bm{\widehat{G}}
implies that it is
conditionally independent from
\bm{x}
given
p_{\bm{x}}
. In fact, the definition of

\bm{\widehat{G}}
along with Claim
9.7.3
implies that this conditional
distribution is exactly

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{C\in\binom{A}{c}}\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{C,V}(p_{\bm{x},C}).

The fact that the conditional distributions of
\bm{q}
given
p_{\bm{x}}
or given
\bm{x}
are
the same is exactly strong amalgamation, Lemma
9.5
(iii)
:
\bm{q}
is
conditionally independent from
\bm{x}
given
p_{\bm{x}}
. Since each coordinate
\bm{q}_{C}
is
clearly
(\iota_{C,A}^{*}(\bm{x}),\bm{y}_{C})
-measurable, it follows that the coordinates of

\bm{q}
are conditionally independent given
\bm{x}
.

In turn, this conditional independence along with item
(d)
in
Definition
9.2
implies that the conditional distribution of
\bm{q}
given
\bm{x}

is exactly

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{C\in\binom{A}{c}}\Theta^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{C,V}(p_{\bm{x},C}),

concluding the proof of the claim.
∎

We next sample all amalgamating types inductively and use Claim
9.7.4
to show
that this gives the correct distribution. First, we let
\bm{z}
be picked at random in

{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathbb{N}_{+}}^{(1)}(\widetilde{\Omega})
(recall that
\widetilde{\Omega}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}([0,1],\lambda)
)
according to
\lambda^{(1)}
and for each
A\in r(\mathbb{N}_{+})
, we define inductively the random

(A,V,\ell)
-amalgamating type

\displaystyle\bm{p}_{A}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}G_{A,V}\bigl((\bm{p}_{B}\mid B\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)),\bm{z}_{A}\bigr).

(9.10)

Claim 9.7.5.

If
A\in r(\mathbb{N}_{+})
and
\bm{x}
is sampled in
{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
according to
\mu^{(d)}
,
then
\bm{p}_{A}
and
\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(\bm{x})
have the same distribution.

Proof of Claim
9.7.5
.

For each
B\in r(A)
, let

\bm{q}_{B}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{B,V}(\iota_{B,A}^{*}(\bm{x}))
. Then it suffices to
show that for every
c\in[\lvert A\rvert]
, we have

\displaystyle\bigl(\bm{p}_{B}\mid B\in r(A,c)\bigr)

\displaystyle\sim\bigl(\bm{q}_{B}\mid B\in r(A,c)\bigr).

We prove this by induction in
c\in\{0,\ldots,\lvert A\rvert\}
.

The result is trivial when
c=0
. When
c>0
, by definition, we have

\displaystyle\left(\bm{p}_{C}\;\middle|\;C\in\binom{A}{c}\right)

\displaystyle=\widehat{G}_{A,c,V}\left(\bigl(\bm{p}_{B}\;\big|\;B\in r(A,c-1)\bigr),\left(\bm{z}_{C}\;\middle|\;C\in\binom{A}{c}\right)\right).

By inductive hypothesis, we know that the first argument of
\widehat{G}_{A,c,V}
above has the same
distribution as
(\bm{q}_{B}\mid B\in r(A,c-1))
, so we have

\bigl(\bm{p}_{B}\;\big|\;B\in r(A,c)\bigr)\\
\sim\left(\bigl(\bm{q}_{B}\;\big|\;B\in r(A,c-1)\bigr),\widehat{G}_{A,c,V}\left(\bigl(\bm{q}_{B}\;\big|\;B\in r(A,c-1)\bigr),\left(\bm{u}_{C}\;\middle|\;C\in\binom{A}{c}\right)\right)\right),

where
\bm{u}
is picked in
\prod_{C\in\binom{A}{c}}([0,1]\times{\mathcal{O}}_{C})
according to

\bigotimes_{C\in\binom{A}{c}}(\lambda\otimes\nu_{C})
, independently from
(\bm{q}_{B}\mid B\in r(A,c-1))
.

Conditioning on
(\bm{q}_{B}\mid B\in r(A,c-1))
and using Claim
9.7.4
, we
conclude that the distribution of the right-hand side is the same as the distribution of

(\bm{q}_{B}\mid B\in r(A,c))
, as desired.
∎

The inductive definition of
\bm{p}_{A}
in (
9.10
) implies the existence of measurable
partial functions

f_{A}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(1)}(\widetilde{\Omega})\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})
such that

\displaystyle\bm{p}_{A}

\displaystyle=f_{A}\bigl((\bm{z}_{B}\mid B\in r(A))\bigr)=f_{A}\bigl(\iota_{A,\mathbb{N}_{+}}(\bm{z})\bigr).

(9.11)

More precisely, the partial functions
f_{A}
are defined inductively as

\displaystyle f_{A}(z)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}G_{A,V}\biggl(\Bigl(f_{B}\bigl(\iota_{B,A}^{*}(z)\bigr)\mathrel{\Big|}B\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)\Bigr),z_{A}\biggr).

(9.12)

Note that a direct consequence of Claim
9.7.3
is that

\lambda^{(1)}(\dom(f_{A}))=1
. Also, yet again
f_{A}
inherits the property that if
\lvert A\rvert\leq\ell
, then
f_{A}
is the constant function that maps every point to the unique point in
the image of
\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}
. The next claim says that
f_{A}
also inherits
equivariance under bijections.

Claim 9.7.6.

If
\beta\colon A\to B
is a bijection, then the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}_{B}^{(1)}(\widetilde{\Omega})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}_{B,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(1)}(\widetilde{\Omega})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}_{A,V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{A}}

is weakly commutative.

Proof of Claim
9.7.6
.

The proof is by induction in
\lvert A\rvert
. Note that if

z\in\dom(f_{B})\cap\dom(f_{A}\mathbin{\circ}\beta^{*})
, then

\displaystyle f_{A}\bigl(\beta^{*}(z)\bigr)

\displaystyle=G_{A,V}\Biggl(\biggl(f_{C}\Bigl(\iota_{C,A}^{*}\bigl(\beta^{*}(z)\bigr)\Bigr)\mathrel{\bigg|}C\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)\biggr),z_{\beta(A)}\Biggr)

\displaystyle=G_{A,V}\Biggl(\biggl(f_{C}\Bigl(\beta\mathord{\downarrow}_{C}^{*}\bigl(\iota_{\beta(C),B}^{*}(z)\bigr)\Bigr)\mathrel{\bigg|}C\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)\biggr),z_{B}\Biggr)

\displaystyle=G_{A,V}\Biggl(\biggl(\beta\mathord{\downarrow}_{C}^{*}\Bigl(f_{\beta(C)}\bigl(\iota_{\beta(C),B}^{*}(z)\bigr)\Bigr)\mathrel{\bigg|}C\in r(A,\lvert A\rvert-1)\biggr),z_{B}\Biggr)

\displaystyle=G_{A,V}\Biggl(\beta^{*}\biggl(\Bigl(f_{D}\bigl(\iota_{D,B}^{*}(z)\bigr)\mathrel{\Big|}D\in r(B,\lvert B\rvert-1)\Bigr),z_{B}\biggr)\Biggr)

\displaystyle=\beta^{*}\Biggl(G_{B,V}\biggl(\Bigl(f_{D}\bigl(\iota_{D,B}^{*}(z)\bigr)\mathrel{\Big|}D\in r(B,\lvert B\rvert-1)\Bigr),z_{B}\biggr)\Biggr)

\displaystyle=\beta^{*}\bigl(f_{B}(z)\bigr),

where the second equality follows from
\beta\mathbin{\circ}\iota_{C,A}=\iota_{\beta(C),B}\mathbin{\circ}\beta\mathord{\downarrow}_{C}
, the third equality follows from inductive hypothesis and
the fifth equality follows from equivariance of
G_{A,V}
under bijections (see
Claim
9.7.2
).
∎

We can finally define the
1
-Euclidean structure
{\mathcal{H}}
over
\widetilde{\Omega}=([0,1],\lambda)

that we will show to be
\ell
-independent and satisfy
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}=\phi_{\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}=\phi_{\mathcal{N}}
:
for each
P\in{\mathcal{L}}
, we let

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{P}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\Bigl\{z\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k(P)}^{(1)}(\widetilde{\Omega})\;\Big|\;M^{\amlg,\ell}_{k(P),V}\bigl(f_{k(P)}(z)\bigr)\vDash P(1,\ldots,k(P))\Bigr\}.

(Recall that
M^{\amlg,\ell}_{k(P),V}\colon{\mathcal{S}}_{k(P),V}^{(d),\amlg,\ell}(\Omega^{2})\to{\mathcal{K}}_{k(P)}

is the function that maps amalgamating types to their underlying model.)

Note that since
f_{A}
is constant when
\lvert A\rvert\leq\ell
, from the inductive definition of

f_{A}
in (
9.12
), we get that
{\mathcal{H}}
is
\ell
-independent. It remains to show that

\phi_{\mathcal{H}}=\phi_{\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}
.

Recalling that
\bm{z}
is sampled in
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathbb{N}_{+}}^{(1)}(\widetilde{\Omega})
, to show that

\phi_{\mathcal{H}}=\phi_{\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}
, it suffices to show that for every
A\in r(\mathbb{N}_{+})
, we have

\displaystyle M^{\mathcal{H}}_{A}(\iota_{A,\mathbb{N}_{+}}^{*}(\bm{z}))

\displaystyle\sim M^{\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A}(\bm{x}),

where
\bm{x}
is sampled in
{\mathcal{E}}_{A}^{(d)}(\Omega^{2})
according to
\mu^{(d)}
.

In turn, by Lemma
9.4
and Claim
9.7.5
, we have

\displaystyle M^{\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=M^{\amlg,\ell}_{A,V}\bigl(\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}_{A,V}(\bm{x})\bigr)\sim M^{\amlg,\ell}_{A,V}(\bm{p}_{A}),

so it suffices to show
M^{\mathcal{H}}_{A}(\iota_{A,\mathbb{N}_{+}}^{*}(\bm{z}))=M^{\amlg,\ell}_{A,V}(\bm{p}_{A})
with
probability
1
. Going one step further, it suffices to show that for every
P\in{\mathcal{L}}
and every

\beta\colon[k(P)]\to A
injective, we have

\displaystyle\Bigl(M^{\mathcal{H}}_{A}\bigl(\iota_{A,\mathbb{N}_{+}}^{*}(\bm{z})\bigr)\vDash P(\beta)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\iff\bigl(M^{\amlg,\ell}_{A,V}(\bm{p}_{A})\vDash P(\beta)\bigr)

(9.13)

with probability
1
.

Recalling that
\overline{\beta}
denotes the function obtained from
\beta
by restricting its
co-domain to
\im(\beta)
(so
\beta=\iota_{\im(\beta),A}\mathbin{\circ}\overline{\beta}
, hence

\iota_{A,\mathbb{N}_{+}}\mathbin{\circ}\beta=\iota_{\im(\beta),\mathbb{N}_{+}}\mathbin{\circ}\overline{\beta}
), note that

\displaystyle\Bigl(M^{\mathcal{H}}_{A}\bigl(\iota_{A,\mathbb{N}_{+}}^{*}(\bm{z})\bigr)\vDash P(\beta)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\iff\beta^{*}\bigl(\iota_{A,\mathbb{N}_{+}}^{*}(\bm{z})\bigr)\in{\mathcal{H}}_{P}

\displaystyle\iff\Biggl(M^{\amlg,\ell}_{k(P),V}\biggl(f_{k(P)}\Bigl(\beta^{*}\bigl(\iota_{A,\mathbb{N}_{+}}^{*}(\bm{z})\bigr)\Bigr)\biggr)\vDash P(1,\ldots,k(P))\Biggr)

\displaystyle\iff\Biggl(M^{\amlg,\ell}_{k(P),V}\biggl(f_{k(P)}\Bigl(\overline{\beta}^{*}\bigl(\iota_{\im(\beta),\mathbb{N}_{+}}^{*}(\bm{z})\bigr)\Bigr)\biggr)\vDash P(1,\ldots,k(P))\Biggr)

\displaystyle\iff\Biggl(M^{\amlg,\ell}_{k(P),V}\biggl(\overline{\beta}^{*}\Bigl(f_{\im(\beta)}\bigl(\iota_{\im(\beta),\mathbb{N}_{+}}^{*}(\bm{z})\bigr)\Bigr)\biggr)\vDash P(1,\ldots,k(P))\Biggr)

\displaystyle\iff\Biggl(\overline{\beta}^{*}\biggl(M^{\amlg,\ell}_{\im(\beta),V}\Bigl(f_{\im(\beta)}\bigl(\iota_{\im(\beta),\mathbb{N}_{+}}^{*}(\bm{z})\bigr)\Bigr)\biggr)\vDash P(1,\ldots,k(P))\Biggr)

\displaystyle\iff\Bigl(\overline{\beta}^{*}\bigl(M^{\amlg,\ell}_{\im(\beta),V}(\bm{p}_{\im(\beta)})\bigr)\vDash P(1,\ldots,k(P))\Bigr),

where the fourth equivalence follows from Claim
9.7.6
, the fifth equivalence follows
from equivariance of
M^{\amlg,\ell}
(Lemma
9.4
) and the sixth equivalence follows
from (
9.11
).

By Claim
9.7.5
and equivariance of
\mathbbm{p}^{\amlg,\ell,\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}

(Lemma
9.4
), we know that with probability
1
we have
\bm{p}_{\im(\beta)}=\iota_{\im(\beta),A}^{*}(\bm{p}_{A})
, so with probability
1
we have

\displaystyle\Bigl(\overline{\beta}^{*}\bigl(M^{\amlg,\ell}_{\im(\beta),V}(\bm{p}_{\im(\beta)})\bigr)\vDash P(1,\ldots,k(P))\Bigr)

\displaystyle\iff\biggl(\overline{\beta}^{*}\Bigl(M^{\amlg,\ell}_{\im(\beta),V}\bigl(\iota_{\im(\beta),A}^{*}(\bm{p}_{A})\bigr)\Bigr)\vDash P(1,\ldots,k(P))\biggr)

\displaystyle\iff\biggl(\overline{\beta}^{*}\Bigl(\iota_{\im(\beta),A}^{*}\bigl(M^{\amlg,\ell}_{A,V}(\bm{p}_{A})\bigr)\Bigr)\vDash P(1,\ldots,k(P))\biggr)

\displaystyle\iff\Bigl(\beta^{*}\bigl(M^{\amlg,\ell}_{A,V}(\bm{p}_{A}))\bigr)\vDash P(1,\ldots,k(P))\Bigr)

\displaystyle\iff\bigl(M^{\amlg,\ell}_{A,V}(\bm{p}_{A})\vDash P(\beta)\bigr),

where the second equivalence follows from equivariance of
M^{\amlg,\ell}

(Lemma
9.4
).

Thus (
9.13
) holds, so
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}=\phi_{\widehat{{\mathcal{N}}}}=\phi_{\mathcal{N}}
, concluding the proof
of the proposition.
∎

As previously mentioned in Section
2.3
, we can now derive Theorem
2.3

from Proposition
9.7
and Corollary
3.8
:

Corollary 9.8
(Theorem
2.3
)
.

\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]\implies\texttt{Independence}[\ell^{\prime}]
whenever
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
satisfy
\ell^{\prime}<\ell
.

Proof.

By Proposition
9.7
, we know that every
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
limit is

1
-
\ell
-independent, in particular,
\ast
-
\ell
-independent, so
Corollary
3.8
implies that it satisfies
\texttt{Independence}[\ell^{\prime}]
.
∎

10
Simulating order variables with the quasirandom orientation

In this section, we prove the
implication
(iii)
\implies
(iv)
of
Theorem
2.2
, which essentially amounts to the almost trivial fact that we can use a
quasirandom orientation to simulate order variables. The proof of this implication is mostly about
bookkeeping.

Proposition 10.1
(Theorem
2.2
(iii)
\implies
(iv)
)
.

If
{\mathcal{N}}
is an
\ell
-independent
1
-
T
-on, then there exist a canonical theory

T^{\prime}
, an
\ell
-independent limit
\psi\in\operatorname{Hom}^{+}({\mathcal{A}}[T^{\prime}],\mathbb{R})
and a quantifier-free definition
I\colon T\leadsto T^{\prime}\cup T_{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-Orientation}}
such that
\phi_{\mathcal{N}}=I^{*}(\psi\otimes\phi^{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-qrO}})
.

Proof.

Let
\Omega=(X,\mu)
be the underlying space of
{\mathcal{N}}
, let
{\mathcal{L}}
be the language of
T
and let

k({\mathcal{L}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\max\{k(Q)\mid Q\in{\mathcal{L}}\}\cup\{1\}
.

For each predicate symbol
Q\in{\mathcal{L}}
and each
{\lhd}\in\prod_{A\in\binom{[k(Q)]}{\ell+1}}{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
,
we introduce a predicate symbol
Q_{\lhd}
of arity
k(Q_{\lhd})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}k(Q)
and we let
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
be the
language consisting of all such predicate symbols.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, define the function

\displaystyle\pi_{n}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{(1)}\times\prod_{A\in\binom{[n]}{\ell+1}}{\mathcal{O}}_{A}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{n}^{(1)}

by letting

\displaystyle\pi_{n}(x,{\ll},{\lhd})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(x,{\ll^{\lhd}})\qquad\left(x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{n},{\ll}\in\prod_{A\in r(n)}{\mathcal{O}}_{A},{\lhd}\in\prod_{A\in\binom{[n]}{\ell+1}}{\mathcal{O}}_{A}\right),

where

\displaystyle{\ll^{\lhd}_{A}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\begin{cases}\ll_{A},&if $\lvert A\rvert\neq\ell+1$,\\
\lhd_{A},&if $\lvert A\rvert=\ell+1$,\end{cases}

that is,
\pi_{n}
simply replaces the order variables of
(x,\ll)
corresponding to sets of size

\ell+1
by those in
\lhd
.

We now define a
1
-Euclidean structure
{\mathcal{H}}
in
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
over
\Omega
by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{Q_{\lhd}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{(x,{\ll})\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k(Q)}^{(1)}\mid\pi_{k(Q)}(x,{\ll},{\lhd})\in{\mathcal{N}}_{Q}\}\qquad\left(Q\in{\mathcal{L}},{\lhd}\in\prod_{A\in\binom{[k(Q)]}{\ell+1}}{\mathcal{O}}_{A}\right).

We also let
T^{\prime}
be the (canonical) universal theory whose finite models are precisely those
(canonical)
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
-structures
M
such that
t_{\operatorname{ind}}(M,{\mathcal{H}})>0
(such a universal theory exists as
this set of structures is closed under substructures). In particular,
{\mathcal{H}}
is a
1
-
T^{\prime}
-on.

Note that since
{\mathcal{N}}
is
\ell
-independent and the functions
\pi_{n}
replace the order
coordinates of order
\ell+1
, it follows that
{\mathcal{H}}
is
\ell
-independent and does not depend on
coordinates in
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
with
\lvert A\rvert=\ell+1
, so by
Lemma
3.6
(iv)
, we know that
\psi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\phi_{\mathcal{H}}

satisfies
\texttt{Independence}[\ell]
.

Let now
P
be the unique predicate symbol in the language of
T_{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-Orientation}}
and recall that the
standard representation of the quasirandom
(\ell+1)
-orientation
{\mathcal{N}}^{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-qrO}}
over

\Omega
is given by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}^{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-qrO}}_{P}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{(x,{\lhd})\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\ell+1}^{(1)}\mid{\lhd}_{[\ell+1]}={\leq}\},

where
{\leq}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell+1}
is the usual order on
[\ell+1]
.

Define the
1
-Euclidean structure
{\mathcal{G}}
in
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}\cup\{P\}
over
\Omega
by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}_{P}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}{\mathcal{N}}^{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-qrO}}_{P},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}_{Q_{\lhd}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}{\mathcal{H}}_{Q_{\lhd}}\qquad\left(Q\in{\mathcal{L}},{\lhd}\in\prod_{A\in\binom{[k(Q)]}{\ell+1}}{\mathcal{O}}_{A}\right).

Note that since
{\mathcal{H}}
does not depend on coordinates in
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
with
\lvert A\rvert=\ell+1
and

{\mathcal{N}}^{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-qrO}}
is completely determined by such coordinates, it follows that
\phi_{\mathcal{G}}=\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}^{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-qrO}}}\otimes\phi_{\mathcal{H}}=\phi^{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-qrO}}\otimes\psi
.

We now define the quantifier-free definition
I\colon T\leadsto T^{\prime}\cup T_{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-Orientation}}
by

I(Q)(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k(Q)})\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\bigvee_{{\lhd}\in\prod_{A\in\binom{[k(Q)]}{\ell+1}}{\mathcal{O}}_{A}}\left(Q_{\lhd}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k(Q)})\land\bigwedge_{A\in\binom{[k(Q)]}{\ell+1}}P(x_{\alpha_{\lhd_{A}}(1)},\ldots,x_{\alpha_{\lhd_{A}}(k(Q))})\right),

where
\alpha_{\lhd_{A}}\colon[\ell+1]\to A
is the unique bijection such that

\displaystyle\alpha_{\lhd_{A}}^{*}({\lhd_{A}})

\displaystyle={\leq}.

Note that when
k(Q)<\ell+1
, then there is a unique element in the empty product

\prod_{A\in\binom{[k(Q)]}{\ell+1}}{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
and the conjunction

\bigwedge_{A\in\binom{[k(Q)]}{\ell+1}}
is empty (which is to be interpreted as always true).

We have to check that
I^{*}(\phi^{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-qrO}}\otimes\psi)=\phi_{\mathcal{N}}
and that
I
is indeed a
quantifier-free definition (i.e., it maps axioms of
T
to theorems of
T^{\prime}\cup T_{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-Orientation}}
).

To check the former, it suffices to show that
I^{*}({\mathcal{G}})={\mathcal{N}}
. In turn, it suffices to check that
for every
Q\in{\mathcal{L}}
, we have
T(I(Q),{\mathcal{G}})={\mathcal{N}}_{Q}
.

When
k(Q)<\ell+1
, this is obvious as there is a unique element
\lhd
in the empty product

\prod_{A\in\binom{[k(Q)]}{\ell+1}}{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
and we have
T(I(Q),{\mathcal{G}})={\mathcal{G}}_{Q_{\lhd}}={\mathcal{H}}_{Q_{\lhd}}={\mathcal{N}}_{Q}
.

Suppose then that
k(Q)\geq\ell+1
and note that

\displaystyle T\left(\bigwedge_{A\in\binom{[k(Q)]}{\ell+1}}P(x_{\alpha_{\lhd_{A}}(1)},\ldots,x_{\alpha_{\lhd_{A}}(k(Q))}),{\mathcal{G}}\right)

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{A\in\binom{[k(Q)]}{\ell+1}}\bigl((\iota_{A,[k(Q)]}\mathbin{\circ}\alpha_{\lhd_{A}})^{*}\bigr)^{-1}({\mathcal{N}}^{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-qrO}}_{P})

\displaystyle=\left\{(x,{\ll})\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k(Q)}^{(1)}\;\middle|\;\forall A\in\binom{k(Q)}{\ell+1},\alpha_{\lhd_{A}}^{*}({\ll}_{A})={\leq}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{(x,{\ll})\in{\mathcal{E}}_{k(Q)}^{(1)}\;\middle|\;\forall A\in\binom{[k(Q)]}{\ell+1},{\ll}_{A}={\lhd_{A}}\right\},

from which we conclude that

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!T(I(Q),{\mathcal{G}})

\displaystyle=\left\{(x,{\ll})\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\ell+1}^{(1)}\;\middle|\;\exists{\lhd}\in\prod_{A\in\binom{[k(Q)]}{\ell+1}}{\mathcal{O}}_{A},\left(\pi_{k(Q)}(x,{\ll},{\lhd})\in{\mathcal{N}}_{Q}\land\forall A\in\binom{[k(Q)]}{\ell+1},{\ll}_{A}={\lhd_{A}}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{N}}_{Q},

as desired.

We now check that
I\colon T\leadsto T^{\prime}\cup T_{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-Orientation}}
is indeed a quantifier-free
definition. We certainly know that
I
is a quantifier-free definition of the form
T_{\mathcal{L}}\leadsto T^{\prime}\cup T_{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-Orientation}}
as
T_{\mathcal{L}}
is the pure canonical theory. To show that it is also a
quantifier-free definition of the form
T\leadsto T^{\prime}\cup T_{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-Orientation}}
, it suffices to show that
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and every
M\in{\mathcal{K}}_{n}[T^{\prime}\cup T_{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-Orientation}}]
, we have
I^{*}_{n}(M)\in{\mathcal{K}}_{n}[T]
.

By definition of
T^{\prime}
, we know that
t_{\operatorname{ind}}(M\mathord{|}_{{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}},{\mathcal{H}})>0
. Since

t_{\operatorname{ind}}(M\mathord{|}_{\{P\}},{\mathcal{N}}^{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-qrO}})>0
(as every
(\ell+1)
-orientation has positive
density in
\phi^{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-qrO}}
) and
\phi_{\mathcal{G}}=\phi_{{\mathcal{N}}^{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-qrO}}}\otimes\phi_{\mathcal{H}}
,
we get

\displaystyle t_{\operatorname{ind}}(M,{\mathcal{G}})

\displaystyle=t_{\operatorname{ind}}(M\mathord{|}_{\{P\}},{\mathcal{N}}^{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-qrO}})\cdot t_{\operatorname{ind}}(M\mathord{|}_{{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}},{\mathcal{H}})>0,

so
\phi_{\mathcal{G}}(M)>0
.

On the other hand, since
I^{*}({\mathcal{G}})={\mathcal{N}}
, we get

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathcal{N}}\bigl(I^{*}_{n}(M)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\phi_{I^{*}({\mathcal{G}})}\bigl(I^{*}_{n}(M)\bigr)=I^{*}(\phi_{\mathcal{G}})\bigl(I^{*}_{n}(M)\bigr)\geq\phi_{\mathcal{G}}(M)>0,

that is,
I^{*}_{n}(M)
has positive density in
{\mathcal{N}}
, so it must be a model of
T
as
{\mathcal{N}}
is a
1
-
T
-on.
∎

11
Concluding remarks and open problems

In this work, we have closed the final implications of the poset of natural quasirandomness
introduced in
[
CR23
]
, namely, we proved in Theorem
2.2
that

\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]\implies\texttt{Independence}[\ell^{\prime}]
whenever
\ell>\ell^{\prime}
. In fact, we proved a stronger
result in Theorem
2.2
that gives further alternative characterizations of

\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
in terms of
\ast
-
\ell
-independence and shows that the “gap” between

\texttt{Independence}[\ell]
and
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
is precisely a quasirandom
(\ell+1)
-orientation
(item
(iv)
).

Our new characterizations of
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
also make progress in some of the other questions
asked in
[
CR23
]
, on which we elaborate now.

In
[
CR23
, Theorem 3.10]
, it was shown that
\phi\in\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
is also equivalent to

\phi\otimes\psi_{\operatorname{lin}}\in\texttt{UInduce}[\ell]
where
\psi_{\operatorname{lin}}\in\operatorname{Hom}^{+}({\mathcal{A}}[T_{\operatorname{LinOrder}}],\mathbb{R})
is the unique limit of
linear orders and it was asked whether a different kind of converse of this result holds. Namely, it
was asked whether every
\phi\in\texttt{UInduce}[\ell]
is of the form
I^{*}(\psi\otimes\psi_{\operatorname{lin}})
for some

\psi\in\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
and some quantifier-free definition
I\colon T\leadsto T^{\prime}\cup T_{\operatorname{LinOrder}}
, in
other words, similarly to Theorem
2.2
(iv)
, the “gap”
between
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
and
\texttt{UInduce}[\ell]
is precisely a linear order. Since the quasirandom

(\ell+1)
-orientation
\phi^{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-qrO}}
can itself be written as
J^{*}(\psi\otimes\psi_{\operatorname{lin}})
for
some
\psi\in\texttt{Independence}[\ell]
and a quantifier-free interpretation

J\colon T_{(\ell+1)\operatorname{-Orientation}}\leadsto T\cup T_{\operatorname{LinOrder}}
, by our
Theorem
2.2
(iv)
, the aforementioned question of
[
CR23
]

is equivalent to asking whether every
\phi\in\texttt{UInduce}[\ell]
is in fact of the form

I^{*}(\psi\otimes\psi_{\operatorname{lin}})
for some
\psi\in\texttt{Independence}[\ell]
and some quantifier-free definition

I\colon T\leadsto T^{\prime}\cup T_{\operatorname{LinOrder}}
(i.e., the “larger gap” between
\texttt{UInduce}[\ell]
and

\texttt{Independence}[\ell]
is precisely a linear order).

In
[
CR23
, Section 10]
, the notion of
B
-compatibility
was introduced as a common
generalization of rank and
\texttt{Independence}[\ell]
: namely, for
B\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{+}
, we say that
\phi

is
B
-compatible
if it has a representation
\phi=\phi_{\mathcal{N}}
where
{\mathcal{N}}
is a theon that
depends only on coordinates indexed by sets
A
with
\lvert A\rvert\in B
. Thus, rank at most

\ell
amounts to
[\ell]
-compatibility and
\texttt{Independence}[\ell]
amounts to

(\mathbb{N}_{+}\setminus[\ell])
-compatibility. It was already observed in
[
CR23
]
that if

B\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{+}
, then every
B
-compatible limit is uniquely coupleable with every

(\mathbb{N}_{+}\setminus B)
-compatible limit (see
[
Cor21
, Theorem 4.8.2]
for a proof). In turn, since

\texttt{Independence}[\ell]\neq\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
, we also know that the set of limits that are uniquely
coupleable with all
B
-compatible limits can be strictly larger than the set of all

(\mathbb{N}_{+}\setminus B)
-compatible limits.

On the other hand, since Theorem
2.2
says that
\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
is equivalent to

\ast
-
\ell
-independence, it is natural to consider the version of compatibility that allows for
order variables. Namely, let us say that
\phi
is
\ast
-
B
-compatible
(
B\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{+}
)
if it has a representation
\phi=\phi_{\mathcal{N}}
where
{\mathcal{N}}
is a
d
-theon (for some
d\in\mathbb{N}
) that
depends only on coordinates indexed by sets
A
with
\lvert A\rvert\in B
. Thus, rank at most

\ell
amounts to
\ast
-
[\ell]
-compatibility and
\ast
-
\ell
-independence (i.e.,

\texttt{UCouple}[\ell]
) amounts to
\ast
-
(\mathbb{N}_{+}\setminus[\ell])
-compatibility. Is it then true that

\phi
is uniquely coupleable with all
\ast
-
B
-compatible limits if and only if
\phi
is

\ast
-
(\mathbb{N}_{+}\setminus B)
-compatible? The backward direction is a straightforward generalization
of
[
Cor21
, Theorem 4.8.2]
and Theorem
2.2
says that the forward direction holds
when
B=[\ell]
for some
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.
